# Acts

# ANNOTATIONS ON THE ACTS OF THE APOSTLES

## THE ARGUMENT

The penman of this Scripture was Luke the evangelist (as appears in the first words hereof) for the most part, an eyewitness of these things he records, being constantly a fellow-laborer with Paul.

His purpose in writing this story was (as he intimates in his first preface) that the church might have the certain knowledge of Christ, his gospel and kingdom, that our faith might not be built on the uncertain reports of pretenders to truth; as also, that we might know how God fulfilled his promise in calling the Gentiles, and the apostles, their charge and office, by preaching the gospel to all nations: and that he might forearm all the faithful, by discovering the rage and malice of Satan, persecuting the true ministers and professors of the gospel, even from the first publication thereof: and the gracious providence of God, preserving and increasing his church, in the midst of all her pressures, giving us an evident assurance of the truth of the gospel, not only in that the powers of hell, all the machinations of Satan, and malice of wicked men, could never prevail against it; but also, that God’s providence, by these oppositions, confirmed it to the hearts of Christians, in the constancy of those that suffered persecution for it.

This story sets out:

1. The truth of Christ after his ascension; performing his promise to the apostles, sending the Holy Ghost the Comforter, unto them.

2. The malice of Satan, laboring to suppress the gospel, by raising up tumults, conspiracies, slanders, persecutions, and (where he could not prevail by violence) heretics to corrupt the truth thereof.

3. The vigilant providence and power of God over his church, overthrowing the malicious enterprises of the enemies, and filling the world with the sound of the gospel, by so few unlearned men.

4. The many divine sermons of the apostles and disciples, concerning the death, resurrection, ascension and kingdom of Christ, the mercy of God in the free remission of our sins by him, the immortality to come: care that ministers ought to have of Christ’s flock, repentance and fear of God, and other principal grounds of faith, that we bearing our trials patiently (knowing that the cross usually accompanies the gospel) and resting confident in his mercy, who is unchangeably the same good God to his now, that he was to the first Christians, we may at last attain the end of our hopes, the salvation of our bodies and souls. Which he grant us, who gave so great a price for our redemption, as the invaluable blood of Christ Jesus. Amen.

## CHAPTER 1

**Verse 1.** *{The former treatise have I made}* That is, the gospel by Saint Luke.

*{all that Jesus began}* This is, not simply to be understood, of all that Jesus did and said; for John chap. 21. 25. declares it impossible; but of some things, which Jesus began to do and teach, that is, as Chrysostome interprets, *from the beginning to the end*; from the story of Christ’s birth, unto the relation of his resurrection, Luke wrote in the gospel: but yet he says not πάντα, all things, but περὶ πάντων, concerning all, that is, ἁδρομερῶς ἢ παχυμερῶς, or παχυτέρως, *crassâ Minervâ*, grossly, or summarily, to wit, of all that which the Holy Ghost thought necessary to dictate. *Chrysost.* To reveal and make known unto me concerning the gospel of Christ, and which his pleasure was, that I should write, for the edification of his church.

*{both to do and to teach}* That is, Christ’s doctrine, and miracles for the confirmation of the same.

**V. 2.** *{taken up}* Into heaven, by his ascension, Luke 24. 51. which story he now more particularly prosecutes, as also those things which, in further order to edification of his church, followed the same in the actions of the apostles, and the success of their preaching according to the commandment of Christ, Mat. 28. 19, 20. Mark 16. 15.

*{after that he through the Holy Ghost had given commandments, &c.}* Some otherwise join these words (*through the holy Ghost*) with the following, (*whom he had chosen*) so the Syriac has it, *quos elegit in spiritu sancto*: *whom he chose by the holy Ghost*, both are true, therefore here needs no controversy: the rule is, that the works of the holy Trinity, *ad extra*, are common to the three persons; so the Father sanctifies, John 10. 36. the Son sanctifies, John 17. 19. 1 Cor. 6. 11. and the Holy Ghost sanctifies, Rom. 15. 16. and so the Father calls, the Son calls, and the Holy Ghost calls: so the Father commands, the Son commands, and the Holy Ghost commands: the first reading teaches, that the apostles’ commission to preach and do miracles was from the Holy Ghost, and therefore their word is to be valued as his word and infallible truth: the second way of reading shows, that the apostles’ calling or election to their ministry was not of any human, but divine authority: neither is the calling and ordination of any faithful minister grounded on human, but divine authority wherein albeit God uses the ministry of man, as in imposition of hands in their consecration, or presentation, institution or induction, into some particular cure; yet as God alone can give them ability to minister, and power and success in their ministering, so he only calls thereto, (or they are not rightly called, but are hirelings and intruders: See Act. 20. 28.) and their authority to preach depends not on the authority of man but God: however the ordinance of God in men of authority thereto, may not be despised, nor can without contempt of God himself: Luke 10. 16. so then we may understand this place thus, that God to the apostles gave command to preach the gospel, and execute that function and charge which he had given them.

**V. 3.** *{by many infallible proofs}* Τεκμηρίοις the word is of τέκμαρ, an end, and imports such undoubted signs, as necessarily, and constantly importing one and the same thing (*Arist. Rhet.* l. 1.) conclude and put an end to any doubt, or controversy; as to speak, walk, or eat and drink, are τεκμήρια, undoubted signs, and proof of life, &c. See *Quintilian* l. 5. c. 19. *Suidas* gives it by ἀπόδειξις, a demonstration: such proofs of Christ’s resurrection were, their seeing Christ, talking, eating with him, and touching him after the same.

*{forty days}* Christ would not presently ascend into heaven after his resurrection, that he might assure his apostles of the truth thereof, by his conversing with them so long at certain times.

*{pertaining to the kingdom of God}* What the apostles were afterward to deliver to the world, either in preaching, or writing, whereby God may reign in us here by grace; and such as concern our being with him in future glory.

**V. 4.** *{assembled together with them}* Conversing with them; having gathered them together (who were dispersed with fear) that they might now be witnesses of his ascension into heaven.

*{not depart}* Or *separate themselves*. The Greek word μὴ χωρίζεσθαι, signifies, both that they should not divide or disperse themselves, nor depart from Jerusalem; where he would have them receive that admirable gift of tongues, as Chap. 2. because at the feast there used to be a very great confluence of people from several parts of the world, and many nations who might be competent witnesses of their speaking of diverse tongues, which in some more private place they had not had; and that by this means, they who were strangers (of several nations) at Jerusalem, returning home again, and reporting that which they heard and saw, might so far prepare those nations for their reception of the gospel.

*{but wait for the promise}* That which the Father had promised; that is, the Holy Ghost; as verse 5.

*{which, saith he, ye have heard of me}* Which I have told you of. Luke 24. 49. John 14. 15. & 16.

**V. 5.** *{For John truly}* Mat. 3. 11. Mark 1. 8. Luke 3. 16. John 1. 26. Acts 11. 16. & 19. 1.

*{with the Holy Ghost}* With the graces of God’s Holy Spirit: ἐν πνεύματι ἁγίῳ in the Holy Ghost, for διὰ πνεύματος ἁγίου, *through the holy Ghost*: or πνεύματι ἁγίῳ with the Holy Ghost: an usual Hebraism.

**V. 6.** *{the kingdom to Israel}* The secular kingdom and worldly prosperity, which they imagined the Messias should set them up. See Luke 24. 21.

**V. 7.** *{not for you to know the times and seasons}* The opportunities for the works of God, which he reserves to himself.

**V. 8.** *{but ye shall receive}* See chap. 2. 1.

*{power, after the Holy Ghost is come upon you}* Or *the power of the holy Ghost coming upon you*: gifts of tongues, healing, interpretation, boldness to preach, enlarged hearts, &c.

*{uttermost part of the earth}* Both to the Jews and Gentiles, according to their commission. Mark 16. 15.

**V. 9.** *{and when he had spoken}* Luke 24. 51. they saw him risen, not rising; and ascending, not ascended; the one perfected, the other begun.

*{a cloud received him, &c.}* The apostles looked after him ascending, until a cloud intercepted their sight of him.

**V. 10.** *{two men stood by them in white apparel}* Two Angels in the shape of men. See Mark 16. 5. Luke 24. 4. John 20. 12.

**V. 11.** *{into heaven}* To which he is gone to take possession for us, by carrying thither the same flesh and blood of which we consist.

*{shall so come}* At the last day, he shall locally come, in the same body, manifestly appearing in the clouds. 1 Thess. 4. 16, 17. Mat. 24. 38.

**V. 12.** *{a sabbath day’s journey}* How far it was lawful to walk on the Sabbath day, the law of God determined not; but their tradition limited it to two thousand cubits, or about two miles. John 11. 18. it appears, that Bethany was fifteen furlongs, that is, almost two miles from Jerusalem; and Luke 24. 50. So far did Christ lead them out, when he would ascend, which is here called a Sabbath day’s journey: so that either Christ having taken leave at Bethany, came back thence with them, and ascended from the mount of Olives, which lay between it and Jerusalem; or else the *sabbath-days* journey here mentioned, must be two miles wanting one furlong; though some learned authors say otherwise.

**V. 13.** *{they went up into an upper room}* εἰς τὸ ὑπερῷον, a room in the top of the house, where they might be freest from distraction by company coming in and out, and also most private and secure from the Jews, who having put their Master to death, would not spare any that should confess him to be the Christ.

*{Peter, and James, and John, &c.}* Probably the evangelist recounts their names, to show that Christ had not rejected them, for their forsaking him in his suffering; but that they were restored to their former office and dignity, that none might derogate from the authority of their preaching or writing.

*{and Judas brother James}* Ἰούδας Ἰακώβου: *Judas of James*, an usual *ellipsis*; the Syriac supplies it with בר *bar*: *son of James*; it may be both his father’s and brother’s name was James; it was the same who is called *Lebbeus*, and *Thaddeus*. Mat. 10. 3. Mark 3. 18. Jude 1. his relation to James is here mentioned to distinguish him from the other Judas the traitor.

**V. 14.** *{continued with one accord, &c.}* The word is of καρτερεῖν, signifying a continuance in performing or suffering something, with an invincible and strong constancy.

*{in prayer and supplication}* Their hazards were then very great; their implacable enemies about them, many, and those also in authority: Christ had often warned them to watch and pray, lest they entered into temptation; therefore thus take they up the armor of Christians, unanimous prayer; what they prayed, is not mentioned; probably among other blessings; it was for deliverance from their enemies, as chap. 4. 24. and for the coming of the Holy Ghost the Comforter, which he had promised them, and for which he commanded them to stay at Jerusalem.

*{with the women}* Those who went to follow the Lord (Luke 8. 2, 3.) and the apostles’ wives.

*{with his brethren}* That is, Christ’s kinsmen according to the flesh: possibly those of whom it was said, John 7. 5. that they believed not in him: God has his times to call.

**V. 15.** *{in those days}* While they attended at Jerusalem for the promised Comforter.

*{the number of names}* The number of the persons, to have been numbered by name.

**V. 16.** *{this scripture}* Psal. 41. 9.

*{which the Holy Ghost, &c.}* By him were all the holy Scriptures endited: 2 Tim. 3. 16. 2 Pet. 1. 21. Now lest any should be scandalized at the fall of Judas; Peter begins from the ancient prophecy, to show that it was the same which was long before prophesied of; all was foreseen and ordered by the providence of God, to accomplish the work of our redemption. See chap. 4. 28.

*{guide to them which took Jesus}* Mat. 26. 47, 48. John 18. 2, 3. For he undertook for thirty pieces of silver, for which the chief priests had contracted with him to deliver him unto them, and he well knowing the place, the garden which Christ with his disciples frequented, led the officers and soldiers thither to take him; and he gave them the signal by which they knew him.

**V. 17.** *{for he was numbered with us}* Christ had chosen 12 apostles, according to the number of the tribes of Israel, of whom they shall be judges, Mat. 19. 28. Therefore one falling away, they were to choose another, which election Peter here proposes unto the rest, and the church there assembled: he says Judas was *numbered with them*; that is, chosen to be one of the twelve.

*{obtained a part of this ministry}* That is, was appointed to the same ministry of the gospel with them.

**V. 18.** *{Now this man purchased a field}* Mat. 27. 7. This the evangelist Luke speaks in respect of the event: because with the thirty pieces (the price of blood) a field was bought by the priests, for the burial of strangers, Mat. 27. 6, 7, 8.

*{falling headlong}* Casting himself from the place where he hanged himself. Mat. 27. 5.

**V. 19.** *{insomuch as that field is called in their proper tongue, &c.}* The Syriac gives it, *in the tongue of that region*; thus it was publicly named in their own tongue: he says not in our tongue, but in their &c. to point out the consciousness of the authors of this impiety. See Mat. 27. 7, 8.

*{Aceldama}* *Haceldama*, or *Aceldama* is a name compounded of two Syriac words, חקל (which in the Chaldee and Syriac signifies a field, of the Hebrew root חלק *chalack*, *divisit in partes, hereditatem &c.* thence by a *metathesis* and transposition of letters חקל *chakel* a part, portion, or field; or possession of fields divided from others, Isai. 61. 7. Hos. 5. 7.) and דם, *dam* blood: Peter also might say it is called *in their proper tongue*, or dialect (See ch. 2. 8.) in regard he was a Galilean; their pronunciation somewhat differing from that about Jerusalem: therefore had they so urged that he was a Galilean, and so one of Christ’s disciples, for (said they) *thy speech bewrayeth thee*. Mat. 26. 73.

**V. 20.** *{let his habitation be desolate}* Psal. 69. 25.

*{his bishoprick let another take}* Or *his office*, or *charge* Psal. 109. 8; that which the Hebrew calls פקודה *pecudah*, (Numb. 3. 32.) *oversight*, or cure.

**V. 21.** *{went in and out among us}* Or *was conversant with us as man*; *and preached unto us*. See Deut. 31. 2. 2 Chron. 1. 10. &c.

**V. 22.** *{From the Baptism of John}* From the time that Christ was baptized of John. Mat. 3. 16. Luke 4. thence began Christ’s public ministry.

*{of his resurrection}* In that he mentions the principal article of our faith (see 1 Cor. 15. 14.) he comprehends all the rest.

**V. 23.** *{they appointed two}* The before mentioned apostles: put two in election, that is, Joseph called Barsabas and Matthias.

**V. 24.** *{and they prayed}* Because the apostles were immediately chosen by God, therefore they here pray God to direct the lot, for the choice of one in Judas’ room.

**V. 25.** *{that he may take part &c.}* See verse 17. that he may be a partner with us, in our apostleship and ministry; here κλῆρος, is put for μερίς, a part, or partnership.

*{his own place}* To hell and eternal damnation, to which God who foresees all things, had justly designed the traitor. See Psal. 9. 17.

**V. 26.** *{they gave forth their lots}* Lots were several ways drawn or given out, either from the lap. Prov. 16. 33. or some vessel, an urn, pot, or headpiece: however, as they were in use for division of lands or goods, or for composing of any controversy, God disposed them, Prov. 16. 33. therefore *Augustine* said well, lots are nothing else: but *in rebus dubiis indicatio divinae voluntatis*, *the declaration of God’s will in things doubtful*; they are here called, *their lots*, after the common use of speaking, in respect of the two, put into election for Judas’ place for apostleship.

*{he was numbered with the eleven, &c.}* Or *acknowledged to be one of the twelve apostles*; συγκατεψηφίσθη: as they say, *communibus calculis est electus*: the word is of ψῆφος, a stone or counter, with which they were wont to give voices in commissions or judgments: sometimes it is taken for the event determined by lot, as here it is intimated; they all rested contented with the lot, as being confident, that God disposed and approved the event thereof: he was numbered with the eleven apostles, by a common declaration of their general consent.

## CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1.** *{Pentecost was fully come}* When the fiftieth day from the Passover was come. See Deut. 16. 9. Levit. 23. 15. which time God chose to send the Holy Ghost; much people being then gathered to Jerusalem, that it might be divulged to the world. See on chap. 1. 4.

*{they were all &c.}* The apostles; for this was divinely and extraordinarily to consecrate and fit them who were to preach the gospel to all nations.

**V. 2.** *{a sound from heaven &c.}* This preternatural wind God sent before, as it were a messenger to stir them up to attention, and to give them notice of the coming and presence of the Holy Ghost: also to intimate in the type, the speedy, unexpected, and irresistible success of the gospel; and that the whole dispensation thereof should be by the power and providence of God.

**V. 3.** *{tongues}* The similitude or appearance of tongues, to signify the gift of tongues, or speaking diverse languages; miraculously infused into them who were to preach to diverse nations; as a remedy for the confusion of tongues. Gen. 11. 7. by which a great part of the world was separated from communion with God and his church.

*{of fire}* To signify the admirable force which should be therein, by the Spirit of God.

**V. 4.** *{filled with the Holy Ghost}* With his gifts and graces; with a greater and more admirable access and increase thereof; more abundant knowledge of the mysteries of salvation by Christ, which before they did not so well apprehend; greatness of mind and constancy, to do and endeavor all things for the testimony of the truth of Christ; the gift of tongues, largeness of heart, and admirable utterance; power and evidence of the Spirit in preaching and praying.

*{to speak with other tongues}* Besides their mother tongue; they spoke languages which they never studied, or understood before that moment: so it was promised, Mark 16. 17. and so it was necessary that they might be able to speak to the tongue of every nation to whom they were to preach the gospel.

**V. 5.** *{dwelling at Jerusalem, Jews devout men out of every nation}* They were of Jewish parents, though they were born, or had dwelt in other nations; these came up from diverse nations, or were of diverse nations hereafter mentioned, and were at Jerusalem at the feast.

*{under heaven}* That is every nation; this *hyperbole* is familiar with the Hebrews. See Deut. 2. 25. thus was fulfilled that which was said by Isaias. Chap. 43. verse 5, 6.

**V. 6.** *{when this was noised abroad}* γενομένης δὲ τῆς φωνῆς ταύτης, *when this voice was made*; that is the report and rumor of these things, being spread abroad, or spoken of among the people; to wit, how the apostles spoke all languages upon the sudden; when *this was voiced abroad*.

*{confounded}* Or *troubled in mind*: or as verse 7. *amazed*, and *marveled*, forasmuch as they knew not the cause thereof, nor what these things would come to at last.

**V. 7.** *{are not all these which speak, Galileans?}* These which speak several languages; are they not such as have been constantly bred in Galilee, and never learned these tongues at home, nor traveled abroad to gain them?

**V. 8.** *{how hear we every man}* Not that in the sound of one voice, every man heard his native language, as if one and the same word spoken by Peter, should sound Greek to the Grecian, Latin to the Roman, Egyptian to the Egyptian (for so had this been a miraculous gift of interpretation, or speaking, in the hearers, (the people) not in the apostles, the speakers) but because every apostle now spoke all languages, as occasion and the auditory served him.

**V. 9.** *{Parthians, and Medes}* For the greater evidence of the miracle, he instances in those men of several nations, now present at Jerusalem, and ear-witnesses of these things.

**V. 10.** *{Proselytes}* Gentiles converted to the Israelites’ religion. See Mat. 23. 15.

**V. 11.** *{the wonderful works of God}* τὰ μεγαλεῖα τοῦ θεοῦ, as Luke 1. 49. the *Syr.* gives it, *mirabilia Dei*: he means the gospel, the wonderful counsel of God for man’s salvation in Christ; which Paul calls *the power of God*. Rom. 1. *the hidden mystery*. Eph. 3. 3. *the unsearchable riches of Christ*. Eph. 1. 18. so that here are noted two causes of their wondering, first that unlearned men, bred in such a place as Galilee should suddenly speak so many tongues: secondly, that they were able to speak so deeply, wisely, learnedly, and divinely in those heavenly matters.

**V. 12.** *{what meaneth this?}* What will this come to? *what will this be?* some of these were to be converted, and therefore it may be they spoke not as condemning, but enquiring what might be the end, use, and fruit of these new and admirable things.

**V. 13.** *{others mocking}* Caviling; and calumniating the apostles; these most likely, were of the scribes and Pharisees, men of carnal wisdom (which is ever enmity to God) justly delivered up to spiritual blindness, and such hardness of heart, that nothing (no not the most evident testimony of God’s power and presence) could move them: thus are there ever some such impudent adversaries of truth, that will not be afraid to jeer at the gifts of God’s own Spirit, and vilify his ministers; See Joh 6. 41. Joh. 8. 13. & 10. 20. & 11. 37. Mat. 11. 9. there is nothing so holy and sacred which precipitate malice dares not blaspheme.

*{full of new wine}* γλεύκους which the vulgar Latin, and Erasmus give *musto*, so the word sometimes signifies must, or new wine: also it signifies sweet, or pleasing wine, as the prophet Isai. 49. 26. uses עסיס *hasis*, which (though the 70. give οἶνον νέον; *new wine*) we there interpret *sweet-wine*.

**V. 14.** *{But Peter standing up with the eleven}* The Holy Ghost had now confirmed and emboldened him, so that without fear of priests and Pharisees, he stood up that he might be heard of the multitude, with the rest of the apostles, as witnesses of the same things, and partakers of the same gifts of the Holy Ghost, and speaks with a loud voice.

**V. 15.** *{These are not drunken}* First he clears himself and the rest of the apostles from that unworthy calumny of drunkenness which they cast on them; and that mildly, as 1 Sam. 1. 13, 14, 15. Acts 26. 24, 25.

*{but the third hour of the day}* Meaning but early days; the third hour according to their account (who reckoned the hours from sunrising, and so made them longer in summer, and shorter in winter) might be, as with us, about nine of the clock in the morning: as if he had said, *they that are drunken, are drunken in the night*. 1 Thes. 5. 7. now it is but early in the morning.

**V. 16.** *{this is that which is spoken by the Prophet Joel}* This which you conceive to be drunkenness, is the gift of God’s Holy Spirit, foretold by the prophet Joel. Joel. 2. 28. it is herein fulfilled.

**V. 17.** *{it shall come to pass}* See Isai 44. 3. he washes off the unjust aspersion, by interpreting and applying the prophecy: and shows their impudency, who dared deride the very mercies of God poured out on his church.

*{in the last days}* When the Messias shall be exhibited in the flesh, for so those days are accounted in the New Testament, 2 Tim. 3. 1. Hebr. 1. 2. 2 Pet. 3. 3. 1 John 2. 18. Jud. 18.

*{I will pour out}* I will give graces, in abundant measure. The meaning is, that God under the gospel would give a greater measure of his grace and Holy Spirit than formerly he had done: greater knowledge, and a clearer demonstration of the truth concerning the mysteries of eternal life and salvation by Christ, which before lay under the shadows of the ceremonial law. Moses said, Deut. 32. 2. *My doctrine shall drop as the rain: my speech shall distill as the dew, as the small rain upon the tender herbs*, but here he said by the prophets, Isai. 44. 3. Joel 2. 28. *I will poure out my spirit upon all flesh.* So are there here two parts of the antithesis: first in this word pouring as if it had been said, under the law God gave grace and knowledge of Christ; but sparingly, not in that abundant measure as since Christ the branch, as it were the golden pipe, emptied his fullness into his church. The other part is in the universality of the receivers; the law was confined to Judea, but the gospel, like an overflowing flood, shall extend to all flesh, all nations of the world.

*{upon all flesh}* Upon all sorts of people, Jews, and Gentiles; bond and free, high and low, &c.

*{young men shall see visions}* Dreams, and visions, were one ordinary means whereby God revealed his will to the prophets. Numbers 12. 6. So we must understand the prophets’ speaking of things to come, attempered to the capacity of the present times wherein they preached and wrote: so when they speak of God’s service, they name; altars, sacrifices, gold, silver, incense, &c. all which when they relate to times of the gospel, are to be understood, as allusions to legal rites, not proprieties of the gospel: so that here, dreams, and visions import excellent gifts of the Spirit, and understanding of things revealed by the Holy Ghost: as if he said, under the gospel, there shall not be a few prophets or ministers, to whom (as of old) God will reveal his will; many shall know it, as Jerem. 31. 34. there shall be a clearer revelation of God’s will in Christ: so that the apostles’ purpose in citing this prophecy is, to invite his hearers to a participation of the same grace, as if he said; the same Spirit which God has now poured out on us, he is ready to pour out on others, far and near; so that, except yourselves hinder yourselves by your obstinacy and unbelief, you shall also receive of the same fountain with us; the same graces. God still offers, and respectively bestows now on his church, in the Spirit of sanctification, though not in the faith of miracles.

**V. 18.** *{and on my servants}* So he limits and restrains the promises to God’s servants only, to them that believe and obey, of what sex or condition soever they are; men, women, bond, free, all are one in Christ. See Gal. 3. 28.

**V. 19.** *{and I will shew wonders in heaven}* As he had two sorts of hearers, so uses he two kinds of arguments: to the holy he cites God’s promises; to the wicked he urges his threatenings, to terrify those scorners, and to bring them to repentance: he names certain prodigies, as presages of God’s wrath, and the calamities of men in the last days; some in heaven, as these fearful *chasmas*, comets, blazing stars, and strange eclipses of the sun and moon; which though they have natural causes, in the ordinary power of God, yet are they sometimes extraordinary and certain tokens of God’s anger, and some imminent miseries coming on men: it is indeed but the voice of experience, *Nunquam futilibus excanduit ignibus aether.* By *signs on earth*, he means earthquakes, inundations, monstrous births, and such prodigious and monstrous things. And the prophet by these expressions, *blood, fire, vapour of smoke, darknesse* of the sun, and bloody or red color of the moon, intimated a turbulent and calamitous condition of the world in the last times; so that whithersoever we shall look, they shall see the terrible signs and effects of God’s anger, as if the whole fabric of the world were upon the point of confusion and dissolution. See Isai. 13. 10. 25. 34. Ezek. 32. 7. Joel 3. 15. Amos 8. 9. Mat. 24. 29. Luke 21. 11.

**V. 20.** *{the sun shall be turned into darkness, &c.}* Joel 2. 31. Meaning, that God will show such signs of his wrath through all the world, that man shall be no less amazed, than if the whole order of nature were changed, or the sun darkened.

**V. 21.** *{whosoever shall call upon, &c.}* Joel 2. 32. Romans 10. 13. He shows the only way to escape those evils threatened, faithful and fervent prayers: the name of the Lord imports all his attributes, wisdom, power, mercy, &c. the only sanctuary in distractions, fears, and dangers, is calling on the name of the Lord in faithful and fervent prayer.

**V. 22.** *{Jesus of Nazareth, &c.}* Now he comes to his purpose, which was to show, that Christ whom they crucified, was the true Messias promised by all the prophets, by whose merit and beneficence, those gifts of tongues, &c. were now poured out on his apostles; and that therefore this was the accepted time and day of salvation, of which the prophet Joel forespoke; also that if they meant to be saved, they must not neglect and over-slip it; he names Christ, *Jesus of Nazareth*, to the end that they might plainly know that he spoke of that very Jesus, whom they in scorn and contempt called *Jesus of Nazareth*; and upon whose cross they fixed that title,

*{a man approved of God among you}* He does not presently affirm that Jesus is the Messias, but comes to it by degrees: first saying that he was a man *approved of God*; as appeared by his miracles, of which the Jews themselves were witnesses.

**V. 23.** *{by the determinate counsel, &c.}* Chap. 4. 28. Not that the counsel of God therein excused the Jews, or Judas’ wickedness, seeing God wrought not in them, but by them, making good use (for man’s redemption) of that they did wickedly. He mentions here the determinate counsel of God, as chap. 4. 28. to let them know who were to believe to salvation that the sufferings of Christ were neither casual, nor by any counsel or power of man, as their first or principal cause, but of God’s good providence, he was delivered to the power of Jews and other sinners of the heathens, that they crucifying him, God’s justice being satisfied in his death, we might be saved from the curse of the law and death eternal.

*{ye have taken}* Thus he would smite their consciences, not yet acquainting them with the end and fruit of God’s counsel herein; lest the Jews should think, either that Christ was not of God, or that God’s determinate counsel excused their fact; he says, *ye have taken, and by wicked hands, &c.*

**V. 24.** *{whom God hath raised up}* Now comes he to prove him to be the true Messias, by his resurrection from the dead. See Rom. 1. 4. for none but he that was God and man (*Immanuel.* Isai. 7. 14. Mat. 1. 23.) could raise himself from the dead.

*{the pains of death}* Meaning the power of death, and bands of the grave.

**V. 25.** *{I foresaw the Lord}* Psal. 16. 8. Hebr. שויתי *shivithi*, I have set. 70. προωρώμην, I foresaw. So here.

*{on my right hand}* Or *at my right hand*. Ever ready to help me. The meaning is, that in all the pressures which he suffered for our sins, he proposed to himself, both the will of God concerning his passion, and his promise and power to preserve him, as also his will concerning man’s salvation by his sufferings.

**V. 26.** *{my flesh shall rest in hope}* *In spe resurrectionis, August. in Psal. 16. 9.* in hope of victory through the power of God, by a glorious resurrection from the dead.

**V. 27.** *{thou wilt not leave my soul in hell}* See on Luke 16. 33. or, *my life*, my person in the grave, Ψυχὴ often imports so, Rom. 13. 1. And the word ᾅδης here used, is in the Hebr. שאול *sheol*, Psal. 18. 5, 6. which sometimes is put for the place of the damned, but is also sometimes to signify the grave, and can no otherwise be reasonably understood, as Job 17. 13. Genesis 37. 35. in both which places, as also Psal. 16. 5, 6. whence this here is cited, the 72. use the word ᾅδης and if it be not reasonably conceived, that Job, Jacob, or David in his own person thought of going to hell, or of ever being there; what necessity is here of understanding this place concerning Christ, otherwise than that God would not leave him long in grave? See verse 41.

*{suffer thy Holy One}* That is, *sanctificatum corpus, Augustine qu. 6.* the sanctified and sacred body of Christ lay not so long in the grave, as to be corrupted,

*{to see corruption}* That is, to be corrupted, *Quomodo enim poterat vel in carne marcescere, cujus nomen unguentum exinanitum est, de virg. lib. 3. inter opera Ambrosii*; *how can he corrupt whose name is an ointment poured forth?* Cant. 1. 3. Augustine to more purpose says, *ep. 120. to Honoratus*, his flesh corrupted not, *quae tam celeriter resurrexit*, *which rose again so soon*.

**V. 28.** *{the ways of life}* In restoring me from death to life, and that most happy and pleasant, as in your presence, Psal. 17. 15. This present life is full of bitterness and troubles; but in the next, we shall be conformed to Christ, and filled with joy.

**V. 29.** *{let me freely speak}* Or, *I may freely, &c.* He now shows, that the Psalm spoke principally of Christ: for David is dead (says he) and we have his sepulcher among us to this day. But this Psalm speaks of one that lives, whose body, though buried, saw no corruption, which could not be said of David’s body, long before turned to dust and corruption.

*{of the Patriarch David}* See 1 Kings 2. 30. As if he had said, I well know that you have the memory of that excellent king, prophet, and patriarch in high esteem, so that you think this Psalm endited by God’s Spirit, speaks only of him, but if you will more nearly look into it and consider the particulars, you may understand that it did not, as to the main, concern David, who died, and his sepulcher to this day with us testifies his corruption and turning to dust, but Christ: for this Psalm speaks of one who must die and be buried, but so soon rise again, that his body might not be corrupted; this is true of Christ, but of David in his own person, as David himself both knew by the Holy Spirit: and prophesied as a peculiar mark of Christ, the Messias. verse 30. 31.

**V. 30.** *{therefore being a Prophet}* And so knew by special revelation, that which was to come concerning the resurrection of Christ,

*{and knowing that God}* Psal. 132. 11.

**V. 31.** *{that his soul}* Psal. 16. 10. See verse 27.

*{soul was not left}* As verse 27.

*{in hell}* Or *in the grave*, Gr. εἰς ᾅδου, as verse 27. See on Luke 16. 23. the word signifies a lightless place, and so is here put for the grave; for the apostle says, that David being a prophet spoke concerning the resurrection of Christ; who was not left in the grave, when he rose from the dead. See *Ioh. Drus. praeter. lib. 5.*

**V. 32.** *{this Jesus hath God raised up, &c.}* This Jesus, the Christ and Messias whom David foresaw in spirit: this Jesus whom you despised and crucified, has God raised up: and of this truth, we (whom God has thus filled with his Spirit) are all witnesses.

**V. 33.** *{by the right hand of God}* By the power of God. By another argument he will here prove Jesus the very Messias of whom David spoke, that is, from his ascension into heaven and sitting at God’s right hand: Christ not only rose again, according to the Scriptures, but also ascended into heaven, which we saw with these eyes; of this ascension David also prophesied. Psal. 68. 18. Ephes. 4. 8. and Psal. 110. 1. cited verse 34. now that which is hereto annexed in the prophecy (giving the gifts of God’s Holy Spirit to men as the speaking all tongues) can neither agree to David nor any creature, but to Christ only; David therefore spoke not of himself, but Christ.

**V. 34.** *{the Lord said unto my Lord}* Psal. 110. 1.

*{Sit thou on my right hand}* Have you glory and power over all. See Ephes. 4. 9, 10. David speaks this concerning Christ, not of himself: for he concerning whom that was spoken, saw not corruption, which David did: therefore that was spoken concerning another, who saw not corruption, See chap. 13. 36, 37. Neither is David so ascended into heaven body and soul, as to sit at God’s right hand, and to have power over all creatures: neither can this agree to any, but Christ only.

**V. 35.** *{thy footstool}* Until I subdue, and subject them all to you.

**V. 36.** *{both Lord and Christ}* The anointed King: so that the Jews must know that he was called Christ, because he was the Lord’s anointed. See Eph. 1. 21. and 4. 10. Phil. 2. 9, 10.

**V. 37.** *{that they were pricked in their heart, &c.}* This is the effect of Peter’s sermon: first their consciences tell them that Jesus was indeed the Messias, of whose death they were guilty; and so liable to God’s heavy judgment, who had cried out, *his blood be on us, and on our children*. Next they break out into a confession of their crime, in that they ask counsel of Peter, and the rest what they should now do, who could by no means undo what they had done.

*{what shall we do?}* Not what must we say: let the wise man show his wisdom; (said *Clemens Romanus, epist. ad Corinth pag. 50.*) not in words only, but good works also.

**V. 38.** *{Peter said unto them repent}* This he did like a good physician, whose part is not only to discover the disease, but to prescribe the remedy, *Poenitentia hujus temporis, dolor medicinalis*; Augustine in Psalm 57. *Present repentance is a medicinable grief.* The first of innocency is not to sin: the second to acknowledge our sins. *Illic currit innocentia integra & illibata quae servet; hic succedit medela quae sanet. Cyprian epist. 3. lib. 1.* there runs entire and untouched innocency which might save: here follows the medicine to heal. But this repentance is not only in knowing, acknowledging our sins, or saying, God be merciful, but in the change of our minds, purposes and evil courses of our lives, as *Augustine de Eccles. Dogmat. cap. 54.* says very well; *Poenitentia vera est, poenitenda non admittere, & admissa deflere*, true repentance is no more to commit things to be repented of, and to bewail sins committed. See also *Tertul. adv. Marcion. lib. 2. cap. 24. fin.* See on Matt. 3. 2.

*{in the Name of the Lord Jesus}* He speaks of and respects here not the form of baptism, but the use and end thereof, which is to implant us unto Christ, Rom. 6. that we may be Christians. See on c. 10. 48.

**V. 39.** *{the Promise is unto you}* Christ is promised both to Jews and Gentiles: but the Jews had the first place.

**V. 40.** *{untoward generation}* This corrupt world: perverse and wicked men: he means principally scribes and Pharisees; and all the enemies of Christ and the gospel.

**V. 41.** *{souls}* Or *persons*. See on v. 27. Here began to appear the fruit of Christ’s prayer, Luke 23. 34.

**V. 42.** *{in breaking of Bread}* This is a term taken from that which Christ did, at his last Supper, Matthew, 26. 26. used to signify the Holy Supper of the Lord, which they used to celebrate, when they made their love-feasts: or feasts of charity. See Acts, 20. 7. 11. Jude 12. though not always so.

**V. 43.** *{fear came upon every soul}* They were all amazed, at the new and marvelous state, increase, gifts, miracles, and power of the church: so that no man dared oppose them.

**V. 44.** *{had all things common}* That community was not of all goods, of all the faithful: but of that which every one would consecrate to the public necessity, or to the relief of any particular person, this was no absolute community, as Anabaptists dream (for that overthrows all property divine and civil) for yet they retained property in their houses, c. 12. 12. and 16. 15. had they not had propriety, they had had nothing to give, or exercise their love and charity withal. Paul professed that he had coveted no man’s gold or silver, c. 20. 33. which had been a vain profession, if it had not been lawful for Christians to possess anything: the community here mentioned speaks them charitable who gave something, not unlawful possessors, who gave not all: and this the words of the text intimate, verse 45. First, in that some parted their possessions and goods, or gave them (which none but proprietors could justly do.) Secondly, in that these donors made a choice (*as every man had need*:) neither needed Paul to have given precepts concerning gathering for the poor, 1 Corinthians, 16. 1, &c. if in the church there had then been any such communion as excluded propriety; for then one had had as much right to any goods or possession, as another.

**V. 45.** *{and parted them}* Distributing their goods, or the price of their possession, sold.

*{as every man had need}* As far as they were able to relieve their poor brethren.

**V. 46.** *{in the temple}* Not that they might join with the Jews in antiquated ceremonies of the law, but because they might there have daily occasion of testifying the truth of Christ to the multitude there assembling.

*{and breaking bread}* The Syriac has it *domi frangebant portionem*; that is, they invited each other home, and eat together; praising God for his benefits, spiritual and temporal. See Isai. 58. 7. This breaking of bread κατ’ οἶκον *from house to house*, understand of their eating and inviting one another, according as some were able and others had need to be relieved, as religious, good, and charitable neighbors use, or ought to do.

**V. 47.** *{having favour with all}* That is, with many, generally, or with all good men: though they had some implacable enemies among the Pharisees.

*{the Lord added to the Church &c.}* It is man’s ministry, but God’s blessing, (see 1 Cor. 3. 7, 8.) who suffers not his ordinance to be fruitless (Isa. 55. 10.) though the reprobate be as the sea to the sweet showers; whatever falls is presently turned into that invincible bitterness of the receiver; yet the elect shall benefit by it: neither is it here to be omitted, that if by the ministry of the gospel God daily adds to his church, number, society, and external communion of the faithful, such as shall be saved; then is it a dangerous symptom or prognostic of their reprobation who separate from the church: this is indeed that temple of God, out of which if any depart, *à spe vitae, ac salutis aeternae alienus est, Lactant. lib. 4. cap. ult.* So Rahab’s house *typum portabat Ecclesiae, Cyprian. lib. 1. Ep. 6.* bore a type of the church, out of which if any go, his blood shall be upon his own head.

## CHAPTER 3

**Verse 1.** *{The ninth hour}* Which was their three of the clock in the afternoon: the time of their evening sacrifice, at which the apostles would be present, not to communicate therein, (See on Chap. 2. 46.) but to inform them that the shadows of the law were abolished by Christ, whom they all prefigured.

**V. 2.** *{lame from his mother’s womb}* Born lame, lame, not by any external violence or accident; but native indisposition of his joints; so infirm, that they carried him up and down, and it was of no less continuance than forty years, c. 4. 22. and so incurable by man: this sets out the truth and greatness of the miracle done in the name of Jesus.

*{called Beautiful}* Built instead of that of Solomon, and so called.

*{to ask alms}* See c. 9. 8. to sustain him with food, and viands necessary for the preservation of his life, a wretched condition of, and in itself; but if we consider the event we may surely conclude, it was a happy lameness, by which he came to the knowledge of Christ, and to feel his divine power and virtue, repairing and curing him: consider the admirable providence of a merciful God, who brings some to his knowledge and their happiness by poverty, some by sickness, some by lameness, others by other grievous afflictions, imprisonment, plundering, banishment; few by prosperity prove finally happy.

**V. 4.** *{look on us}* They would have him diligently mark by what power he should be healed; they being well assured of the certain event, by the Spirit of God, would have him mark by whose name he should be healed and to whom he should ascribe the honor and praise for so great a mercy received: as if they said, believe that you shall receive something of us: look on us, but believe in Christ Jesus.

**V. 5.** *{expecting to receive something}* He looked for money, but obtains that which no money could have purchased for him.

**V. 6.** *{silver and gold have I none}* As if he said, If I had wherewith to relieve you in that you desire, I would give it you, but having none, I give you that which is better, that which Christ has given me power freely to distribute.

*{in the name of Jesus Christ}* By the power and command of that Jesus Christ, whom in contempt and scorn you called Jesus of Nazareth, be healed, rise up and walk.

**V. 7.** *{took him by the right hand}* Being confident on the promise of Christ. Matth. 10. 8. Mark 16, 17, 18.

*{and immediately his feet, &c.}* This speed shows that the cure was by divine power, which can do all things how and when God pleases.

**V. 8.** *{he leaping up}* This shows a perfect cure and sets out the truth and excellency of the miracle done in the name of Jesus Christ: here were no remainders of the old malady which are commonly seen long in the most absolute cures by the art of men, but having at once received perfect soundness and vigor of his limbs, he leaps up, and walks with the apostles into the temple,

*{praising God}* Attributing his health to God alone: to conceal the mercies of God is ingratitude; to ascribe them to second causes, sacrilege.

**V. 9.** *{all the people saw, &c.}* So publicly was it done, that none could modestly deny it: therefore the enemies acknowledged, cap. 4. 16. that a notable miracle was done by them.

**V. 10.** *{they knew it was he}* That it was the same cripple which they saw daily lying at the gate of the temple begging, therefore they wondered to see him so suddenly walking: who they all well knew had been lame from his infancy and never able to go, and that for the space of about forty years, c. 4. 22. the Syriac adds here, by way of interpretation, *quod is esset ille mendicus qui, &c.*

**V. 11.** *{all the people ran together to them}* With deaf ears, taking no notice of the name of Jesus by which the lame was restored; they come about the lame man, and those that cured him, looking on them with admiration: here (all) is again set for (many.)

*{that is called Solomon’s,}* It is likely that this porch was built in the place where Solomon’s formerly stood (1 Kings 9. 3.) and thence was named so. For the temple built by Solomon being demolished by the Chaldees, Zerubbabel and Ezra in their re-edification thereof, imitated the old pattern, as near as they could. See John 10. 23. He names this place, because hither came that concourse of people who were witnesses of these things.

**V. 12.** *{as though by our own power}* He corrects the abuse of men, who commonly attribute that to man’s power or godliness, which appertains only to God.

**V. 13.** *{the God of our fathers}* Chap. 5. 30. The same only true God, whom Abraham, and all our holy fathers worshiped. He teaches them first the efficient principal cause of this miracle, *The God of our Fathers*; and next, the final in these words, *hath glorified his son Jesus*; in that he gave such divine power to the apostles to do these miracles in, and by the name of Jesus raised from the dead, whereby he was mightily declared to be the Son of the living God, the Messias and Savior of the world, and in these words, *the God of Abraham, Isaac &c.* He remembers them of the promises of God concerning their Messias, made and often repeated to their fathers, Genesis, 12. 3. Gal. 3. 8. that they might know that they now proposed no other God than the same whom all their fathers worshiped, neither any other doctrine than that which they all received, and constantly in their generations professed.

*{whom ye delivered &c.}* Hereby he aggravates their inhuman wickedness that he might move them to repentance, and faith in Christ, that they might obtain mercy and be saved.

**V. 14.** *{but ye denied the Holy One}* Matt. 27. 20. John 19. 15. when Pilate had determined to acquit him as innocent, urging the most impious and dishonorable end of their request, which was to put their King to a shameful death, they cried out, *We have no King but Cesar*; John 19. 15. whereby they publicly denied Christ the Holy One, and long expected Messias to be their King and in his stead, professed their allegiance to Caesar, no ways compelled thereto for fear of the Romans, whose deputy again and again, declared him guiltless, and himself willing to dismiss him.

*{desired a murderer}* That is Barabbas, Mark 15. 11. John 18. 40. This aggravates and sets off the horrid atrocity of the malicious Jews, who having the free choice which of the two should be saved, the holy Jesus, or the murderous Barrabas, they would have the murderer freed, and the innocent and most beneficent Jesus crucified. Sin and wickedness know no limit: when once the sinner forsakes the known right, he cannot stay till he come to hell.

**V. 15.** *{and killeth the Prince of Life}* Or *author of life*, Christ Jesus. So still the impious and unjust world favors the wicked, and persecutes the just.

**V. 16.** *{through Faith in his Name}* Because by faith he apprehended him who was risen from the dead, whose name he heard of us; or name may here import Christ himself, or his power.

**V. 17.** *{through ignorance ye did}* He excuses not their malice; but because ignorance and blind zeal led many of them into their sin, he puts the penitent among them in hope of pardon. See 1 Tim. 1. 13. it is like that which is said, 1 Cor. 2. 8. and Luke 23. 34.

*{your rulers}* He means some of them; not all. See Luke 23. 50, 51. John 3. 1.

**V. 18.** *{by the mouth of all his Prophets}* See verse 21. The prophets were many, yet being all inspired with one Spirit of God, they spoke all one truth: in respect whereof, the Holy Ghost here attributes but one mouth to them all.

**V. 19.** *{time of refreshing shall come}* It is a figurative manner of speaking, taken from workmen reposing themselves in the cool shades, to represent eternal rest, after the labors and travels of this life. See Luke 16. 25. Revelation 7. 15, 16. and 14. 13. or he points here at that which Paul writes Romans 11. 23. 26. the final calling of the Jews by the gospel preached, to which those words (*which before was preached unto you*) may also relate, verse 20.

**V.†** *{He shall send Jesus Christ}* To judge the world: this he says to show them, that the contempt of the gospel cannot be unpunished; and also to give them assurance of comfort, who believe in him.

**V. 21.** *{whom the heaven must receive}* That is contain, having received his humanity which was thenceforth resident in heaven, and shall be no more here on earth, until he come to judgment, for he has a true, and therefore circumscribed body; not confounded with the deity, as to have any of its incommunicable properties, as ubiquity, omnipresence, &c. He has still a true and real body, though glorified, as ours shall once be which shall not in that state be everywhere or in more places than one at once.

*{times of restitution of all things}* Unto the perfection of the kingdom of Christ, wherein all shall be repaired which sin has disordered and ruined; wherein the creature shall be delivered from the bondage of corruption into the glorious liberty of the sons of God. See Rom. 8. 21.

*{since the world began}* Or *from all ages*.

**V. 22.** *{A Prophet shall the Lord}* Deut. 18. 15. Chap. 7. 37.

*{of your brethren}* Of the stock of Abraham; for he spoke this unto the Jews, who were the children of Abraham, of whom Christ came according to the flesh; and so was one of their brethren.

**V. 23.** *{every soul}* Every person, as Chapter, 2. 27. Romans, 13. 1. Genesis, 46. 26. Here is destruction threatened to all contemners of Christ and his ministers. See Luke 10. 16.

**V. 24.** *{All the Prophets from Samuel}* After Moses, he names Samuel, between whose times, the word of God was precious, 1 Samuel 3. 1. from Samuel, all the prophets foretelling of Christ, as many as have spoken, have foretold of these times of grace.

**V. 25.** *{ye are the children of Prophets}* Or *patriarchs*, Abraham, Isaac Jacob, &c. you are they to whom the promises were made, and the prophets addressed, and with whom God made the covenant, See Rom. 9. 4.

*{and in thy seed}* Genesis, 12. 3. Galatians, 3. 8. Genesis, 18. 18. and 22. 18. and 26. 4. That is, Christ Jesus, of the seed and lineage of Abraham, according to the flesh.

**V. 26.** *{unto you first}* To you before the Gentiles, is the gospel and doctrine of Christ’s resurrection to be preached, Matt. 10. 6. Acts, 1. 8. and 13. 46. or ἀναστήσας, *raised*, here is of the same signification with ἀναστήσει, verse 22. and Deuteronomy, 18. 15, 18. 1 Samuel 2. 35. 1 Kings 14. 14. the word קום used among other things to signify, *constituit*, he appointed to an office; the 70. give ἀναστήσει (See 2 Samuel 23. 1.) So Zacharias said, *God hath raised up an horn of salvation*, Luke 1. 69. and the people said, Luke 7. 16. *A great Prophet is risen up among us*, that is, being appointed of God, is sent among us; for *no man taketh this honour to himself, but he that is called of God*, Hebr. 5. 4.

*{to bless you}* In giving you faith and repentance by the Spirit of regeneration: (Gal. 3. 9. 14.) the blessing has a condition annexed to the promise; if you convert and repent you of your iniquities; the Syriac expresses it, אן תתפנון *in tethpenun*, *if ye convert, &c.* we must perform this condition if we will have the blessing: sin brings the curse: therefore no man can be blessed in impenitency, and continuing in sin.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1.** *{Captain of the Temple}* Or *ruler*; some think that he was a ruler or commander over the Levites, who ministered by turns, and guarded the temple. See 2 Chronicles, 35. 4, 5. but more probably he calls him στρατηγὸν τοῦ ἱεροῦ, the ruler or tribune of these soldiers, whom the Romans allowed the Jews for the guard of the temple: for Pilate said, Matt. 27. 65. *ye have a watch*. See Luke 22. 4. John 18. 12. It was one who had committed to him the guard of the temple. *Vatabl.* See also *Io. Drus. praeterit. lib. 5.*

*{the Sadducees came upon them}* Who deny the resurrection of the body from the dead, See Luke 20. 27. Matt. 22. 23.

**V.†** *{being grieved}* *They were angry*; the Syr. gives it: some color had they, for if any man intruded into the office of a preacher, it belonged to the priests to suppress such audacity; as also to retain the people in the doctrine of the law, and prophets to prevent their seducements, by any new doctrines; therefore, seeing men, to them unknown, or not allowed by any public authority, preaching in the temple, they reproved them, not understanding, or acknowledging their extraordinary calling, and the main being that the Sadducees’ atheistical opinion contradicted, they exasperated the priests, and rulers against the apostles. See Chap. 23. 8.

**V. 3.** *{They laid hands, &c.}* Hands not yet clean from the blood of Christ: the argument of the wicked is violence: now the apostles have experience of the truth of Christ’s words. See Luke 21. 12.

**V. 4.** *{many of them which heard the word believed}* Many who heard what Peter had said unto the people, Chap. 3. 12, &c. the word never descended in vain, See Isa. 55. 10.

*{about five thousand}* Of those who adhered to the apostles; so the blood and injury of the martyrs, is still the seed of the church: the apostles were now shut up in prison; but the church flourished the more she was wounded, ὁρᾷς θείαν ἐνέργειαν φανεράν, *Oecumen.* you see the working of God manifest herein. It is no small demonstration of his power, detaining believers against all the rages of Satan, and giving them more boldness and constancy, by how much more they are opposed and injured: in this number we must understand the whole church then being, not a new accession of so many: at Peter’s first sermon, Acts 2. 41. they were about three thousand: now they are at his second sermon increased to five thousand: thus appeared the effect of Christ’s promise to make them fishers of men, See Phil. 1. 13, 14.

**V. 5.** *{Rulers and Elders}* Which the Hebrews called זקנים *zekinim* , old men: these were the judges of their greater Sanhedrim; they call a council to oppose the truth by a seeming legal proceeding, desiring that it might appear justly damned: but there is no counsel against God and his truth. See verse 25, &c. Psa. 2. 1, 4. Prov. 21. 30. Isa. 46. 10.

*{and scribes}* See on Luke 20. 1.

**V. 6.** *{as many as were of kindred, &c.}* Thus strengthened they their party, to countenance the matter: these had indeed by God’s appointment the charge of things ecclesiastical: these were Aaron’s successors; for which they had also about 1500 years’ prescription: these were the men, who now flourished among the Jews in honor and wealth; yet these now combine and assemble against the gospel.

**V. 7.** *{by what name}* By what authority, and by whose command have you cured the lame man: hence, as in many other things, appears their stupid, or willful ignorance, that they would question the authority of an act which none but God could do: they asked not with any purpose to believe in Christ, but that they might awe and terrify the apostles under pretense of their jurisdiction and authority to command in all things to be done in and about the temple, especially concerning preaching, lest any new and erroneous doctrine should prevent the people, or any without their ordination should presume to teach them.

**V. 8.** *{Peter filled with the Holy Ghost}* Assisted by the grace of God’s Holy Spirit enabling, and emboldening him to speak, who before was terrified by the voice of a maiden.

**V. 9.** *{if we this day}* Or *Seeing that.*

*{examined of the good deed done}* Meaning, whereas you question us for doing good, who ought to question and punish evil doers only, and to defend and encourage the good.

**V. 10.** *{be it known unto you all}* Such admirable confidence had the Holy Ghost now given him, that he thus openly professed and declared the truth of Christ, before the most implacable enemies thereof.

*{by the name of Jesus, &c.}* ἐν τῷ ὀνόματι, through the power of Jesus. See on Chap. 2. 22. and 3. 6. 16.

*{whom ye crucified}* See Chap. 2. 36.

*{by him doth this man stand}* Chap. 3. 16.

**V. 11.** *{this is the stone}* This is he of whom the Scriptures speak, Psalm 118. 22. See also, Matt. 21. 42. Mark 12. 10. Luke 20. 17. 1 Pet. 2. 7.

*{of you builders}* You priests and elders, and governors who should edify the church of God; which you (as it has elsewhere often come to pass) endeavor all you can to pull down; lest your honor, and the revenue of the temple and priesthood should be impaired, or decay: so dangerous a let and hindrance to men’s salvations, is love of this present world and the things thereof.

*{is become the head of the corner}* The *corner stone*, ἀμφοτέρους‡ συνάπτων τοὺς τοίχους, &c. *Oecumen.* *uniting both walls into one*, that is, both Jews and Gentiles into one church. See Ephes. 2. 14, 20, 21.

**V. 12.** *{salvation in any other}* ἐν ἄλλῳ, that is, by or through the mediation of any other.

*{none other Name, &c.}* None other mediator of redemption; or intercession, 1 Tim. 2. 5.

*{among men}* ἐν ἀνθρώποις, that is, as the vulgar has it, *datum hominibus*: *given to men*; to wit, appointed by God for their salvation.

**V. 13.** *{unlearned and ignorant men}* ἀγράμματοι, *illiterate men*, *men without letters*: not brought up in the schools of the scribes and Pharisees, but in a school incomparably better; that is, in the school of Christ: therefore this is not simply spoken (for they had spent some time in the study of the Holy Scriptures, and were all θεοδίδακτοι, *taught of God*, 1 Thes. 4. 9. John 6. 45. and 14. 26.) but in relation to ordinary means of attaining learning by the mastery and teaching of men, human learning, or the Pharisees’ esteem of them: ἰδιῶται *private men*, Suidas interprets it by the foregoing word, ἰδιώτης, ὁ ἀγράμματος, *an unlearned man*: and ὁ ἀμαθής, *an ignorant and unskillful man*; such as private, unemployed men commonly are: the word is of ἴδιος private. See more hereof, *Iob. Drus. praeterit. lib. 5. Theod. Beza. annot. maior.* on this place.

**V. 14.** *{nothing against it}* It was so evident a miracle, and good work, that they could neither deny it, nor say anything against them, as the Syriac gives it.

**V. 15.** *{conferred among themselves}* They would not let the apostles hear that which an evident and undeniable truth extorted from their own mouths, verse 16. because they would maintain their credit with men, and still pretend that the apostles were delinquents, worthy of suppression and punishment, as innovators, and disturbers of the peace.

**V. 16.** *{What shall we do, &c.?}* Here is a true pattern of malicious injustice; they convent them as malefactors, and then seek for, and consult about some accusation against them: something they would do, but knew not what; because however unjust they were, they would by no means seem so to men: this is the misery and madness of great sinners, they had rather sin still, than seem to have sinned, resolving to palliate, if they cannot justify their faults, *majore facinore*.

**V. 17.** *{but that it spread no further, &c.}* They count it a principal piece of policy, to keep the people ignorant, that they may the more easily beguile them: it is a great, but mischievous skill, in such motions to lessen the light, that their legerdemain may not be discovered by the spectators.

*{straitly threatened them}* ἀπειλῇ ἀπειλησώμεθα, *threatening, let us threaten*; a usual Hebrew pleonasm; for, *let us strictly*, or *straitly threaten them*.

*{to no man in this name}* That is, that they preach concerning Christ no more. They more valued their credit with men than their own or the people’s salvation; therefore however convinced in conscience, they would hide the light of Christ from the people.

**V. 18.** *{not to speak at all}* That is, not to speak any word concerning Jesus, that he had taught, done or suffered, and do no more miracles in his name, or pretend to his power for the doing of the same.

**V. 19.** *{judge ye}* We appeal to your own consciences herein: God commands us to preach in this name, you under pretense of your authority, command us not to preach; shall we now obey God for you? Will God judge it right, will he excuse us, if against his commands we obey yours?

**V. 20.** *{We cannot but speak the things}* We cannot choose but obey Christ, who has commanded us so to do: for the dispensation is committed unto us, and woe shall be to us if we preach not the gospel. See 1 Cor. 9. 16. *it is the power of God to salvation*, Rom. 1. 16. How fearful a sacrilege were it, to withhold it from the Israel of God. We have heard, seen and believed the things, and therefore speak. See Psalm 116. 10. Acts 17. 16. *I will not make mention of him, nor speak any more in his name* (said the prophet Jeremiah in his great temptation, Jer. 20. 9.) *but his word was in my heart as a burning fire shut up in my bones, and I was weary with forbearing, and I could not stay*; for the grace (and Spirit) of prophecy, incited him unwilling to speak, says *Theodoret* in *Jer.* where the Spirit of God is in the minister, no fear or threatenings of men, can so silence him, but that at last, the word of truth shall break out.

**V. 21.** *{all men glorified God}* All that believed, or many men: they found no fault in them, and without some colorable pretense of justice, dared not hurt or punish them for fear of the people, who praised God for the good which he had done by them.

**V. 22.** *{above forty years old}* It may seem strange, that being so old, and daily laid at the gate of the temple, into which Jesus so often entered (considering that it appears not, that he ever denied to heal or cure any that entreated it) he did not receive help from him all this time: but certain it is, he was reserved unto this time, and means of healing by the all-disposing providence of God, for the further confirmation of the apostles, and truth of the gospel.

*{this miracle of healing}* Gr. τὸ σημεῖον τοῦτο, &c. this sign, See Chap. 3. 7, 8.

**V. 23.** *{they went to their own}* To glorify God, and to encourage them, by letting them know, how God had assisted them.

**V. 24.** *{they lift up their voice}* Either all speaking this same thing, as they were guided by one Spirit; or which is most probable to avoid confusion, (that the company might distinctly hear, and say, Amen.) some one or few in the name of the rest, thus prayed.

**V. 25.** *{Why did the Heathen rage}* Psalm 2. 1. למה *lammah why*? What cause had they to rage against the Lord? Or, to what end do they rage? What will their success be, who fight against God?

*{vain things}* Such are all consultations and combinations against *the truth and ordinance of God*: God is stronger than all, *and his counsel must stand*, John 10. 29. Isaiah 8. 10. and 19. 11. 17. and 44. 26. and 46. 10. Jerem. 32. 19. Ezekiel 7. 26. Hos. 10. 6. 1 Sam. 8. 7.

**V. 26.** *{against the Lord}* That is, *against Christ Jesus his truth, and his ministers; whosoever opposes, despises or persecutes these, rise up to oppose and persecute Christ himself.* See Numbers 16. 11. Luke 10. 16. Acts 9. 4. 1 Thes. 4. 8. Thus did Saul, the princes of the Philistines, and others stand up against David, *who was a type of Christ*: so here: Herod, Pontius Pilate with the Romans and Jews, conspired against the sacred person of Christ himself.

**V. 27.** *{against thy holy child Jesus}* *Your Holy Son Jesus*, παῖδα here, as v. 25. is set, not so much to import years, as dearness, or obsequiousness to God, as c. 3. 13. So Joshua, though in years a man, is called נער *nahar, the child or servant of Moses*, Exod. 33. 11. If such plots and combinations were against Holy Jesus; let us in all our sufferings *run with patience*, *looking up unto the Author and finisher our Faith*, let us consider him that suffered such things for us (See Hebr. 12. 1, 2, 3, 4.) it is no dishonor to us to suffer of our brethren that which Christ suffered of his, *nec illis gloria est facere, quod fecerit Judas*, nor is it any glory to them, to do that which Judas did, *Cyprian. lib. 1. epist. 3.*

**V. 28.** *{for to do whatsoever}* Though they meant nothing less than to obey God in crucifying Christ, but had several other ends, yet God ordered this to our redemption. See Acts 13. 27. Isa. 10. 7.

*{thy hand and counsel}* Your power and your providence: for as Cyprian forecited says well: *nihil interest quis tradat aut saeviat, cum Deus tradi permittat, qui disponit coronari*: it matters nothing who betrays or rages, seeing God permits those to be delivered up (or betrayed) whom he appoints to be crowned.

**V. 29.** *{behold their threatenings}* Consider what they threaten: divert their rage and malice, who enterprise against you and your truth.

*{and grant, &c.}* Because God only can give patience, constancy and perseverance in truth, and also boldness to preach the gospel, they pray him to give it, who well knew they could not have these gifts of themselves; here is the saints’ refuge in all their wants and necessities, earnest prayer.

**V. 30.** *{by the name of thy holy child Jesus}* See on v. 10. 27. by the power, and in the name of your Holy Son Jesus: for their aim and hearts’ desire was to advance his honor and truth.

**V. 31.** *{place was shaken}* By this sensible sign, God showed his presence, and readiness to hear and confirm them in their ministration.

*{all filled with the Holy Ghost}* They were strengthened, and enabled with greater confidence and largeness of heart to speak. Mark here, first the result of impious council: the Jews consulted against the gospel, labored to suppress it by power, threatened and charged the apostles to speak no more in the name of Jesus; God gave them greater courage to preach it, and more excellent gifts than before: all opposition more propagated the truth. Secondly, here it appears, that the safest refuge in danger, and the happiest issue of fear is, when it awakes us to a more fervent calling on the name of God; which to the perplexed soul is, as the ark to the tired dove: as David said Psa. 116. 7. *Return unto thy rest, O my soul*: in which sense it is true which *Gregor. Nazianzen* said, *epist.* 16. Κάμνουσα ψυχὴ, ἐγγὺς τῷ Θεῷ *the weary soul is near God.*

**V. 32.** *{were of one heart}* So unanimous, as if they had all but one and the same heart, and one soul: because they were all united and guided by one Spirit of God, whence you may know, that where strife, envy, malice, and contention is, there an evil spirit governs. See Jam. 3. 14, 15, 16. &c.

*{was his own}* They gave, as if the needy brethren had as good interest in their goods, as themselves who were owners thereof. See on c. 44.

**V. 33.** *{witness of the resurrection, &c.}* This by a synecdoche is put for the whole gospel: this the Sadducees lately quarreled at, therefore they testified it the more powerfully.

*{great grace was upon them all}* As God gave them that great grace of real charity (*with which the present world is little acquainted*) so was this very effectual for the gaining a good opinion among the people, both concerning their persons and doctrine, which they saw so admirably exemplified; and thereby they were the more easily invited to join themselves to their society whom they perceived so far from any secular and worldly ends, that they gave freely what they had to the relief of others who wanted.

**V. 34.** *{any among them that lacked}* That is, who was destitute and not relieved.

*{for as many as were possessors, &c.}* Not that they who believed, did all of them sell all that they had, but as need required: as if it were said, the owners did not only relieve those who wanted, with their rents divided among them; but as there were further necessity, they spared not the fee-simple, but sold of that also.

**V. 35.** *{down at the apostles’ feet}* That is, submitted it to their disposal: this intimated, as Oecumenius notes, τὴν πολλὴν τιμὴν, &c. that great honor and reverence of the hearers toward the apostles.

*{according as he had need}* They who most wanted, had most relief given them.

**V. 36.** *{Joses, who by the Apostles was surnamed Barnabas}* This Levite was of Cyprus: he is mentioned as an example of that which was said; the apostles named him Barnabas (*the son of consolation, or a Comforter*) not only for that he gave so liberally to comfort the needy Christians, but probably in respect of those excellent gifts he had in comforting afflicted minds of hearers. *Hieronym. de scriptor. eccles.* calls him Ἰωσήφ, so also many Greek copies: so also the Syriac. Others read Ἰωσῆς. Some, but improbably think him called so from the Syriac word בר *bar* and נבא *nabba* consolation of the verb ביא *baia, consolatus est*, (c. 20. 1. and 16. 40, &c. Ephesians, 6. 2. Colossians, 4. 8, &c.) others more probably derive it from בר *bar, son*, and the Hebrew word נבא *naba* to prophesy, intimating him a son of consolation in his gift of prophecy: and indeed a principal part of the prophets’ or ministers’ function is to comfort the hearers, by applying the promises of the gospel unto their consciences. This Barnabas Chap. 11. 24. is said to be *a good man, and full of the Holy Ghost*, that is, an excellent prophet or teacher: a man of excellent gifts.

**V. 37.** *{having land, sold it}* Here is rehearsed one instance of that is said, v. 34.

*{at the apostles’ feet}* See on v. 35.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 1.** *{Named Ananias with Sapphira, &c.}* The scope of this history is to show how odious to God that perfidiousness is, which counterfeiting sanctity would impose upon God, as if he saw not what men do: (for otherwise, Ananias giving a part, had been commendable) so great a mischief is hypocrisy, so dangerous the perversion of a holy purpose and the withdrawing of things consecrated to the church’s use (though by himself who gave) that the sacrilegious perished by detaining a part of that whole which he would seem to give: by this was Ananias (as Chrysostom says) κλέπτων τὰ ἑαυτοῦ φωραθείς, taken stealing his own goods. It is the heart, not the hand which God looks into: the poor widow (Luke 21. 3.) cast in but two mites, and it was better accepted than all those who offered greater sums. Here Ananias sells a possession, and presents the price, reserving to himself, but a part thereof, and yet is condemned for it: if he that takes from the church but a little of that which was his own, be sacrilegious, and stricken dead for the same, what punishment will the same divine justice lay on him, who by fraud or violence takes away the possessions wherewith other men endowed the church? See more hereof *Chrysost. in Act. hom.* 12.

**V. 2.** *{kept back part of the price}* Syriac, *he hid a part*. So he did; for pretending to be as charitable as the best, he sold, and seemed to bring the whole price, as others did, but he concealed a part, which he would reserve to himself and his wife. So Suidas and Chrysostom interpret νοσφιζόμενον by ὑφαιρούμενον, *surripientem, intervertentem* — and ἰδιοποιούμενον which Aemilius Portus also renders, *Illum qui res aliquas suas facit, suffurantem*, *impropriating* or *stealing*. See what ambition will do: Ananias would not come behind the best in seeming charitable: therefore though he was loath to part with his money; yet you see how dear he would purchase the good opinion of men. In the meantime, not considering that God saw him, and would once severely revenge it.

**V. 3.** *{but Peter said}* The matter being divinely revealed to Peter; or he might know the possession, and conjecture the value of it; the first conjecture seems most probable.

*{to lie to the Holy Ghost}* That you should thus lie, or deceive: of ψεύδω, *fallo, frustro*. So Sophocles cited also by Suidas, μὴ ψεῦσαι —— τῆς ἐπιούσης ἐλπίδος, ψεύσασαί σε deceive not your approaching hope. So *ementita auspicia*, Cicer. Lying presages, deceiving hope. He calls this lying to the Holy Ghost: for when they had dedicated the possession to God, for the relief of his servants, the fraud and falsehood concerned his interest, and this their seeming charity was hypocrisy and lying to God: though they so behaved themselves in this business, as if they had to deal with men only, not with God: they presumed that man could not discover this sacrilege, and detention of that which was consecrated to the use of the church, neither considered they that God would.

**V. 4.** *{while it remained}* οὐχὶ μένον σοὶ ἔμενε; *remaining, did it not remain to thee?* That is, it was your own rights; you might have justly kept the possession to yourself, or the price thereof, to your own uses, neither would that have made you unworthy of the society of Christians: the fault is aggravated in that he was urged by no necessity: had the apostles compelled him to sell, there might have been some color for his detention of some part of the price; but now, it being his own to dispose of, his sin was, that dedicating the whole to the church’s use, he kept back a part thereof. Here it appears, that they had no law or decree made, or in use among primitive Christians, to alienate and sell their possessions; for Peter clearly shows, that Ananias might justly have kept his own in land or money: for otherwise, he would have said; it remained not to you, but to the church, by right of community. They who now would have all things in common, have no ground from these practices of the primitive church, as they pretend: their community was not compulsory, but free and voluntary: otherwise, it had neither been thankworthy, nor such a leading example of piety in the particulars, as the Holy Ghost here sets out: it is considerable which Chrysostom says, *hom.* 12. See (says he) how they (who were owners of possessions) *permitted not the Apostles to sell* (them) *but the* (owners) *their selves sold, and gave the price to them*, to distribute. Neither could they have given them, nor the apostles justly received; had not the givers been *bonae fidei possessores*, *true and lawful proprietors*. It is theft without the owners’ consent, to give that which is not our own, and no less to receive it.

*{was it not in thine own power}* No man compelled you to sell and give; but to make God a proffer of all, and to perform but a part; this was to tempt God (as verse 9.) to try whether he saw and would revenge.

*{thou hast not lied unto men, but, &c.}* You have not lied to us only, or chiefly, but unto God, whose interest you have invaded, by concealing that which your own pretense made sacred.

**V. 5.** *{hearing these words fell down}* Thus God sometimes presently revenges sins, to terrify others, and to restrain them from the like, as here: so God is known when he judges, Psalm 9. 16. Neither may any think sacrilege no sin now, because God does not presently punish it with sudden death, as here: the punishment following will once show it an abominable sin, and come too soon on him on whom it ever comes. He is the same God still of unchangeable justice: and if we mark well, this age has afforded many remarkable examples, of sacrilege heavily punished; eternity will show innumerably more.

**V. 6.** *{and the young men arose}* Which were of the church, they *arose*, that is, set themselves about it; an usual *Hebraism*, Josh. 8. 3. and 24. 9. Jud. 20. 8, &c.

**V. 7.** *{came in}* To the apostles and others with them, in the temple, or private house, where they used to assemble. See chap. 2. 46. See also v. 12. Unto the foregoing example of divine justice, Luke annexes the narration of another in Saphira, who consenting to her husband’s sin, shared with him in the like punishment: to the evidence of the truth of this history, the circumstance of time is recorded: about the space of three hours after Ananias was dead, Saphira his wife, not knowing what was done, came into the place where the apostles were.

**V. 8.** *{yea for so much}* She who had agreed with her husband what to say, thus vailed her sin with a lie: a fault too common now a days, to endeavor to cover sin with sin. She knew, and her conscience told her it was a lie, and so a grievous sin; yet she who consented to her husband’s sin, adds sin to sin. Such is the sinner’s misery, one sin unrepented, draws on other sins, until destruction come.
