**V. 9.** *{to tempt the Spirit of the Lord, &c.}* It is the same in effect which he said, verse 4. to lie unto God, or in the presence of God: as often as men sin against their own consciences, they do presumptuously tempt God, or try whether God will be just or no.

*{behold the feet of them}* Meaning they that buried your husband are returned: and here also by a divine revelation, he foretells of her death presently to ensue.

**V. 10.** *{then fell she down straightway}* They that are in the same faults must expect the like punishments or worse (except in case of timely repentance) which being prolonged are ever greatened. It were well to be wished, that the sacrilegious of these times would timely be warned by these examples; and remember that it is the same God of unchangeable justice now looking on this sin, who so severely punished it in these two: and that detaining that which they have received any ways from other unjust possessors, is consenting with them in the same crime, and that, except in case of restitution and true repentance, they must once suffer grievous punishment, because God is impartially just: nor will it avail the hypocrite that he makes conscience of some sins, or sinners, if he willfully dispense with any; the apostle saying — *thou that abhorrest idols, committest thou sacrilege?* Rom. 2. 22.

**V. 11.** *{and great fear came upon all}* All that saw or heard of this severe judgment of God, which was to warn all to be sincere, and to beware of hypocrisy. Such restraints flesh and blood have sometimes need of, to make them know that it is a *dreadful thing to fall into the hands of the living God*, Hebr. 10. 31. See 1 Sam. 6. 19, 20. 2 Sam. 6. 7. 9.

**V. 12.** *{many signs and wonders}* Miraculous things done by the apostles, whereby not only the power of Christ, and his justice in this example of Ananias and Saphira, but also his mercy appeared in healing all diseases, that thereby they might be invited to the faith; and these were necessary to the gospel, as the other for restraining those corruptions wherewith Satan would otherwise have infected the church’s minority.

*{they were all with one accord}* This he says, to show that none among them was schismatical, to forsake their public assemblies, or in neglect thereof to hold private conventicles. Of *Solomon’s Porch*, see chap. 3. 11. Here appears their constant resolution, to testify the truth of Jesus, from which no fear or danger could now deter them.

**V. 13.** *{of the rest, durst no man join himself to them}* They were so terrified with this example against hypocrites, that they who were not sincere, dared not now make false shows of religion, contemn the apostles, or encounter with them by argument.

**V. 14.** *{believers were the more added}* The example of Ananias, &c. was so far from discouraging men from Christianity, that it more increased the church: these evil branches cut off the Lord’s vine, the church, flourished the better.

**V. 15.** *{into the streets}* Or *in every street*, the apostles did so many and admirable miracles, in healing the sick, restoring the lame: &c. and that so publicly, notwithstanding the rage and vigilant malice of the chief priests and Pharisees; that the people brought their sick out into the open streets to be cured, even such as could not come out themselves, but were by their friends brought out of doors on beds and couches.

*{the shadow of Peter passing by}* See also c. 19. 12. Not that the body of the apostle, much less the shadow, or garments, had any such inherent virtue, as to heal the sick; but God exercised the power of healing so abundantly, as that the weakest and otherwise improbable means were made effectual thereto; that men might know that it was his power only, testifying the exhibition of the Messiah.

**V. 16.** *{bringing sick folks}* Hearing the rumor of the apostles’ healing: and this was by the providence of God, that thereby the truth of Jesus might appear, and the gospel be more propagated.

**V. 17.** *{filled with indignation}* Or *envy*, or *preposterous emulation*: ζῆλος, in Scripture signifies many things; sometimes good, 2 Cor. 9. 2. Gal. 4. 18. Tit. 2. 14. But here it imports an evil affection, as c. 7. 9. and 13. 45. and 17. 5. 1 Cor. 3. 3. and 13. 4. &c. Here was indeed a zeal, but without knowledge; which is but a kind of blind heat (so Christ taxed the ignorance of the Sadducees, Matt. 22. 29.) violent zeal without knowledge, is but as strength in a blind Samson, serving to destroy himself and others: holy zeal, is to religion as the natural heat to the body, wherein the life thereof is preserved; but it must be fetched (like that coal wherewith one of the Seraphims touched the prophet’s lips, Isa. 6. 6.) from *the altar, and word of God*, not human fancies.

**V. 18.** *{put them in the common prison}* Where common malefactors were put: here is a fruit of blind zeal: they were not able to deal with the apostles by Scriptures or reason; therefore they betake themselves to open violence. See John 16. 2. Luke 21. 12.

**V. 19.** *{but the angel of the Lord}* God could otherwise have delivered them, but thus he would now confirm them when they should see by experience, that he gave his angels charge over them, and had made them ministering spirits for their good. See Psa. 34. 8. Hebr. 1. 14.

**V. 20.** *{stand and speak in the temple}* There was a time when Christ would not have them preach, some things afterwards to be preached, Mat. 16. 20. and 17. 9. But now was that season of which he said Matt. 10. 27. *preach on the housetops*; that is, in public: God’s dispensations have their times and seasons: and he delivers his, that they may more boldly preach the truth.

*{all the words of this life}* See John 6. 68. That is, the gospel which is the word of life eternal; which directs believers to eternal life in Christ.

**V. 21.** *{when they heard that, they entered}* When they had received commission so to do, they feared not the most apparent dangers, but committed themselves and the issue to God’s providence: faith advises not with flesh and blood, which would easily suggest; Behold Lord, they sought our lives but now, for preaching there, and shall we expose ourselves to their malice by a further provocation? But presently they obey God’s word, to whom they lived, and for whom they now feared not to die.

*{but the high priest}* Malice is restless: they call a council, (that is, *other Priests and Scribes, their colleagues*, as the Syriac gives it; not that *Sanhedrin*, or great council of 71. judges which sat on highest causes within their jurisdiction. See Mat. 5. 22.) they must seem just and legal who least intend to be so: they send to the prison, confident that they had the apostles sure enough.

**V. 22.** *{and found them not}* So easily can God when he pleases, frustrate the counsels and expectations of the enemies. These very servants were witnesses of their fighting against God, by whose divine power they must know the apostles were delivered out of prison; so closely guarded, that they could not else have made any escape, but that they willfully shut their eyes, cannot see things in the clearest light.

**V. 23.** *{the prison truly found we shut, &c.}* This miraculous deliverance might have converted the adversaries, who might easily have concluded, that a divine power, against which there is no striving, had delivered these prisoners, the doors being all fast, shut with all safety, ἐν πάσῃ ἀσφαλείᾳ, with all possible diligence which might secure them, the guard watching without: but their hearts were hardened, and they could think of nothing but the impious and vain pursuance of their own counsels against the truth of Christ.

**V. 24.** *{they doubted, &c.}* διηπόρουν; it comes of ἀπορέω, *ad angustias detrudi inexplicabiles haesito*: it is commonly said of those who know not which way to turn themselves; such shall be the condition of the wicked, when they shall see the evident presages of God’s eminent judgment, Luke 21. 25. Thus God’s judgments often startle the wicked, but convert them not.

**V. 25.** *{then came one and told them, &c.}* This message troubles them more: while they are consulting to destroy them for preaching, they hear that they are preaching again in the temple. There is no counsel against God, Prov. 21. 30.

**V. 26.** *{then went the captain with the officers}* See on c. 4. 1. No doubt they sent now with greater indignation than before, as conceiving their authority concerned: yet dared they not use violence as before, because they feared the people might take part; they feared men but not God: yet he makes that fear of a guilty conscience a bridle to restrain the rage of the wicked.

**V. 27.** *{set them before the council}* Here was some show of equity; to hear them before they would condemn them.

**V. 28.** *{did not we straitly command you}* Chap. 4. 18. The enraged high priest charges them with three pretended crimes: first contumacy against their authority: *did not we straitly command you, &c.*

*{behold ye have filled Jerusalem, &c.}* Here was the second charge of schism and heresy: that was by their law, likewise capital, Deut. 13.

*{this man’s blood upon us}* That is, to make us seem guilty of Christ’s death: thus hypocrites will be guilty, but not seem so. This was their third charge: it seemed to be an intolerable disgrace and dishonor to the chief priests and elders, to be accused openly of injustice and shedding innocent blood.

**V. 29.** *{we ought to obey God rather than man}* That is, when men command or forbid us anything contrary to the word of God: we ought to obey God, and not man. See c. 4. 19. Dan. 3. 18.

**V. 30.** *{the God of our fathers}* Chap. 3. 13.

**V. 31.** *{his right hand}* τῇ δεξιᾷ αὐτοῦ. God with his mighty power has set up him to be a prince and Savior. See c. 2. 36. This is the sum of the apostles’ doctrine, the ground on which the anchor of their souls fasten who shall be saved. The office of Christ is described by two things. First in that he is a prince to govern his kingdom, to beat down the rebellious, and to protect his servants and subjects. Secondly in that he is also a Savior, as his name Jesus imported, and the angel interpreted it, Mat. 1. 21. Herein he declares, as their impiety who would strive against their prince and Savior; so their madness who persecute him whom God exalted by his mighty power. See Psa. 2. 4, 6. Phil. 2. 9.

*{to give repentance}* This shows how Christ reigns in and for his, giving them hearts to repent, that through him they may be reconciled to God, Rom. 5. 1. This they say, to win (if it were possible) some of their enemies to Christ, showing them the only way to be saved in him: it is no more in man’s power to repent, than to create himself; this Christ gives by the Spirit of regeneration, and hereunto is remission of sins most certainly annexed. So that the Jews might hence have gathered an argument against despair, though they had crucified Christ if they could but have repented. See Luke 24. 47. and c. 2. 38.

**V. 32.** *{whom God hath given to them that obey him}* *That obey Christ*, *who believe in him*; the Syriac has it: indeed there is no true obedience without faith, nor faith without obedience. Oecumenius gives this sense; that he gives remission of sins, we are witnesses, and the Holy Ghost also, who would not have come upon us, εἰ μὴ πρότερον αἱ ἁμαρτίαι ἐλύοντο, except our sins had first been forgiven; and that the Jews, notwithstanding their sin in crucifying Christ should not be deprived of their share in the same gift, if they would now repent and obey: but we must not understand that God gave the Spirit of regeneration to any because he obeyed: the effect cannot precede the cause; the Holy Ghost gives a heart to believe and obey, without which we can neither believe nor obey. But if this (*whom God hath given*) relate to the extraordinary gifts of the Holy Ghost, (as speaking tongues, power of healing, &c.) then the sense may be, as Oecumenius intimates; God gave these extraordinary graces, to us whom he made obedient to the faith, and so will he to you, if you will obey. Now the sum of this argument is, that Christ is thus exalted: first we are eyewitnesses, who eat and drank, and conversed with him after his resurrection, and after his charge given us to testify these things, saw him ascending into heaven. Secondly, the Holy Ghost witnesses the same in our gifts and tongues and power to do these miracles (which yourselves have seen, or otherwise certainly know) in the name and by the power of Jesus whom you crucified: which miracles it had been utterly impossible to have done by his command and power, except he had not only lived, being raised from the dead, but also were so highly exalted, to glory and supreme power, as to be able to give us ability to do all that he herein commands us.

**V. 33.** *{they were cut to the heart}* διεπρίοντο, πρίω, is properly to cut with a saw: here it signifies a bitter perturbation of anger; taken from them that saw or grind their teeth for anger: the Syriac gives it, *they were cut asunder with anger*: ready to burst with anger and indignation, so that they consult again to kill them: and why are they so angry? The apostles giving them no uncivil or provoking words, it must needs appear an influence of Satan, who hates all truth; therefore they (as our modern heretics and schismatics) regard not the most evident proofs from Scripture; but burn with a devilish and revengeful malice against the apostles.

**V. 34.** *{Gamaliel}* This was Paul’s tutor, chap. 22. 3. whom God’s providence stirred up to moderate and allay the precipitate heat and fury of the rest of the council who were resolved to destroy the apostles: God sometimes raises up friends among our enemies to speak for us and to save us. See Gen. 37. 20, 21. Joh. 7. 50, 51.

**V. 35.** *{take heed to yourselves}* Do nothing rashly, and out of passion: but advise well, before you enterprise that which may hurt yourselves.

**V. 36.** *{rose up Theudas}* A notorious impostor; who under pretense of being a prophet, moved sedition among the Jews: now he speaks hereof as of a thing not very fresh, nor much before those times: this council was held about the year of our Lord 35. See *Is. Casaubon. exerc.* 2. N. 57. and *Theod. Bez. in loc.*

**V. 37.** *{in the days of taxing}* The insurrection which this Judas the Galilean (not Judas the Gaulonite) made against the Romans, was, when they sent out another tax, after that which is mentioned, Luke 2. 2. See *Isaac Casaubon., Exercit.* 2. N. 59. *Ioh. Drusii praeterit. lib.* 5. and *Theod. Bez.* ibid.

**V. 38.** *{now I say refrain}* He persuades them to moderation by a dilemma: either this work which these apostles and followers of Christ act, is of man, or of God: if of men, it will come to nought without your troubling yourselves with them: but if it be of God, you can never reasonably hope to prevail against it: it is vain and impious to fight against God.

**V. 39.** *{lest haply ye be found, &c.}* μήποτε; *lest*, that is, beware, take heed lest, &c. It is a concise and short manner of speaking: as Gen. 3. 22. *lest he put his hand, &c.* That is, *prospiciamus ne*, &c. See Mat. 25. 9. Budaeus notes that this word, is not so much, *percontantis*, as *addubitantis & addubitanter decernentis*.

**V. 40.** *{When they had beaten them}* This kind of punishment, it seems, the Romans permitted the Jews to execute within their synagogues, but not publicly, Mat. 10. 12. 2 Cor. 11. 24.

*{they commanded that, &c.}* In some things they are advised by Gamaliel, but other things they act against his counsel, in that they beat them and prohibited their preaching, in which they but fought against God.

**V. 41.** *{rejoicing that they were counted worthy, &c.}* They were not Stoics, men not sensible of injuries and disgraces, but they who had in their infirmity forsaken their Master, now in strength of Spirit, rejoice that they may suffer for his name: they remember now that Christ told them, that they are blessed who suffer so, Mat. 5.

**V. 42.** *{and daily in the Temple, &c.}* Constancy accompanies their joy: they are not deterred by their enemies’ threatenings, but preach the word freely, in season and out of season. (2 Tim. 4. 2.) oppositions of truth, are as the conflicts of the wind with fire, which inflame it more: such is the admirable work of God’s Spirit giving confidence in Christ, and assurance of victory in him: *gaudet Petrus in verberibus, qui ante in verbo timebat. Gregor. hom.* 30. *in Evang.* *Peter rejoices in stripes, who before, was afraid at* (a damsel’s) *words.*

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1.** *{The Grecians against the Hebrews}* These were Jews of Palestine, which here are called Hebrews, born and bred there; and Jews of Greece, here called Grecians: whose ancestors were Jews, though they had dwelt in Greece, and used the Greek tongue as their native. See *Jo. Drus. praeter.* l. 5.

*{were neglected in their daily ministration}* They were not duly relieved by those who had the care and oversight of the poor.

**V. 2.** *{and serve tables}* To make provision for, or serve at tables, in their ordinary diet provided for the poor; and at their feasts of charity; because they had greater work in hand; that is, to preach the word of God, as, verse 4.

**V. 3.** *{Look out among you seven men, &c.}* Thus they show them a means to take away the grievance, and to compose the difference, that they may hold unity, and establish some discipline and order, without which no church can subsist: they name seven, not tying other churches to that number, but considering what was sufficient for them; and they show how church officers ought to be qualified, as in the following words appears — *of honest report, &c.*

**V. 4.** *{but we will give ourselves continually, &c.}* They mean not to assign the charge to some surrogates, or deputies, that themselves might live at ease, but to follow a more principal part of their ministry, consisting in these two specified duties, prayer and ministry of the word.

**V. 5.** *{Philip}* Chap. 21. 8.

*{a proselyte of Antioch}* See chap. 2. 10.

**V. 6.** *{and laid their hands on them}* By this imposition of hands the primitive church signified a consecration of a man to God, in some office or design, as chap. 13. 3. This rite the Hebrews used in their solemn sacrifices, and in their prayers, and private blessings, as Gen. 48. 14. Mat. 19. 13. And this same rite the Christian church used in ordination of ministers, as here, so 1 Tim. 5. 21. and many other places it appears; to avoid intrusion into that sacred office; and to signify the approbation of the church thereby declaring them fit, and authorized to their ministry, by it, as by their public seal. See 1 Tim. 4. 14. 2 Tim. 1. 6. Hebr. 6. 2. Which being now cast off, and despised by schismatics, has brought in so much confusion and misery upon the church: every vain babbler now, (without any ordination by imposition of their hands whom God trusted with that power) intruding at his pleasure, into the office of the ministry.

**V. 7.** *{obedient to the faith}* That is, the doctrine of the gospel, Rom. 1. 5. and 16. 26. The sense is, that now many of the Levitical priests (before, the most implacable enemies to Christ, and the most active adversaries of the gospel) were converted to the faith, and truth of the gospel; though it be most probable, that this (πολύς τε ὄχλος, *a great multitude, or company*) rather relates to the Jews in general, than peculiarly to the priests, the Syriac gives it, *& populus multus ex Judaeis obediebat fidei*, *much people of the Jews obeyed*; multitudes, among whom were also some, or many priests.

**V. 8.** *{Full of faith}* That is, of an excellent measure of faith, and powerful in miracles.

**V. 9.** *{certain of the Synagogue}* It is likely that the Romans permitted other nations, having trade at Jerusalem, to have their synagogues, or church by themselves.

*{the Libertines}* Thus the Romans called the issue of their slaves when they were *manumitted*, or set at liberty, as Beda thinks. *Chrysost. hom.* 15. takes these Libertines for any whom the Romans *manumitted*. *Philo. legat. ad Caium*, cited by *Theod. Beza*, describes them thus; that Tiberius suffered a good part of the city beyond the Tiber, to be inhabited of the Jews, whereof the most part were Libertines, who being taken captives and brought home, were afterward set at liberty, and not compelled to change any thing of their country’s rites; all which makes it more probable, that it was not a college for youth, but a synagogue for those who came from Rome, or other places to Jerusalem, being men of this condition.

*{Cyrenians}* A sort of philosophers, of whom Cicero speaks *l. 1. de fin. Diog. Laert. l. 2. Aristip.*

*{of Cilicia}* A country in Asia the lesser, in which was Tarsus, the city where Paul was born, c. 22. 3.

**V. 10.** *{They were not able to resist}* Here also was the promise of Christ, Luke 21. 15. fulfilled. So much were these sophisters deceived, who promised themselves an easy encounter with, and victory over, an unlearned antagonist; they knew not that they had to deal with the invincible wisdom of the Holy Ghost speaking in him.

**V. 11.** *{then they suborned men that said}* They instructed false witnesses what they should say against Stephen: to such artifices, Satan’s instruments still turn themselves, not able to make their party good by Scripture, and evidence of truth, they flee to false witnesses, lies, and calumnies.

*{we have heard him speak, &c.}* The devil the father of liars, as also his children perjured false witnesses are ashamed of no lies: otherwise they would not have accused this saint of blasphemy: what was the matter of that blasphemy they accused him of, is set down v. 14. They counted it high blasphemy for any man to speak concerning the abrogation of the ceremonial law; because they could not for the veil laid over their hearts, look unto Jesus the end of the law; or the demolishing of the temple of Jerusalem, with which possibly they dreamed, the world must have its end. Now because Stephen probably preached the abolition of the ceremonial rites, when all was fulfilled in Christ the truth and substance of all legal shadows, they presently conceive that he preached blasphemy. ’Tis a common folly, ignorant men accusing them of error, whom they understand not; discover their malice and ignorance.

**V. 12.** *{They stirred up the people}* Being foiled in their disputes, they burn with revenge, composing their plot against him, first by suborned witnesses, next by clamoring among the people, and complaining of him to the priests; and then by laying violent hands on him to convent him before the council.

**V. 13.** *{To speak blasphemous words, &c.}* The accusations run very high; first that he spoke words against that holy city, and the temple; and secondly that he also blasphemed the law: it is usual for the most impious to accuse the servants of God, of greatest impieties: so they accused the most Holy Jesus: such accusations fasten not on the just, (for God will finally judge) but render the accusers the more unjust and guilty.

**V. 14.** *{That this Jesus, &c.}* This they spoke in scorn and contempt: so they misconstrued, and wrested the words of this holy preacher; as the enemies of religion still do.

*{the customs}* Or *rites*.

**V. 15.** *{as it had been the face of an Angel}* With an admirable splendor and majesty, such as we read of Moses, Exod. 34. 30. an argument of a good conscience, and the divine presence of the Holy Ghost assisting him.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1.** *{Are these things so}* This says he, having heard the accusations of the false witnesses; as if he would give Stephen a fair liberty to defend himself, whom he yet prejudged, resolved beforehand to condemn anything which should be alleged in defense of the truth of Christ. See also John 9. 22. Therefore he demanded not why Stephen preached, as he did, but whether he preached so, as intimating that if he preached Christ, he was to be condemned.

**V. 2.** *{men, brethren, and fathers, &c.}* Thus he styles them, because however they were professed enemies of the gospel, yet were they governors of the church of Israel, which God had not yet rejected, and thus he would gain attention to that which he spoke, in hope it might take with some of them, for whom he also prayed: and so did it. See chap. 8. 1, 9. 19, 20.

*{the God of glory}* See 1 Cor. 2. 3. He to whom all honor and glory is due, Rev. 4. 11. and 5. 12. Rom. 11. 36. This he says to distinguish him from all false gods; and to show that he worshiped and preached none but the true God.

*{when he was in Mesopotamia}* In Ur of the Chaldees, Gen. 11. 28. He speaks here of Mesopotamia, as comprehending Babylon, and Chaldea in it: and this he says to intimate, that the true religion was not (as some of them vainly conceived) tied to Jerusalem, or Palestine: for God appeared to Abraham being yet in Mesopotamia, and called him thence, and that he went not immediately into Canaan, but dwelt a time in Charran, a city of Mesopotamia, unto the death of his father Thare: the purpose is to show them, that there was truth of religion with Abraham, before he ever saw Canaan, or that he received circumcision, and before any of the ceremonies appointed by the ministry of Moses were in use; before there was either temple, or tabernacle. See Rom. 4. 10. &c. Carran was a city famous for the destruction of Crassus, and the Roman army there. *Plutarc. Cras. Dio. Roman. lib.* 40. *Plin. nat. hist. lib.* 5. *cap.* 24.

**V. 3.** *{Get thee out of thy Country}* Gen. 12. 1. Hebr. 11. 8. He will show them, that neither the land of Canaan which Abraham had yet never seen, nor the ceremonies of the law not yet given by the ministry of Moses, nor the temple which was not yet built, did anything avail Abraham, as to the favor of God; but this faith which the author to the Hebrews forecited, speaks of; and therefore it was a frivolous objection concerning their rites, and holy place: as if God could nowhere else be served but at Jerusalem, or would no otherwise than by legal ceremonies: nay but faith and obedience to God’s command here set off the excellency of Abraham; both which have here a sharp trial; in that God commanded him to go out of his country, from his kindred, and that to a place and people utterly unknown to Abraham; herein Abraham’s obedience was an evident demonstration of that faith which was imputed to him for righteousness, Rom. 4. 3.

**V. 4.** *{Then came he out}* When he had received a command from God; which commends the readiness of his faith, subjecting all his interest and affections, to God’s will and pleasure.

*{he removed him}* God removed him, or removing him caused him to dwell, by commanding him to go into Canaan, which he would give to his posterity, Gen. 11. 26.

**V. 5.** *{gave him no inheritance, &c.}* These things are said in respect of the general and full possession, which he gave him in his posterity: for otherwise he purchased a field of Ephron, Gen. 23. 17. Thus God tried Abraham, and thus he tries many of those whom he calls heirs of the world, (Rom. 8. 17. Tit. 3. 7. 1 Cor. 3. 22.) suffering them for the present to want possessions, that they may learn with Abraham to cast themselves wholly on God’s providence, and to follow his guidance, and to look for *a city which hath foundation* (in heaven) Hebr. 11. 10.

*{when as yet he had no child}* This sets off the excellency of Abraham’s faith. See Gen. 15. 2. &c. believing the promise of God in things to carnal reason improbable: for the promise of the land of Canaan to be given to Abraham’s seed and posterity, when as yet he had no child; his wife was barren and old, himself in years, seemed in relation to second causes, more than improbable; yet *Abraham believed, against hope, in hope, that he might become the father of many nations, according to that which was spoken, so shall thy seed be*, Rom. 4. 18.

**V. 6.** *{that his seed should sojourn}* This also shows Abraham’s faith depending on God, while yet to carnal reason there appeared no probability of enjoying those promises; God telling of a posterity, but to be as he said, Gen. 23. 4. גר־ותושב *ger-vethoshab*. 70. πάροικος καὶ παρεπίδημος, *a stranger and sojourner*; that they should live in bondage, and be evilly entreated so long.

*{four hundred years}* Gen. 15. 13. beginning to reckon the years from the birth of Isaac. See Exod. 12. 41. yet *Abraham believed* the Lord: true faith has heavenly eyes, and can see the truth of God through a thousand difficulties: see the ground, Rom. 4. 18, 19, 20, 21. *Abraham was fully perswaded, that what he had promised, he was able to perform*: God’s promises are for determinate times, and therefore sometimes to us, seem slow, but are ever sure; and the more difficulties seem to intercept them, the more comfortable shall their performance be to us at last.

**V. 7.** *{The nation to whom}* That is, the Egyptians. That was added to comfort him that was not weak in faith: the strongest faith is subject to temptation, and therefore has need of confirmation.

*{I will judge}* κρινῶ *I will punish*. So 1 Cor. 11. 31. which he did in sundry plagues, Exod. 7. 20. & 8. 6, 17, 20. Exod. 9. 6, 10, 23. & 10. 14, 22. & 12. 29. & 14. 27, 26. Fear not the enemies, but be patient: God will assuredly judge for his people. See Deut. 32. 23. Rom. 12. 19. Israel long suffered, but at the very appointed day, God revenged them, and delivered them with a mighty hand.

*{after that}* The 400. years ended, Israel the seed of Abraham shall come out of Egypt, *the self-same day*, Exod. 12. 41.

*{and serve me in this place}* That is, on Mount Horeb. See Exod. 3. 1, 12. Exod. 17. 1, 6. Deut. 1. 2, 6, 19. & 4. 10, 15. & 18. 16. & 29. 1. God delivers his people out of pressures, and miseries, that they may serve him. See Luke 1. 74, 75.

**V. 8.** *{and he gave}* Gen. 17. 9. which signified the cutting off carnal affections. *Theophylact.* Which was to continue until the promised seed was come, and had fulfilled all righteousness, Gal. 5. 2, 3. Mat. 3. 15.

*{Abraham begat Isaac}* Gen. 21. 3. Mat. 1. 2.

*{Isaac begat Jacob}* Gen. 25. 26. Mat. 1. 2.

*{Jacob begat the twelve Patriarchs}* Gen. 29. 3. &c.

**V. 9.** *{the Patriarchs moved with envy}* Gen. 37. 28.

*{God was with him}* He favored, blessed, and delivered him, verse 10.

**V. 10.** *{and delivered him}* This was the effect of God’s presence, never inactive for his servants’ good: so he did at last deliver Joseph from the servitude and calamity into which his brethren sold him, from the malice and calumny of his lewd mistress, and from prison: if God be with you, fear not, Rom. 8. The soul cannot be in the body without some shows, and effects of life; much less can the Spirit of God be with the elect in vain.

*{and gave him favour}* Gen. 41. 37.

**V. 11.** *{then came a dearth, &c.}* Gen. 41. 54.

**V. 12.** *{but when Jacob heard}* Gen. 42. 1.

**V. 13.** *{at the second time}* Gen. 45. 4.

**V. 14.** *{all his kindred}* πᾶσαν τὴν συγγένειαν, the word signifies both consanguinity and affinity, as does the word *cognatio* among the Latins (which the interpreter of the Syriac here uses) import kindred, or alliance: and so συγγενής, is *kin*, or *ally*, either man or woman, as Suidas interprets: the number here mentioned, compared with that number coming out of Egypt, Exod. 12. 37. *six hundred thousand men besides children*, considering their abode in Egypt, and Pharaoh’s cruelty to destroy their children, may well be reckoned among the admirable works of God, Deut. 10. 22.

*{threescore and fifteen souls}* Whereas these persons are variably reckoned 66. 70. & 75. we may conceive that this variety proceeds from the several accounts; in one account they only are reckoned, who lineally descended from Jacob, to be sixty six persons, Gen. 46. 26. In a second account, the whole number of the promised seed going into, or being in Egypt, are reckoned seventy persons; for so, if we account, Jacob; Joseph, Ephraim, and Manasses, with the sixty six of the first account, the number is seventy specified, Gen. 46. 27. In a third account are reckoned Jacob’s sons’ wives (excepted in the former reckoning as appears, Gen. 46. 26.) to make up the seventy five persons in this word συγγένειαν, signifying as has been said, as well affinity (or kindred by marriage) as consanguinity or kindred by blood; one thing in this and the like places, is ever necessarily to be remembered that the apostle did not in vain warn us of too much curiosity about fruitless questions. See 1 Tim. 1. 4. Tit. 3. 10.

**V. 15.** *{so Jacob went down into Egypt}* Gen. 46. 5. Hereby he would show them that their fathers had the truth of religion, and God’s favor in Egypt, before they had either ceremony, temple, or Canaan to repair to, to the end that they might not vainly put sanctity in place, or ceremony.

**V. 16.** *{and were carried over}* Meaning Jacob, and Joseph at least, if not all the rest of the patriarchs dying in Egypt.

*{into Sychem}* Understand this distributively, as if he said; some were buried in Sychem which Jacob bought, Gen. 33. 19. as Joseph, which fell to his lot in his son Ephraim, Joh. 24. 32. And some (as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob Gen. 50. 13.) were buried in Hebron, in the field that Abraham bought for money of Ephron (Gen. 23. 17.) See Mat. 26. 8. John 12. 4.

*{of the sons of Emmor}* Or, *besides that of the sons of Emmor of Sychem*, besides that place, or sepulcher which Jacob bought of the sons of Emmor of Sychem, where Joseph’s bones were buried; here is set παρὰ τῶν υἱῶν, for παρὰ τὸ μνημεῖον τῶν υἱῶν Ἐμμὼρ, that is, *praeter illud Sepulchrum filiorum Emmor*.

**V. 17.** *{when the time of promise drew near}* That is, when the time of fulfilling God’s promise made to Abraham, drew near; for multiplying his seed, Gen. 15. 5. then God increased them, when men endeavored maliciously to destroy them.

**V. 18.** *{until another King arose}* Another Pharaoh: (so their kings were called, as the Roman emperors Caesars) succeeded that Pharaoh, who had Joseph and his kindred in so much honor.

**V. 19.** *{The same dealt subtilly with our kindred}* The Syriac has it, *he compassed or set himself against our kindred*: the word κατασοφισάμενος, of κατασοφίζω (which is sophistically to deceive, to beguile or circumvent with a fallacy) imports, craftily deceiving, or plotting against one: he alludes to that which Pharaoh said, Exod. 1. 10. come, said he נתחכמה לו *nithchachma lo*. 70. κατασοφισώμεθα αὐτοῖς; the word here used, חכם *chacham* signifies *astutus fuit*, he was crafty, and, *sapuit*; he was wise: and so חכמה *chochma*, is craft, as well as wisdom: so here it imports a clandestine and secret destroying the Israelites: for so he first gave in charge to the midwives: but that not taking, he used open violence, commanding the male infants to be cast into the river. See Psalm 105. 14.

**V. 20.** *{in which time Moses was born}* Exod. 2. 2.

*{was exceeding fair}* Hebr. 11. 23. Or *fair to God* ἀστεῖος τῷ θεῷ the Hebrew has it טוב, which with them signifies, *good, or beautiful*, Exod. 2. 2. The 70. give it ἀστεῖος, and Gen. 24. 16. Rebecca is said to be טבת *tubath*, where the 70. give it καλὴ, *very fair to look upon*, *beautiful to God*, which Stephen here adds, as emphatical; for every excellency is attributed to God, so Nineveh is said to be עיר גדולה לאלהים *gir-gedolah lelohim*, *a city great unto the Lord*; that is a very great, or beautiful city. So Moses was fair τῷ θεῷ, that is, by the singular providence and counsel of God that hereby Pharaoh’s daughter might be moved to save him, and bring him up as her son, thereby fitting him for the great work to which God had assigned him; of his beauty Justin also speaks *hist.* l. 36.

**V. 21.** *{for her own Son}* Exod. 2. 9, 10. *Hebr. and he was unto her* לבן *leben*, the 70. as here, εἰς υἱὸν (*chald.* לבר) Elias Hutter renders it, *quem illa adoptavit in locum filii* *whom she adopted her son*; so she kept him and brought him up, as if he had been her own son.

**V. 22.** *{in all the wisdom of the Egyptians}* In philosophy, and all the liberal sciences of the Egyptians.

**V. 23.** *{to visit his brethren}* ἐπισκέψασθαι; which as it sometimes imports visiting to punish, Jer. 5. 9. μὴ ἐπὶ τούτοις ἐπισκέψομαι? *Shall I not visit for these things?* So also it signifies, to visit for help and comfort; as physicians visit their patients: so they said, Luke 7. 16. the Lord ἐπεσκέψατο *has visited his people*: so were the governors of the church called ἐπίσκοποι, *quorum est inspectio in praesidia Christi, & summa cura*, says Budaeus: because they were *quasi speculatores*; overseers of the faith and manners of the people committed to their charge. See chap. 20. 28. So the apostles visited the churches which they had planted, to reform their errors in faith and conversation and to confirm and comfort them in the truth: now that Moses having been brought up forty years in King Pharaoh’s court, should on a sudden have it come into his heart, to look into the condition of his afflicted brethren the Israelites, it appears to be by the instinct and motion of God’s Spirit, to do that which neither they, nor Moses before thought of.

**V. 24.** *{seeing one of them}* Exod. 2. 11.

*{smote the Egyptian}* See Exod. 2. 11. He did not herein give an imitable example for any private revenge, but to show them that he was appointed a deliverer for Israel, which he hoped they would have understood, verse 25. The power of the sword is in the lawful magistrate only.

**V. 26.** *{the next day}* Exod. 2. 13.

*{he shewed himself}* He offered himself again unto them, in that posture of a deliverer out of those afflictions, and that appointed by God.

*{would have set them at one}* συνήλασε: Syr. interpret, *Suadebat eis*; *he gravely exhorted them to peace*: it is a principal part of a governor’s office, to compose contentions, and to preserve peace.

*{Sirs ye are brethren, why do ye, &c.}* He remembers them of their near conjunction, and the bonds of nature which ought to unite them in love and peace: all dissension is evil, but that worst, which breaks the bonds of nature, or holy profession. It was Joseph’s charge to his brethren, Gen. 45. 24. *See that ye fall not out by the way*; and their argument of reconciliation to Joseph, *forgive the trespass of the servants of the God of thy father*, Gen. 50. 17. Divisions between brothers and professors of one religion, are offenses multiplied by all the bonds which should unite them together.

**V. 27.** *{But he that did his neighbour wrong, thrust him away, &c.}* Which shows that the injurious are most averse from peace, an evil conscience hating any discovery, and sin being impatient of any reproof. See Prov. 15. 12. Yet they to whom God has committed this charge, must perform it for their own indemnity. See Ezek. 3. 19.

**V. 28.** *{wilt thou kill me, as thou didst, &c.}* Moses dealt meekly with him, but he that was faulty (as Oecumen. says) πατρῴᾳ χρώμενος τῇ τῶν εὐεργετῶν ἀσελγείᾳ, *using his country* (a Jewish) *procacity against their benefactors* rejected him; by this his intended recrimination assailing to cause Moses to desist from reproving: so the guilty would ever veil their crimes by others’ failings.

**V. 29.** *{Moses fled at this saying}* See Exod. 2. 15. &c. When Moses perceived that it was carried to court by some of his own brethren, and that Pharaoh sought to slay him, he saved himself by flight into another nation and dominion.

*{a stranger in the land of Madian}* Or *Midian*: a people that came of Madian Abraham’s son by Keturah, Gen. 25. 1, 2. Here Moses married with Zipporah, daughter of Jethro, or Jether (he was also called Reuel, Exod. 2. 18. and Hobab. Numb. 10. 29. Jud. 4. 11.) priest or prince of Midian: where he had two sons born.

**V. 30.** *{When forty years were expired}* Exod. 3. 2. Here was the long trial of Moses’ faith; and the mischief that one or few men’s sins brought upon all Israel, driving away their deliverer; and as much as in them was, lengthening their heavy oppression forty years: this was their ingratitude to God and Moses, whom they sorely wanted all that time.

*{in the wilderness of mount Sina}* Where he kept the flock of Jethro his father-in-law, on the backside of the desert of Arabia. Exod. 3. 2. (with so much humility, as if he had never been brought up at court, as a son of King Pharaoh’s daughter) with admirable patience expecting God’s time for Israel’s deliverance: this place was called the wilderness or desert of Sina, or Sin; which is all that country that lies between Elim (where Israel found twelve wells of water, &c. Exod. 15. 27.) and Sinai. Exod. 16. 1. There was much pasture for cattle: it took name from that very high Mount Sinai, which was also Agar. Gal. 4. 25. and Horeb, the mountain of the Lord, which is a part thereof, it having two heads or tops; that on the east is Horeb, that on the west part of the mount, Sinai: it was called the mountain of the Lord, because it was great, high and excellent (See on verse 20.) or because it was sanctified of God by his appearing to Moses first, Exod. 3. 2. &c. and again when he came down upon it to give the law, Exod. 18. 5. and 19. 3, 17, 18. fifty days after their departure from Egypt. See *Jerom To.* 3. *Fabiol. Oct. Mans.* and *de loc. Hebr.* Horeb signifies dryness, of חרב *charab, aruit*. So Hag. 1. 11. I called חרב *Choreb a drought, or dryness* for this wilderness had not waters, Deut. 8. 15. this mount had the name of Sina, Exo. 19. 1, 18. either of the bushes there growing, (for סנה *seneh* is a bush or bramble, *Jerom, Fabiolae q. s.*) or because God there appeared to Moses in the bush burning, but not consuming.

*{an angel of the Lord}* The angel of the covenant Christ, as appears, verse 32. Here he shows by whom Moses was called to that great and difficult charge of bringing Israel out of Egypt; not by any counsel, or design of man, but by the Son of God, Christ, who is styled the angel, Mal. 3. 1. in respect of his office of mediatorship: for the same is here avowed, verse 32. to be the God of the fathers, Abraham, Isaac and Jacob.

*{of fire in a bush}* Thus God appeared to Moses, who at other times, and on other occasions appeared to the holy fathers in other assumed visible representations of his presence, accommodated ever to those things which he would thereby teach them, or declare unto them: so here he appeared in the form of fire in a bush, not consumed thereby, to intimate that Israel in their fiery trials and hard afflictions should not be consumed, but admirably declared to be the people, whose God was among them to keep them so that the most devouring creatures should not destroy or harm them.

**V. 31.** *{when Moses saw it he wondered}* To see the fire in the bush, and it not consumed, which bore a type of the church in fiery trials not destroyed. See *Jerom q. s.* This was done, that, Moses might be touched with the sense of God’s presence, and so be made more attentive to that which he would then give him in charge.

*{the voice of the Lord came unto him}* ἐγένετο φωνὴ, it is an usual Hebraism, Jer. 7. 1. &c. 70. ἐγένετο λόγος κυρίου, &c. Jer. 1. 4. The Syriac has it here, *the Lord said unto him* בקלא *beqolo, in a voice*; that is, the Lord spoke to him, not in a vision or dream (as sometimes he did to the patriarchs and prophets) but in an audible voice.

**V. 32.** *{I am the God of thy fathers, &c.}* That Moses might not doubt of his calling, God who called him, reveals himself to him; to which end also he mentions his mercies to the fathers, unto whom he made the promises of delivering his people, their posterity, from the bondage they were now in; to assure him that he would perform the same, and that the time was now come wherein he would do it: hereon was Moses’ confidence grounded, because he had the sure covenant of God with the fathers, and his word to himself for warrant: from this place, Exod. 3. Christ proves the resurrection of the dead, Matth. 22. 31.

*{Moses trembled and durst not behold}* So dreadful is the presence of God to flesh and blood, though appearing in mercy. See Exod. 19. 16. Daniel 8. 17. This was to strike the greater reverence into Moses. See also Exod. 20. 20. *nulla quippe admiratio est sine quadam formidine, Augustin in Psal.* 105. *there is no admiration without a certain fear*, how terrible will it be, when he comes to take vengeance on those who have obstinately contemned him in his ordinance and holy laws?

**V. 33.** *{put off thy shoes from thy feet}* In sign of reverence, and resignation of yourself unto my providence. See Ruth. 4. 7.

*{is holy ground}* In respect of God’s present manifestation of his presence and majesty there, not otherwise.

**V. 34.** *{I have seen, I have seen}* Exod. 3. 7. ראה ראיתי *rao raithi*, 70. ἰδὼν εἶδον, *I have surely seen*; this doubling among the Hebrews is emphatical: God is said to see our evil or affliction, when he regards it so as to undertake our deliverance: as on the contrary he is said to shut his eyes, or ears, or to turn away; when he will not regard, or deliver: this is spoken to our capacity: and so he is said to hear their groanings, and to come down; though indeed the evidence of every fact or passion be ever present with him, and though he who fills heaven and earth, comprehending all, comprehensible of none, changes not place (Jer. 23. 24.) yet is he said to descend when he evidently manifests his presence: this Stephen here mentions to beat down all opinion of merit in their fathers, God saw nothing in them but afflictions to pity. See verse 51.

**V. 35.** *{by the hands of the angels}* This Moses whom they ungratefully calumniated as a delinquent did God honor in making him a ruler and deliverer of Israel, by the hand of the Angel of the covenant, Christ. Gr. ἐν χειρὶ *in the hand*, that is, by the hand. Hebrews used to say ביד *bejad in the hand*, for *by the hand*. So Levit. 26. 46. &c. they called anything which was an instrument to act by יד *jad*: but here it imports the principal agent, Christ; (as 1 Cor. 10. 4.) by whose power Moses was enabled to that great work.

**V. 36.** *{shewed wonders}* Exod. 7. 9. &c.

*{in the red sea}* Exod. 14. Hebr. ים סוף *jam suph; mari junci*, or *juncosum*; because of the abundance of bulrushes growing on the shore, some think it is so called: nor is it called the Red Sea; from the color of the ground or sand appearing through the shallow waters, as *V. Schindler. Pentagl.* notes. So *Aristophanus Avib.* calls it ἐρυθρὰν θαλάτταν. See *Strabo lib.* 16. *Plin. lib.* 13. 25. Some give the Hebrew by *mare finis*, because it is *mare ultimum terrae sanctae*: the bound of the holy land (*Schindl.* cites *Benjamin itenerar.*) so indeed it is to the southward, Exod. 23. 31.

*{and in the wilderness}* Exod. 16. &c.

**V. 37.** *{a Prophet shall the Lord, &c.}* Deut. 18. 15.

*{like unto me}* Or *as myself*, that is, Christ in his human nature in all things, sin excepted, like man, truly man by whom God will speak unto you in the last times, Heb. 1. 1. &c. as now in Israel’s minority by me and my ministry.

**V. 38.** *{This is he that was}* Exod. 19. 3.

*{Church in the wilderness}* Or *congregation*.

*{lively oracles}* The powerful words which God spoke to Moses. See Numb. 12. 6, 7, 8. John 6. 68. See Deut. 32. 47.

**V. 39.** *{Our Fathers would not obey}* He shows here, that their fathers of whom they so much gloried, were rebellious against God, who delivered them by the ministry of Moses, as they now were against Christ; who came to save them from sin and destruction: so that neither they, nor their fathers had any merit to save them.

*{in their hearts turned back}* That is, in their desires and affections. (See Numb. 14. 3, 4.) Understand here by Egypt, all that superstition and idolatry to which that impious nation was given; which interpretation appears from the following verse.

**V. 40.** *{Saying unto Aaron}* Exod. 32. 1.

*{make us gods to go, &c.}* That is, images to be carried before us; or to prepare our way: this shows their mind to return to Egyptian idolatry, and impious manners, and the abominable levity of that people, who having had such late and admirable experience of God’s favor toward them, and presence with them, were so soon turning back to the senseless vanities of heathens, who had no knowledge of the power or law of God.

*{we wot not what is become of him}* Thus stupidly and ungratefully do they contemn that admirable deliverance which God wrought for them, by Moses; they knew he was gone up into the mount by God’s command, that he had left Aaron and Hur to govern them, until he returned, Exod. 24. Yet so quickly had they forgotten God, of whose power and gracious presence, they had so much, so late experience, Psa. 106. 13, 21.

**V. 41.** *{They made a calf in those days}* In imitation of the idolatrous Egyptians, who worshiped Apis under the shape of an ox. See *Ammianum Marcellin. l.* 22. *Alexandr. genial. dier. l.* 6. *c.* 2. Psa. 106. 19, 20. Rom. 1. 23. and *Minuc. Fel. Octav. Athanas. contr. Gent. orat.*

*{and offered sacrifice unto the idol}* Sacrificing was then proper to God alone (as Augustin proves, *de civ. Dei. l.* 10. 4.) but here was their gross idolatry, though they might possibly pretend that they would serve God by the idol, their worship being not (as some now would excuse it) by any means terminative in the creature seen. See 1 Cor. 10. 20. But these making an idol, forsook God.

*{rejoiced in the works of their own hands}* See Exod. 32. 6. So prone are idolaters to adore their own, though most irrational and foolish, inventions, as this was; and to please themselves with (that which displeases God) the most sacrilegious profanations: this is the devil’s joy; *cujus gaudium est ruina sanctorum. Prosper, res. ad. Gal.* *whose joy is man’s ruin.*

**V. 42.** *{Then God turned, and gave them up}* So Rom. 1. 25, 26. God withdrew that talent of general grace, or light of knowledge which they made so bad an use of, giving them up to wretched apostasy: yet as *Fulgent. de praedest. ad Monim. l.* 1. truly says: *no man justly sins, though God justly permits him to sin: for justly is he forsaken of God: who forsakes God and because man forsaking God, sins; God forsaking the sinner, does justice.*

*{to worship the host of heaven}* The sun, moon, and stars. Deut. 17. 3. 2 Kings 23. 5.

*{as it is written, &c.}* Amos 5. 25. He denies that they offered unto him, because they corrupted his worship; and so in effect he was not worshiped at all. See Isa. 43. 22. God not accepting the sacrifice of the wicked. Isa. 1. 11. they are an abomination to him. Prov. 28. 9. Psa. 50. 16, 17, 23.

**V. 43.** *{ye took up the tabernacle of Moloch}* Moloch, or Melchem, which some call Saturn (*Minuc. Fel. q. s.*) others Mars: the abomination of the children of Ammon. 1 Kings 11. 5, 7. to whom they sacrificed their sons and daughters.

*{your god, Remphan}* That which the prophet Amos 5. 26. calls כיון *Chijun*: see hereof *Jo. Drusius com.* 1. *ad Voc. N. T. Remphan*, and *praeterit l.* 5. Acts 7. 43. that for *Chijun*, the Greeks read Ῥιμφὰν, Ῥαιφὰν, Ῥεφὰν, (as the Syriac has it רפן) Ῥαφὰν, as *Justin. Martyr. dial. cum. Try. Jud.* or Ῥαιφὰ as Oecumenius, it shows much variety of reading, rather than matter: other idols were variably called likewise; it may be it was the stature of a giant, as the Egyptians were wont to make Hercules, whom they called Chon, which varied into another tongue, might easily make Chijun, and it may be Ῥεφὰ or Ῥεφὰν, might in the change from Hebrew to Greek, come of רפא *rapha*, (a giant) as Gen. 14. 5. *and smote* רפאים *the Rephaims* in Asteroth, that is, the giants, or men of strength, or monstrous stature: it may be that Stephen spoke the words of the prophet in his own tongue, and that Luke writing in the Greek, followed their manner of speaking: it is not material, the sin here reproved was not in the name, but thing, which was an idol, and abomination, however variably called. Most probably it was Moloch or Saturn: now Saturn among the Egyptians (where the 70. translated) was called Ῥεφὰν: see the notes of *Ludovicus de Dieu* upon this place; and *Athanasius Kirkerus c.* 5. *Prodrom. Copti. pag.* 147.

*{and I will carry you, &c.}* Or *therefore*, &c.

*{beyond Babylon}* Into Persia; a country in the east part of Asia; the prophet Amos, 5. 27. has, *therefore will I cause you to go into captivity beyond Damascus*: that is, further than the people of Damascus, which Stephen here interprets, *beyond Babylon*: for as the learned Junius says, the people of Damascus were carried away (by the king of Assyria *Thiglath-Pileser*) into (Kir) Cyromedia near Susa (that royal city, where Cyrus had his magnificent palace. Ester 3. 15. & 8. 15. & 1. 2, 3. and other Persian monarchs) 2 Kings 16. 9. But the Israelites both much further into Persia, and also into the region of the Caspians; as appears, Ezra 8. 17.

**V. 44.** *{the tabernacle of witness}* ἡ σκηνὴ τοῦ μαρτυρίου, that which the Hebrews called אהל מועד *ohel mohed*; either the *tabernacle of condition*, because Israel was commanded to come thither to ask counsel of the Lord (compare Exodus 33. 7 with Exodus 40. 2) or *the tabernacle of the congregation*. Exod. 33. 7. because there Israel congregated in solemn assemblies; and here Stephen calls it, *the tabernacle of witness*, because here God principally witnessed, or showed his glorious presence, and where the ark of the covenant was, and the law and testimonies, concerning the appointment and making hereof. See Exod. 25. Exod. 26. & 27.

*{that he should make it. &c.}* Exod. 25. 40.

**V. 45.** *{in with Jesus}* With Joshua, who bore both name and type of Jesus Christ, in that he brought Israel into the promised rest.

**V. 46.** *{desired to find a tabernacle, &c.}* David found favor to know where the ark should rest, and the temple should be built, as also by whom; when he had begged it in earnest prayer. See Psa. 132. 2 Sam. 24. 18. 2 Sam. 7. 10, 13. 1 Chron. 21. If then unto David’s time the tabernacle had no fixed seat, it must follow, that the worship of God is not tied to place.

**V. 47.** *{But Solomon built him an house}* 1 Chron. 17. 12. 1 Kings 6. 1, 2. &c. 2 Chron. 3. 1, 2.

**V. 48.** *{the most high dwelleth not in temples made, &c.}* Chap. 17. 24. That is, is not comprehended in any place, though he will be worshiped in place: he taxes the impiety of them, who trusted in an imaginary sanctity derived to them from the temple, which they ever profaned, abused, and neglected the true worship of God, there to have been duly performed. These words he infers, lest any should foolishly conceive, that after the temple was built and consecrated, the worship of God was so tied to that place, as if God were shut up therein: no, no, the most High, fills, and comprehended of none, comprehends, heaven, earth, all places.

**V. 49.** *{Heaven is my throne}* Isa. 66. 1, 2.

**V. 50.** *{hath not my hand made, &c.}* Hands are attributed to God, according to our capacity: the scope is to intimate, that God has no need of gold, curious ornaments of his temple, nor sacrifices; as Psa. 50. 10, 11, 12. &c. and that the substance of God’s worship consists not in outward ceremonies, neither did he require any offerings for his own sake, but to exercise men in the duties of piety, and obedience to him: whereby it appears, how much distant human inventions are, from true religion and worship of God.

**V. 51.** *{ye stiffnecked}* He alludes to the words of Jeremie 7. 26. and other like. See Exod. 33. 3. Deut. 9. 6. meaning that they were ever obstinate, and would not submit to the guidance of God’s word.

*{uncircumcised in heart and ears}* Who carry the external sign of circumcision in the flesh, but have not the effect thereof, and inward regeneration of the heart. See Rom. 2. 28, 29. Jer. 9. 26.

**V. 52.** *{which of the prophets}* He shows that their disobedience to God, and persecution of the prophets was not new with them, but even as it were hereditary; so that their pretense of succession and holy ancestors was but a vain ostentation.

*{of the just one}* That is, Christ, 1 John 2. 1. Acts 22. 14. Christ our righteousness. 1 Cor. 1. 30. in whom only we are justified, Rom. 5. 1.

*{betrayers and murderers}* They betrayed him, who hired Judas to betray him; and they murdered him, who delivered him up (with their importune and outrageous suffrages and outcries) to be crucified. See c. 2. 23, 36.

**V. 53.** *{by the disposition of angels}* By the ministry of angels, *by the mandate* (as the Syriac has it) or *proclaiming of angels*, Deut. 33. 2. Gal. 3. 19. Hebr. 2. 2. The meaning is, if you despise the law given by so glorious and dreadful a ministry; no marvel if you despise the gospel proposed to you in so simple and mean a manner, as by infirm men.

**V. 54.** *{were cut to the heart}* See chap. 5. 33. It is very true, *nunquam sine querela, aegra tanguntur*: *Senec. de ira. cap.* 10. *you can never touch the sore without grief of the patient*: all would be praised, *nulli grata reprehensio est* (says *Salvian de gub. Dei lib.* 8. *init.*) none love reproof (none but the wise, Prov. 9. 8. & 19. 25.) that which is worse, the most wicked castaway would rather hear his own false praises, than true reprehension; and be deceived with jeering applause, than saved with wholesome admonitions.

**V. 55.** *{the glory of God, &c.}* In this extraordinary vision, he saw the admirable glory of Christ in heaven, (as mortal man could apprehend) to strengthen him in this trial.

**V. 56.** *{I see the heaven opened, &c.}* This he testifies, to assure them, that they did but vainly strive against the truth of Christ, whom they supposed to be destroyed by their malice, for he was now in the highest glory, reigning with God in heaven.

*{on the right hand of God}* See Psalm 110. 1. Mark 16. 19. Hebr. 1. 3. This is a translatitious speech complying with our understandings, taken from things familiar and known to us, with whom the right hand is the place of honor. See 1 King. 2. 19. Psalm 45. 9. Here understand not with the foolish *Anthropomorphites* (of whom *Socrat. hist. eccles. l.* 6. *c.* 7.) that God has parts and lineaments like man, but as *Peter Chrysologus* well expresses it. *Serm.* 57. *Deus loca nescit, divinitas recipit nil sinistrum*: *God is not in any place* (circumscribed) *the deity has no left hand*: and as *Gr. Nazianzen, or.* 37. says of such like attributes, καὶ τοῦτο ἡμέτερον, *this also belongs to us*: and is attempered to our capacity: here he intimates Christ’s standing as an advocate with the Father, 1 John 2. 1. who is elsewhere described sitting as a judge Mark 16. 19. &c. So says *Ambros. lib.* 10. *ep.* 82. *stabat Jesus quasi advocatus —— sedens enim judicat*, all imports his power and kingdom over all, and his care of us.

**V. 57.** *{then they cried out}* This they do to interrupt his further audience: so wicked men contend with clamor and violence: so grievous was it to Christ’s enemies to hear of his glory: what will it be to see it, and feel his power (See chap. 22. 23.) when he shall come to judge the living and the dead?

**V. 58.** *{and stoned him}* Thus that impiety which began with show of justice ended in tumultuous and raging violence: thus they were to proceed against false prophets, Deut. 13. 10. and to cast out blasphemers. Levit. 24. 14. But here tyranny turned judgment into wormwood; and the wicked and damnable condemn and destroy the innocent and just: the Romans permitted them not this power, but this they acted in a popular rage. See John 18. 31.

*{the witnesses laid down their clothes, &c.}* For the true witnesses against blasphemers were to begin stoning, Deut. 17. 7. They were before, murderers in their tongues by their false witness, and now their seared consciences fear not to imbrue desperate hands, in innocent blood.

*{at a young man’s feet whose name was Saul}* See chap. 22. 4, 20. afterward called Paul, that excellent apostle of Christ, chapter 9. on whom God showed so great mercy in his conversion, 1 Timothy 1. 13. &c. in how desperate a condition was he, whose youth was so active in persecuting Christ? Yet God showed mercy on him, that none might despair, who can but repent and convert.

**V. 59.** *{Lord Jesus receive my spirit}* Probably he used more words, but these were the sum of all. See Psalm 31. 6. This is the true comfort of the elect, that they are assured that Christ Jesus, who died for them, in their dissolutions receives their souls, into his safe and blessed custody (See 1 Peter 4. 19.) to live with him who is the life, and God of the living. Luke 23. 43.

**V. 60.** *{And he kneeled down}* The most decent gesture of humble suppliants. See 2 Chron. 6. 13. Psalm 95. 6. Dan. 6. 10. Acts 9. 40. and 20. 36. & 21. 5. Ephes. 3. 14. Ezra 9. 5. Luke 22. 41.

*{Lord lay not this sin to their charge}* This was the precept and practice of our Savior; Matth. 5. 44. Luke 23. 34. This is invincible charity, which shall give us boldness before the Lord, and assurance that we are his, where God gives this heart, all the injuries of the world shall make the sufferers more happy: and certainly this prayer was not without singular effect, as appears in the example of Saul afterward converted, 1 Tim. 1. 16.

*{he fell asleep}* That is, quietly died, as if he had fallen into a sweet sleep: which term the Scripture often uses to give us secure and certain hope of resurrection from the dead.

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1.** *{And Saul was consenting, &c.}* Chap. 7. 58. So that though he acted not, yet his consent makes him guilty of the same crime: a mind to do evil, is sin in the seed, which time brings forth, James 1. 14, 15. There is an adultery of the heart, Mat. 5. 28. and a murder of the heart, Mat. 22. John 8. 44. God esteems the evil we would do, as done, though we have not done it, and so, justly punishes it, *merito etiam malus punitur affectus, etiam cui non succedit effectus. Augustin. de C. D. lib.* 16. *cap.* 4. *deservedly is an evil affection punished, though the effect* (or success thereof) *follow not*, they that allow of the evil which others do, become guilty of the same sin by their allowance: so were the Jews guilty of all the blood, shed by their fathers from Abel, so many ages before they had being in the world, Mat. 23. 35. because in shedding innocent blood, they showed their consent with them, who first did so; consider their state who are now pleased with all the injuries done to God’s servants; the Syriac has it, *Saul was desirous, and a partaker in his death*.

*{at that time there was a great persecution}* When the people quietly suffered Stephen’s death, then they who before were awed with fear of the people, fell violently on the Christians: hitherto their rage was only against the apostles, but now it was against all the church.

*{all scattered abroad}* Divided in persons, not in doctrine; fear of their persecutors compelling them to save their lives by flight: now began they to do that which Christ fore-commanded them Mat. 10. 23. and so began the partition wall between Jews, and Gentiles to be broken down, Ephes. 2. 14. and whereby it appeared that the time was now come, wherein the Gentiles were to be called and joined in communion with the people of the God of Abraham, who believed in Christ: and now began to be fulfilled that prophecy of Noah, Genes. 9. 27. *God shall enlarge* (or persuade) *Japhet, and he shall dwell in the tents of Shem.*

*{except the apostles}* They yet stayed by it, having received command first to preach the gospel at Jerusalem, Luke 24. 47. not because they were more free from persecution than other Christians; they were then, as many faithful ministers of Christ since, more hated and persecuted than others; but because God would yet a little longer by them hold forth his grace and mercy in Christ, to the murderers of his dear Son Christ Jesus, and bloody persecutors of those, who by his charge and commission, offered them gracious conditions of God’s mercy, if they would yet repent and believe in him for the free remission of their sins; which they obstinately rejecting and putting from them, judged themselves unworthy of any mercy, and therefore were justly rejected, and utterly destroyed. See c. 13. 43.

**V. 2.** *{carried Stephen}* The body of Stephen, these faithful servants of Christ, (notwithstanding the fury of their enemies, those raging butchers) buried solemnly, and with great mourning, considering the church’s loss in him: a moderate, and pious mourning for the dead is justifiable, by the example of Christ, John 11. 35. the patriarchs, Gen. 50. 1. &c. and the saints here, which is observable against the unnatural apathy of Stoics, rather than Christians.

**V. 3.** *{Saul, he made havock}* Which he afterward relates of himself, c. 26. 10, 11. See also c. 9. 1. Gal. 1. 13. He spared no sex, but broke into houses, to drag out Christians to prison, and worse than that, caused them to blaspheme, c. 26. 11. All this in his conversion, sets off the mercy of God, who made so excellent a pastor, of so bloody a wolf.

**V. 4.** *{went every where preaching, &c.}* Thus does God turn the malice of the enemy to the propagation of the gospel, while they being driven from Jerusalem, carry the light thereof, unto the Gentiles, *augetur religio Dei, quanto magis premitur. Lactant. lib.* 5. *c.* 20. *God’s religion flourishes the more, by how much more it is oppressed.*

**V. 5.** *{then Philip went down}* Probably, this was Philip the deacon, chap. 6. 5. for the apostles stayed at Jerusalem, verse 1.

*{the city of Samaria}* See on John 4. 4. and on Luke 17. 11. The city of Samaria is here put for Samaria (as *Urbs Roma*, for Rome) *Samaria* was the name of a mountain, city, and region. See 1 Kings 16. 2 Kings 17. *Samaria* was the metropolis of that province: it being demolished by Hircanus, was built again by Herod, and named Sebaste. *Euseb. Joseph. Antiq. lib.* 13. *cap.* 17. The country of the Samaritans lies between Judea and Galilee. John 4. 4.

*{and preached unto them Christ}* That is, repentance, and remission of sins by Christ, as Luke 14. 27. *preached unto them concerning Christ.* Syr.

**V. 6.** *{with one accord gave heed, &c.}* This shows the admirable power of God uniting hearers’ hearts, and preparing them to attend, where he purposes to save: the Syriac paraphrases here, *they attended unto him, and* מתטפיסין *methtephysin, acquiescebant omnibus*, *they consented to all*, or *were persuaded to all that he said*, טפס מתטפיסין *tephos, acquiescere obedire*, &c. *I. Buxtorf. Jun. lex. Chald. Syr.* The same word is rendered sometimes to be persuaded, as chap. 21. 14. sometimes, to obey, as Gal. 5. 7. Heb. 13. 17. &c. See chap. 5. 29. Sometimes to believe, as John 3. 36. The meaning of this place is, that the Samaritans diligently hearkened to Philip, preaching, and carefully marked what he said, it is a main duty of a hearer: faith is by hearing. Rom. 10. 17. Here was now the first degree thereto, reverend attention to the word of God; no wonder that faith is now so rare, how few attend to God’s word preached.

**V. 7.** *{unclean spirits}* See Mark 1. 23. Luke 4. 33, 36. and 6. 18. and 8. 29. so called, because they are delighted with the spiritual uncleanness and filthiness of sin, and thereto allure and instigate men.

*{Crying with a loud voice}* By which it appeared, that they went out by constraint, not willingly. See Mat. 8. 29, 31. which declares the omnipotency of Christ, in whose name, and by whose divine power they were forced out, however unwilling and reluctant they were.

*{taken with palsies}* This is that which was promised, Mark 16. 17, 18. for under these few recited species, we must understand all maladies; according to the promise of Christ, Mark 16. 17, 18.

**V. 8.** *{great joy in that city}* This was the effect of faith, and hope of reconciliation to God, and future salvation; the effect of God’s kingdom, See Rom. 14. 17. (such variable effects does one and the same word preached, produce; devils and wicked men are vexed, and cut to the heart with it (c. 7. 54.) but the elect, who have interest in Christ, are glad and rejoice at it), in this one example observe, what confidence concerning religion and sanctity may be reposed either in places, or councils and societies of men; the gospel was exploded, blasphemed and persecuted, at Jerusalem, the once holy city, by the scribes, Pharisees, high priests, elders and their great councils; and the same is joyfully received in the despised *Samaria*.

**V. 9.** *{A certain man called Simon}* This was a magician, witch, or conjurer, who of a long time had besotted, and deluded the people of Samaria with his magical enchantments, so that they had generally a great opinion that he was some one of the gods, or that he had some extraordinary favor and power with them; so did he profess himself some great one, the Syriac has it he said, *I am the greatest*: of his blasphemies see *Euseb. lib.* 2. *cap.* 13. *eccl. hist. Irenaeus. advers. haeres. lib.* 1. *cap.* 20. *Epiphanius. adv. haeres. lib.* 1. *haeres.* 21. *init.* it is reported that in *Claudius Caesar’s* time there was a statue set up in Rome, in his honor with this blasphemous inscription, *Simoni deo Sancto*, that is, to *Simon the holy god*, *Justin. Martyr. apol.* 2.

**V. 10.** *{to whom they all gave heed}* They marked and believed his words, as oracles; so easily are men persuaded to believe lies: whereas Luke so diligently sets down their great opinion of this impostor, he thereby shows the divine power of the Holy Ghost in Philip’s ministry, who was to break through such difficulties, that he might plant the gospel there.

**V. 11.** *{of long time he had, &c.}* Old diseases are more hardly cured; they having been so long time stupefied with lying miracles, might seem the most inept hearers of the gospel, but truth shall prevail, against all the power of Satan.

**V. 12.** *{but when they believed}* Faith ought to precede baptism, in men and women of years, when they who were aliens and strangers come to be baptized; for it is necessary, that they should confess their faith, and testify their conversion, before they be admitted by baptism.

*{if perhaps, &c.}* Not that God’s mercy to the penitent is fortuitous or uncertain (*if perhaps*) in Scripture is not always set to express doubting, sometimes it relates to a condition of the sinner’s pardon; as ἐὰν ἄρα, &c. Ezek. 3. 11. אם — ישמעו Hebr. *si audierint. Ar. Montan.* on which place says *Ambros. de paenit. lib.* 2. *c.* 5. *nesciebat ergo eos posse aut non posse converti? non ergo semper dubitantis ista est locutio*, but God does therefore propose his mercies and means salvation, under conditions, that man may know both his duty which God requires at his hands, and the free and bountiful reward thereof, which God will give to them that obey his voice; such are faith, repentance, conversion, obedience, &c. which are not causes, but conditions of our obtaining mercy, neither proposed dubiously on God’s part, who well knows (as that man cannot have them except he give them, nor shall have them in vain, so) the certain issue and effect of them in that man to whom he pleases to give them.

**V. 23.** *{in the gall of bitterness}* Meaning, in a bitter and desperate present condition. Gr. εἰς χολὴν πικρίας, here first, εἰς is put for, ἐν in: and χολὴ πικρίας, for χολὴ πικρὰ, in which words he shows the hypocrisy and ambition of Simon, as odious and displeasing to God, as bitterness is to the palate of man.

**V. 24.** *{pray ye to the Lord for me}* At least for fear of God’s judgments, he commends himself to the prayers of the church; whether he truly converted or not, the Scripture being silent, we cannot determine.

**V. 25.** *{When they had testified}* This imports their office and duty enjoined them by Christ, c. 1. 8. which they faithfully did, preaching the word, as they had received it from Christ; not only in Samaria, but in many villages adjoining, such was their unwearied and invincible industry.

**V. 26.** *{unto Gaza, &c.}* It was destroyed by Alexander the Great, and so remained waste: some say, another of the same name was long after built in another place.

**V. 27.** *{an Eunuch of great authority}* Or, *a great officer*, or *nobleman of the Queen’s court*. See Gen. 37. ult. & 40. 2. Esther 4. 5.

*{Candace Queen, &c.}* This name was not peculiar to one queen, but was given to all that reigned there, *Plin. nat. hist. l.* 6. *c.* 29. as Pharaoh to the kings of Egypt, or Caesar to the Roman emperors.

**V. 28.** *{read Isaiah the Prophet}* This shows that he had some knowledge of the true God, and that he came not to worship according to his own fancy; for he joined reading God’s word, with his external worship which was to direct him therein.

**V. 29.** *{Then the Spirit said unto Philip}* By revelation, or divine inspiration, the Holy Ghost thus moved him to do that whereof he would presently show an admirable effect in the conversion of the eunuch.

**V. 30.** *{Philip ran thither, &c.}* He readily obeyed God’s command, and found him to whom he was sent, studious of the way of God: therefore he asked him, whether he understood that which he read, with purpose (according to the guidance of God’s Spirit) to interpret it unto him.

**V. 31.** *{how can I except, &c.}* That is, except some who understands it interpret it unto me, here appeared a very religious comity in so great a person, so well taking Philip’s question, and meekly acknowledging his ignorance: therefore he invited Philip to come into his chariot and sit with him.

**V. 32.** *{he was led as a sheep to the slaughter}* Isaiah 53. 7. as a sheep in respect of his patience, and as a sacrifice for our sins: as a sheep for our redemption, ἀλλὰ ποιμαίνει τὸν Ἰσραὴλ. *Gr. Nazianzen orat.* 35. *yet is he the shepherd, to feed and keep his Israel*, Psa. 8. 1.

**V. 33.** *{in his humiliation}* Because he took on him the form of a servant, subjected himself to death, and state of condemnation, due to us; God highly exalted him, Phil. 2. 8, 9.

*{his judgment was taken away}* When he had suffered death, and fully satisfied the justice of God due to our sins, even in the lowest degree of his humiliation, then was he delivered safe from that judgment, which he subjected to for us.

*{who shall declare his generation}* τὴν δὲ γενεὰν αὐτοῦ, *his age*, that is the continuation of his life, for, as Rom. 6. 9. Christ being raised from the dead, dies no more: in the Hebr. Isa. 53. 8. דורו *doro* (70. γενεὰν, as here) דור *dor*, is *duratio, aetas, seculum*, &c. So Gen. 15. 16. Sometimes it being doubled, it signifies, many ages, or eternity, Isa. 13. 20. & 60. 15. Exod. 17. 16. Isa. 49. 11. &c. *his kingdom shall have no end*, Luke 1. 33. and who can declare, or reckon that which is eternal.

*{for his life is taken from the earth}* His death was the passage to his endless and eternal glory, however this seem improbable to carnal reason with the prophet there foretold, concerning Christ his exaltation into his eternal kingdom and glory from this ground to be attained — *for his life is taken from the earth*: yet the Holy Scripture testifies, that by the admirable counsel of God, it was so determined, that Christ through afflictions and death should enter into his glory, Luke 24. 26. and because he *became obedient unto death*, *even the death of the crosse*, *therefore God also hath highly exalted him, and hath given him a name, which is above every name*, &c. Phil. 2. 8, 9.

**V. 34.** *{Of whom speaketh the Prophet this?}* He inquires of the person concerning whom the prophet spoke, because that was a necessary degree toward the opening of this prophecy; when we doubt concerning the meaning of the Scripture, we should consult them who have knowledge and the gift of interpretation.

**V. 35.** *{Philip opened his mouth}* This was an usual manner among the Hebrews, to express a beginning to speak of some weighty matter.

**V. 36.** *{What doth hinder me to be baptized?}* It appears hence, that among other parts of Christian religion, Philip had preached to him concerning baptism, and that this great man had admirably profited in so short a time of hearing, as to desire to become a Christian.

**V. 37.** *{If thou believest, &c.}* With a sincere and perfect heart, without which baptism cannot save; he had here to do with a man of years, and yet an alien, and therefore might not admit him into the church of Christ until he had made profession of his faith. See on verse 12.

*{I believe that Christ is the Son of God}* As baptism and the effect thereof is grounded in Christ, so the eunuch desiring to be baptized, speaks only of Christ: he knew that there was one God, who from the beginning had promised a Messiah to save the world, now he acknowledges by that he heard from Philip, that Christ was he, prefigured under all the legal sacrifices and ceremonies: under this title he comprehends all that the Scripture says of Christ our Savior: the like confession of faith, See in Peter, Matthew 16. 16, and here we must know that naming Christ, he understood the sacrament of baptism to be administered in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost: and so, as in faithful prayer when we name one of the persons, in him we address our petitions to the whole blessed Trinity; so where we read of men baptized into Christ, we must know that it was performed in express terms naming all, as Christ appointed, Mat. 28. 19.

**V. 38.** *{They went both down into the water}* They were wont to dip the whole body, or go down into the water as here, and Matth. 3. 16. and sprinkling with water has also been of use in the church; the effect is all one: and indeed, baptism is sometimes expressed by washing, 2 Cor. 6. 11. Tit. 3. 5. and sometimes by sprinkling. Hebr. 10. 22. 1 Pet. 1. 2. which does well answer to the sprinkling of the blood of Jesus, as was prefigured in the type, the sprinkling of the blood of the Paschal Lamb. Exod. 12. 3. The virtue of the sacraments consists not in the quantity of elements, but in the power of God’s ordinance: as in the Lord’s Supper, it skills not, how much or little you eat or drink (with discreet moderation) but how worthily: to him that eats more (as to the gatherers of manna) there is not the more; nor the less grace to him that eats or drinks little; so in baptism. See on verse 39.

**V. 39.** *{That the Eunuch saw him no more}* He was suddenly taken away, and transported by the Spirit of God, as 1 King. 18. 12. Ezek. 3. 12, 14. This was done that the eunuch might know that Philip was sent to him by God. *Jerom de loc. Hebr.* says, this water was a brook at the foot of Bethsur, or Bethseron, where the eunuch was baptized.

**V. 40.** *{But Philip was found at Azotus}* That is, he was presently at Azotus, a town in Palestine: some think this city was also called Ashdod, one of the five cities of the Philistines; Joshua 15. 47. wherein was that idol Dagon. 1 Sam. 5. 3. *Jerom de loc. Hebr. Act.*

## CHAPTER 9

**Verse 1.** *{And Saul}* Chap. 22. 4, 5.
