Luke being to describe the admirable conversion of Saul, first shows what a one he was before his conversion; a bloody and violent enemy of the church; that none might despair of God’s saving grace and mercy, who can convert, and become new creatures, in faith and obedience to God.

*{breathing out threatenings}* This signifies a vehement heat of an enraged heart, which his very breath discovered in his words: being blooded in the death of Stephen, he pursues other Christians, with the like cruel rage: the Syriac reads here, *Saul was ever full of injuries, and cruel anger against the disciples of our Lord*: but we must understand (*against the disciples*) to be all that there professed Christianity, as verse 2. appears.

**V. 2.** *{and desired of him letters}* That his persecution might have the countenance of justice and authority he requires commission from the high priest: for the Romans having conquered all Judea, with all the adjacent countries, permitted the Jews to live according to their own religion (whence the high priests, as presidents of their great council, had power to examine and imprison any of theirs, who offended therein, though they had no power to determine in matters capital) therefore Saul desired their commission to convent the Christians.

*{to Damascus}* Damascus, the chief city of Syria, bearing north from Canaan; from Jerusalem six days’ journey.

*{any of this way}* That is, professed Christians, chap. 24. 14. All which aggravated his sin, in that he not only raged in Jerusalem, but pursued them into other cities, and in that he spared neither man nor woman, though they are commonly privileged, or pitied among armed enemies, by דרך *derech, way*, the Hebrews understood any *course of life, or profession*: Psalm 91. 11. 1 Kings 15. 26.

**V. 3.** *{suddenly there shined a light}* He willingly persecuted, who desired license so to do, but was converted against his intentions, and besides his will: God suffered him to gain letters, to approach near Damascus, to conceive hope, and to frame bloody thoughts for the execution of those cruel designs, and then suddenly he stayed his course, who ran so headlong to his own destruction: so true is that of *Fulgentius ad Monim. lib.* 1. *praevenit impium, ut fiat justus, subsequitur justum, ne fiat impius; praevenit elisum, ut surgat, subsequitur elevatum, ne cadat*; *he prevents the impious, that he may become holy, he follows the holy, that he may not become wicked; he prevents the broken that he may rise, he follows him that is raised, that he may not fall*; and that suddenly and without, and beyond their expectation. See chap. 22. 6.

**V. 4.** *{and he fell to the earth, &c.}* Cast down by the power and appearing majesty of Christ (See John 18. 6.) astonishing and terrifying him. Here the authority and letters of the high priests could nothing avail him, but he is enforced to yield to the all-subduing power of Christ, whose name and memory he so violently endeavored to put out of the tongues and hearts of all men.

*{why persecutest thou me?}* See on chap. 4. 26. This shows that those who persecute the servants and professors of Christ, in them persecute Christ himself, who lives in them. Galat. 2. 20. See Matth. 25. 40. Isaiah 63. 9. Zach. 2. 8. for he is the head of the church, Colos. 1. 18. the saints are his members. Eph. 5. 30. It should teach persecutors a cautious moderation (See c. 5. 35.) and us who suffer, patience; *rejoice in as much as ye are partakers of Christs sufferings*. 1 Pet. 4. 13. See verse 5. Christ in his own sacred person, and human nature, was now in heaven far above the reach of any creature, but he takes all that is done here on earth against his servants on his own account, therefore he says, not, why do you persecute my servants, who believe in me, but *why persecutest thou me?* Neither says he, Matth. 25. 35. the poor was hungry, and you gave him no meat, &c. *but I was an hungred, and ye gave me no meat*, neither says he, Mal. 3. 9. you have robbed the ministers of tithes and offerings, *but ye are cursed with a curse, for ye have robbed me*.

**V. 5.** *{and he said, who art thou Lord?}* These words proceed from an astonished mind, in suspense and doubt what to do: being dejected and humbled, he is now prepared to receive God’s commands, who before in his Pharisaical height of mind was upon a furious march against him.

*{Jesus whom thou persecutest}* See on verse 4. in that you persecute them that profess my name, who are one with me their head.

*{to kick against the pricks}* This was a proverbial speech, noting their contumacy and folly, who would obstinately hurt themselves in pursuit of others: it signifies here, a resisting God to a man’s own hurt, Jer. 7. 19. 1 Cor. 10. 22.

**V. 6.** *{what wilt thou have me do?}* He thought he had done right before (See John 16. 2. Phil. 3. 6.) but now finding the power of God resisting him, he submits, and desires to know the will of God, thus extraordinarily calling him from his intentions. See Psa. 25. 4.

*{arise and go into the city}* That is, into Damascus (v. 8.) now near, verse 3. God could easily have instructed him, but he would have him go into the city, that Ananias might instruct, and baptize him; first that he might teach Saul humility, the ground and foundation of all true religion, by sending and assigning him to their ministry, whom but now he not only proudly despised, but impiously resolved to afflict and destroy. Secondly God would also hereby teach all men to reverence and submit to his ordinance in the ministry of men thereto called and authorized by him.

*{it shall be told thee there what thou, &c.}* That is, there Ananias shall instruct you concerning my will: thus Christ who could as easily have told him immediately what he would have him do, would yet send him to Ananias (Ananias is ignorant of God’s will herein, as Saul; at present, and starting at, and declining the office enjoined him) to confirm the authority of man’s ministry; to teach all hearers to attend unto it, as the very ordinance and power of God to salvation, Rom. 4. 16. and to free the preaching of Paul from all suspicion of compact: he could likewise, *eadem opera* have instructed the devout centurion Cornelius (chap. 10.) by the angel, but he caused him to send for Peter to Joppa, to come to inform him for the same end.

**V. 7.** *{hearing a voice}* That is, Saul’s voice (as c. 22. 9. See Dan. 10. 7.) not the voice of Christ, except some confused sound, the meaning whereto they knew not.

*{but seeing no man}* No man speaking to Saul.

**V. 8.** *{he saw no man}* He was smitten blind by the brightness of that light which shined unto him: to teach him that he was spiritually blind in his opinion of wisdom, and rage against Christ and should now, in being obedient unto the heavenly vision (Act, 26, 19.) be illuminated with a better, even an heavenly light, showing the way to eternal life and salvation.

**V. 9.** *{and neither did eat nor drink}* So much was he amazed at that which happened to him; it may be then was his rapture which he speaks of, 2 Cor. 12. 2.

**V. 10.** *{a disciple at Damascus named Ananias}* Thus God would beat down Saul’s arrogance; sending him, not to any of the apostles for direction, but to an inferior Christian.

*{to him said the Lord in a vision, &c.}* This was necessary to prepare Ananias, lest otherwise he should have been hindered by fear of Saul, from the duty enjoined him for Saul’s conversion: though he knew that God called and commanded, yet he excused: so did Moses, Exod. 3. 11. &c. Exod. 4. 1, 10. &c. therefore God confirms him.

**V. 11.** *{called Saul of Tarsus}* This was a famous city of Cilicia near Antioch, chap. 21. 39. now called Hama.

*{for behold he prayeth}* It appears that God revealed to Ananias, that Saul prayed, and how he would enlighten him body and soul, and therefore commanded him to repair to him for the accomplishment thereof.

**V. 12.** *{and hath seen}* Or, *he saw*; it is doubtful whether these words be a continuation of God’s speech to Ananias, or Luke’s concerning Saul’s vision; some think the former most probable.

**V. 13.** *{Lord I have heard by many of this man, &c.}* Here was Ananias’ infirmity: flesh and blood objected many lets, and fears to divert men from, or to hinder them in the service of God; but the Spirit of God prevails in them against all.

**V. 14.** *{and here he hath authority, &c.}* His name was spread far and near for a bloody persecutor, the more famous was his conversion: and it appears that the saints at Damascus had knowledge of his coming now, and commission against them.

**V. 15.** *{a chosen vessel unto me}* σκεῦος ἐκλογῆς, *a vessel of election*; it is an Hebraism, importing a choice, or elect vessel; σκεῦος has a more general signification, and is put (as כלי *cheli* with the Hebrews) for a vessel, receptacle, or any instrument or utensil: here it signifies one whom God had chosen, to bear the divine treasure of the gospel, to the Gentiles, 2 Cor. 4. 7.

**V. 16.** *{I will shew him how great things he must suffer}* He must suffer many things for my name’s sake, whereof I will before inform him, to frame him to invincible patience; as if he had said, you shall not need to fear him, as heretofore a persecutor, for henceforth he must himself *suffer great things for my names sake*; for his professing and preaching the truth of the gospel.

**V. 17.** *{putting his hands upon him, &c.}* Ananias shows his obedience in going, and God his purpose to confirm the authority of the external ministry, in directing it so, that Saul should receive his sight by the imposition of Ananias’ hands, who could have restored him to a double sight, by his own immediate power; concerning imposition of hands, see chap. 6. 6.

*{brother Saul, the Lord, &c.}* He being assured by Christ of his conversion and adoption in Christ, salutes him by the name of brother: also he shows his authority and mission from Christ, which that Saul might be assured of, he names a private token, Christ appearing to him by the way as he came: and the end of his commission, that he might receive his sight, and be endued with such admirable gifts of the Holy Ghost, as might further enable him to his charge, and confirm him in the faith and truth of Jesus.

**V. 18.** *{fell from his eyes}* These scales falling off, show that his blindness was not ordinary, but such as might mind him of his former spiritual blindness: for though, being brought up at Gamaliel’s feet, he had much profited in that kind of learning, yet himself, after his illumination, accounted that knowledge no better than blindness, Phil. 3. 7, 8.

*{and was baptized}* It was necessary for him by this solemn sign and seal of God’s covenant of grace, to give his name to Christ, to testify his conversion, and to confirm his faith in Christ, and him in the profession of the gospel.

**V. 19.** *{when he had received meat, &c.}* A prudent and moderate care of health must be had, whereby we may be enabled to our calling. 1 Tim. 5. 23. Superstitious abstinence is will-worship, Colos. 2. 18. and doctrine of devils. 1 Tim. 4. 4. 1 Tim. 5. 23.

*{certain days with the disciples}* He joined no more with Christ’s professed enemies, but with the saints: it skills much what company we keep. See 1 Cor. 5. 9, 11. Prov. 1. 15. & 2. 12. & 5. 8.

**V. 20.** *{and straightway he preached Christ, &c.}* Declaring him to be the Son of God, and Savior of the elect, which doubtless he copiously delivered. (See verse 22.) by that largeness of heart which the Holy Ghost gave him: beating down that double error of the Jews, who thought, 1. that this Jesus the son of Mary, was not Christ, 2. that the Messiah, was to be a mere man, a victorious and glorious secular prince.

**V. 21.** *{were amazed}* Because in his sudden conversion they perceived the hand of God: this made his preaching more powerful and effectual in them.

*{is not this he that destroyed, &c.}* Remembering his former actions, they admire that such a persecutor would now preach the same gospel which he so lately, and violently opposed: and they glorified God for him, see Gal. 1. 23, 24.

**V. 22.** *{proving that he is very Christ}* By comparing or matching places of Scripture, see c. 17. 11. & 18. 28. So the word συμβιβάζειν signifies, says Budaeus on this place, *ex collatione sacrorum vaticiniorum docens & probans*, *teaching and proving by comparing sacred prophecies*.

**V. 23.** *{after that many days were fulfilled}* That is, three years, God would not presently expose him to conflicts with, and assaults of, adversaries, but for a convenient time restrain the fury of persecutors: for it appears that after his conversion, he went into *Arabia and returned again unto Damascus*, and three years after he went up to Jerusalem, — Gal. 1. 17, 18.

**V. 24.** *{but their laying of wait was known of Saul}* 2 Cor. 11. 32. This was after his return from Arabia, that the Jews at Damascus plotted to destroy him: but God discovering their designs, he foreknowing the danger, escaped from them.

**V. 25.** *{the Disciples took him by night}* That is the faithful Christians, thus provided for his safety.

**V. 26.** *{but they were all afraid of him}* Between his conversion and coming again to Jerusalem, were three years expired; Gal. 1. 17, 18. wherein he preached in some parts of Arabia, and thence came back again to Damascus: in this time was the conspiracy of the unbelieving Jews against him: he escaping, and coming to Jerusalem, and desiring to unite in external communion with the disciples, as now he did in the faith of Christ, they remembering his former rages against them, suspected his pretenses which was a great trial to him.

**V. 27.** *{But Barnabas took him, &c.}* Of him see chap. 4. He being thereto incited by the Holy Ghost, presented Saul to Peter and James, acquitting him from the jealousy they had of him: which he did by three arguments. 1. Because he had seen the Lord Jesus in the way, when he was going to Damascus to persecute. 2. Because Christ had spoken to him. 3. Because he had boldly, and openly preached the gospel at Damascus.

**V. 28.** *{was with them coming in, and going out}* Conversant with them, that is, Peter and James, Gal. 1. 18, 19.

**V. 29.** *{he spake boldly}* He openly professed the gospel of Christ.

*{the Grecians}* Acts 6. 1.

**V. 30.** *{forth to Tarsus}* See on verse 11. that he might preach the gospel to his own countrymen.

**V. 31.** *{then had the Churches rest}* When Saul (against whom the adversaries were implacably enraged) was gone out of their sight.

*{were edified}* In that the church still increased in the number of the faithful, and they in knowledge, faith, love, and holiness: this word οἰκοδομούμεναι, *edified* is a metaphorical expression taken from building, spiritually the church the house of God, 1 Tim. 3. 15. (as every faithful servant of Christ, is a temple of the Holy Ghost, 1 Cor. 3. 16. 1 Cor. 6. 19.) and we are all living materials, 1 Peter 2. 5. prepared and fitted here by the word preached, which is compared to *an hammer that breaks the rock*, Jerem. 23. 29. so as that there shall be in the finishing and setting all up in heaven, no noise thereof; as in the building of Solomon’s temple, 1 Kings 6. 7. *there was neither hammer nor ax heard*.

**V. 32.** *{all quarters}* Where the saints were, whom he was to visit, to confirm them in the faith.

*{at Lydda}* This town in Jerom’s time was called Diospolis: it was a famous place, not far from the Mediterranean Sea: near to this was Joppa, now named Japha. (Syr. לוד *lud*, and Hebr. לד *lod*,) 1 Chron. 8. 12. in the tribe of Benjamin. See Ezra 2. 33. Nehem. 7. 37.

**V. 33.** *{a certain man named Æneas}* Mentioning the disease, and the antiquity thereof before the cure, he sets out the excellence of the miracle, and certainty of the history.

**V. 34.** *{Jesus Christ maketh thee whole, arise, &c.}* This he said, being assured of the will of God herein, showing that himself was but a servant unto the power of Christ, who wrought the cure. Here appeared the excellent faith of Peter, assured of the truth of Christ’s promises: see the marvelous difference of a man left but a little to his own strength (as he was when he denied Christ, Mat. 26. 70. &c.) and the same confirmed by the power of the Holy Ghost, as Peter now was, when he confidently told Aeneas, *Jesus Christ maketh thee whole*; and in full assurance of a cure beyond all art of man, or power of the creature, bids him, arise, and make his bed.

*{make thy bed}* As they who have slept, rising cast up their bed: this shows that he was perfectly cured and enabled so to do.

*{he arose immediately}* Which shows that it was done by a divine power: art works in time and by degrees, but God when he pleases in an instant.

**V. 35.** *{all that dwelt}* As many as saw it; very many.

*{and Saron}* Σάρωνα, Hebr. שרון *Sharon*, Isa. 33. 9. 1 Chron. 27. 29. 70. Σαρων, a champaign country, or tract of fruitful land between Caesarea of Palestine, Mount Tabor, and the lake of Genesareth, running along beyond Joppa: this place it seems was near Lydda. See *Jerom de loc. Act.* and *Joh. Drus. ad Voc. N. T. com.* 1. *Sarona.*

**V. 36.** *{was at Joppa}* A city by the seaside. See on v. 33. and c. 10. 5.

**V. 37.** *{whom when they had washed}* They used to wash their dead, and to anoint them before they put them into the linen in which they buried them.

**V. 38.** *{they sent unto him two men, &c.}* Here they showed their faith and charity, desiring that the pious woman might recover, and be longer profitable unto the church.

**V. 39.** *{Peter arose and went}* Both they who sent for him, and Peter going were directed so to do, by the Holy Spirit: and providence of God, for the advancement of his glory, and the confirmation of the truth of the gospel, else might it have seemed a strange request of the one, or undertaking of the other, to raise the dead.

*{shewing the coats}* Which she made to give to the poor.

**V. 40.** *{put them all forth}* Probably he either removed all these, who with importunate mourning might distract him praying: or, all, here, as verse 35. and many other places, goes for *many*. Syr. *he put out all the men.*

*{opened her eyes}* Her life being restored at that word, *Tabitha arise*, she looked and sat up.

**V. 41.** *{presented her alive}* Syr. *he gave her to them alive*: that is, restored again to life, and doubtless, to good health and strength, for in all the admirable cures done by Christ or any of the apostles or disciples, in his name we never read that the disaffected person was less than perfectly recovered.

**V. 42.** *{many believed}* This was the end and fruit of the miracle: to bring them by faith to Christ and eternal life.

**V. 43.** *{he tarried many days}* Until Cornelius by God’s appointment, sent for him hence, c. 10. 6, 24, &c. it is likely that he stayed here to instruct them further in the truth of Christ, and to confirm them in the faith.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1.** *{Cornelius}* An Italian captain of a garrison; soldiers of the Romans at Caesarea.

**V. 2.** *{a devout man}* It seems he was a proselyte in heart, though not of the circumcision and open profession.

*{with all his house}* This was a good sign of his devotion, that his servants also feared God: and a good example for masters of families thus to order them: it was holy Abraham’s praise, Gen. 18. 19.

*{to God always}* His prayers are commended, 1. from the object, he prayed to God; not as the superstitious, to creatures: 2. from their perseverance, they were not by certain fits, in some fear or danger only (then even Jonah’s mariners will be devout, Jon. 1. 5.) but always without ceasing, though not without intermission.

**V. 3.** *{He saw in a vision}* Not in a trance or rapt of spirit, but in a vision, and that not obscurely, but sensibly and evidently: it is called in the Gr. ὅραμα, *visum*; that which is evidently and certainly seen, as this appearance of the angel was; so the Syriac gives the Greek φανερῶς, by בגליא by which they meant, *aperte, palam, manifeste*, so it seems was this, for it was not only about the ninth hour of the day, three hours before night, about which time they used to offer the evening sacrifice, (Exod. 29. 41.) with prayers, but the angel of God appeared to him, not in a dumb show, but bringing a divine oracle, which he clearly spoke and delivered to him in an audible voice and words which he understood. See verse 30.

*{the ninth hour}* Which was one of the Jews’ set hours of prayer, Chap. 3. 1. See Exod. 29. 41.

**V. 4.** *{thy prayers and thine alms, &c.}* It is a manner of expression borrowed from their burnt offerings, which they called הולות *holoth, ascensiones*, holocausts, burnt offerings, Deut. 12. 6. &c. or *ascensions*: (it is of the verb הלה *halah, ascendit*, &c.) or incense, whereof David says, Psa. 141. 2. *let my prayer be set forth before thee, as incense*: and Rev. 8. 4. *the smoke of the incense which came with the prayers of the saints, ascended up before the Lord out of the angels hand*. So came up Cornelius’ alms with his prayers for a memorial before God; *with such sacrifices God is well pleased*, Heb. 13. 18. Such contributions to the saints the apostle calls, *an odour of a sweet smell a sacrifice acceptable, well pleasing to God*, Phil. 4. 1. Without charity God accepts no sacrifice of prayers, Mat. 5. 23, 24. Prov. 21. 13.

**V. 5.** *{now send men}* The angel seems to resign the office of teaching to Peter, whom God had assigned thereto: so did Christ to Ananias, chap. 9. 10. God will be heard in those men whom he appoints to preach, Luke 10. 16. and it is very dangerous to contemn his ordinance.

*{to Joppa, and call}* See on chap. 9. 36. It was a port town, where Jonah took shipping, when he would flee from God, Jon. 1. 3. Here dwelt Simon the tanner where Peter lodged, verse 6. Hebrew יפו *japho*, Greek, Ἰόππη: a very ancient town of Palestine, so named for its beauty, of יפה *japhah, pulcher, formosus*, as some think: others say of יפת *japheth*, in Jerom’s time it was called Joppa, *ib. in loc. Actor.* now Jaffa, G. Mercat. Atl. min. *in terra sanct.*

**V. 6.** *{he lodgeth with one Simon, &c.}* These particular instructions not only informed him where to find Peter, but also confirmed him in his faith and obedience to God’s command.

**V. 7.** *{a devout soldier}* See on verse 2. Though it were in carnal reason full of danger to change religion, especially in compliance with those who were commonly accused of attempting new matters in the government of Palestine and Syria, yet Cornelius’ faith in God overcomes these difficulties, and presently sent according to God’s command: in matters of religion we must rely on God’s commandments, wherein delays are dangerous, and not on carnal reason. The soldier he entrusted, was formed according to the happy example of his master, verse 2. and in his devotion not only expresses his master’s religious care to teach his family by precept and example (that most powerful rhetoric) but also his obsequious readiness to learn good: in every calling, men may serve the Lord: these shall rise in judgment to condemn wicked soldiers.

**V. 8.** *{declared all these things unto them}* Which he did, as to furnish them for their treaty with Peter, so to encourage them in this service, wherein the cause of God, and his and their salvations were interested: his choice of such men of trust is imitable.

**V. 9.** *{about the sixth hour}* About noon, which was another hour for prayer, Psal. 55. 17. He went up to the top of the house, which was flat roof, that being a place, as most remote of all the house, from disturbance by any domestic business, so, most fit and accommodate for prayer, which ought to be free from all distractions.

**V. 10.** *{he fell into a trance}* A supernatural elevation of the mind, by the Spirit of God, filling all the sense and faculty of the soul with a divine revelation.

**V. 11.** *{and he saw heaven opened}* This was the vision which he saw, we may not think this vision, real or sensible according to the outward man: his ecstasy gave him other eyes, that is, spiritual.

**V. 12.** *{were all manner of fourfooted beasts}* Wherein seemed to him to be all manner of beasts, and creatures, as well those which by their law were to be accounted unclean, as others.

**V. 13.** *{and eat}* That is, indifferently without any respect to the distinction of clean and unclean: and therefore God affected him with so sharp, and sudden an hunger, verse 10. because he meant to present him a vision suitable thereto.

**V. 14.** *{common or unclean}* That is, which the prescript of the law made such. See Mark 7. 2. See Deut. 14. 4. &c. Peter did not yet well understand how this rapt and vision agreed with that law of God; therefore lest he should have done anything rashly or with a relucting conscience, he expresses his scruple, most probably, that he might be better informed and satisfied therein, lest he should do that which by the law would render him profane or unclean.

**V. 15.** *{what God hath cleansed}* By taking away the difference; which he had put for the time of the ceremonial law only, by this he was taught, not to abhor the Gentiles, seeing God had taken away the difference, Ephes. 2. 14.

*{call thou not common}* Or *unclean*, μὴ κοίνου, (See on Matth. 15. 11.) Syriac *ne contamines*: do you not say, or make as unclean, that is in your esteem or value of them: so the Hebrews used to speak: as Proverbs 17. 15. מצדיק רשע *matsdik rashah*, *justifying the wicked*: that is, accounting the wicked just; so the 70. ὃς δίκαιον κρίνει τὸν ἄδικον, &c.

**V. 16.** *{Received up again}* That is, seemed in this vision to be taken up again.

**V. 17.** *{While Peter doubted, &c.}* διηπόρει. Syr. מתדמר *methdamar*, *wondered*: but διαπορεῖν signifies (as has been noted) such a suspension of the mind, as perplexed and distracted it so, that one knows not what to do.

*{what this which he had seen, should mean}* τί ἂν εἴη. So the Syriac, *what it should be*: that is, what it should signify, what the Lord would hereby instruct him to do.

*{behold the men}* The servants and the soldier which Cornelius sent, verse 7, 8.

**V. 18.** *{and called}* As strangers in civility use to do: the rather, because they being Gentiles (with whom the Jews conversed not) would not by any importunity give offense: a good pattern for all godly soldiers.

**V. 19.** *{while Peter thought on the vision}* While his thoughts were troubled, and he seriously meditated on that which God had showed him and said unto him.

*{the Spirit said unto him}* By an inward revelation, the Holy Ghost further, and more evidently instructed him what the meaning of the vision was: God will never leave them without sufficient instruction who faithfully meditate on that he reveals to them.

**V. 20.** *{doubting nothing}* μηδὲν διακρινόμενος. διακρίνεσθαι, is in matter of judgment to waver, as now inclining to one side; and then to the another: Budaeus interprets this place, *without any dispute*, *not doubting*, *citra haesitantiam; qui enim haesitant; secum ipsi disputant sententiis alternantibus*: *without hesitation or sticking: for they that doubt, or stick at a thing, dispute with themselves with variable conclusions*; this shows how our minds ought to be resolved toward God’s word, we must not waver. See Rom. 4. 20. & 14. 23. There can be no peace or rest of consciences, but on God’s word, and resolute adhering to the same.

*{I have sent them}* I moved him that sent them: so that here is nothing fortuitous, but all directed by God’s Spirit, and holy providence.

**V. 21.** *{What is the cause, &c.}* What business have you with me? He knew not yet whither all this tended, and therefore inquired more diligently.

**V. 22.** *{a just man, &c.}* He being a Gentile, and a soldier, they give Peter this character, in three excellent attributes. 1. that he was a just man. 2. that he feared God. 3. that he lived in the good opinion and report of all the Jews that knew him; and this, not (as officious sycophants use) to flatter him, but to do him right, and specially to free Peter from such jealousy as might otherwise, make him fearful to come to him: in all, here is a good example for servants, ingenuously to give their masters their due honor, and faithfully to obey them.

*{was warned from God by an holy Angel}* See Matt. 2. 12. This was their main argument to persuade Peter to go, because God called him; and had an hand in all this, and that it was no motion of man, who may ofttimes propose things inconvenient and evil (as Balac did when he sent for Balaam to curse the Israel of God) but this was of God who cannot err or command any evil.

**V. 23.** *{and lodged them}* Courteously entertained them as guests and strangers, ξενίζειν signifies also ξενίας χάριν τινὰ ὑποδέξασθαι. Suidas: *to receive one for hospitality sake*, or *to hospitality*: a good example to all good men. See Heb. 13. 2. 1 Tim. 5. 10. Gen. 18. 2, 3. Gen. 19. 1, 2, 3. Exod. 2. 20. Judg. 19. 20. specially for prelates, 1 Tim. 3. 1.

*{On the morrow, &c.}* Here was Peter’s ready obedience to God, he would not beyond necessity delay to execute that which he understood God would have him do: so holy Abraham, having received that grievous precept to sacrifice his only dear son Isaac, *rose up early in the morning to take his journey* according to God’s command, Gen. 22. 3. Delayed duty (besides the danger of prevention by some unexpected accident) loses the value which a cheerful expedition might have given it: God loves a cheerful giver, and a quick servant.

*{and certain brethren}* So the unanimous Christians esteemed each other. See c. 9. 17. These out of thankfulness and honor to Peter, would accompany him on the way, but God’s providence so disposed of it, that they also might be witnesses of his grace and mercy to the Gentiles.

**V. 24.** *{his kinsmen, &c.}* To the end that they also might be partakers of the grace and mercy of God with him.

**V. 25.** *{worshipped, &c.}* Not as God, with divine worship; yet with too much humility, which Peter corrects: see Revel. 12. 19. and 22. 8. προσεκύνησε here translated (*worshipped*) signifies also *saluted reverently*. So the Hebrews use their verb שחה *shacha*, to fall down, or bow down to adoration divine, or civil, Gen. 23. 7. *Abraham arose* וישתחו *vaijstachu*, *and bowed himself to the people of the land*, 70. προσεκύνησε τῷ λαῷ—the word here used: so that προσκυνεῖν, importing both to *adore*, or *worship* with divine worship; and to salute with civil worship, such as man owes to man respectively: we cannot reasonably think, that Abraham adored the people as God, or Cornelius Peter; yet it appears that Cornelius being stricken with reverence at the presence of so great a servant of God exceeded the bounds of mere civil respect, as appears by Peter’s words, verse 26.

**V. 26.** *{I myself am also a man}* This shows that Cornelius falling down, too much inclined to the *impropriation* of God’s honor, and giving it to a creature, which Peter prohibits by this reason, *I my self am a man*: he need say no more: man must adore his creator, but not be adored by his fellow creature: here is no kissing of Peter’s feet.

**V. 27.** *{as he talked with him}* As Peter went on talking with Cornelius into his house, probably they declared either to other, the work and providence of God in their meeting, Peter excusing himself thereby, for his coming to the Gentiles; and Cornelius, for his sending for him.

**V. 28.** *{A man that is a Jew}* John 4. 9. & 18. 28. The law of God forbade them to marry with heathens, to have familiar conversation with them, &c. but their tradition barred them from eating with them, or coming into their houses, &c. chap. 11. 3. So that where Peter says it is *unlawful*, beyond that which the law of God prohibited, you must understand Peter to speak ἀπὸ κοινοῦ, after the common tradition, or sense of the Jews.

*{one of another nation}* Being a Gentile and not a Jew.

*{common or unclean}* Meaning in respect of the diversity of nations, for now he hereby understood, that God would call the Gentiles to faith and salvation by Christ.

**V. 29.** *{I ask therefore, for what intent, &c.}* It is probable that he said this for the company’s sake assembled there, that they also might be acquainted with the whole passage, and so be better satisfied, and more attentive unto that which they should hear; for the servants by the way, and Cornelius in that discourse which they might have going into the house, probably had given Peter some light concerning the business.

**V. 30.** *{Until this hour}* Until this time of day. See verse 3. until about three of the clock in the afternoon.

*{a man stood before me}* The angel in man’s shape, verse 3.

**V. 31.** *{thy prayer is heard}* See verse 4.

**V. 32.** *{send therefore}* See verse 5.

**V. 33.** *{immediately therefore I sent unto thee}* This probably he said to assure Peter, that he would be a careful and attentive hearer of the mysteries of faith, which is a great encouragement unto ministers, to a cheerful execution of their ministry, whereas on the contrary a drowsy or inattentive auditory, not only disheartens the minister, but intercepts the blessing of God thereby to have come upon them, and hinders the power of the word preached.

*{thou hast well done}* This is a kind of thankful acknowledgment of Peter’s care and pains taken in coming to him.

*{We are all here present before God}* Thus he declares for himself, his friends and family, their attention, and future obedience to his doctrine: O that this thought were in all that hear the word of God, *we are all here present before God*.

**V. 34.** *{Of a truth}* Certainly. See Deut. 10. 17. Rom. 2. 11. 1 Peter 1. 17. as if he said, hitherto I have been carried along with the common stream of the Jewish error, thinking that God’s grace and mercy were entailed upon the Jews, and that they only were heirs of all his promises in Christ, and the only nation of the world which he would accept, and own for his people; but now I know for a truth, that God is no accepter of persons.

*{no respecter of persons}* Or, *has no regard in his acceptance, to the external condition of the person*, as, of what nation, family, name, or quality he be of, he speaks here of those following degrees of grace, faith and obedience, which wherever they are found, ’tis evident they are acceptable to him.

**V. 35.** *{he that feareth him}* He that is truly religious toward God, and just toward man: by the fear of God the Hebrews understood, the whole worship of God: Peter, intimating that Cornelius feared God in that he believed in him that came into the world to save sinners, shows that the sacraments are seals of adoption, in children, and of faith, of those who are of years, as Rom. 4. 11. So that they are deceived, who so tie the Holy Spirit, and the effects thereof, to the receiving the sacrament of baptism, as if none could fear God, or be accepted of him, and saved by him, who is not baptized: for first, none before Christ received this seal, as since we do: and next, it is the contempt, not the privation, which God herein condemns. (See v. 44. and John 3. 5.) Here it appears, that Cornelius was accepted of God, before he had received the external seal of his covenant.

**V. 36.** *{preaching peace}* This is, reconciliation between God and man, through Christ, Isa. 57. 19. Ephes. 2. 14, 16, 17. Col. 1. 20.

*{Lord of all}* Not only the God of the Jews, but of the Gentiles also; and he will gather his elect out of all the world, Rom. 3. 30. & 10. 12.

**V. 37.** *{After the baptism}* That is, after John, the forerunner of Christ, had preached, and baptized in his name.

**V. 38.** *{God anointed Jesus}* That is, endued his human nature, with the graces of the Holy Ghost, and consecrated, or dedicated his whole person to the office of a mediator, both which were prefigured, by the ancient rite of anointing.

*{for God was with him}* In the fullness of the deity, as he was the eternal Son, Col. 2. 9. and in power, grace and favor, as he was *man, and Mediator*. John 8. 29. & 16. 32.

**V. 39.** *{and we are witnesses}* We the apostles who attended on him, chosen for this end, that we might testify what doctrine he preached, and what miracles he did; as verse 41, 42.

*{Whom they slew, &c.}* Thus speaking of the death of Christ, he mentions the ingratitude and inhumanity of his countrymen the Jews toward Christ: and that we might consider what he suffered to deliver us from the curse of the law. See Gal. 3. 13. Deut. 21. 23. Here see how necessary, a good end proposed, and a good intention is to every action: for albeit, as Lactantius says well, *lib. 3. cap. 15. nihil interest quo animo facias, quod fecisse vitiosum est*: *it skills not with what intention you do that which is wicked to be done*: yet doing anything with an evil intention, though the thing done were so far decreed of God, as that he will draw good out of that evil (as it was in Christ’s death for our redemption) yet is that same action a sin in the agent, or agents, and God’s goodness (so much above all evil, so invincible, that he draws a good end out of the wicked actions of men) does no ways justify the sins of men, because he never decrees sin. See Gen. 50. 20. 1 King. 12. 8. 15. 19. 1 Sam. 10. 26. 27.

**V. 40.** *{him God raised}* Lest the Gentiles should think, therefore Christ, whom these men preach, is destroyed, he presently infers, that (which declares him to be the Son of God, Romans 1. 4. and conqueror of death and hell) he was raised up from the dead.

**V. 41.** *{Not to all the people}* God showed Christ alive from the dead, but not to all the Jews, but to all those who were appointed witnesses of the same; and those not a few: he was seen alive after his resurrection from the dead, to above five hundred brethren at once, 1 Cor. 15. 6. He appeared not to the enemies, because his state of humiliation and suffering by them, was finished; and it concerned not them (whose impenitency and unbelief he knew and foresaw) otherwise than that they shall see him coming again in the clouds to judge them Rev. 1. 7.

**V. 42.** *{commanded us to preach}* See chap. 1. 8. Luke 24. 47, 48. Mark 16. 15. Mat. 28. 19. Because the power of Christ’s resurrection and the glory of his kingdom, which is now, by men’s spiritual blindness and unbelief, much clouded, which shall in his coming to judge the living and the dead, clearly and terribly shine out to all the nations of the earth, therefore Peter annexes this declaration unto the foregoing discourse, as that which Christ principally gave them in charge to preach, and which is of a very high concernment for all auditors of God’s word to attend, and diligently mark and observe.

*{of quick and dead}* Of those who being found alive at his coming, shall be changed; and of those that shall be raised from the dead, to come to judgment, 1 Thes. 4. 15. Rom. 14. 9. 2 Tim. 4. 1. 1 Pet. 4. 5.

**V. 43.** *{to him give all, &c.}* Jer. 31. 34. Mich. 7. 18. Luke 24. 27. as if he had said, this doctrine which we according to the commandment of Christ, preach to you and must preach to all nations, is no new doctrine, but such as all the holy prophets attested concerning Christ, even free remission of sins and salvation, by him who died for us wretched sinners, and rose again for our justification, who believe in him, Rom. 4. 24, 25. It is more than probable, that Peter cited many places of the prophets, and opened the same unto these hearers, thereby evidently proving those things by him alleged, which for brevity’s sake are not here recorded, it being sufficient that the sums of these things whereof Peter discoursed, are here set down.

*{through his name}* By his merit.

**V. 44.** *{while he yet spake}* The Holy Ghost accompanied the sincere preaching, and attentive hearing the word, and hereby an evident miracle confirmed that which Peter had preached unto them, that God’s mercy in Christ belongs as well to the *Gentiles* as to the *Jews*.

*{the Holy Ghost}* That is, the graces of the Holy Spirit, in illumination, regeneration, and the gifts of tongues, &c. By this miracle God declared, that the merit of Christ’s passion belongs as well to the Gentiles as to the Jews; and that (the Spirit, thus coming on these before baptism) the righteousness of God is not attained by anything external, without the inward working of his Spirit, which here came on Cornelius, being neither circumcised nor baptized. See on verse 35.

**V. 45.** *{Because that on the Gentiles}* They did not before this understand, that the grace of God appertained unto them, which had been so long (like the dew in Gideon’s fleece) distilled only on the Jews, Psal. 147. 19, 20. This made those Jews which came with Peter, amazed, that such an admirable measure of God’s Spirit was also poured out upon the Gentiles, as on the day of *Pentecost* on the *apostles at Jerusalem*.

**V. 46.** *{for they heard him speak}* It was the vulgar error of the Jews, that the covenant of grace, and the Messias, belonged only to themselves, but now they perceive that the Gentiles also had interest therein, hearing them speak unstudied languages, they knew that they had received the Holy Ghost.

*{then answered Peter}* This is an usual manner of speaking with the *Hebrews, answered*, for, then said, &c. so the Syriac ואמר *vemer*, *and he said*: Peter seeing what was done, better understood what those gifts of the Holy Ghost meant, to wit that the believing Gentiles should also be partakers of salvation by Christ.

**V. 47.** *{Can any forbid water}* He reasons from the thing signified, unto the sign, and this question is in effect a vehement assertion, as if he had said, we ought not to withhold the sign; it were a sin to deny it: his reason may thus, more at large, be formed. Those that are in Christ by the inward effect of the Holy Ghost, ought to be implanted into the church by baptism; but these Gentiles are in Christ by the inward effect of the Holy Ghost, therefore they ought to be implanted into the church by baptism: the same reason will conclude for baptizing infants, whom the Holy Ghost has made, and pronounced holy, if born of believing parents, 1 Cor. 7. 14. The meaning of these words is; seeing God has given them the inward grace and spiritual part, should we, who are but ministers, deny to communicate to them the external sign and seal of incorporation into Christ’s body the church?

**V. 48.** *{To be baptized in the name of the Lord}* For baptism is the sign whereby believers are openly declared to be the people of God: that which he says, *in the Name of the Lord*, we must understand as relating to the use, not the form of baptism: they were baptized in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Ghost, as Christ commanded, Matth. 28. 19. but here, baptizing in the name of the Lord, imports, consecration to the Lord Christ, and implantation into the church, whereof he is the head. So chap. 2. 38. chap. 8. 16. and chap. 19. 5.

*{to tarry certain days}* Cornelius and the church in his house prayed him to tarry; both that they might be further instructed, confirmed, and comforted by his ministry, and also to express their thankfulness to him for so great a blessing as God had sent them by his ministry: the best have ever need to learn, and to be confirmed: and by our desire to know more, we may, not obscurely, gather how we have profited: no man has ever tasted of true grace offered in the gospel, but he desires more.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1.** *{That the Gentiles also had received}* Which was yet a wonder to them: a mystery hid from the beginning of the world, Ephes. 3. 10, 11. which caused the holy angels to stand at gaze. See 1 Pet. 1. 12. The Jews held the Gentiles as wicked, incorrigible, desperate sinners, and mere aliens from God. See Ephes. 2. 12. But now that which God here did, was so public (by reason of those Jews which were of Caesarea with Peter; relating the miracle to the apostles and brethren at Jerusalem) that it might sufficiently teach them the contrary.

**V. 2.** *{that were of the circumcision}* So were also all the believing Jews, who did not quarrel with him, neither are here mentioned: but this points at them only, who were too much addicted to legal ceremonies, inso much that they thought circumcision simply necessary to salvation. See chap. 15. 1, 5. and would have the believing Gentiles also circumcised.

**V. 3.** *{thou wentest in to men uncircumcised}* Chap. 10. 28. Thus they accuse Peter, as if it had been some grievous sin: whereas the law of God forbade not all commerce with Gentiles, but that only which might corrupt them; not that which might gain others to God and salvation by them.

**V. 4.** *{Peter rehearsed the matter in order, &c.}* Peter willingly submitted to the judgment of the church, to give them account of that he had done. (See 1 Cor. 14. 29.) By this means he both taught us what every one of us ought to do, and also better instructed them who erred. Peter pretended not to be above the church.

**V. 5.** *{I was in the city Joppa praying}* This whole narration. See chap. 10. 9. &c.

**V. 15.** *{as on us, &c.}* Chap. 2. 4. In the like power, though not in the same signs of fiery tongues, nor with the same degrees of fullness.

**V. 16.** *{John indeed baptized with water}* John 1. 26.

**V. 17.** *{What was I that I should withstand}* This was Peter’s conclusion when he had rehearsed the matter: the meaning is; seeing that God has made the Gentiles equal in grace, with the Jews converted to Christ, it was not in me to deny them an equal share and communion in the external seals and signs thereof: he that shall exclude those who have received the graces of God’s sanctifying Spirit, by barring them from the external communion of the church; in the rule (by Peter’s conclusion) are resisters of God.

**V. 18.** *{They held their peace}* They rested satisfied; which is indeed the property of good men, who contend not so much for victory, as for truth; whereas the perverse will be satisfied with no reason.

**V. 19.** *{They which were scattered}* Chap. 8. 1. He further prosecutes here, that which was only hinted, chap. 8. 4. concerning the propagation of the gospel, by the ministry of those who were dispersed by reason of the cruel persecution: it ought to be a ground of comfort unto us who have long feared a sad darkness coming on the Christian world, and a dangerous eclipse of the gospel: yea but the Spirit says, Rev. 2. 10. *fear none of these things, &c.* The more Satan labors to oppress the elect, and obscure the truth, the more he marks them for saints whom the impious world hates, and that for truth which Satan stirs up his servants to oppose: stars never shine so clear as in the uncouth shade of night.

*{Phenice}* A region of Syria,

*{Cyprus}* An isle in the Carpathean Sea, between Cilicia, and Syria.

*{Antioch}* A famous city in Syria, near Cilicia.

**V. 20.** *{Cyrene}* There were two cities of this name, one in Lybia, chap. 2. 10. the other in Assyria.

*{the Grecians}* He means not here the scattered Jews (who used the Greek tongue in their synagogues; as chap. 6. 1. for of them he spoke, verse 19.) but the Grecians which were Gentiles.

**V. 21.** *{the hand of the Lord}* The power and grace of God, in miracles, and inward working of the hearers, accompanying their ministry, See Luke 1. 66. & 5. 17.

**V. 22.** *{they sent forth Barnabas}* Of him see, chap. 4. 36. The burden of forming and ordering the church of Christ, appointing and designing pastors and ministers to the several congregations, and holding them in an holy unity, they lay now upon the apostles, therefore to confirm them that were converted to the faith, they send this son of consolation to Antioch, where a famous church was now beginning, to order and establish the same.

**V. 23.** *{had seen the grace of God}* Meaning, the admirable effects thereof, bestowed upon the people of Antioch in their conversion to Christ.

*{cleave unto the Lord}* Deut. 10. 20. & 30. 20. with ardent desire, and zeal resolving to serve him, and to rest assured in expectation of his promises.

**V. 24.** *{he was a good man}* Meaning Barnabas, verse 22.

*{much people was added, &c.}* They are here said, *added to the Lord*, who were won to the faith in Christ, and the church there present: mark here the fruit of ministers’ unity; many were added to the Lord; for Barnabas’ preaching was to confirm that truth which others had taught: it is not so where one casts down that which another builds; consider well Mat. 18. 7.

**V. 25.** *{to seek Saul}* His ambition was, that the gospel and glory of Christ, in the conversion and salvation of souls might be advanced; therefore he that might have been in great honor and esteem at Antioch, goes to seek Saul, that they might join in the work of Christ.

**V. 26.** *{with the Church}* Or *in the church* ἐν τῇ ἐκκλησίᾳ. Hence appears their great diligence in preaching and propagating the truth of the gospel, and that set out by the circumstance of time, *a whole year*, which wanted not an excellent effect, specified in the next following words.

*{were called Christians first, &c.}* Whereas before they were called disciples, and Nazarens (under which name they used to curse the professions of the gospel thrice a day: as Jerom in Isaiah 49. speaking of the unbelieving Jews, says, *ter per singulos dies sub nomine Nazarenorum maledicunt in synagogis suis*. See c. 24. 5.) Now at Antioch they were first called Christians. So much honor had this city, that from thence Christ, as it were spread his *banner*; that it might appear to the world, that there was a people who would follow him, and glory to be called by his sacred name.

**V. 27.** *{came Prophets to Jerusalem}* See chap. 13. 1. God then furnished his church with variety of gifts: *he gave some to be apostles, some prophets, &c.* See Ephes. 4. 11. and inspired these with revelations for the good of his church. See chap. 21. 9.

**V. 28.** *{named Agabus}* Chap. 21. 10. He foretold of the great general dearth in Claudius Caesar’s days, not by judicial astrology, but the Spirit, as it follows in the next words: these new calamities he foretells that the Christians, having such timely notice thereof, might (as good Joseph did) lay up store and so provide for themselves and others’ safety, in the sad calamity which was then coming upon the world.

*{signified by the Spirit}* By divine revelation: whereby God gave them occasion to relieve the brethren at Jerusalem.

**V. 29.** *{every man according to his ability}* See Luke 11. 41. Meaning every one of the Christians at Antiochia; because they of Jerusalem might probably be in more necessity, by reason of the malice of the Jews there, and their persecution. See Rom. 15. 26. 1 Cor. 16. 1. 2 Cor. 8. 4. & 9. 1, 2. Gal. 2. 10.

**V. 30.** *{sent it to the Elders}* Their pillars and ministers of the church: 1 Pet. 5. 1, 2. 2 John 1. 3 John 1. Gal. 2. 9. which elders, as Clem. Alexandrinus says, *Strom. lib. 6.* *he is indeed an elder* of the church, and a true minister of the will of God, ἐὰν ποιῇ καὶ διδάσκῃ τὰ τοῦ κυρίου, &c. if he do and teach the things of God, &c. *Ignatius Mart. epist. ad Trallianos*, speaking hereof says, οἱ δὲ πρεσβύτεροι, ὡς σύνεδροι Θεοῦ, &c. Vedelius interprets it, *Presbyteri vero, sunt concessus quidam Dei*; an assembly (or synod) of God: and presently after he says without *these, there is no elect church, no congregation of saints*: and in the same epistle, he says they are σύμβουλοι καὶ συνεδρευταὶ τοῦ ἐπισκόπου, *counselors, and assessors of the bishop*. See the same, *ep. ad Magnes.* therefore Clemens Romanus *ep. ad Corinth. 1.* says, you that laid the first foundations of sedition, ὑποτάγητε τοῖς πρεσβυτέροις *be obedient* to your *elders*, Chrysostom *To. 4. in 1 Tim. 3. 8. hom. 11.* says; having spoken of bishops, &c. omitting the order, or constitution of elders, he passes to deacons: why so? ὅτι οὐ πολὺ τὸ μέσον αὐτῶν, καὶ τῶν ἐπισκόπων because there is not much difference between them and bishops, &c. καὶ ἃ περὶ ἐπισκόπων εἶπε, ταῦτα καὶ πρεσβυτέροις ἁρμόσει. And that which he speaks of bishops, agrees also to elders: for they are superior (says he) only in ordination, in imposition of hands. Jerom on Tit. 1. says, *idem est ergo Presbyter qui Episcopus, & antequam diaboli instinctu diceretur in populis ego sum Pauli, ego Apollonis, communi presbyterorum consilio, ecclesiae gubernabantur*, much more says he there; the sum is, that the same men were bishops and elders; and that that time spoke indifferently of bishops, as of elders: whereto he cites chap. 22. 17. Acts, where it is said Paul sent to Ephesus, and called the elders of the church: and verse 28. he bade them take heed to themselves, *and to all the flock, over which the Lord hath made you* (ἐπισκόπους) *bishops* or *overseers*. *Ambros. in Ephes. 4.* (if that be his work) says, *primi Presbyteri Episcopi appellabantur, &c.* the first elders were called bishops: yet the same takes not away order, in 1 Tim. 3. *Episcopus primus est, ut omnis Episcopus presbyter sit, non tamen omnis Presbyter Episcopus: hic enim Episcopus est, qui inter Presbyteros primus est*: the bishop is the first, so that every bishop is an elder, but every elder is not a bishop: for he is a bishop, who among the elders is the first or chief. The first council at Rome, held 1074. *Anno Domini, c. 15.* shows that the elder is comprehended under the name of bishop: Severin Bin. *Concil. To. 7.* So says Isidor, that Paul calls the same elders under the name of bishop. *Concil. Aquisgranens. Anno Domini. 816. ib. To. 6.* yet that they are not the same, Epiphanius (*haeres. 75.* against the Arians) contends.

*{Barnabas}* See chap. 12. 25.

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1.** *{King Herod}* He was nephew of Herod the great, and son of Aristobulus; a viceroy, or deputy king to the emperor of Rome, for the government of the Jews: Syr. surnamed Agrippa. See Joseph. *Antiq. l. 19. c. 4. 5. &c.* See on Mat. 14. 1.

*{stretched forth his hands}* Or *laid hands on*, or *began to lay hands on some Christians*.

**V. 2.** *{killed James}* This was one of the sons of Zebedeus, Mat. 4. 21. and 10. 2. There was another so named which was the son of Alpheus: of whom, verse 17. See Matthew 20. 20.

**V. 3.** *{the days of unleavened bread}* These words intimate the cause why he deferred Peter’s execution, for reverence of the Passover, which lasted eight days. See chap. 20. 6. Sins never go single: Herod’s tyranny will draw on hypocrisy to veil it: he would seem to reverence the institution of God in the Passover and by all means to avoid the profanation thereof; not for any good conscience toward God, but because he feared to offend and provoke the Jews: however, God used the hypocrisy of this bloody tyrant, to the preservation and safety of Peter, whom he would miraculously deliver and preserve, as for the confirmation of his faith, so for the advancement of God’s glory in the propagation of the gospel.

**V. 4.** *{four quaternions of soldiers}* The number being sixteen, was divided by fours, to keep divers wards, or watch by turns: therefore the Syriac has it, *he delivered him to sixteen soldiers*; all which impious diligence of theirs, served to manifest the evidence of the miracle, and God’s merciful providence in delivering and preserving his from the fury of their enemies and persecutors.

**V. 5.** *{prayer was made without ceasing}* Or *instant and earnest prayer was made*: ἐκτενὴς, is *sedulus, diligens*. Suidas interprets ἐκτενῶς by ὁλοψύχως, *with all the strength of the soul*; and so this prayer was made with all fervency of spirit, and perseverance: it was their own cause; therefore they give all the assistance they can, according to the apostle’s rule, Hebr. 13. 3. Colos. 4. 2. 1 Thes. 5. 17. This shows that they believed that God would hear them according to his promise, Psal. 50. 15. Mat. 18. 19. &c.

**V. 6.** *{the same night}* The Syriac interpreter paraphrases thus *in the same night on whose morning following he would bring him out*. God many times suffers his to come to the pit’s brink, and then delivers them; that they may be the more sensible of his mercy, and have the more evident matter of rejoicing and praising him: also that he might by such experience give them assurance, that in the greatest dangers, he can, and will deliver them when he knows it best.

*{sleeping between two soldiers}* All these circumstances show Herod’s bloody intent, and God’s power in delivering Peter, in spite of his chains and guards.

**V. 7.** *{the Angel of the Lord}* Chap. 5. 19. For he has made his angels ministering spirits for their sakes, who are heirs of salvation. See Hebr. 1. 14. Psal. 34. 7.

*{the light shined}* As to light Peter, so to assure him of assistance from heaven: whether it shined to Peter only, or in the place where he was or went, it skills not; the guard probably were either asleep, or so stupefied, that they could either not perceive, or not resist: neither is it behooveful to inquire what light this was? whence or how it shined? whether it were only the refulgence of an angelical nature, or of some natural causes permitted to the power of the angels? If these, or the like things had been necessary to salvation, they had been written.

*{the prison}* Or *house*. Gre. ἐν τῷ οἰκήματι, *in the house*, which was the prison.

*{smote Peter on the side}* To awake, and lead him out: which shows the merciful providence of God, watching over us when we sleep; and providing for our safety, when we think not of our own dangers, and keeping us, when sleep has shut up all our senses. See Gen. 19. 16.

*{his chains fell off}* This was done by the power of God, whom all creatures obey. See Chap. 16. 27. So easily can God (whose will is his word; and his word, his work) deliver us: so admirable is the power of faithful prayer to open prison doors, make chains fall off, and to cause the sun and moon to stand still in the heaven, Josh. 10. 12, 13. to open and shut heaven for rain, James 5. 17.

**V. 8.** *{thy sandals}* Which were a kind of high shoes, tied on with strings, after the manner and convenience of those hot countries. Herein God’s fatherly providence appears: he knows what things we have need of, Mat. 6. 8. and therefore will have us supplied with all necessary accommodations, as in other infinite examples; so in the angel’s bidding Peter gird himself, bind on his sandals, and cast his garment about him, and follow him.

**V. 9.** *{and wist not that it was true, &c.}* He knew not yet whether it were a real thing, or a vision: he knew that God could deliver in life and in death, therefore he readily obeyed the voice of the angel, though at present amazed at the glory of the same, he knew not whether it were real which he saw and heard, or a vision.

**V. 10.** *{the first and second ward}* The watch which kept court of guard in the first and second place: God could at once have set him free by carrying him from so many difficulties; but thus he seemed to give him many deliverances, because from many, and through many difficulties; that the divine power, and the church’s comfort might more evidently appear.

*{leadeth unto the city}* That is, by which they went from the prison into the inward parts of the city: it is not likely that the prison was out of the city.

*{which opened to them of his own accord}* αὐτομάτη; the word signifies, that which moves, or does something of *itself*, *non alieno impulsu*, not moved by another; but this was done by the command and power of God, whose will all creatures must obey.

*{forthwith the Angel departed}* Having performed his charge; and that for which God had sent him; he left Peter free, to go to his company.

**V. 11.** *{When Peter was come to himself}* For before, he was as a man in a trance, or dream; out of himself (See verse 9.) amazed at that which befell him: but now recovering a better understanding, he knew what God had done for him. This manner of speaking the Latins also used, who in great and sudden joy, or vehement anger, used to say—*vix sum apud me*—

*{from all the expectation of the people}* For they looked that Herod would have put him to death, as he purposed, and had done to James before, verse 2.

**V. 12.** *{where many were gathered together praying}* Because they could not publicly assemble, they divided company, and met to pray and preach, as they could in those dangerous times, wherein the enemies of the gospel so violently raged: hither Peter hastens, to declare God’s goodness in his deliverance: and herein they tasted the comfortable fruit of their prayers.

**V. 13.** *{came to hearken}* To ask who was there: the Syriac gives it, *that she might answer*: to wit, those who knocked.

**V. 14.** *{she opened not the gate for gladness}* Sudden joy (as any other extreme affection) disturbs reason, and for a time, prevents the offices thereof, (as here, in Rhode) insomuch that some have died with sudden joy.

**V. 15.** *{thou art mad}* μαίνῃ, because they thought she spoke improbable things: some read *art thou mad?* for by saying thus, she discomposed them, and likely, interrupted their prayers: Syr. *omnino turbata es*: *verily you are troubled in mind*.

*{it is his Angel}* Meaning according to the common opinion, an *angel guardian*, which they might possibly suppose, came to signify his death, whom they in reason might give for dead. See Mat. 18. 10. Psal. 34. 7. & 91. 11.

**V. 16.** *{But Peter continued knocking}* Among so many dangers of the church, he might easily know they would be fearful to open doors in the night, until they could be well assured who was there: therefore he continued knocking, that he might show himself, and declare what God had done for him, for their comfort and confirmation.

**V. 17.** *{beckoning unto them with the hand}* κατασείσας Budaeus says that κατασείω is *non voce sed manu silentium indico*, to *warn silence by the hand*: this did Peter, lest at any sudden voice of joy of the brethren, some notice might be taken by those who passed by, or dwelt at the next houses, and so Peter might be further pursued, and they endangered.

*{unto James}* The son of Alpheus, James the less, by Mark 15. 40. called the brother of (that is, the kinsman of) the Lord, Gala. 1. 19. yet surviving, verse 2. Matthew 10. 3. Luke 6. 15. Acts 15. 13. Antiquity took him to be superintendent, or bishop of the church of Jerusalem. See chap. 21. 17, 18. Gala. 2. 9. *Chrysost. hom. 33. in Act. Jerom. To. 1. catalog. script. eccles.*

*{another place}* Some more secret and secure place, for fear of pursuit, when they should miss him in the prison; for in all likelihood missing him there, they would have made very diligent inquiry for, and narrow search for all conventions and houses of Christians who were most likely to harbor him; and had they found him, both he and others with him must probably have perished by Herod’s raging cruelty.

**V. 18.** *{there was no small stir, &c.}* Syr. *there was great contention*; probably, all being troubled with fear and admiration, they would lay the supposed fault of the prisoner’s escape, on the keepers; they were not ignorant of Herod’s tyranny, nor the ordinary danger of letting prisoners escape.

**V. 19.** *{put to death}* ἀπαχθῆναι, *to be led away*; that is, to execution: so the Syriac, *he commanded that they should die*.

*{he went}* Meaning Peter.

**V. 20.** *{was highly displeased}* Or *bare an hostile mind*, *intending war*.

*{with them of Tyre and Sidon}* Two rich and ancient cities of Phenicia, on the coast of Syria; one of the frontier *canton*, or free towns. See Isaiah 23. 8. It may be, that their wealth begat them insolency, to affront Herod; or his ambition and covetousness might possibly instigate him to quarrel with them.

*{came with one accord}* Having formerly suffered grievous things, they now, out of dear experience, learned rather to contend with words, and treaty, than arms; and to try all means to save blood: every state may begin a war, but it will be in the power of the conqueror only, to end it, and he must be more than wise who shall compose it.

*{the king’s Chamberlain}* Gr. *that was over the kings bedchamber*.

*{their country}* See 1 Kings 5. 9, 11. Ezek. 27. 17.

**V. 21.** *{Upon a set day}* Probably, it was appointed for audience of the Tyrian, and Sidonian commissioners about the peace.

**V. 22.** *{the people gave a shout saying}* As profane sycophants, they applauded him, and gave him the honor due to God.

**V. 23.** *{immediately the Angel, &c.}* God sent an angel to deliver Peter; and now he sends another to punish the sacrilegious Herod: that which *Josephus Ant. l. 19. c. 7. & Euseb. l. 2.* speak of other signs and presages about him, there being no other certainty, must be left on the credit of those authors.

*{he gave not}* Because he suppressed not their impious flattery, but rather encouraged them by countenancing the same.

*{eaten of worms}* Or *vermin*: some write that his grandfather also was eaten of lice.

**V. 24.** *{the word of God}* Was happily preached, and received of many, with good fruit of faith and obedience; as Col. 1. 6. Acts 19. 20.

**V. 25.** *{returned from Jerusalem}* To Antioch, which both the Syriac, and some Greek copies express. And this is here set down as a preparation and passage into the following history: for the better understanding whereof, we must consider that which we read chap. 11. 29, 30. concerning the disciples’ determination to send relief unto the Christian brethren in Judea, by the hands of *Barnabas* and *Saul*.

*{fulfilled their ministry}* Or *charge*, chap. 11. 29, 30. which now had been to carry the benevolence of the Antiochian Christians, to the saints at Jerusalem.

*{John whose surname was Mark}* This is said, to distinguish him from the other Mark: this John Mark was he whose mother is mentioned, verse 12. He going with Paul and Barnabas, was afterward the occasion of a bitter dissension between them, chap. 15. 37, 39.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1.** *{Certain Prophets}* So were they called, not only who foretold things to come, as Agabus, but those also who interpret Scriptures, 1 Cor. 14. See on chap. 11. 27.

*{Manaen which had been brought up with Herod}* Or *Herod’s foster brother*; that was Herod Antipas who slew John Baptist, Matthew 14. 1. This commends Manaen’s religion, who in contempt of the world embraced the gospel, as Moses had done the truth of religion, and worship of the true God: or at least, shows how God has in all sorts and degrees of men, some who fear him: he had in impious Ahab’s house, an Obadiah, 1 Kings 18. 3, 13. a Moses in wicked Pharaoh’s; some believers in Nero’s, Phil. 4. 22. and Herod’s, Luke 8. 3.

**V. 2.** *{ministered}* In the public service of God, as prayer, preaching, administration of the sacraments, and other ministerial offices. And here is mention made of fasting, to show how intent and serious they were in holy duties, from which they admitted no distraction by things at other times necessary for the maintenance of life and health.

*{the Holy Ghost said}* By a prophetical revelation either to some one or more of them.

*{separate me Barnabas}* That they may have like authority with the other apostles, infallibility of the spirit, and commission to preach unto the Gentiles, Gal. 2. 9.

**V. 3.** *{laid their hands on them}* That imposition of hands signified their setting apart to this particular employment, the consent of the church, and their blessing. See chap. 6. 6. and 14. 26.

**V. 4.** *{Seleucia}* A sea town of Cilicia.

*{to Cyprus}* Chap. 11. 19. An island in the Mediterranean Sea, famous for fruitfulness and amenity.

**V. 5.** *{Salamis}* A city of Cyprus on the east: hence there was commodious passage into Syria: it was since called Constantia. *Atlas minor, G. Mercat.* Here they preached the word of God in the synagogues of the Jews: for though they were sent out chiefly for the conversion and confirmation of the Gentiles; yet they were therein first to preach the gospel to the Jews, when and wherever they found opportunity thereto, serving, verse 46. 47. Again, it is something considerable that they could hardly then find any other places for public assemblies, the heathens’ temples being furnished with such abominations, as afforded the servants of God no convenience of entering into them.

*{to their ministry}* To the work of the ministry.

**V. 6.** *{Paphos}* A city of Cyprus.

**V. 7.** *{the deputy}* ἀνθύπατος, the *Proconsul*, or *Propraetor*, a Roman officer, of whom see *Fenestella de Magistrat. Rom. cap. 11. Pompon. Laet. de Magistrat. Rom. Volaterran. de Magist. & sacr. Rom.*

**V. 8.** *{but Elymas}* It is an Arabic word, and signifies a *magician*, *wise man*, &c. See *Joh. Drus. ad Voc. N. T. tom. 2. cap. 7.*

**V. 9.** *{then Saul}* Becoming a Christian he changed his Hebrew name Saul, for a Roman name Paul, being specially assigned, an apostle, and doctor of the Gentiles.

**V. 10.** *{thou child, &c.}* See Matth. 13. 38. John 8. 44. 1 John 3. 8. So Christ told the impious Jews, John 8. 44. *ye are of your father the devil*. So Paul here calls this enemy of all righteousness, *child of the devil*, that he might discover the author of those impostures by which Elimas had bewitched the people; as also to beat down the arrogancy of the false prophet, who impudently professed himself to be *Bar jesus*: the Syriac calls him בר־שומא which says the learned Tremelius, in Latin sounds *filium nominis*, a *man of name*: it is an *Hebraism*, signifying a famous man. So Gen. 6. 4. such are called אנשי השם that is, *men of renown*: and as בן in Hebrew, so בר in Syriac oftentimes signifies *praeditum*, endued, qualified, or meet, as Deut. 3. 18. Jud. 21. 10. 1 Sam. 14. 52. בן־חיל *filius roboris* a son of strength; a man endued with strength (or a valiant man) Job 41. בני־שחץ *sons of pride* (that is, proud). So in this place, *a son of name*, signifies a man renowned; (or famous) in what his name was famous both the Hebrew name, which the Greek retains, בר ישוע as also the interpretation thereof *Elimas* do show: for *Bar jesus* signifies *praeditum salute*, endued with health or safety; and *Elimas* which in Hebrew they would write אלימעץ, signifies, *divine counsel*: for they who were bewitched, and befooled by his art, ascribed salvation, or safety unto him, forasmuch as they thought him to be partaker of the divine counsels: so Satan uses to veil and adorn his artifices with ample titles: so those enchanters, before Moses and Daniel, like this magician, were styled with an honorable name, being called חכמים, that is, *wise men*.

*{pervert the right ways}* To lay false imputations on the gospel, and the way of salvation, to hinder the work of God’s grace.

**V. 11.** *{the hand of the Lord}* The power of God which shows itself in punishing his enemies.

**V. 12.** *{being astonished}* See Matthew 7. 28. which he saw accompanied with such power of God, in persuading, reproving, and doing miracles; and of such majesty and sanctity.

**V. 13.** *{Pamphylia}* A country in Asia the less: hither they came from Paphos, now called *[?]aphos*§1 in Cyprus. *Atlas min.*

**V. 14.** *{Antioch}* This was another Antiochia in Asia, bounding upon Lycaonia, Isauria, and Pamphylia, not that Antioch in Syria, chap. 11. 19.

**V. 15.** *{after the reading of the Law}* Which they so distributed into parts, to be read every Sabbath day, that they read all the law and prophets once a year. See Luke 4. 16. See *Tremel. in loc.*

*{rulers of the synagogue}* For no man might presume to preach without lawful authority, and calling thereto by the governors by God appointed. See on chap. 18. 8.

**V. 16.** *{beckoning with his hand}* To get audience and warn them to attention. See on chap. 12. 17.

**V. 17.** *{exalted the people}* This relates to that dignity of Joseph in Pharaoh’s court, and that which his father and brethren had for his sake, until another Pharaoh arose who knew them not. He speaks these things concerning their fathers to make them know, that whatever excellency they or their fathers had, was not of their merit, but God’s free mercy: and that they now taught no new religion, but the very same in which their fathers were saved, all depending on God’s free election of the Jews whereby he became their God. See Gen. 17. 7. Deut. 4. 7. Psal. 147. 19, 20. Ezek. 16. 3. &c. Josh. 24. 2, 3. &c.

*{when they dwelt as strangers}* Exod. 1. 1.

*{and with an high arm}* That which Moses expresses Exod. 32. 11. *with great power* וביד חזקה *and with mighty hand*, 70. ἐν ἰσχύϊ μεγάλῃ, καὶ ἐν τῷ βραχίονί σου τῷ ὑψηλῷ: the meaning is, by showing his might and power, in destroying Israel’s enemies, and removing all other obstructions to their coming out of Egypt. See also Exod. 13. 14, 16.

**V. 18.** *{forty years suffered their manners}* ἐτροποφόρησεν &c. τροποφορέω, signifies to bear with, or to endure one’s manners or qualities. So Suidas’ interpreter gives it here; but the Syriac ותרסי אנון *vetharse innun*, *and he nourished them*, as if it were ἐτροφοφόρησεν αὐτοὺς, that is, ἔθρεψεν αὐτοὺς, *he nourished them*, *bearing them like a nurse*, as Moses speaks, Numb. 1. 12. Deut. 32. 11. Indeed he did both: he endured their many rebellions with great mercy, and did feed and carry them like a tender nurse; though here the apostle aims at the first, that he might show, that God’s free election of their fathers was the cause why his goodness would not strive with their ungrateful wickedness: they many times provoked him to anger, yet God (though he often corrected them) destroyed them not, but with admirable lenity, bare their perverse qualities, that in the fullness of time he might perform the promise made to their fathers.

**V. 19.** *{destroyed seven nations}* That he might plant Israel in that land. See Gen. 15. 17. Josh. 3. 10. Judg. 5. 6.

*{he divided their land}* Josh. 14. 21.

**V. 20.** *{he gave unto them judges}* Judg. 3. 9.

*{the space of, &c.}* Reckoning from Moses to Samuel’s government, there were sixteen judges, *Moses, Joshua, Othniel, Ehud, Deborah, Gedeon, Abimelech, Thola, Jares, Jephte, Abessam, Elam, Abdon, Sampson, Eli, Samuel*; who governed so long in all. See Deut. 1. 34. Judges 3. and 5. and 8. and 9. and 10. and 11. and 15. 1 Sam. 4.

**V. 21.** *{afterward they desired a King}* 1 Sam. 8. 5.

**V. 22.** *{he raised up unto them David}* 1 Sam. 16. 13.

*{I have found David}* Psal. 89. 20.

**V. 23.** *{of this man’s seed}* Isaiah 11. 1. that is, of the lineage of David, God raised up Jesus Christ our Savior. See Isaiah 11. 1. Rom. 1. 3. &c. Mat. 1. Luke 3. 31.

**V. 24.** *{when John had first preached}* Mat. 3. 1.

*{before his coming}* Gr. πρὸ προσώπου τῆς εἰσόδου αὐτοῦ, word for word, *before the face of his entrance*: the prophets preached in several ages before Christ’s coming, but not as John, who in view of him: as the Hebrews said לפני *liphne* to, or before the face here of Christ present it may be said. See Luke 1. 76. John pointed him out present, John 1. 29. and preached and administered the sacrament of baptism, and doctrine of repentance, a due preparative thereto.

**V. 25.** *{Whom think ye that I am?}* John 1. 20.

*{I am not he}* See John 1. 21, 25.

**V. 26.** *{unto this salvation}* Meaning the gospel (which is the gladsome tidings of salvation in Christ) was first addressed to the Jews.

**V. 27.** *{because they knew him not}* They knew not Christ to be the Messias; or knowing him by his works exceeding all created power, would not yet acknowledge, because he in the form of a servant, Phil. 2. 7. answered not their expectation of a glorious temporal monarch.

*{nor yet the voices of the Prophets}* That is, as the Syriac interprets, *the writings of the prophets* according to which he here also says, *which are read every Sabbath, &c.*

*{they have fulfilled}* See chap. 4. 48.

**V. 28.** *{and though they found}* Mat. 27. 22.

**V. 29.** *{when they had fulfilled all}* All that God would have done by them, who so used Christ, that nothing of all the prophecies concerning his sufferings was unfulfilled. See Luke 24. 44. John 19. 30.

*{they took him down}* That is, Joseph of Arimathea, and Nicodemus. See Mat. 27. 58.

**V. 30.** *{but God raised him}* Mat. 28. 6. Lest any should suspect that he could not therefore be their Messias and Savior, who died by the hands of the wicked; he hence proves him to be the promised Messias who was to die for the people, (Dan. 9. 26. Isa. 53. 6, 8, 10, 11, 12.) and in his resurrection was declared to be the Son of God, Rom. 1. 4.

**V. 31.** *{and he was seen, &c.}* See on chap. 10. 41. and Mat. 28. 6.

**V. 32.** *{glad tidings how that the promise}* Intimating that he taught no new matter, but that the ancient promises concerning the Messias were now fulfilled.

**V. 33.** *{as it is also written}* Psal. 2. 7. Heb. 1. 5. In that he was incarnate, God began to fulfill his promise which was accomplished in his resurrection and ascension, as appears in the next verse.

**V. 34.** *{I will give you}* Isaiah 55. 3.

*{the sure mercies of David}* Hebr. חסדי דוד הנאמנים *chasde David hanneemanim*, *the stable mercies of David*: 70. τὰ ὅσια Δαυὶδ τὰ πιστὰ, which S. Luke follows; *the faithful or certain holy things of David*: that is, the promises which he made of his free mercy to David, concerning the eternal kingdom of Christ, 2 Sam. 7.

**V. 35.** *{thou shalt not suffer}* Psal. 16. 10. See chap. 2. 27.

**V. 36.** *{after he had served, &c.}* Or *after he had in his own age served the will of God*: the meaning is, after David had lived uprightly toward God, in his appointed time he died, and therefore that which he foretold in these prophecies, appertained unto Christ, who saw no corruption, not unto David himself.

**V. 37.** *{saw no corruption}* Did not corrupt, verse 35. and chap. 2. 27. for he rose again the third day.

**V. 38.** *{through this man}* It is preached and declared unto you, that through Jesus you shall have remission of sins: this is the conclusion of the apostle’s sermon: he had showed that Christ whom they preached was the very Messias promised to the fathers, and removed the Jews’ objection, the scandal of the cross; he now further declares what great benefits are offered in Christ, and how severely God will revenge the contempt of his beloved Son, if they refuse mercy offered, verse 40.

**V. 39.** *{are justified from all things}* Whereas by the law, and ceremonies thereof, no man could be justified or acquitted of the guilt of his sins; they that believe in Christ are justified (Rom. 5. 1.) the law had but the shadow of that which was to be performed in Christ, Gal. 3. 2, 22. Rom. 3. 20. Hebr. 10. 1, 4.

**V. 40.** *{spoken of in the Prophets}* Hab. 1. 5. Isaiah 28. 14. the substance of whose words he here cites and applies to them, who contemning the word of God, do in effect think they have made a league with death and hell, so that the judgment of God shall not come on them, who have made lies their hope; whereas Christ is that only precious cornerstone and sure foundation laid in Sion, *in whom whosoever believeth shall not perish.* See Isaiah 28. 16.

**V. 41.** *{Behold ye despisers}* This seems to be taken from Isaiah 28. 14. where the prophet says, *Hear the word of the Lord* אנשי לצון *anshe latson*, *ye men of derision*: Ar.
