Mont. *irrisores*. V. Shindl. *pent. viri illusores. Vatablus & Vulg.* (it is of לוץ *luts, derisit, cavillator est*) mockers and despisers of God’s word: the rest, for substance, out of Habak. 1. 5. which by analogy and proportion he accommodates to his present purpose: as God, when the people most securely despised prophecies, brought heavy plagues and destructions on them by the Chaldees who demolished their temple, and ruined their city (wherein they so much gloried and trusted) because of their contempt of his word, and other crying sins following the same. 2 Chron. 36. 15, 16. &c. So did it now come upon them again by the Romans, for their despising Christ and the gospel.

**V. 42.** *{might be preached to them the next Sabbath}* Or *in the week between*, *on some day before the next Sabbath*, as some interpret: but verse 44. the convention was on the next Sabbath: so much were they affected with that they heard, that they desired much to hear it again, that they might better understand and remember the same; we could often hear what we love.

**V. 43.** *{many of the Jews}* Though some were averse and more enraged against the gospel, yet God still has a part. *Proselytes*; Suid. *quasi* προσηλυθότες, because they came to the religion of the Jews from paganism. See chap. 2. 10. These and other converts the apostles exhort to constancy in the faith of Jesus, well knowing with what fierce encounters Satan uses to charge the infancy of true religion, and our first good beginnings.

**V. 44.** *{almost the whole city}* A metonymical expression; city for citizens: some coming of zeal to the gospel; some of malice, to cavil: so are our assemblies mixed still.

**V. 45.** *{filled with envy}* ζῆλος here signifies, *an envious indignation*, at the great confluence of the Gentiles following the apostles. See on chap. 5. 17. They envied the growth and happy success of the gospel.

*{contradicting and blaspheming}* Contradicting, and caviling at the doctrine of the gospel, and likely reviling their persons, discrediting and calumniating them: blasphemy, and persecution of the ministers of Christ seldom go asunder.

**V. 46.** *{It was necessary}* Because so commanded, Mat. 10. 6. This sets forth the odiousness of their ingratitude to God, in that they, of whom God had so great a care before all the nations of the world, to have the means of salvation by Christ made known unto them, obstinately rejected the same.

*{and judge yourselves}* This your unbelief and contempt of the gospel, speak you unworthy of eternal life, which is only by Christ, your own fact judges you not worthy, &c.

**V. 47.** *{I have set thee to be a light, &c.}* Isaiah 49. 6. To give the people, that sat in darkness, &c. Mat. 4. knowledge of the way to eternal life. See Joh. 17. 3.

**V. 48.** *{When the Gentiles heard this they were glad}* When they understood their own interest in Christ, and their calling to the hope of salvation, they received the gospel with greater reverence and joy of heart: they had hitherto no rest in their consciences, but Christ now thus proposed to them, and apprehended by faith, gives them that, Rom. 5. 1. therefore they cannot but rejoice. See chap. 8. 39. Rom. 14. 17. 1 Pet. 1. 8.

*{to eternal life, believed}* Here is the fountain of all saving grace, God’s free and eternal mercy ordaining the means, as he has the end: they now obtaining the means (knowledge of Christ by the gospel preached to them, and feeling the power thereof in their faith and conversion) believe, and give thanks to God for this favor, being assured of the end thereof to life eternal.

**V. 49.** *{published throughout all the region}* In spite of all oppositions: so true is that which Christ intimated in the parable of the leaven, Matthew 13. 33. concerning the process of the gospel.

**V. 50.** *{devout and honourable women}* Which had embraced the law of Moses, and the Jewish religion, and were led with blind zeal against the gospel which they knew not: so that calling them devout, the evangelist speaks according to the world’s estimate of them: the corruption of the best is worst; where Satan can but gain such agents to his party, there are commonly the bloodiest persecutions of the prophets and ministers of Christ: Jezabel, Herodias, and these Pisidians here are examples enough; I would we had no more.

**V. 51.** *{Shook off the dust}* See Matth. 10. 14. Mark 6. 11. Luke 9. 5. and 10. 11. It was a sign of execration with the Jews.

*{came to Iconium}* Some say this was a town in Lycaonia near to the famous mountain Taurus, *Plin. nat. hist. lib. 5. 27.* mentions it: others say, there are others of the same name in other provinces. See chap. 14. 1.

**V. 52.** *{the disciples were filled with joy}* Because of the success of the gospel, and because the Spirit of God reigned in them, to direct and make them glad: the scope is to show that the disciples were so far from being discouraged with the Jews’ blasphemies, calumnies, oppositions, and persecutions, that they were more confirmed hereby, and glad for their sufferings for the truth of Christ. See chap. 5. 41.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1.** *{And it came to pass}* κατὰ τὸ αὐτὸ, *likewise*, or *as it was a little before that time*, at Antioch in Pisidia, chap. 13. 14.

*{in Iconium}* Chap. 13. 51.

*{went both together into the}* As they did at Antioch, chapter 13. 14.

*{of the Greeks}* Proselytes frequenting the Jews’ synagogues, though they received not circumcision.

**V. 2.** *{unbelieving Jews}* ἀπειθοῦντες, *those that believed not*, or *those that obeyed not the gospel*, as not suffering their hard and obstinate hearts to be persuaded to receive the truth thereof. So Syriac, *qui non obediebant*.

*{against the brethren}* The believers, who with brotherly love and unity call upon one heavenly Father.

**V. 3.** *{long time, &c.}* ἱκανὸν χρόνον, *a sufficient time*. See Luke 8. 27. & 20. 9. & 23. 8.

*{boldly in the Lord}* *Of the Lord*, as the Syriac gives it: or rather *trusting in the Lord*.

*{unto the word of his grace}* The word in which his grace and free mercy is preached and declared to those that believe in Christ.

*{and granted signs}* These words express how God gave testimony to the doctrine which Paul and Barnabas preached. See Mark 16. 20.

**V. 4.** *{was divided}* ἐσχίσθη; they were *cut studia in contraria*, into contrary factions, or opinions, so that some took part with the Jews, and others with the apostles.

**V. 5.** *{an assault made}* 2 Tim. 3. 11. Here appears the effect of unbelief; sedition and violence offered to the servants of Christ: impiety cannot contain itself, but on every occasion breaks out from worse to worse.

**V. 6.** *{fled to Lystra}* Matth. 10. 23. See here verse 20. when they were in hazard of being destroyed, they fled according to our Savior’s command, Matthew 10. thereby to decline the fury of their adversaries: this was wisdom, not inconstancy.

**V. 7.** *{there they preached}* Thus, by their flight they reserved themselves to better opportunities of doing good, in these cities of Lystra and Derbe (where they found Timothie, chap. 16. 1. Rom. 16. 21.) these were towns near Lycaonia in Asia the lesser.

**V. 8.** *{cripple from his mother’s womb}* This sets off the excellency of God’s power in the cure, as chap. 3. 2. &c.

**V. 9.** *{perceiving that he had faith to be healed}* Perceiving by his attention, countenance, or some words by him uttered, that he had faith for his cure which unbelief hinders, Matth. 13. 38. Mark 6. 5.

**V. 10.** *{with a loud voice}* That the people there present might hear: and that Paul might thereby profess his confidence of the cure.

*{he leaped and walked}* Showing a sound recovery, as chap. 3.

**V. 11.** *{the gods are come down}* Their idol worship was not terminative in their images, but they vainly thought they worshiped some deity by those images, so that though their idols were present with them, yet here they dreamed that those gods who were thereby worshiped, were now come personally down among them. So blind is flesh and blood unto the clearest light of the gospel; though Paul had preached Christ unto them, and his doctrine was presently confirmed with a singular miracle, which by their own principles of reasons, none but a divine power could have done, yet they impute that work to their false and imaginary gods; so little can miracles prevail for the spectators’ conversion and confirmation, except the Holy Ghost open their hearts to understand and believe the truth.

**V. 12.** *{called Barnabas, Jupiter, &c.}* They called them by the names of their false gods: *Barnabas, Jupiter*; their chief idol god: and *Paul, Mercury*; whom they feigned to be the interpreter of their gods, because Paul here appeared the chief speaker.

**V. 13.** *{brought oxen and garlands}* Trimmed with garlands of flowers, according to the custom of the heathens.

*{unto the gates}* Meaning the gates of the house where the apostle lodged; for their temple was without the town: and therefore the priest brought the sacrifices (as he thought) to the gods themselves.

**V. 14.** *{rent their clothes}* In token of their detesting and abhorring it: knowing that in such impious and foolish sacrilege, themselves should have been made idols, and robbers of God, had they but consented to receive such vain honor from them.

*{crying out}* That all the people might be undeceived, and not held any longer in those snares of the devil.

**V. 15.** *{we also are men}* See chap. 10. 26.

*{of like passions}* ὁμοιοπαθεῖς; *mortal*, as you are, subject to death, as all others. See James 5. 17. not without the infirmities of flesh and blood: Budaeus interprets this place, *eadem conditione mortalitatis, &c. simili conditione vobiscum humanitus afficimur*. In the same condition of mortality, &c. in the like nature are we humanly affected, as you are.

**V. 15.** *{turn from these vanities}* That is, from idolatry, superstition, &c. this is after the Hebrew manner, who called idols הבל *hebel*, *vanity*. So Deut. 32. 21. *they provoked me to anger* בהבליהם *behablehem*, *with their vanities*, which the 70. give there, ἐν τοῖς εἰδώλοις αὐτῶν, Jer. 10. 8, 15. the same word they give by τὰ μάταια, *vanities*, the word here used. See also 1 Kings 16. 13, 26. Jer. 8. 19. Certainly adoration of senseless idols, is the most irrational vanity that ever Satan put into the stupefied heart of man: how much more truly do dumb creatures naturally judge of your gods, (said Minucius Felix *Octav.*) *Non sentire eos sciunt, rodunt, insiliunt, insident*: *they know they have no sense, they gnaw them, leap on them, sit on them*; the birds would breed in the mouth of your god, and spiders hang their slender threads on their head, &c.

*{which made heaven and earth}* Genesis 1. 1. Psalm 146. 6. Rev. 14. 17. Paul and Barnabas had here to deal with heathens who knew not God; so that it was necessary to lead them by certain degrees to Christ the Savior: therefore they begin from that which was not so much remote from common sense; drawing them off from the worship of their inactive and senseless idols, to the consideration of the true God, who both created heaven, earth, and all creatures, and still governs and preserves the same: probably they spoke much more concerning, God’s justice, mercy, and eternity, and so prepared the way to lead them up to the knowledge of Christ the Savior.

**V. 16.** *{who in times past}* Psal. 82. 12. Acts 17. 30. Here they seem to prepossess an objection which their hearers might have made; if God were always of such power, why did he not sooner reveal the same unto us? How came it that he so much prospered our ancestors, who served the same gods, with the like worship and rites, as we have hitherto done? Had he not approved this service, he would not have favored us therein: the apostles answered to this purpose: God was not ignorant of these your impious manners; but in his just judgment gave you up to your own vile affections (Rom. 1. 24, 28.) to perish therein: but now that God vouchsafes you the knowledge of his will for your salvation, neither consent of multitudes erring, nor antiquity of sin, ought to hinder you from embracing a saving truth; but rather the longsuffering of God, must lead you to repentance.

*{to walk in their own ways}* To live after their own fantasies: not that God ever approved of their wickedness, but because he justly gave them over (Rom. 1. 24.) to their sin and superstition, not bestowing on them his laws and ordinances, as he did on Israel, Psal. 147. 19, 20.

**V. 17.** *{he left not himself without witness}* Here he prepossesses an objection: if any should say, how should they not err, seeing that God neither revealed his will nor himself unto them? The answer is that God might be seen in the works of his wisdom, power, and providence, in all the known courses of nature, and things appointed for the common use of man, as showers, fruit, corn, &c. See Rom. 1. 19. As also in his admirable patience, and invincible goodness, in that he gives to the just and unjust, *indiscretos pluvias*, unsevered showers (Cyprian *de bon. patient.*) and all other secular goods. See Mat. 5. 45.

*{with food and gladness}* With gladness for the abundance with which he crowns the year. See Psal. 65. 9. &c.

**V. 18.** *{with these sayings scarce, &c.}* All that they could say, could hardly persuade these obstinate idolaters to forbear sacrificing to them. So hard is it to make carnal hearts understand the things of God: so hard, to remove an error, to which time, custom, and popular example has given deep root.

**V. 19.** *{certain Jews from Antioch}* See chap. 13. 14. Here appears Satan’s restless malice in assaulting the ministers of Christ: now trying them with sacrilege, and robbing God of his honor, in accepting sacrifices and divine honor, when that artifice failed; he incites the Jews, and they the people, to persecute and stone them. See also the inconstancy of the vulgar; but even now mad to adore them as gods, and forthwith as little sober in the other extreme, persecuting them, as if they had been the worst of men, stoning Paul, and dragging him out of the city as dead.

**V. 20.** *{as the disciples stood round about him}* That is, the believers, who followed the apostles: these forsook not Paul, but compassed him round to keep him, if possible, from further rage of the seditious multitude.

*{he rose up}* He revived, and came to himself again, and entered again into that injurious city, to rest, and refresh himself.

*{to Derbe}* See verse 6. It was a city of Isauria in Asia the lesser, as some say, though near to Lycaonia, *Joh. Drus. ad voc. N. T. tom. 1.* Jerom *de loc. Act.* says *Derbe civitas Lycaoniae provinciae*, which the sixth verse seems to intimate: but that may be interpreted, *they fled unto the cities of Lycaonia, to Lystra and Derbe, and unto the region that lieth round about*: and that not reckoning Lystra and Derbe, as cities of Lycaonia, but showing that the apostles did not only flee to the cities of Lycaonia, but also to Lystra and Derbe, and other places in the adjacent region: the Syriac seems to favor this interpretation, which says, *they fled into the cities of Lycaonia*, ולוסטרא ודרבא *velystra vedarbi*, *and Lystra, and Derbe*, *they fled into the cities of Lycaonia, and into Lystra, and into Derbe*.

**V. 21.** *{and when they had preached the Gospel, &c.}* This was their undaunted courage and unwearied industry to preach the gospel, notwithstanding so many dangers.

*{and had taught many}* μαθητεύσαντες ἱκανοὺς, *and had taught a sufficient number*: that is had by teaching brought many to the faith of Christ, making them disciples, who were baptized, for the word μαθητεύειν, does not here signify only τὸ διδάσκειν, to teach, but by teaching of the capable; and baptizing to make disciples of Christ, as Mat. 28. 19. So the Syriac interprets here, *they made many disciples*: the Jews, and proselytes were made Moses’ disciples when they were circumcised, and catechumenists and infants of Christian parents are made Christians, and disciples of Christ by baptism.

*{to Antioch}* Of *Pisidia*, c. 13. 14.

**V. 22.** *{confirming the souls}* That is, the minds of the believers, which was their care to strengthen and comfort them: it is not enough to sow good seed, it must be watered and fenced. So in God’s field.

*{through much tribulation, &c.}* This they say, lest the weak should doubt of the truth of the word, by reason of the persecutions following the preaching thereof. See Mat. 7. 14. Luke 21. 19. Afflictions are the *Marahs* in the way to our promised rest. See 1 Pet. 4. 13. Rom. 8. 17. 2 Tim. 2. 11. 2 Tim. 3. 11, 13. It is God’s great mercy, *Nequitiae impunitatem non relinquere*, not to leave wickedness unpunished, and now to correct with the rod, that he may not condemn in hell forever. *Augustine de verb. Dom. Luc. serm. 37.*

**V. 23.** *{ordained them elders}* χειροτονήσαντες: this word signifies making of such a choice, as was made or confirmed by lifting up of hands, to signify *suffrages*, or consents: it is also used to appoint or ordain in general, as c. 10. 41. The Syriac reads; *and they appointed unto them elders in every congregation*. It seems that Paul and Barnabas did not all alone in ordinations and church government; other Christians showed their consent, or approbation of the persons who were ordained elders, by lifting up their hands; as very well knowing of what behavior they had been among them: so ἀποχειροτονέω signifies, to disallow of some act, election, or decree: Suidas interprets the word χειροτονία by ἐκλογὴ: his interpreter gives it, *electio, delectus—per suffragia confirmatio, populi totius consensus*, an election, choosing by voices, a confirmation or consent of all the people. See on chap. 11. 30. and Tit. 1. 5. &c. 1 Tim. 3. 7. 1 Pet. 5. 1. 2 John 1. 3 John 1.

*{commended them to the Lord, &c.}* They prayed for their safety and happy success, under God’s blessed protection.

*{on whom they believed}* Which shows, as their confidence, so our comfort in God who never forsakes them that believe and truly seek him. See Psal. 9. 10.

**V. 24.** *{Pisidia}* c. 13. 14.

*{Pamphylia}* c. 13. 13.

**V. 25.** *{in Perga}* c. 13. 13.

*{Attalia}* A town in *Pamphilia*.

**V. 26.** *{had been recommended}* Chap. 13. 1, 4. Recommended in their prayers, to the power and free assistance of God’s Holy Spirit, enabling them to the work of their ministry, and to convert the Gentiles unto whom they went from Antioch, chap. 13. 3, 4.

*{the work which they fulfilled}* Herein is set out the industry and fidelity of these men in the ministry.

**V. 27.** *{opened the door of faith}* By the preaching of the gospel, and power of the Holy Spirit, he gave them entrance into the communion of faith in Christ. See Rev. 3. 7, 8.

**V. 28.** *{there they abode long time}* To edify those Christians, and to rest and refresh themselves, as Christ sometimes retired with his disciples from the multitudes.

## CHAPTER 15

**Verse 1.** *{Certain men that came down}* Epiphanius *haeres. 28. 2.* says, Cerinthus and others: this policy of Satan whereby he assailed the church by civil dissension, was concerning the office of Christ; whether we are justified only by faith in Christ’s righteousness, or must therewith join the works of the law.

*{except ye be circumcised}* Gal. 5. 2. Luke briefly states this troublesome question: these impostors would have laid a necessity of keeping the whole law, on men’s consciences; for *every man that is circumcised, is a debtor to do the whole law*, and so *Christ is become of no effect unto him*, Gal. 5. 3, 4. but he is still under the curse of the law: these adversaries acknowledged Christ to be the true Messias, and had given their names to him, only they would with faith in Christ, join a necessity of observing the law for salvation; which possibly seemed an excusable error unto some: but Paul, who for small external matters would not have contention to the violation of peace (Rom. 14. 1.) would not in this cause be silent. First because a necessity of observing the whole law being granted, man’s salvation, which only can be in the free grace and mercy of God in Christ should be grounded on the law; and so it would follow, that Christ died without cause or in vain, Gal. 2. 21. Secondly, by such a concession, Christian liberty must be taken away. See Gal. 4. 1. &c. Thirdly, by this means Satan would easily have obscured the honor of Christ, and light of the gospel, if he could have carried on this stratagem: Paul therefore here stands up for defense of God’s free mercy in our salvation, for discharge of our consciences from the curse and heavy burden of the law; and that the glory of God, his grace and mercy, saving us, might like a clear light from heaven shine to all the world.

**V. 2.** *{no small dissension}* στάσεως, for ἀντιστάσεως: the Syriac gives it *commotion*: it was an eager contention, Satan (as his manner is) stirring up factious men, to trouble the peace of the church, and to corrupt the doctrine thereof, Paul and Barnabas like constant champions of Christ defending the truth.

*{they determined to send}* To wit, the brethren of Antioch (for a remedy against this emergent mischief) determined to send Paul and Barnabas with some others, to the apostles and elders then being at Jerusalem, to desire them to decide this controversy; to this Paul willingly subscribed, though he were nothing inferior to the *very chief apostles*, 2 Cor. 12. 1. (for as Cyprian *de simpl. praelat.* says well, *Hoc erant utique & caeteri Apostoli, quod fuit Petrus, pari consortio praediti & honoris & potestatis*, *the rest of the apostles were as Peter, endued with equal partnership of honor and authority*) that he might thereby satisfy the believers concerning those false apostles, who pretended the authority of the church at Jerusalem for the maintenance of their heresy: he knew his doctrine was of God, and not to be subjected to the judgment of men: yet for the satisfaction and confirmation of the weak, he willingly submitted to their judgment who were at Jerusalem.

**V. 3.** *{brought on their way by the Church}* That is, by the believers. Some of them being appointed to accompany Paul and Barnabas on their way, lest they should seem to have gone of their own accord, or to have dissented from the church of Antioch.

*{Phenice}* Chap. 11. 19.

*{declaring the conversion}* To all the churches as they passed along they showed the brethren for their comfort and confirmation, what God had done in the conversion of the Gentiles to the faith of Christ: it is a joy of heart to all good men to hear the gospel’s flourishing.

**V. 4.** *{they were received of the Church}* Of all the church they were acknowledged for singular instruments of God’s glory, and men’s salvation, by the propagation of the gospel.

*{they declared all things}* That they might understand by the success of their ministry, that the calling of the Gentiles (which the adversaries labored by their false doctrines, and mixture of antiquated ceremonies to annul and overthrow) pleased God.

**V. 5.** *{but there arose up}* Some understand this, as if these (according with the seducers who came to Antioch,) rose up to trouble them at Jerusalem also: but others more probably refer these words to the relation which Paul and Barnabas made to the brethren at Jerusalem, concerning those things which were agitated at Antioch; for the decision whereof they were come up to advise with them at Jerusalem.

*{sect of Pharisees}* See on Luke 14. 1. ἀπὸ τῆς αἱρέσεως, of the heresy of the Pharisees: these had given their names to Christ, but yet retained some relics of the antiquated ceremonies; Cerinthus and Ebion were condemned for the same, that they endeavored to mingle the ceremonies of the law, with the gospel of Christ: so while they would be Jews and Christians, they became neither: these being hypocrites, and therefore too intent to external rites, whereby they might appear more conspicuous to men, would pervert the truth of Christ, for their own vainglory’s sake.

**V. 6.** *{Apostles and Elders came together}* To these appertained the decision of all controversies of faith: these being informed of the present business, came together to consider what course to take therein; they being by the influence of the Holy Ghost, both qualified and authorized to be the most competent judges in such cases: possibly they discussed this question, the laity being present (as verse 12, 22.) but lest any should dream that the vulgar were admitted to judge of this controversy, the apostles and elders which were ministers are here named.

*{of this matter}* περὶ τοῦ λόγου τούτου *of this word*: it is a common Hebraism; word put for the business spoken of.

**V. 7.** *{much disputation}* συζήτησις, here used, signifies, among other things, a conferring of reasons, *pro* and *con*: for the finding out a truth, by stating the question and allegation of arguments thereto serving. See chap. 9. 29. and Mark 1. 27.

*{men and brethren}* Chap. 10. 20. and 11. 13.

*{a good while ago}* At our beginning to preach the gospel to the Gentiles. Peter in this great dispute it seems had hitherto held his peace, possibly lest he might seem to prescribe his fellow apostles; but now he opportunely takes occasion to declare his judgment in this matter, for the defense of the tenet of Paul and Barnabas, teaching that justification, and salvation, are by faith in Christ, without the deeds of the law (Rom. 3. 28.) Now this ἀφ’ ἡμερῶν ἀρχαίων, *jampridem, jam inde ab initio*, or as some translate, *ab antiquis diebus*; from the days of old, or *a good while ago*, relates to the calling of Cornelius and his friends, chap. 10. and the controversy following the same, chap. 11. 2, 3. &c.

*{should hear the word of the Gospel}* His purpose is here to show that Christ is sufficient to the believing Gentiles: and that there needed no legal ceremonies to perfect them: this God revealed to him, that they should receive the gospel and believe to salvation, not that they should be circumcised or charged with any legal ceremonies.

**V. 8.** *{giving them the Holy Ghost}* As if he said, when I had accordingly preached the gospel to them, presently God gave them the Holy Ghost, as he had done to us; when they were not circumcised, neither observed the ceremonial law. Seeing then God looks principally on the purity of the heart, and has sufficiently testified his reception of the Gentiles, by equaling the uncircumcision with us of the circumcision, in the same gifts and graces of the Holy Ghost, why should we force upon them who are begun in the Spirit, to be perfected in the flesh? See Gal. 3. 3.

**V. 9.** *{put no difference}* Touching adoption and eternal life: though heretofore he distinguished between Jews and Gentiles, by circumcision and other legal rites; yet now in Christ both are made one, *the middle wall of partition between us being broken down*, Ephes. 2. 14. See Mat. 27. 51.

*{purifying their hearts by faith}* Chap. 10. 43. 1 Cor. 1. 2. He opposes to legal purifications, the substance thereby figured, purification of the heart, and whole man, by the blood of Christ, and the spirit of regeneration, Heb. 9. 14.

**V. 10.** *{why tempt ye God}* He calls it a tempting of God to lay more upon men’s consciences, than either God has laid on them, or they are able to perform: as here by imposing circumcision; they would bind them to that which God commanded not the Gentiles, and lay a necessity of their keeping the whole law upon them, which none can do. Therefore to waive the means of salvation by the free grace of God in Christ, and to put salvation on terms so impossible, as is our perfect obedience to the whole law, is to tempt God.

*{to put a yoke upon the neck}* Mat. 23. 4. Gal. 5. 1.

*{were able to bear}* For every one that is circumcised, is bound to keep the whole law, Gal. 5. 3. under a severe curse, Gal. 3. 10. If he fail in anything, he is guilty of the breach of all, James 2. 10. This none of the fathers could bear; it were mere tempting of God to profess it, seeing Christ bare it for us, 1 Pet. 2. 24.

**V. 11.** *{we believe that through the grace, &c.}* The elect Jews were not saved by their works of the law (Gal. 3. 11. Rom. 3. 20.) but by grace in Christ; therefore he equals them herein with the Gentiles, and professes his hope of salvation, by no other means: seeing then we cannot obtain salvation by grace, except the yoke and curse of the law be taken off; neither can salvation be by the works of the law. And lastly, because they cannot be subject to the curse of the law who believe in Christ, therefore it is vain to go back from the fountain of salvation, unto those beggarly rudiments, which were but *a schoolmaster to bring us to Christ*, Gal. 3. 24.

**V. 12.** *{all the multitude kept silence}* As entertaining his discourse with a reverend silence: this shows the part which the people have to act in councils concerning controversies in religion, which is a modest silence, that they may hear and learn of those whom God has assigned to the ministry of his word.

*{and gave audience to Paul, &c.}* They relating what signs and wonders God had done by them among the Gentiles, confirm the sentence of Peter: for those their works concluded, that God was pleased with their ministration to the Gentiles.

**V. 13.** *{after that they held their peace}* James would not interrupt the discourse of Paul and Barnabas, but when they had spoken, then he also spoke: ’tis a Babel and confusion of tongues where many speaking at once, none are distinctly heard.

*{James}* Son of Alpheus. See on chap. 12. 19.

**V. 14.** *{Simeon}* That is, Peter, 2 Pet. 1. 1. It seems the Gr. said Σίμων, (as reading שימון instead of שמעון because they could not pronounce the letter ע) for so he is commonly named Simon, not Simeon. So *Irenaeus, l. 3. c. 12.*

*{for his name}* To bear his name, to acknowledge and worship him for their only God. Gre. ἐπὶ τῷ ὀνόματι αὐτοῦ. That is, as the Syriac gives it, לשמה *to his name*; which might be named his people. See v. 17. and Rom. 9. 25, 26.

**V. 15.** *{to this agree the words of the Prophets}* Concerning the calling of the Gentiles the prophets in many places spoke, though here be but one cited, v. 16. Note here the rule by which the apostles themselves proceeded, the written word of God; they equal not any human traditions with it, they take not on them to judge the Scriptures, much less to determine anything against them: for there is but one truth, and one Spirit of God, speaking both in the prophets and apostles.

**V. 16.** *{after this will I return}* Amos 9. 11. Gre. ἀναστρέψω, which word has divers significations; among which, sometimes it imports actively to return; so it may relate to God returning to show mercy to his people, who in his justice left them to themselves; as Rom. 1. 24. See Acts 17. 30. Sometimes passively, or neutrally, *retrorsum reverti cogo*. So if it relate to the people converting, the sense may be; that God would cause them to return from their sins, and calamities, of which the prophet there spoke, that they might under the Messias, be restored to their former glory and happiness: according to which Suidas says ἀναστροφὴ is called the very restoring, or returning into one’s former place whence he was driven; *alicujus in pristinum statum restitutio*, says Aemilius Portus; a restoring one to his ancient state, which was bestowed on Israel in Christ. Luke cites not the words of the prophet, but so varies them as to the same sense more clearly interpreted concerning the repair of Israel by Christ.

*{tabernacle of David}* Meaning the *church of God* to be repaired, and restored by the Messias. Some (says Oecumenius) understand it concerning the resurrection of Christ, whom God the Father raised, and us in him: for *no otherwise could our nature be repaired and raised up except by his hand who first made us*—

**V. 17.** *{that the residue of men might seek after the Lord}* Respecting the general sentence of the prophets concerning the calling of the Gentiles, he speaks the same sense with the prophet Amos 9. 12. in words somewhat varying, instancing first in the residue of Edom: but James here more generally, in the residue of men; that is, as he expresses, *all the Gentiles*, as the prophet also says כל־הגוים *col hagg[?]im*§2, *all the nations upon whom my Name is called*.

*{who doth all these things}* Do not think much that God calls the Gentiles, whom you count unclean, *God hath done it*, who does all things well. See Psal. 118. 23. Mat. 21. 42.

**V. 18.** *{known unto God are all his works}* Thus he strengthens weak minds against suspicion of novelty: indeed this calling of the Gentiles is sudden to men; but God knew this from the beginning, determining in eternity whatsoever he would bring to pass in time: he it is, who both revealed these things to the prophets, and fulfilled the same in the appointed time.

**V. 19.** *{my sentence is, that we trouble not them}* Out of Scripture he concludes with Peter, that the yoke of the law was not to be put upon the Gentiles; it had been a wretched perplexity to their consciences to put them vainly to seek any other means of justification, than faith in Christ.

**V. 20.** *{pollutions}* 1 Cor. 8. 1. &c. Pollutions of idols, are things polluted by idols to whom they were offered.

*{of idols}* Because some thought it no offense to be present at the idols’ temples, and there to feast with idolaters, which the apostle says, is to *drink the cup of the devils*, 1 Cor. 10. 21. The Syriac gives it, *from the pollution of things offered*, *a vanitatibus idolorum*, the interpreter of Irenaeus, *l. 3. c. 12.* See on chap. 14. 15.

*{and from blood}* He knows that these ceremonies to which they were accustomed many years, could not without great distraction be suddenly abolished, until they could better learn their liberty in Christ. Therefore though they were dead in Christ’s passion, no more to be revived (as Gal. 5.) yet they did not presently drag them out of doors, but gave them time, as it were, for a decent burial of them.

**V. 21.** *{for Moses of old time}* He signifies hereby that the Jews could not so suddenly bear the abolition of the legal ceremonies, to which they had been long accustomed by the continual hearing of the law read in every synagogue; and that therefore they ought to purchase unity with a charitable compliance, until they could be more fully informed concerning Christian liberty.

**V. 22.** *{then pleased it the Apostles, &c.}* Here was a holy, and no less happy unity: here neither John, Andrew, or any of the apostles, contradicted; but, as the matter was referred, and judged, so all rested satisfied, and the people modestly consented. So Clemens Romanus (who lived in this time, Phil. 4. 3.) speaking of ordinations by the apostles, and their successors, intimates that it was done συνευδοκησάσης τῆς ἐκκλησίας πάσης *the whole church thereto willingly consenting*.

*{to send chosen men}* That the adversaries at Antioch might not calumniate Paul and Barnabas, as dissenters from the rest of the apostles, they send with them Barsabas and Silas, chief among the brethren at Jerusalem, to testify that they were of the same judgment with Paul and Barnabas.

**V. 23.** *{and wrote letters, &c.}* This they did, that by the testimony of the commissioners, and the letters of the council the false apostles might be silenced, and the believers confirmed in the truth and unity of their profession.

**V. 24.** *{which went from us}* *They went out from us, they were not of us*: see 1 John 2. 19. It is a mischievous artifice of Satan, to divide Christians: he knows where there is holy unity, there God is, and therefore he can never prevail: therefore he labors to have a party in us, against us: he can never so dangerously wound religion, as by the seeming religious, therefore raises he up men among ourselves to speak perverse things. See chap. 20. 30.

*{subverting your souls}* ἀνασκευάζοντες, it comes from the word among other things signifying to demolish and cast down: fitly here used, seeing that the false apostles by their superstructions, or laying other foundations for salvation, besides the free mercy of God in Christ, destroyed souls: he builds to ruin, who builds his salvation on any means, but the sole merits of Christ; *other foundation can no man lay, than that which is laid, which is Christ Jesus*. See 1 Cor. 3. 11, 12. Ephes. 2. 20. Gal. 1. 8. He subverts and casts down, who builds not on Christ alone.

*{to whom we gave no such command}* Thus pull they off the vizards of those pretenders to the authority of the apostles, and possibly commission from them so to teach; whereas indeed they taught that which razed the foundation of the truth of the gospel: for the righteousness of Christ, and the righteousness of the law, grace, and merit, legal ceremonies intimating an expectation of Christ to come, and Christian liberty, declaring and professing a discharge from the duties of the ceremonial law, and the curse of the moral, by Christ already come, are utterly incompatible. See Rom. 9. 30, 31. Rom. 10. 3, 5, 6. Rom. 4. 4, 5. Gal. 2. 2. & 5. 2, 3. Therefore the apostles in this first council, thus seriously disavow that doctrine which teaches to mingle the legal ceremonies, and the observation thereof with the gospel.

**V. 25.** *{it seemed good unto us, &c.}* All the true members of Christ agree in the fundamentals of religion (though there sometimes fall out varieties of judgments and differences in things subordinate) because the Spirit of God is ever with them, uniting them, if not with infallibility, as here, yet with a sufficient measure of grace: compare verse 28, 39. and see Mat. 28. 20.

*{to send chosen men}* Such as are fit to interpose, and be employed in this so weighty a business.

*{with our beloved Barnabas and Paul}* This they oppose to the calumnies of those false apostles whereby they vilified Paul and Barnabas: and for their confirmation who were stumbled thereat; hereby they declare their consent with Paul and Barnabas, commending them for their singular zeal in that they had ventured their lives for the truth and testimony of the Lord Jesus; daring and suffering for the name of Christ is commendable. See 1 Pet. 4. 14, 16. It is the cause, not the punishment which makes the martyr.

**V. 27.** *{We have therefore sent Judas and Silas}* They express their names, to acquaint the church of Antioch with them. See on verse 22. This Silas proved an excellent assistant to Paul in preaching the gospel, and persecutions for the same endured, verse 40.

**V. 28.** *{It seemed good to the Holy Ghost, &c.}* Herein they render a reason of their decree concerning the causes of justification; because so it pleased the Holy Ghost, whose truth and guidance they followed. This they say, lest the Antiochians should think this decree human, they cited their authority, as if they had said, we have determined as we were guided by the Spirit of God: in that they say (*and to us*) appears the authority of the faithful ministers in controversies of faith. Rom. 1. 5. Mal. 2. 7.

*{necessary things}* *Necessary* is not here to be referred to the ceremonies themselves here mentioned; but to the present occasion and persons, whose unity and salvation was necessarily to be advanced by all means: it was therefore an accidental and temporary necessity: laws concerning things simply necessary, ought to be perpetual; so was not this: for when this tumultuous dissension was over, and the brethren better informed, Paul shows that nothing is unclean, which God has made for man’s use. See 1 Cor. 8. 8. & 10. 19. 1 Tim. 4. 4. Tit. 1. 15.

**V. 29.** *{that they abstain from meats, &c.}* This did they, that they might hold the unity in the faith of Christ, between the believing Jews and new converted Gentiles: in things of their own nature indifferent (such as were the three first things here prohibited for the time) we ought to hold charity, and by no means to break the sacred band of unity, in things which God neither commands nor forbids, we ought to have a tender care, first of the church’s unity, and next of their consciences, who are infirm brethren. See Col. 3. 14. 1 Tim. 1. 5. Rom. 15. 2. Rom. 14. 1. &c.

*{and from fornication}* This part of the moral law (which is indispensable, and continually, and universally binding) is here inserted for the Gentiles’ sake, who before their conversion were shamelessly given to fornication, and all uncleanness of lust.

**V. 30.** *{when they were dismissed}* From the council at Jerusalem.

*{gathered the multitude}* Though the determination in controversies of faith, be properly in men of learning and integrity of life, yet all having equal interest in the common salvation, there was good reason why the whole multitude of believers should be acquainted with (and their consent had to) all the proceedings and the issue thereof.

**V. 31.** *{they rejoiced, &c.}* As the truly religious cannot choose but be grieved at the divisions of that body, whereof they are members; so must it be great joy to see all sweetly composed to God’s honor: nor could there be any more comfortable news, than their assurance by the testimony of the Holy Ghost, and the apostles of Christ, that they are freely justified by faith in him, without the works of the law; for therein men have peace with God. See Rom. 5. 1.

*{consolation}* Or *exhortation*.

**V. 32.** *{being Prophets also}* See 1 Cor. 14. 29, 32. & 12. 18. Eph. 4. 11. that is, men of singular gifts in teaching, and interpreting Scriptures. See 1 Cor. 14. 3.

**V. 33.** *{let go in peace}* With good love and blessing of the brethren.

**V. 34.** *{it pleased Silas to abide}* They were dismissed by the church at Antioch, at their pleasure to depart, and return to the apostles at Jerusalem, but Silas to do that church more service, abode there a longer time.

**V. 35.** *{with many others also}* Without emulation they united in the work of Christ, as they who truly zealed God’s honor, not their own: this shows their happy consent in preaching the gospel, and that in the absence of teachers, the church was supplied with other: so that Paul and Barnabas resolving to visit other places, left not that destitute.

**V. 36.** *{let us go again and visit, &c.}* Here was Paul’s diligence, not only to plant, but also to water: it was not enough once to preach the gospel to them, but in regard of men’s frailties, it ought to be cherished and fomented with new exhortations and consolations: the rather, because Satan (the envious man) is ever ready to cast in his tares, wheresoever the servants of God have sowed the good wheat. See Mat. 13.

**V. 37.** *{to take with them John}* Whose surname was Mark, chap. 12. 25. See chap. 13. 13.

**V. 38.** *{and went not with them to the work}* That is, to that ministration assigned them, chap. 13. 2. It seems that it was no small scandal that he deserted them at Pamphilia, that Paul so strictly stood upon it, that he would not have him admitted into the society of this present visitation of the churches: so dangerous a thing it is for him to look back, that has once put his hand to the plow. See Luke 9. 62.

**V. 39.** *{the contention was so sharp}* Such as prevailed to embittering their minds, so that Barnabas went away with John to his own country Cyprus, Chapter 4. So mischievously vigilant is Satan to take all occasions of dividing good men: he is contented and well pleased with the confederacies and combinations of the wicked, for that is the strength of his kingdom; he envies only the unity of the religious: the more cautious ought good men to be, lest they be embarked in contentions.

**V. 40.** *{Paul chose Silas}* Though they differed about John, v. 38, 39. yet neither forsook his office and ministry: this storm divided them in their persons, not in their faith: and by the providence of God to the church’s good, for their confirmation and further edification in the faith of Christ.

**V. 41.** *{confirming the churches}* Bringing to their minds that which they had formerly taught, and further instructing them, as need required. Thus the invincible goodness of God brings good out of evil: (their division was evil, but the edifying of the churches) good.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1.** *{Derbe and Lystra}* Chap. 14. 6.

*{named Timotheus}* Rom. 16. 21. Phil. 2. 19. 1 Thes. 3. 2. to him the apostles wrote two epistles.

*{certain woman}* See 2 Tim. 1. 5. She is named Eunice, daughter of Lois.

*{a Greek}* A Gentile proselyte, without having received circumcision, chap. 14. 1.

**V. 2.** *{Iconium}* chap. 14. 1.

**V. 3.** *{circumcised him}* That he might make the better use of him in his ministry among the Jews, who abhorred the uncircumcision: so far complied Paul with the weak to win them to Christ. See 1 Cor. 9. 20, 21. His mother was a Jew, so on that part he belonged to the circumcision.

**V. 4.** *{that were ordained}* In the council at Jerusalem, whereof see chap. 15. 28.

**V. 5.** *{increased in number}* This shows the double effect of Paul’s visiting those churches: 1. in their confirmation who believed before. 2. in new accessions of many others unto the faith.

**V. 6.** *{Phrygia}* A country of the lesser Asia, which Asia is now called Anatolia, or Natolia, *Atlas Min. G. Mercator*.

*{Galatia}* A country of the same, joining to Phrygia; to this church Paul wrote his epistle. See in chap. 18. 23.

*{forbidden of the Holy Ghost}* By a secret revelation: God chooses not only men, but also appoints time and place, when and where he will have his word preached.

*{in Asia}* In Asia the less.

**V. 7.** *{to Mysia}* A country by the Hellespont bordering on Troas: mentioned *Plin. lib. 5. cap. 30.*

*{Bithynia}* A country in Asia near Troas.

*{but the spirit suffered him not}* *Non negata illis populi gratia, sed retardata*, Prosper *de vocat. Gen. lib. 2. cap. 3.* *his grace not being denied that people*, but only retarded by a secret judgment, says the same, *contr. Collat. c. 25.*

**V. 8.** *{Troas}* A city of Asia, called also Alexandria, and Antigonia, *Plin. l. 5. c. 30.* See on verse 11.

**V. 9.** *{a man of Macedonia}* That is, an angel in man’s shape: or the appearance of a man; for it was a vision, Macedonia was a province of Greece.

**V. 10.** *{immediately we endeavoured}* This expresses their ready mind to obey God, and to follow his calling. See Gen. 12. 4. & 22. 3.

*{assuredly gathering}* By Paul’s vision, and those things which happened unto them. See chap. 9. 22.

**V. 11.** *{loosing from Troas}* Going from thence: setting sail thence, for it was a maritime town, *Jerom de loc. Heb. Act.*

*{Samothracia}* An isle near Thrace.

*{Neapolis}* A city and part of Macedonia.

**V. 12.** *{to Philippi}* A chief or first city, and part of Macedonia.

*{and a colony}* A Roman colony: whose inhabitants came from Rome to dwell there.

**V. 13.** *{prayer was wont to be made}* It appears not who they were, who here prayed; but it is probable, that the Jews and proselytes there prayed for fear of the infidels, not daring so to do elsewhere; and here it seems was a private oratory or chapel: for so the word προσευχὴ here used, does also here signify, as well as the action of prayer there performed: and so the Syriac expresses it, *we went out of the city by the river side, because there was to be seen*, בית צלותא *an house of prayer*.

**V. 14.** *{of the city of Thyatira}* Mentioned also, *Plin. lib. 5. cap. 30.* a city of Asia, *Jerom de loc. Heb. Act.* Here dwelt Lydia, that devout servant of God, of this city. See Revel. 1. 11. & 2. 18. 24.

*{whose heart the Lord}* God who elected her to salvation, made the means thereto effectual, by preparing her heart and affections to receive the gospel: where God, who has the key of David opens not, there the gospel, (though never so powerfully preached) is hid, and nothing affects the stupid drowsy hearer: see 2 Cor. 4. 4. See here chap. 13. 48.

**V. 15.** *{and her household}* God’s covenant is with us and our children, Gen. 17. 9, 10. and therefore the seal thereof ought so to be: whole families were baptized, whereof children were commonly a part: if any say there were no children in Lydia’s, and the jailer’s household, or other like families, we require their proof: if any desire our proof (that children ought to be baptized, who are born of believing parents) by some express place of Scripture; we that are in 1600 years’ possession and prescription, more reasonably, require them to prove by any express place of Scripture that children were not baptized by the apostles (when they baptized households, and nations, as they were commanded, Mat. 28. 19.) or ought not to be, by us.

**V. 16.** *{a spirit}* This was the spirit of Satan, who would testify the truth, that he might discredit the truth of the gospel, and bring it into some suspect. See Mark 1. 25. 34.

*{of divination}* Πνεῦμα Πύθωνος: this is an epithet of Apollo, who was wont to give answers to them that inquired. He was named Pythius, which Deut. 11. 18. is called אוב *ob*; so 1 Sam. 28. 7. See Hen. Ainsworth on Deut. 18. 11.

**V. 17.** *{these are the servants of the most High}* When he speaks a lie he speaks of his own; here he spoke truth, that he might make the hearers jealous of the truth of that which so impure a spirit professed: some do good in show, that they may thereby be enabled to do real mischief. See Phil. 1. 18. The more cautious must we be, lest we be seduced under shows of truth and sanctity: Satan can transform his ministers into angels of light. 2 Cor. 11.

**V. 18.** *{I command thee in the name, &c.}* This spoke he by the motion of the Holy Ghost informing him both of the dangerous effects of Satan’s increasing rage, and also of the power he would give him to cast out Satan. See Mark 1. 34. Luke 4. 41. Mark 16. 17.

**V. 19.** *{hope of their gain was gone}* It seems she used to receive a price of her divination and telling things to come.

*{marketplace}* Or *court*.

**V. 20.** *{to the magistrates}* Or *principal governors of the Roman colony*.

*{being Jews do exceedingly trouble}* The truth of the gospel ever seems troublesome to the wicked in whom Satan reigns, because it overthrows him and his kingdom: they mention their being Jews, because their name was now odious to the Romans.

**V. 21.** *{Customs which are not lawful}* ἔθη, *rites or manners*: in this they discover, that whereas any error, or impious practice in their superstitions, and abominations was tolerated, if not favored among them, only truth and sanctity were despicable: they were Romans, see verse 12.

**V. 22.** *{the multitude rose up together}* Any pretense enrages the vulgar against the truth of Christ. See Revel. 12. 15.

*{rent off their clothes}* That is, rent Paul’s and Silas’ clothes.

*{commanded to beat them}* As malefactors: this was Satan’s fury against the ministers of Christ. See Revel. 2. 10. 2 Cor. 11. 25. 1 Thes. 2. 2.

**V. 23.** *{when they had laid many stripes upon them}* See 2 Cor. 11. 24.

**V. 24.** *{made their feet fast in the stocks}* ἀσφαλίζω, properly signifies to confirm or strengthen. Budaeus gives it *munio: protego*, &c. and *idoneis sponsoribus cavere*: which we say, *to take good security*; and so ἀσφάλεια, is *security*, they put them here εἰς τὸ ξύλον, *into the wood*: Latin *numella: machina lignea, in qua caput & collum committebantur*, Shindler. *Penteg.* in הפך *stocks* or *pillory*, into which prisoners’ feet or heads were put, (to secure their keepers) that they should not make any escape: such has been the world’s entertainment of the saints. See Gen. 39. 20. Psalm 105. 18. Mark 6. 17. 1 Kings 22. 27. Jerem. 20. 2.

**V. 25.** *{prayed and sang praises unto God}* Satan’s purpose was to make them blaspheme; or to desert or suspect their cause and calling into Macedon as inauspicious: but the more they are afflicted the more they rejoice and praise the Lord. See chap. 5. 41. Rom. 5. 3. Colos. 1. 11. 1 Pet. 4. 13. *why do the heathens rage, and the people imagine a vain thing?* Psal. 2. 1. &c. There is nothing more vain than violence, or counsel against the truth of Christ: the more you labor to suppress it, the more it shines, *florescit vulnere*, it flourishes in its wound.

*{and the prisoners heard them}* They did not pray as the Pharisees, ambitious of the praises of men, but to testify the confidence they had in a good cause, and in a good conscience.

**V. 26.** *{suddenly there was a great earthquake, &c.}* Thus God assured them that their prayers were heard. See Chap. 4. 31. He could without an earthquake have delivered them, as Chap. 12. 10. but thus the Lord of the creatures would manifest his power and presence, lest any should think that they had by some endeavors attempted to break prison, or that it had been only some fortuitous escape: and thus he would be manifest to posterities, that the sighing of his prisoners comes before him, who can at his pleasure deliver those who are appointed to die. See Psalm 34. 16, 19. Psalm 145. 18.

**V. 27.** *{would have killed himself}* Fearing that he must needs suffer for the escape of his prisoners; for (had he been informed of all that now happened) to a man unacquainted with the power and providence of God, it might seem a ridiculous plea, to say, that the prison doors opened themselves, and that all his prisoners’ bands fell off: here we may see the miserable resolutions and comforts of the unregenerate in afflictions.

**V. 28.** *{Do thyself no harm, &c.}* Hereby it may appear that these servants of God were not so solicitous for their own lives and liberties, as for the propagation of the gospel, and men’s salvation: otherwise being loose, and the doors all open, they could have been silent; and they might easily have escaped: but they being informed by the spirit that God therefore awaked the keeper, that he might be converted and saved; Paul and Silas start not, neither tempted they God, or lost the use of the miracle by their stay; it being the main end of all this; that God might appear on the part of that religion, which the world then so much condemned, and that this keeper and his family might be saved.

**V. 29.** *{called for a light}* Possibly to be brought into the place where the prisoners were; for prisons usually have light burning in some of their watches all night; and thereby he might see, or find that the prison doors were open, verse 27.

*{he sprang in}* εἰσεπήδησε, *he leaped in* (or came speedily in among them, as one that leaped) to see if the prisoners were all there. So εἰσπηδάω is interpreted, *saltu irruo*, &c. εἰσεπήδησεν εἰς τὴν οἰκίαν, Demosth. He leaped or rushed into the house.

*{came trembling and fell down, &c.}* He that a little before confidently put their feet into the stocks, now comes trembling to their feet: mark how behooveful it is for some men to be humbled with fear and affliction: how securely before this hour would he have despised all that ever Paul and Silas could have said to him? This short fear has made him docile: you may perceive the reason why God so often exercises his dear children with fears: but why (may some say) did not Paul and Silas reprove him, and take him up from their feet, as Peter did Cornelius, chap. 10. 26. because they knew this man did it not to adore them, but for present fear of God’s judgments to humble himself: and indeed it was a Roman fashion of begging pardon to fall at his feet of whom they entreated it: had here been anything prejudicial to God’s honor, Paul would not have forgotten his former zeal among the Lystrians, chapter 14. 14. &c.

**V. 30.** *{and brought them out}* Out of the inner prison, verse 24. into some more convenient room; possibly into his own lodgings, as is after expressed, verse 34.

*{What must I do to be saved?}* Some being startled with fear and sense of afflictions return to their former purposes more obstinately. See Exod. 7. 3. Mat. 28. 5, 11, 15. but he so profited by his fear, that he desired to be informed, what he must do to be saved, as Chapter 2. 37. This was God’s free mercy, (Rom. 9. 15.) for Paul had not yet preached Christ unto him since his imprisonment.

**V. 31.** *{believe in the Lord Jesus Christ}* This brief answer contains the sum of the gospel offering us salvation, and all blessedness in Christ apprehended by faith.

**V. 32.** *{and they spake unto him the word of God, &c.}* More largely expounding that which they proposed in brief.

*{to all that were in the house}* So proved it an happy affliction to those prisoners also, who by this means came to faith and salvation.

**V. 33.** *{took them in the same hour of the night}* See what admirable changes God can suddenly work: but now this keeper would have destroyed himself; presently he humbly begs information how he might be saved; which having received; he delays not to show his thankfulness. See also Gen. 40. 23. and 41. 9. &c. Esther 6. 1, 2, 3. Faith cannot be unfruitful of good works.

*{was baptized, he and all his}* See on verse 15. This care of his family is exemplary. See Chap. 10. 7, 24, 48. See on verse 15.

**V. 34.** *{he had brought them into his house}* Into his own lodgings, where himself and family kept: see the admirable change of him and his in one night, now he observes not the governor’s charge concerning the close and safe imprisonment of Paul and Silas, but uses them with all humanity: now he washes their stripes, or wounds received by those stripes. They that have true faith, and assurance in their interest in Christ, will obey God in being charitable, rather than men who command them to be cruel.

*{he set meat before them}* τράπεζαν, a table: τράπεζα is here put to signify a table furnished with meat (as among many Greek authors) the 70. use it. Dan. 1. 13, 15. τῶν ἐσθιόντων τράπεζαν—*that eat the portion of the kings meat*, Psalm 78. 20. *can he give bread also, can he provide* τράπεζαν τῷ λαῷ αὐτοῦ, *a table for his people*, Hebrew שאר *flesh for his people*. So Psalm 23. 5. Psalm 69. 22. &c. Paul and Silas were fasting, and not a little tired with watching, stripes, and injurious usage; therefore, though about midnight, the converted keeper set before them such food as he had ready in his house, to refresh them: no hour is unseasonable to feed the hungry or to do works of charity, thus faith works by love and charity. See Gal. 5. 6.

*{and rejoiced believing, &c.}* They *rejoiced in the faith of God*, the Syriac has it: it is true which Ambrose says, *l. 5. ep. 18. nullus major dolor quam is qui peccati mucrone vulnerat conscientiam*, *there is no greater sorrow, than that which with the sword of sin wounds the conscience*; therefore the sinner feeling a sweet calm within him, in peace of conscience by his justification by faith, (Rom. 5. 1.) must needs rejoice in spirit: here was now no exaction of fees, imperious and cruel usage of the prisoners, after the keeper once believed in Christ, but the true fruits of charity, and so true joy to him and his whole family what joy or peace of consciences others are like to have at last, may not obscurely be gathered from Christ’s words, Matthew 25. 43, 45. who speaking of the form of judgment to come, among other arguments of condemnation recites this, *I was sick, and in prison, and ye visited me not; in as much as ye did it not to one of the least of these, ye did it not to me*: where note, first that Christ takes some things on his own account, as done to his own person, which are done to his elect, secondly, that if neglecting to visit, relieve, and comfort the imprisoned, be a just cause of damnation; then most sad and fearful must be the condition of those keepers, who as much as they could, have taken away the relief and comfort of their prisoners, by unreasonable exactions, and hard usage of them. But they have warrant and custom for that they do: that indeed might possibly secure them from men here, but who can give them indemnity from the sentence of Christ already foretold them? Does any of them so much love his own soul, as to cry with this happy convert, verse 30. *what must I do to be saved?* you see what follows, verse 31. but lest your fruitless faith should at last fail you, go learn of Zache, Luke 19. 8. to restore the damage, that Jesus may say to your conscience, *This day is salvation come to this house.*

**V. 35.** *{and when it was day}* Remembering, and possibly being terrified with the earthquake.

*{sent the serjeants}* Syr. *duces gestantes virgas*, according to the Greek, ῥαβδοῦχοι: so called of the staves or rods which they carried. Some say that these were messengers, known by their staff of office which they were to carry, as apparitors to cite, and bear out process of judges and magistrates: and that the *Lictors* were distinguished from these, by the axes and rods which they carried.

**V. 36.** *{Keeper of the prison told this saying}* Being glad for their enlargement, he cheerfully brought them news of this new clemency.

**V. 37.** *{but Paul said unto them}* That is, unto the sergeants.

*{uncondemned}* ἀκατακρίτους as they say, *indictâ causâ*, without cause alleged, without any legal trial, or hearing of us: Syr. *insontes*, guiltless men.

*{being Romans}* Not because he was born at Rome, or of Roman parents (he was an Hebrew, born at Tarsus) but because the citizens thereof were by their privilege freemen of Rome; and so by their Valerian law might not be bound, and by their Sempronian law, they might not be beaten or executed without the Romans’ consent: thus Paul pleaded his privilege for the defense of the gospel, lest the word of God should be despised with their persons.

**V. 28.**† *{they feared when}* Because the punishment was great for any man that injured a citizen or freeman of Rome.

**V. 39.** *{and they came and besought them}* This can God do for all his servants, who have been disgracefully and despitefully used: in this entreating and leading them out of prison, they sufficiently confess their precipitate fury in abusing them who were innocent: this was for fear of the Romans, lest they should punish their city, or them for violating their privileges; therefore they desire them to depart from them.

**V. 40.** *{and entered into the house of Lydia}* Verse 14. doubtless to confirm them in the faith which they had preached to them, and probably they would have stayed longer, but that the imperious and armed entreaty of the wicked magistrates awed them: Lydia and hers hearing of their marvelous deliverance, could not but be comforted, and confirmed in the truth: and though Paul now left them, yet he after visited the Philippians with an epistle to contain and confirm them in the faith.

## CHAPTER 17

**Verse 1.** *{Amphipolis}* A city of Macedonia, now called Chrysopolis: there was another of the same name in Syria, says Jerom *de loc. Hebr. Act.*

*{Apollonia}* Another city in Macedonia: there is also another of the same name in Africa, which was called Pentapolis. Jerom *q. supr.*

*{Thessalonica}* A chief city of Macedonia: so called of Philip’s victory over the Thessalians: to the church hereof the apostle wrote two epistles.

**V. 2.** *{as his manner was}* He was constant in preaching the truth of Christ, and the adversaries were constant in opposing it: by that which men use to do, you may know to which party they belong.

*{went in unto them}* Into their synagogue: he was none of them who crept into houses, to lead captive silly women, 2 Tim. 3. 2. &c. truth seeks no corners, the good seed is sowed in the daytime, but the tares while men sleep, Mat. 10. 25. truth knows itself a stranger on earth; and that it easily finds enemies; the kindred, seat, hope, favor and dignity thereof are in heaven: only it rejoices in this one thing; as Tertullian *Apol. cap. 1.* says, *ne ignorata damnetur*, *lest it be condemned unknown*: therefore *edito gaudet & aperto*, *it joys to be publicly seen*: *dum conventicula sibi diversa constituunt, veritatis caput, atque originem reliquerunt*, Cyprian *de simpl. praelat.* *while men appoint them divers conventicles, they left the author and fountain of truth.*

*{out of the Scriptures}* This shows whence all sound knowledge of God and religion must be derived: in things human, human reasons have place; but in matters of religion, only the word of God (without traditions of men) must determine so as that in that we must rest. Faith can be grounded on nothing but infallible truth; and that can be found only in the word of God, in which there can be no falsehood, because God, the author thereof, 2 Tim. 3. 16. cannot lie, 1 Tit. 1. 1. Well said Ambrose in his answer to Valentinian the emperor, *lib. 5. ep. 31. Caeli mysterium doceat me Deus ipse qui condidit, non homo qui seipsum ignoravit: cui magis de Deo, quam Deo credam?* *let God himself teach me the mystery of heaven, who made heaven, not man who knows not himself; concerning God, whom shall I rather believe than God himself?*

**V. 3.** *{opening and alleging}* That is, as the Syriac has it, *expounding and showing*: indeed παρατιθέμενος, imports a making things as manifest, as that which is set before our eyes. The sum of his sermon was, that Christ for our redemption ought to die, and rise again (Rom. 4. 25.) and that this Jesus (whom Paul preached) is indeed the Christ and long expected Messias.

**V. 4.** *{consorted with Paul}* προσεκληρώθησαν, as it were chosen by lot, they joined: it imports a near and firm conjunction, the Syriac interprets it, *they adhered to Paul and Silas*: so that hence it appears that their reasoning in the Jews’ synagogue was not without good effect: *some of the Jews believed*, and many of the *Grecians*, this word imports not a cold assent to the truth which they heard, but also a serious affection to the same, and the Christian party that they might be further conformed and confirmed: a wavering suspense, and lukewarm neutrality of professors, much disadvantages the cause of religion.

*{devout Greeks}* Chap. 11. 20.

**V. 5.** *{which believed not}* See Chap. 14. 2. because they believed not the gospel, they envied the prosperity and propagation thereof; and were moved with zeal to defend the ceremonial rights, in which they had been brought up: the word (ζηλόω) here used, signifies sometimes zeal to good, sometimes to evil, as here; so Acts 7. 9. that is, envy.

*{certain lewd fellows}* τῶν ἀγοραίων τινὰς, &c. *idle fellows*, who having no employment at home, use to walk lazily up and down markets and public places; the dregs of cities, mercenary, worthless burdens of the earth; commonly watching opportunities of moving sedition, in hope of finding some advantages in change.

*{the house of Jason}* Who was Paul and Silas’ host, whom Paul mentions, Rom. 16. 21.

*{sought to bring them out}* To bring out Paul and Silas, that they might stone them.

**V. 6.** *{they drew Jason}* In an illegal and tumultuous way violently pulled him out of his house, as a delinquent; as if hospitality, and harboring the servants of Christ had been a sin.

*{and certain brethren}* Some other believers. See verse 4. who it seemed had but newly embraced the gospel among them.

*{these have turned the world upside down}* Or, *who have stirred up the world to insurrection and sedition*: a grievous crime of Christians, had it been true; but being false, the greater was the accusers’ sin, who charged them with such untruth: so odious is sin, that the very wicked are ashamed of it, which is the reason why they commonly call others by names proper to themselves, that they may transmit over, or charge their sins upon others. (See chap. 24. 5. Genes. 3. 12, 13. 1 Sam. 15. 15, 21. 1 Kings 18. 17.) they would, the guilt also, if they could; so in the primitive church, whatever calamity came upon the people, or commonweal, it was presently imputed to the Christians.

*{are come hither also}* Meaning, they are ill-affected to the state, and trouble this city as they have done others.

**V. 7.** *{whom Jason hath received}* He has harbored them: see on verse 6.

*{contrary to the decrees of Caesar}* An odious crime, but impudently cast on Christians: Paul and Silas endeavored to advance the spiritual kingdom of Christ, without any injury to the Roman empire: which the Jews well knew, but cast this aspersion upon them, only to render them odious to the people: therefore they pretended Caesar’s right, that they might abuse the decrees of Caesar, to the destruction of God’s servants: the ancient Christians were no disturbers of states: *rogamus Auguste, non pugnamus*, said Ambrose *lib. 5. epist. 23. ad Marcellin.* *we entreat, O Caesar, we fight not*; therefore Theodosius had rather to bind men to him, *religione quam timore*, *with religion rather than fear*, Ambros. *de exi. Theodos. orat.* the Christian (said Tertullian, *ad scap. cap. 2.*) *is no man’s enemy, much less the Emperor’s, whom knowing to be appointed of God, he must needs, love, reverence, honor and wish safe*—therefore (says the same), *we honor the Emperor so far as it is lawful for us, and expedient for him*, *ut hominem à Deo secundum—& solo Deo minorem* *as a man next unto God—and less than God only,* they prayed for the emperors—*long life, prosperous reign, valiant arms, faithful Senate, or Council, good people, and a quiet world*: *ib. Apol. cap. 30.* yet these men could never be secure from calumny, as if they had been enemies to peace.

**V. 8.** *{They troubled the people, &c.}* This people not knowing the business, and hearing something pretended against the Roman state (having formerly smarted under their power) were troubled, for fear they might again deal severely with them as disturbers of peace; and probably the Roman commanders there being, might also be troubled at the appearance, or fear of some insurrection.

**V. 9.** *{When they had taken security of Jason}* λαβόντες τὸ ἱκανὸν, Syr. *and had taken sureties*: that is, bail, for their appearance. So Budaeus interprets it, *cum satis accepissent ab Jasone, & ab aliis judicio sistendi sponsione*, &c. here they being partly satisfied with their answer (as is most probable) and a legal security taken, they appease the people.

**V. 10.** *{Berea}* Another city of Macedonia: hither they sent Paul and Silas, that they might keep them from the rage of the Jews, and reserve them to the greater good of the church.

*{went into the Synagogue}* Here was his invincible love toward the Jews: after so many violent outrages of theirs against his life, he ceases not to venture again for their salvation. See Rom. 9. 2, 3.

**V. 11.** *{These were more noble}* That is, than the Thessalonian Jews, for this τῶν ἐν Θεσσαλονίκῃ, is elliptical: *than those (Jews) in Thessalonica*: which their daily searching of the Scriptures confirms, the Jews had the writings of the prophets, but rarely the Gentiles before their conversion: see verse 13.

*{they received the word}* That is, the gospel of Christ preached unto them, which they of Thessalonica rejected and persecuted, but these cheerfully and readily embraced: herein they were more excellent than others. See 1 Thes. 2. 13.

*{searched the Scriptures daily, &c.}* For they testify of Christ. John 5. 39. there Christ is learned; there shines an heavenly light, which like that eastern star, (Mat. 2. 2. &c.) leads the wise men to Christ: these Bereans’ diligence, is exemplary to us.

**V. 12.** *{Therefore many of them believed}* By the testimony of Scriptures, and example of other believers, the Spirit of God working thereon.

**V. 13.** *{When the Jews of Thessalonica, &c.}* These unbelievers being of restless and implacable malice, envied those who believed, therefore came they over to Berea, to hinder the preaching of the gospel there, by stirring up new troubles. See 2 Thes. 1. 5. &c.

**V. 14.** *{to go as it were to sea}* To take shipping as if he meant to go quite away out of those parts; which they did, that the Jews might give over their pursuit.

*{but Silas and Timotheus abode, &c.}* They being not so much known nor maliced as Paul, stayed a little while, further to edify the believers there.

**V. 15.** *{to Athens}* A city and famous university of Greece: so did the providence of God bring to pass, that Satan’s malicious endeavors to suppress the gospel, caused it more to spread abroad; as here it did, at Athens, the Greece of Greece. See *Atlas. min. G. Mercat. in Achaia.*

**V. 16.** *{His spirit was stirred in him}* παρωξύνετο—*was embittered in him*. So chap. 15. 39. ἐγένετο οὖν παροξυσμὸς—διηγείρετο *incitabatur*, Oecumen. *in loc.*—*was stirred up*. So bitterness of Spirit moved him, that he could not bear it, but broke out into reproof.

**V. 17.** *{with the devout persons}* With the proselytes. Syr. *with them that feared God.* See chap. 13. 43.

**V. 18.** *{Epicureans}* A sect of philosophers, as also were the Stoics, of the errors of the Epicureans, See Epiphan. *haeres. 6. Gent. haeres. ord. 8.* of the Stoics, see Epiphan. *haeres. 5.* D. Laert. *lib. 10. ib. lib. 7. Zenon*,

**V. 19.** *{Areopagus}* Or *Mars-hill*, it was the highest court in Athens, a court where the Judges, called *Areopagites*, sat concerning the highest titles and causes, especially religion: hither they brought Paul as a violator of their religion, to be judged, as Socrates was, for that he denied their idols to be gods, and because he would, as they said, bring in ἕτερα καινὰ δαιμόνια, other new gods. D. Laert. *l. 2. Socrat.*

*{what this new doctrine, &c.}* Thus godless men think of truth: whereas truth is more ancient than any error: the gospel was in the old testament, as the wheat in the blade, in time, and by degrees ripening, and showing itself. Heathenish superstitions are but imitations of God’s truth and holy worship, inventions of Satan to beguile ignorant souls with shows of truth.

**V. 20.** *{Thou bringest certain strange things, &c.}* It is strange to them who were aliens from the covenant of God: this they would know, to satisfy their own curiosity.

**V. 21.** *{either to tell or hear}* This is the fruit of idleness, and levity of misgoverned minds. See 1 Tim. 5. 13. these two things go together, inquisitiveness, and talkativeness: the Grecian histories show the disposition of this people: the meanest mechanic among them, would be meddling and discoursing of state matters; insomuch as in that dangerous liberty of tongues, that republic (though the mistress of knowledge to all Greece) could never be well or long settled, nor free from tumults, and dangerous concussions of state, so discomposing and disjointing the members thereof, that they were long before they could grow together again, recover their ancient strength, or be free from some mischievous gangrenes, until they lost themselves and all Greece with them: such popular liberty, being a dangerous symptom, of a disaffected commonwealth, it were to be wished, that those, who sit at the helm, would seriously, and timely consider how this disease has crept upon us, now some years together, lest once—flourishing England come to the like misery.

**V. 22.** *{Mars-hill}* See verse 19.

*{and said, ye men of Athens}* So was he ready to give account of the hope which was in him. See 1 Pet. 3. 15.

*{too superstitious}* δεισιδαιμονεστέρους: the word is used sometimes in the good part: but here, as commonly, in the evil: so Clem. Alexandr. *adm. ad Gent.* &c. δεισιδαίμων, is *metuens numen*, or a superstitious man: one who by fear, is driven to believe a deity, or worships for fear, as the superstitious usually do. Lactantius describes such to be, *qui superstitem memoriam defunctorum colunt*, &c. who worship the images of their *Ancestors*, as their *penates*, and household gods: they who devised to themselves new rites of adoring the dead (instead of God) whom they imagined to be received into heaven and deified, *lib. 4. cap. 28.* and also they who worshiped many, or any false God, he had excellently well said before. *l. 2. c. 18.* that such had not the reason of men left in them; and that there is no doubt, but that, *Religio nulla est, ubicunque simulachrum est*, *there is no religion, where there is an image.*

**V. 23.** *{your devotions}* σεβάσματα; *your adorations* (of σεβάζομαι, to adore with religious worship. Budaeus) or *the gods which you worship*: the Syriac give it בית דחלתכון *beth dachalothocun*: *the house of your religions*; *your idol temple*.

*{TO THE UNKNOWN GOD}* Syriac דאלהא גניזא *daloho genizo*; *of or to the hidden God*: he dwells in the thick darkness, 1 Kings 8. 12. Hebrew גנז *ganaz*, is *texit, velavit*, &c. the same which ἀποκρύπτω with the Greeks: God is indeed, as Lactantius says, *lib. 1. cap. 8. inexcogitabilis, ineffabilis, & nulli alio satis notus quam sibi*; *incomprehensible, ineffable, not very much known to any but himself*: Some heathen authors mention this altar, as Pausanias *Attic.* Laert. *in Epimenid.* Lucian. *Philopatride*: but something differing, in their relation of the inscription, and occasion of the same: Oecumenius says, that the whole inscription was, Θεοῖς Ἀσίας, καὶ Εὐρώπης, καὶ Λιβύης, Θεῷ ἀγνώστῳ καὶ ξένῳ, *to the gods of Asia, Europe and Lybia, to the unknown and strange God*: hereby Paul takes occasion to bring them to the true God, whom yet they knew not.

**V. 24.** *{God that made the world}* Chap. 7. 48. The apostle here teaches them, who that God was, whom they worshiped under the notion of *UNKNOWN*; whom he describes by his effects, and works of creation, and preservation of the world, concerning the incomprehensible essence of God, though before an auditory of philosophers, he said nothing, only by his work of creation and providence, he shows which is indeed the true and only God: in these words he refutes the foolish tenet of Epicures affirming the world to be eternal. See in verse 26.

**V. 25.** *{with men’s hands}* His worship consists not in things external, human traditions, or the works of men’s hands, idols, images, &c. but in spirit and truth (See John 4. 24.) according to the holy prescript of his own word: he is independently and self-blessed: wanting nothing of ours (Psalm 50. 8.) he gives all creatures what they have and are: when he blesses us with ability to give (which is acceptable service to him. Phil. 4. 18. Hebr. 13. 16.) and rewards that which we do herein, in faith and obedience to him, he only crowns his own graces in us: but here he intends not these things, but external ceremonies &c.

**V. 26.** *{hath made of one blood}* Of one Adam: herein he admonishes the Athenians to consider the end for which God gave them life and being: it is a brutish stupor, not to mind why God made us reasonable creatures.

*{determined the time before}* Job 7. 1. & 14. 5, 6. For the birth and life of every man, as also the places of their habitations: this he says to confute the Epicures, who denied God’s providence, dreaming that all things were made by a fortuitous concourse of their imaginary *Atomi*: (Clem. Alexandrinus *strom. lib. 1.* Greg. Nazianzen *orat. 33. fine.* Epiphanius *haeres. 6.* Athanas. *de incarn. verb.* Augustin *de C. D. lib. 6. cap. 5.* &c.) God in his eternal counsel ordained all these things, and changes which we see in their appointed time: he divides the earth to the sons of men, and his providence governs all things, which his wisdom not only foresaw, but disposed of. See Psal. 115. 3.
