**V. 27.** *{that they should seek the Lord}* It is not as Anaxagoras said, man was born to see the sun and the heavens (Lactant. *l. 3. c. 9.*) but to seek, know, and serve the Lord their Maker: he not only gave us life, but also means by which we may come to know him, in the great volume of nature, and the book of grace, the holy Scripture.

*{if haply they might feel after him}* ψηλαφήσειαν αὐτὸν, might feel and find, he intimates that God, though he be incorporeal, yet in his works which are corporeal and obvious to sense, so complies with their infirmity, as that he sets himself before them (as Oecumenius says) ὥσπερ ἂν εἰς μέσον τὶ ψηλαφώμενον as it were something *palpable*: the Syriac has it, *that they might find him by his creatures*: in them indeed, that which may be known of him, as his wisdom, power, and goodness, may and do evidently appear. Romans 1. 20.

*{not far from every one of us}* We are not to seek the knowledge of God in things far remote from us; we need not go out of ourselves to find him; he fills all things. Jer. 23. 24. indeed the carnal eye cannot see him, nor the ear hear him: *lo he goeth by me, and I see him not.* Job 9. 11. yet the effects of his present goodness, are obvious to every sense. See Augustin. *Paulinae. ep. 112. cap. 11.*

**V. 28.** *{in him we live, &c.}* That is, by his power, by which he continually preserves the creature in his being. Col. 1. 17. Hebr. 1. 3.

*{your own Poets have said}* As Aratus a Greek; this he cites, not to derive any proof from his authority, who was an heathen, and alien, but to shame the Athenians, with whom he had now to do. See also 1 Cor. 15. 33. Tit. 1. 12.

**V. 29.** *{we ought not to think}* Esa. 40. 18. from Aratus’ testimony he infers, that the worship of idols is impious vanity: how foolish and irrational is it for man, the image of God, to adore the image and work of man? and what madness is it for a man to think that any can make the image of an infinite, incomprehensible God, when he well knows that the most curious artificer can neither draw the picture of a soul, nor carve its image.

**V. 30.** *{times of this ignorance}* Chap. 14. 16.

*{God winked at}* ὑπεριδὼν ὁ Θεὸς, may here signify the same which ἀμελεῖν Hebr. 8. 9. not to regard, or to contemn, contrary to ἐπιβλέπειν. Luke 1. 48. God did not manifest himself unto them, as unto the Jews. Psal. 147. 19, 20. and this shows God’s patience, in that he did not presently destroy them, not their impunity for the life to come: herein he prepossesses an objection which they might possibly have made: if the religion which we profess, be false and erroneous; why did God suffer the world so long to err: he says God bore with that in his long-suffering; but now he requires you to repent: be not you so curious to examine what became of others before you, as careful for your own salvation; the only means whereto, is now presented you: that was a severe judgment, when God gave you up to your own lusts, and blindness of mind (as Rom. 1. 24. Heb. 8. 9.) this is a gracious mercy which he offers you in the gospel and covenant of grace.

*{but now he commandeth all men}* Now he causes the gospel to be preached to all nations, to draw them from their horrible sins: and now if they refuse to do the known will of their Master, they must expect more severe judgments.

**V. 31.** *{righteousness}* ἐν δικαιοσύνῃ *in justice and equity*: as 2 Cor. 5. 10. he mentions the judgment to come, to terrify the Epicures, that they might no longer beguile their own souls, in hope of future impunity. See Psal. 96. 13. & 9. 8, 9.

*{by that man whom he hath ordained}* Rom. 2. 16. Christ Jesus: possibly Paul names his humanity, in respect of the Gentiles’ ignorance of the mystery of the blessed Trinity, and Christ’s incarnation: and lest they should so mistake that sacred mystery, as thereby to confirm their opinion concerning plurality of gods.

*{given assurance}* Or *offered faith*; the meaning is, that God confirmed and declared, as it were by a most evident testimony, that Christ should judge the world, in raising him from the dead; wherein he was with power, declared to be the Son of God. Rom. 1. 4.

**V. 32.** *{Some mocked}* Some were like stony ground, some like thorny, and some like the good. Matth. 13. they who tasted the truth and mercy of God therein, desired to hear more. See Chap. 13. 42.

**V. 33.** *{So Paul departed}* He dismissed his auditors, having performed his part in sowing the seed of the gospel among them.

**V. 34.** *{certain men clave unto him}* To wit, those whose hearts God had opened, so that the word might be a savor of life unto them.

*{Dionysius the Areopagite}* A senator or judge in that court of Mars-hill in Athens, whom Luke names, because he was no vulgar auditor, but an eminent man, and of great authority with the Athenians.

*{Damaris}* It seems that she was some honorable woman. See verse 11.

## CHAPTER 18

**Verse 1.** *{Corinth}* A chief city of Achaia, situate between the Egean and Ionian seas.

**V. 2.** *{Aquila}* A Jew converted to the faith of Christ. See Rom. 16. 3.

*{Pontus}* A province in Asia.

*{Claudius}* The emperor of Rome.

**V. 3.** *{and wrought}* This he did in respect of the present necessity, that he might not disadvantage the gospel by being burdensome to any. Chap. 20. 34. 2 Thes. 3. 8.

*{tentmakers}* Their trade was to make tents of skins, then much in use in those hot countries.

**V. 4.** *{a Greek}* See chap. 11. 20.

**V. 6.**† *{when Silas and Timotheus were come}* See chap. 16. 1. &c. and 17. 15. we here read the proceeding of Paul in preaching the gospel at Corinth, accommodating his doctrine to their capacity, of which he seems to speak, 1 Cor. 3. 1, 2. but after the coming of Silas and Timotheus from Macedon unto him, as he had ordered, chap. 17. 15. he began to be fervent in the cause of Christ, declaring him to be τὸν Χριστὸν Ἰησοῦν, that Christ Jesus, promised by the prophets.

*{was pressed in spirit}* He had an extraordinary and vehement motion of the spirit, by a divine inspiration, to testify to the Jews, that Jesus was that Christ, the Messias, whom they so long expected.

**V. 6.** *{he shook his raiment}* Mat. 10. 14. and chap. 13. 51.

*{your blood be upon your own heads}* This is an Hebrew manner of speaking. So Josh. 2. 19. דמו בראשו *damo berojsho*, Gr. ἔνοχος ἑαυτῷ ἔστω; *he shall be guilty to himself*: that is, of his own destruction. So 2 Sam. 1. 16. *thy blood shall be upon thine own head.* So the Jews cried, Mat. 27. 25. *his blood be upon our heads, &c.* that is, the guilt and punishment thereof; this phrase seems taken from the Jews’ custom of laying their hands on the head of the guilty persons by which they intimated, that the witnesses laid that crime, concerning which they testified, on the head, or to the charge of the person accused, and so devoted him to punishment; as their sacrifices were destinated to death, by the laying the sacrificers’ hands on the head of the beast to be offered: See Levit. 1. 4. & 3. 2. Exod. 29. 10. So that blood is said to be laid on the head, when one is guilty of the sin which deserves death: the sense of Paul’s speech is, be you, or you are guilty of your own destructions.

*{I am clean, &c.}* καθαρὸς ἐγὼ that is, ἀθῷος, *innocent guiltless*; or ἀναίτιος; *unblamable*, not in the fault: οὐκ ἐκοινώνησα τῷ κακῷ: *I have no share in the evil*: it is none of my fault, if you perish.

*{henceforth I will go unto the Gentiles}* This he said, that emulation of the Gentiles might incite them to believe: but their obstinacy made an evil use of everything, and so rendered them incurable. chap. 13. 46.

**V. 7.** *{named Justus}* This was a proselyte: the Syriac calls him טטוס *Titus*; whether Justus were here a surname; as, chap. 1. 23. and Col. 4. 11. it is uncertain, the vulgar reads *Titi Justi*.

*{one that feared God}* So chap. 10. 2. Cornelius was said to be a devout man, and one that *feared God*: a religious and good man.

**V. 8.** *{and Crispus a chief ruler}* See 1 Cor. 1. 14. ἀρχισυνάγωγος *a chief, or one of the chief rulers of the synagogue*, for verse 17. Sosthenes is mentioned (as his colleague, or one in the same office) *a chief ruler of the synagogue.* See on Mark 5. 22. Mat. 9. 18. and on Luke 13. 14.

**V. 9.** *{be not afraid, but speak, &c.}* Be not afraid to speak boldly: because the ingratitude and perverseness of hearers, often discourage ministers, thus God animates them to their duty. See Isai. 58. 1. Jer. 1. 17, 18, 19. Ezek. 3. 4, 5, 8, 9, 11. we may not, for fear of the invincible malice of some, neglect the salvation of others. See chap. 20. 27.

**V. 10.** *{for I am with thee}* I will take your part and assist you; See Mat. 28. Rom. 8. 31. however God often permits his ministers to the violence of enemies, yet he is with them in life and death; yea, then most, when they are most destitute of man’s assistance. See 2 Tim. 4. 16, 17, 18. but here God promised a more evident and peculiar protection to Paul, by making void all the mischievous endeavors of the adversaries.

*{I have much people in this city}* This is a second argument, whereby he encourages Paul to speak confidently: there were many in Corinth whom God had ordained to salvation, so that his labor should be blessed with happy success: here is an argument of industry and patience to ministers: God has his elect, whom in his appointed time he will effectually call to Christ by the word preached, though he suffer them for a season to lie like the pure wheat among the perishing chaff.

**V. 11.** *{continued there}* ἐκάθισε *he sat there*, that is, at Corinth. So the Syriac gives it by יתב which signifies, *he sat*, *remained*, or *dwelt* of the Hebrew ישב often used to the same purpose.

*{a year and six months}* So full of labor and difficulty was the planting of churches, that Paul, so extraordinarily gifted, was so long in this one, yet had he assistants. See 1 Cor. 2. 3. 1 Cor. 3. 10.

**V. 12.** *{Gallio was Deputy}* Affliction commonly follows preaching of the gospel: malice is restless as the devil, whose influence it is: the Jews, as it seems by the change of their deputy, were animated to attempt against Paul; hoping that a new judge would favor them against the Christians.

*{of Achaia}* Gallio is called *proconsul*, or deputy *Consul* of Achaia, that is of all Greece, of which Achaia was but a part: So Suidas interprets Ἀχαΐα, ἡ Ἑλλὰς: *Achaia*, that is *Greece*: So the Romans (says he) seem to call *Achaia, Greece*; transferring the name of the then chief and ruling part thereof, to the whole conquered nation: so say it was because they subdued the rest of the Grecians through the aid of the Achaians Pausanias; &c. Sure it is that Greece was made a *Consular Province*: some write that Gallio obtained this deputyship of Claudius the Roman emperor, by the favor of Nero, Claudius’ adopted son.

*{with one accord}* So unanimous, and industrious are the wicked to do mischief: it is a wretched shame for Christians to divide, when Satan’s kingdom can be of one consent.

**V. 13.** *{contrary to the law}* They mean the law of Moses; according to which the Romans had permitted them to live: this they said, not being able to look to the end of the law Christ Jesus. 2 Cor. 3. 13. Now however the Romans favored neither the religion of Jews nor Christians, yet forasmuch as innovations in religion, are often the causes of dangerous confusions of states, all such changes were suspected, and odious to the Romans, so that they made severe decrees against the authors thereof.

**V. 14.** *{if it were a matter of wrong}* Between party and party: it is as if he said; I will meddle only with civil government, and not with your religion.

**V. 15.** *{and names}* The profane man thought the Jews worshiped one name, and the Christians another, as the heathens, who upon the matter, adored bare names, and fancies of men.

**V. 16.** *{drave them from the judgment seat}* He superseded the cause, and therefore would not have them stay there longer to trouble the court, with controversies (as he thought) unnecessary.

**V. 17.** *{the Greeks}* The Grecians which were not converted: the Syriac reads it, *the profane.*

*{none of those things}* Neither for that which concerned religion, to defend God’s servants: nor for the state’s interest, to maintain peace, and defend the oppressed.

**V. 18.** *{having shorn}* As Nazarites did at the end of their vow, Numb. 6. 18. the meaning is, that Paul shore his head. (See chap. 21. 24.) not as some others think, that this is to be understood of Aquila and Priscilla.

*{in Cenchrea}* A port of the Corinthians.

**V. 19.** *{came to Ephesus}* A city of Ionia, and metropolis or chief city of Asia the less.

*{left them there.}* That is, he left Aquila and Priscilla at Ephesus.

*{reasoned with the Jews}* So zealous was he still for their salvation, so often did he expose his life for them.

**V. 20.** *{he consented not}* Not that he was not willing to spend his time, and do his uttermost endeavor for their salvation who were willing to hear; for he ventured often to preach to the obstinate, and he promised to come again unto them; but it seems some secret instinct of God’s spirit now led him to Jerusalem.

**V. 21.** *{keep this feast}* Not that he now observed days, or the antiquated ceremonies of the law (See Gal. 4. 10.) but because he might in that solemnity, and concourse of people, more advance the gospel.

*{if God will}* 1 Cor. 4. 19. Jam. 4. 15.

**V. 22.** *{Caesarea}* Called *Cesarea Stratonis* a port by which they went to Jerusalem; some say built by Herod.

**V. 23.** *{Galatia, and Phrygia}* Chap. 16. 6. Galatia, or Gallo-grecia; so named of the (*Galli, qui Graecis, mixti, olim loca ea coluere. Atlas min. Amster. 1634. pag. 600. cap. 20.*) *The Gauls and French, who mixed with Grecians anciently inhabited those places.*

**V. 24.** *{a certain Jew}* 1 Cor. 1. 12.

*{named Apollos}* See 1 Cor. 3. 6. and 1 Cor. 4. 6. he was born at Alexandria, a city in Egypt.

*{an eloquent man}* Though *the kingdom of God be not in word, but in power*. 1 Cor. 4. 20. nor eloquence of speech, or *the enticing words of man’s wisdom*, but *in the evidence of the spirit*. 1 Cor. 2. 1. 4. yet is eloquence used, to the setting forth of the truth of God, and edification of his church, as an *Egyptian jewel* to adorn the tabernacle, and here commended in Apollos, by the holy Ghost, whose gift it is: Lactantius noted well that the philosophers, orators, and poets, were therefore very pernicious, in that they easily ensnared incautious minds: *suavitate sermonis*, *with sweetness of speech*; therefore his advice is, even in delivering the truth of Christ, to sweeten the speech for the winning of them to Christ, who will neither hear nor read anything, *nisi expolitum, ac disertum*, *except it be polished, and eloquent*; and where *nemo rem veritate ponderat, sed ornatu, lib. 5. cap. 1.* *no man values the matter by the truth, but politure and trimness thereof.*

*{mighty in the Scriptures,}* Much versed and learned in them, which enabled him to convince the adversaries. Titus 1. 9. Syriac, *argutus erat in Scripturis*: this is added that none might think it to be the vain and frothy eloquence of profane men: it was a divine and powerful eloquence.

**V. 25.** *{instructed in the way of the Lord}* κατηχημένος, instructed *vivâ voce*, The Syriac has it, *he was taught*. Some interpret, *he was initiated, &c.* See Luke 1. 4. the word signifies one that is entered into religion, or catechized, as those who were taught the rudiments and principles thereof.

*{fervent in spirit}* Zealous to teach and to win men to Christ: See Rom. 12. 11. 1 Kings 19. 14. Zeal is the life of religion, without which it is inactive (See Rev. 3. 15, 16.) but it must be zeal with the true knowledge of God. See Rom. 10. 2. Acts 21. 20. and 22. 3. Tit. 2. 14. Rev. 3. 19.

*{taught diligently}* His zeal and diligence is here commendable, though he had not yet much knowledge of the doctrine of the gospel, as appears by the next clause: however, he well used his talent, being faithful in a little.

*{the baptism of John}* That is the doctrine of John, which we may not understand as if John were the author thereof, or as it were different from the doctrine of Christ and his apostles (for he preached repentance, and faith in Christ for remission of sins, and salvation, which they also preached) but because John was between the prophets and Christ, whose way he was to prepare; the mysteries of salvation were not so clearly manifested; as afterward by Christ himself and his apostles: John taught the rudiments, as his hearers were capable, but the apostles themselves could not understand some things often taught by Christ, until they received, the holy Ghost, to lead them into all truth: therefore though Apollos were instructed in the way of the Lord, having learned that Christ was the only redeemer of man, yet he was yet ignorant of many things appertaining to the manner of our redemption, and the kingdom of Christ; which he afterward more fully learned and happily taught.

**V. 26.** *{they took him unto them and expounded}* If Priscilla, the woman here mentioned, contributed aught to this work, it was where her husband, Apollos, and herself were private: such instruction Paul commends in Timothie’s grandmother Lois, and his mother Eunice, of whom (most likely) he had learned the holy Scriptures, (See 2 Tim. 1. 5. and 3. 15.) though otherwise in the church, (or other place made public by the company) he suffered not a woman to teach, but to be in silence. See 1 Tim. 2. 11, 12. possibly the man and wife hearing Apollos, both joined in the invitation of him unto them, but that Aquila performed the part of interpretation, and instruction in the way of God, though it be attributed to both, because both equally affected it; there is no patronage here for that late revived practice of the old exploded heresy of the Pepulians, or Quintilians, who as Augustin *Tom. 6. de haeres.* says *mulieres sacerdotio honorant*, they honor women with priesthood. We may more safely say that the most excellent of mere men know not all; and that those who are inferior in gifts may add something to them.

*{the way of God}* The doctrine of the gospel.

**V. 27.** *{to pass to Achaia}* See verse 12. why he would go thither is not here expressed, but certainly it was to advance the gospel there.

*{the brethren wrote}* Letters of recommendation for him who was not much known to the churches.

*{helped them much}* Confirming them who believed, and refuting the adversaries through the grace of God thereto enabling him.

**V. 28.** *{That Jesus was Christ}* The Jews had no controversy with Christians concerning a Christ to come; the whole dispute was, whether Jesus the Son of Mary were he: this now Apollos proved by Scriptures accommodated to him: and thus *Apollos watered*, 2 Cor. 3. 6. herein the Scripture is sufficient to accomplish and make the man of God absolute, 2 Tim. 3. 16. we cannot learned Christ in any human traditions, or inventions of men: the sun can be discerned by no light but his own, because that excels all other worldly lights: neither can Christ the sun of righteousness be known by any, but his own light, which shines in the Scriptures: these testify of him, and discover him unto us. John 5. 39.

## CHAPTER 19

**Verse 1.** *{Through the upper coasts}* In the upper parts of Asia, are Phrygia, Galatia, Lydia, Lyconia, Ionia, in which was Ephesus.

**V. 2.** *{have ye received, &c.}* They are demanded concerning the visible gifts of the Holy Ghost, as speaking tongues, healing, &c. Which then flourished in the church, though they were not then common to all that believed, and were baptized, as in this their answer may appear.

*{whether there be any Holy Ghost}* Or *whether the Holy Ghost be* (as John 7. 39.) they meant whether there be any such gifts of the holy Ghost, given to any: they answered according to Paul’s question; which being concerning the gifts and graces (not the infinite and eternal essence of the deity) they reply according to the same. (See chap. 8. 16.) for it were very absurd to say that they had not heard of the holy Ghost being baptized of John’s disciples, John seeing him descending on Christ in his baptism, in a visible form. Mat. 3. John 1. concerning this, neither did the apostle ask, nor they answer, but concerning those gifts which then flourished in the church, of which they said, they had not heard.

**V. 3.** *{unto what then were ye baptized?}* That is, what religion, what doctrine professed you, in your baptism? or the question was not here, of baptizing, but *de primordiis* concerning their beginning, and constitution of the church of Ephesus, where appeared not yet any public form of a church in discipline and other ecclesiastical rites, and conventions: so that he inquires, how they were grounded, whether by imposition of hands, or the gifts of the holy Ghost, whereby it might appear, that they were divinely called, as the gifts of tongues, prophecies, &c.

*{unto John’s Baptism}* That is, we professed the same doctrine, which John Baptist taught: and we received baptism, as a seal of our professing the same, and of our dedication unto Christ.

**V. 4.** *{John verily baptized}* Mat. 3. 11. as if he said, the ministry of John was not to gather disciples to himself, but that he might prepare and remit them to Christ, that they might in him only, expect and obtain remission of sins and salvation.

*{that is, on Christ Jesus}* Theophylact on Luke 24. expresses that well; as not excluding the other persons of the holy Trinity, but in contradistinction to the Jewish baptizings or washings; that was says he πνευματικὸν οὐκ Ἰουδαϊκὸν, spiritual, not Judaical. See on chap. 2. 38. and on 18. 48.

**V. 5.** *{When they heard this}* That is, when they heard the doctrine of John, they were baptized by some of John’s disciples who had so preached unto them the doctrine of repentance, and salvation by Christ: not that Paul did rebaptize them: for otherwise it would follow, that Christ whom John baptized, and we, are not baptized with the same baptism: whereas he bore the same circumcision with the Jews, and the same baptism with us Gentiles, that he might declare himself the Savior of both. So that these words (*when they heard this*) relate not unto the words of Paul, but unto their hearing of John’s doctrine: and therefore Paul is not said to have baptized them, but to have laid his hands on them: that is a posture and action of confirmation, not initiation, and baptizing, verse 6. against rebaptizing them who were baptized by heretics (except of those who razed the foundation of faith, and used not the Catholic form of baptism) See the council of Carthage, 1 *Anno* 258. and the other councils concerning the same. Severin. Ein. *To. 1. Concil. fol. 175, cap. 1. &c. Paris. 1636. ib. To. 5. 482. cap. A. B. ibid. 455. cap. 2. E. ibid. T. 1. 99. cap. 1. A. B. & 158. 2. E. ibid. To. 9. 368. 1. cap.* & Bart. Carranza *summ. Concil. Rothomag. 1633. pag. 673.* Augustin. *To. 4. expos. ep. ad Rom. inchoat. prope fin. ibid. To. 3. de fide ad Petr. (si sit ejus liber) ibid. de verb. Domini in Matth. serm. 11.* Epiphan. *exposit. fidei. Cathol. 13.* Justin. Martyr. *Quaest. & resp. ad Orthod. quaest. 14.* Chrysostom’s words on Hebr. 6. 6. are remarkable: when he had said τὸ βάπτισμα σταυρός ἐστι, &c. Baptism is the cross, in which our old man is Crucified with Christ; being made conformable to his death, &c. ὁ τοίνυν δεύτερον ἑαυτὸν βαπτίζων πάλιν αὐτὸν σταυροῖ, therefore he that rebaptizes himself, crucifies him (the Lord of life) again. Auxentius the heretic rebaptized. Ambrose said, he (therein) despised the council of God. *epist. 32. fin.* Baptism is a new birth of the whole man: as we can be born but once in the flesh, so can we be born but once in the Spirit: therefore as there was but one circumcision (which signified the same) so ought there to be but one baptism.

**V. 6.** *{Laid his hands upon them}* Chap. 6. 6. & 8. 17. he laid his hands upon them for confirmation, we read not that he rebaptized them.

*{they spake with tongues}* Chap. 2. 4. & 10. 46. That is, they spoke divers languages, which before they knew not.

*{and prophesied}* By a divine and evident inspiration, they preached and expounded the word of God as able ministers of Christ. See 1 Cor. 11. 4. & 14. 1.

**V. 8.** *{the kingdom of God}* See on chap. 1. 3. and on 20. 25. that is, concerning the mysteries of salvation comprehended in the gospel; the Jews dreamed of a political kingdom of the Messias, which they supposed should be in ceremonial rites, but Paul declared that it should be a spiritual kingdom; as Rom. 14. 17.

**V. 9.** *{and spake evil of that way}* As chap. 18. 26. of that doctrine of the gospel.

*{and separated the Disciples}* This warrants not separation from a true church, or for any defects therein less than fundamental: it is true that when the nations are drunken with the cups of Babylonish fornication, then the Lord calls from heaven, *come out of her my people, &c.* (Revel. 17. 2. and 18. 4. See Ephes. 5. 7, 11. 2 Thes. 3. 14. 2 Cor. 6. 14, 15. Tit. 3. 10.) and that in case of scandal, we must not company with the incorrigible wicked: but here we see, though Paul withdrew the weak disciples from the blasphemers of Christianity and the gospel, yet he made no schisms nor yet deserted the cause, for which he daily disputed with the adversaries.

*{one Tyrannus}* This seems to have been some philosopher, or public professor of rhetoric, converted by Paul: Syr. *in the school of a certain man whose name was Tyrannus.*

**V. 10.** *{by the space of two years}* This commends Paul’s great diligence for the planting of the gospel.

*{so that all they which dwelt in Asia, &c.}* This was that admirable door, which Paul said was opened unto him, 1 Cor. 16. 9, not that all the people, but that all the Christians of Asia heard him, the sweet savor and report of the gospel spreading itself abroad (like that odor of the precious ointment, which filled the house. John 12. 3.) into all parts roundabout.

**V. 11.** *{special miracles}* Great miracles, whereby he confirmed the authority of Paul’s apostleship, and ministry.

**V. 12.** *{handkerchiefs}* This makes nothing for the superstitious use of relics: God’s providence did this that by such cures, he might prepare the absent, and those who had never seen the face of Paul, to a reverend esteem, and opinion of the gospel and truth of Christ, in whose name they were so cured, and also, that in these means, so slender and improbable of themselves, the power of Jesus healing them might the more clearly appear.

*{and the diseases departed from them}* As Mat. 8. 15. Luke 4. 39. John 4. 52. the diseases left them, that is, they were freed from their diseases, and the unclean spirits departed from them, being forced so to do by the power of Christ.

**V. 13.** *{Exorcists}* Syr. *Jews who wandered about, and adjured devils or conjured devils*: these possibly, out of a foolish and impious emulation of the apostles casting out devils, practiced such things, intending if they could have effected anything herein, to have made men believe, that the like miracles done by the apostles, were but *magical exorcisms*, concerning *exorcists*, and *exorcisms*, yet in use in the *Church of Rome*, see Durand. *rational. To. 1. lib. 2. cap. 6. ib. lib. 6. c. 63. n. 8.* and the *Ritual Roman Paul 5. jussu edit. de sacr. bapt.* where they use these words, *exorcizo te immunde Spiritus, in nomine Patris ✝, & Filii ✝, & Spiritus Sancti ✝ ut exeas & recedas ab hoc famulo Dei, &c.* So *ibid. de benedictionibus* —— *exorcizo te creatura salis per Deum ✝ vivum, &c. exorcizo te creatura aquae, &c.* And *Benedict. esculent. exorcizo te creatura olei, &c.* See *ib. de exorcizand. obses. à daem.* Concerning the *Jewish Exorcists*, Josephus writes some fabulous things. *Antiq. lib. 8. cap. 2.* The gift of casting out devils continued long in the primitive church. Justin. Martyr. *dial. cum Try. Jud.*

**V. 14.** *{Seven Sons of one Sceva}* He mentions these for the greater evidence of the history, these were eminent persons (sons of one of the chief priests) who with false miracles, and abuse of the sacred name of Christ to their own secular gains, endeavored to confirm their impious superstitions, and to render the doctrine of the gospel suspected.

**V. 15.** *{But who are ye?}* What are you that I should obey you? God commands me, Jesus (whom Paul preaches) commands me; but do not I know that you have no such commission, no power to cast me out?

**V. 16.** *{leapt on them}* The possessed man fell violently upon them, and by the force of the evil spirit in him, mastered them, tearing their clothes, and wounding them: sometimes the devil willingly co-operates with the conjurers, and witches, suffering himself to be commanded, and possibly, driven out also (probably the Hebrews might consider this in their calling a witch, *the mistress of a devil*. So 1 Sam. 28. 7. they call the witch of Endor בעלת־אוב *behalath ob, dominam Pythonis*, a Pythoness, one having a familiar spirit. Chalde, *one who can draw out a devil*); this he does where he can entice men or women to confederate with him for mischief, and hold them, and others by them, in superstition: but here he had some other end, and God would thus defeat their mischievous practices: making all serve to the further advancement of the gospel which Paul preached.

**V. 17.** *{fear fell on them all}* Herein appears the fruit of this divine revenge on them who impiously profaned the holy name of Jesus: first that by their example, others feared to contemn that doctrine, the patronage whereof God so severely undertook: secondly in that all turned to the magnifying the sacred name of Jesus, the rumor of this accident making all afraid, not only to blaspheme it as some of the Jews did, but even to take it in their profane mouths, to abuse, as these sons of Sceva did.

**V. 18.** *{and confessed}* Terrified with this judgment on others, the remorse of their own consciences, and desiring some remedy, and comfort, they publicly confessed their errors, and detested those their sins. See Mat. 3. 6.

**V. 19.** *{Curious arts}* That is *Magic*, as the Syriac expressed it *divinations by judicial astrology*, &c.

*{fifty thousand pieces of silver}* Or, *fifty thousand pence*, which the word also signifies: what this comes to of our sterling money is not agreed on by the writers, but the least estimate, brings it about, eight hundred pounds.

**V. 20.** *{So mightily grew, &c.}* See chap. 12. 24. κατὰ κράτος, *vehemently* *with great power*, as the Syriac has it, intimating a powerful course of the word, casting down the kingdom of sin and Satan: so does κράτος signify victory also: the sum is, that these superstitious and impious things being taken away, the word of God so much prevailed in the affections and lives of men to faith and obedience, that many were daily added to the church of Christ.

**V. 21.** *{purposed in the Spirit}* That is, by the motion of the Holy Ghost: therefore he yielded not to his friends’ importunity, chap. 20. 22, 22.

*{Macedonia}* chap. 16. 9.

*{Achaia}* Chap. 18. 12. Here it is taken for that territory of Greece, which bore that name, and not as, chap. 18. 12. for all Greece.

**V. 22.** *{Timotheus and Erastus}* See Rom. 16. 23. 2 Tim. 4. 20. to prepare and expedite those things which concerned the confirmation of those churches, so that he might in his passage make the more haste to Jerusalem.

*{in Asia for a season}* That is, in Ephesus, which is in Asia.

**V. 23.** *{the same time}* When Paul was preparing to go from Ephesus, and thought all things well composed, upon a sudden Satan raised an unexpected storm.

*{about that way}* *About the way of God.* Syr. That is, concerning the gospel, and truth of God which Paul preached.

**V. 24.** *{Silver shrines}* ναοὺς ἀργυροῦς. *Silver temples*, as the Syriac interprets, which were made in some such like form.

Concerning the destruction of that same magnificent idol temple of Diana at Ephesus, you may read in the story of Erostratus. Plin. *lib. 36. cap. 14.* The silver shrines, or medals of silver with the form of their idol Diana’s temple, impressed or wrought thereon, Demetrius sold to them who came to Ephesus either of superstition to worship, or curiosity to see the magnificent structure of that temple.

**V. 25.** *{Whom he called together}* When he saw the esteem of this temple lessen by the light of the gospel, and so his trade growing dead, he called together all the *artificers*, whose living depended on that superstition, and made a seditious speech to them.

*{by this craft we have our wealth}* This is all our trade: a dangerous argument to carnal men, whose wealth is the God they adore: such men will easily be persuaded for their private ends, to trouble the peace of a church or state, and to defend their gains though never so impious with tumult and blood.

**V. 26.** *{this Paul hath persuaded, &c.}* He truly confesses the state of the cause: Paul had showed them that they were no Gods which are made with hands; yet Demetrius wickedly endeavors to suppress that truth: this blindness puts him and his fellows precipitately on to violent tumults.

**V. 27.** *{not only our craft}* This our manufacture our making medals, is in danger to be reproved as impious and unprofitable.

*{but also the temple}* It is a rare impiety of the vulgar which borrows no pretense from religion to countenance itself withal: and meddling with religion without some private interests, is as rare in carnal men, if Paul’s preaching had nothing hindered Demetrius and his fellows’ profits, he would hardly have stirred out of doors about it.

*{whom all Asia and the world worshippeth}* To the argument of profit, and pretended religion, he added another reason, from the magnificence of the temple, and greatness of their idol Diana; now he enforces another leading motive, from universality, and consent of worshipers.

**V. 28.** *{They were full of wrath}* This argument of profit, works strongly on worldly men’s affections: therefore now guided by passion and hearing the cause of the impair and decay of their trade; they never examine the truth and effects of the gospel, but put it to popularity, and tumultuous outcries.

*{great is Diana of the Ephesians}* They feigned Diana to be Jupiter’s daughter, and their *tutelary goddess*, in this fantastic opinion, they cry up her imaginary greatness, and their supposed interest, and thereby unanimously and publicly profess themselves defenders of that their superstition, with their uttermost power.

**V. 29.** *{the whole city was filled}* Such unconsiderable sparks fomented by the breath of an inconstant vulgar oftentimes break out into outrageous flames; when yet the cause of the impious tumult, is rumor of people ignorant of what they do, and desire of change. See Numb. 26. 1. 2 Sam. 15. 6, 11, 13.

*{Gaius and Aristarchus}* See on Rom. 16. 23. Colos. 4. 10. herein they bewray their diffidence as to the truth and justice of their cause, who dared not put it to the test of any principles of truth or good reason, but used violence, and injurious treating of the servants of Christ, dragging them into the theaters without any legal examination of the cause.

*{the theatre}* A public place, to which the custom was to resort in any trouble, or business about the commonwealth.

**V. 30.** *{suffered him not}* This was their care of his safety, who better knew the disposition of that people, it is an example to hearers, to love and preserve their ministers. See chap. 17. 10.

**V. 31.** *{Chief of Asia}* Ἀσιαρχῶν. Syr. *primores Asiae*: this was God’s providence over Paul, to stir up some chief men in this so great a danger to countenance and favor him, these send to him to desire him, not to venture himself to an enraged multitude.

**V. 32.** *{the assembly was confused}* This company is here called ἐκκλησία: the Syr. gives it well, *turbae quae erant in theatro*; the multitude of the Ephesians now assembled in the theater: this convention was very confused, some crying one thing, and some another.

*{the more part knew not wherefore}* The people like great waters are easily enraged, but seldom well informed, why: false rumors fill their ears, and ungoverned passion their hearts with sudden and vehement desire of some new unconsidered undertakings; the end whereof they neither prudently foresee, nor cautiously examine.

**V. 33.** *{the Jews putting him forward}* It seems that the heathens of Ephesus were equally enraged against the Jews and Christians; and that the Jews put him on, to justify their nation and religion, that they might cast all the occasion of this discontent and tumult, on the Christians.

*{beckoned with, &c.}* See on chap. 12. 17. Gre. κατασείσας τὴν χεῖρα *shaking his hand*, by which they that spoke in public seemed to desire silence, that they might be heard; according to which the vulgar, Vatablus, &c. give it, *manu silentio postulato*, *having required silence by his hand*.

**V. 34.** *{That he was a Jew}* They knew that the Jews worshiped not their idol, therefore would not hear him.

*{all with one voice}* So many wicked men can Satan unite in mischief: consent and unanimity of multitudes is no universal and certain mark of truth.

*{Cried out}* The word here used is of Κράζω, to *yawll*, or *cry out*, κράζειν or κρώζειν, is spoken of crows who in great flocks cry altogether, hence is this expression accommodated to the disturbed multitude.

**V. 35.** *{what man is there that knoweth not}* To appease this tumult, he takes it for granted, that their religion was so publicly known to the world, that there need no tumult or outcries to profess the same: probably he understood Demetrius’ malice, and hypocrisy, in that under pretense of zeal to their religion, he had abused the whole city, by his stirring up this tumult, all being but to maintain the gain of himself and fellow artificers: this sore, which most of the people knew not, he in his way prudently dissembled, magnifying with them the feigned god which they worshiped, that he might thereby attain his purpose, which was to appease the present tumult. This speech of the town-clerk savors only of carnal wisdom, whatever was pretended here contributes no patronage to true religion.

*{a worshipper of, &c.}* νεωκόρος, is that which the Latins called *aedituus*, *the Temple keeper*. He that has charge of the church. It is interpreted by Suidas, ὁ τὸν νεὼν κοσμῶν καὶ εὐτρεπίζων, one that adorns and prepares the temple. His meaning is, that Ephesus was so established in their religion and worship of Diana their supposed tutelary goddess, that there was no such cause of fear as Demetrius suggested, that they should be persuaded to forsake their religion for any other.

*{which fell down from Jupiter}* *Which descended from heaven*, the Syriac has it. Concerning Canetias the maker of this idol, see Plin. *l. 16. c. 40.* But their covetous priests persuaded them, that it came down, or was sent down from heaven; the like fictions had other heathens concerning the Palladium of Troy, and the Ancile of Rome, concerning this Ephesian idol you may see more, said Διοπετὴς.

**V. 36.** *{these things cannot be spoken against}* Suidas says, ἀναντίρρητον (the word here used) is ἀντιλογίαν μὴ δεχόμενον, that which admits no contradiction; a certain and doubtless thing. So pronounces he concerning their vain superstition, grounded on lying fables, impious prescription, and rash consent of multitudes; whereas it is neither in time, multitude, or power of men, to make falsehood truth.

*{ye ought to be quiet}* Or *appeased*.

**V. 37.** *{are neither robbers of Churches, &c.}* That said he truly (would we could say as much of our impropriators) but though he thus said to appease the tumult, yet the doctrine which Paul preached, is inconsistent with idols.

**V. 38.** *{the Law is open}* Or *the Court-days are kept*. The law is the best appeaser of tumults; and justice the securest bond of peace, the nerves and Sinews of all commonwealths, without which the people must needs perish, if by no other means in self-destruction: it is that same, *salsedo combinans*, without which, all bodies compounded, end in present dissolution.

*{deputies}* That is, Judges appointed to determine personal controversies.

**V. 39.** *{in a lawful assembly}* Or *ordinary courts*, or *course of justice* (the sacred patron of right and propriety) not in unlawful tumults.

**V. 40.** *{in danger to be called in question for, &c.}* Being under the Roman state, the city was in danger to be fined or otherwise punished, for that causeless uproar, the Roman law was, that all factious and seditious persons under their government should lose their liberties, and Roman freedoms, so that this sober man blamed Demetrius and his fellows for troubling the people.

**V. 41.** *{he dismissed the Assembly}* Persuading them every man peaceably to repair home to his own house. So God used the carnal wisdom and eloquence of this heathen to the preservation of his servants. This example is not unworthy of prudent Christians in authority: to establish law, and justice (without which no society of man can flourish, or indeed subsist) to preserve public peace, and to appease all popular and unlawful outrages.

## CHAPTER 20

**Verse 1.** *{And embraced them}* The Greek word signifies also *to salute*, (1 Cor. 16. 19, &c.) with wishes of happiness. So Suidas says, ἀσπασμὸς, ἡ εὐχὴ, &c. it is most likely that thus they took leave, praying for each other’s prosperity.

*{into Macedonia}* See c. 19. 21, 22.

**V. 2.** *{much exhortation}* Confirming them in the faith and truth of the gospel.

**V. 3.** *{the Jews laid wait for him}* When they could not prevail against him by open violence, they assail him by treachery, and secret plotting against him.

*{to return through Macedonia}* And thence to go toward Syria: We may not tempt God, by running upon known dangers, but use the best means we can, to prevent and avoid them.

**V. 4.** *{Berea}* Chap. 17. 10.

*{of Derbe}* Chap. 14. 6.

*{Tychicus}* Ephes. 6. 21. Tit. 3. 12.

**V. 5.** *{Troas}* Chap. 16. 8. those seven companions and partakers of Paul’s travels and dangers mentioned, verse 4. which Luke the writer of this history, went before him to provide for his safety and necessary entertainment, some of them being left with him to minister to him, and assist him, as occasion served.

**V. 6.** *{we sailed away from Philippi}* Chap. 16. 12. there they embarked.

*{days of unleavened bread}* That is (seven days of) the *Passover*, wherein no leaven might be eaten, or found in their houses, Exodus 12. 19. In these days, *He would not set forth to travel, probably that he might not give offense to the weak Jews*: not that he might observe the now-antiquated ceremony of this feast, but that he might therein meet with occasion of propagating the gospel, by instructing them concerning the fulfilling and abrogation of *the Law by Christ*, which they did not yet sufficiently understand.

**V. 7.** *{first day of the week}* That is, the Lord’s Day, 1 Corinthians, 16. 2. Revelations 1. 10. vulgarly called Sunday: on this day, *Christians used to have their solemn Assemblies, for public Prayer, Preaching, and Administration of the Lord’s Supper.*

*{to take bread}* Chap. 2. 46. though by breaking of bread, the *Hebrews* understood any ordinary meals, yet this meeting seems to have been that they might hear the word, and receive the Holy sacrament of the Lord’s Supper.

**V. 8.** *{were many Lights}* Because many persons were there assembled: they would not be in darkness, lest they should bring any suspicion on their calling, and profession.

**V. 9.** *{and was taken up dead}* A remarkable example for *Church-sleepers*.

**V. 10.** *{fell on him}* See 1 Kings, 17. 21.

*{trouble not yourselves}* This he says to comfort them: be not too much perplexed at this accident, God has restored his life again.

**V. 11.** *{until break of day}* This was upon an extraordinary occasion, he being straitened in time upon his departure thence, and zealous of their edification and confirmation in the faith: A singular example of Paul’s unwearied diligence, and his auditory’s vigilance, patience and willingness to hear the word: It will once rise in judgment to condemn negligent speakers, and drowsy, secure, and impatient hearers of these times, who might deservedly hear, that which Christ once said to his infirm disciples; Matthew, 26. 40. *What could you not watch with me one hour?*

**V. 12.** *{were not a little comforted}* As it much afflicted them, that the preaching, which brought life and salvation to all believers, should be an occasion of death to the young man; so were they exceeding glad of his recovery, as for the young man’s sake, so specially, for their own confirmation by this miracle.

**V. 13.** *{we went before}* Namely, Luke who wrote this history, and the rest mentioned. v. 4.

*{unto Assos}* A chief city of Mysia, called also Apollonia. *Jerome de loc. Hebr. Act.* Plin. *lib. 5. cap. 30.* on the *Asiatic* shore.

*{to go afoot}* Overland.

**V. 14.** *{Mitylene}* An island of the Cyclades in the Ægean Sea: so named of the chief city thereof, now called *Metelino*, Jerome, *qu. s.* says, it is over against *Asia*. See Plin. *lib. 5. cap. 31.* See *Atlas min. G. Mercat. Amsterd. 1634. pag. 604.*

**V. 15.** *{Chios}* Another island of the same, between Samos and Lesbos, Plin. *qu. s.* Jerome, says it lies before Bithynia.

*{Samos}* Or, *Samus*, another isle over against Ephesus, in the Ægean Sea, Jerome *qu. s.* & Plin. *lib. 5. cap. 31.*

*{Trogyllium}* Another little isle in the bay near Ionia.

*{Miletus}* A town on the Ionian shore, not far from Ephesus, Plin. *lib. 5. cap. 29.* Jerome says, it is a Sea-town of Asia, distant from the mouth of the River *Meander* ten furlongs; here Paul spoke with the elders of the *Church* of Ephesus. verse 17. See *G. Mercator. Atl. min. Amsteld. 1634. pag. 600. col. 2. init.*

**V. 16.** *{if it were possible, &c.}* See Chapter 18. 21. See on verse 6.

**V. 17.** *{called the Elders of the Church}* That is, the governors, and pastors thereof. See on Chap. 11. 30.

**V. 18.** *{you know}* This he said, not ostenting his labors, but to propose an example to those whom he appointed to govern that *Church*: and in citing eyewitnesses, he secretly appeals to their own consciences herein.

*{from the first day}* Chap. 18. 1.

**V. 19.** *{serving the Lord, &c.}* This he speaks of his public office and ministry.

*{with all humility}* Or *modesty*. Humility is the bond of concord. *Ambr. ep. 84. lib. 10.* the safe and most becoming ornament of *Church-governors*, who ought not by any means to be lords over God’s heritage, 1 Pet. 5. 3.

*{temptations which befell me}* That is, afflictions, as 1 Peter 1. 6. James 1. 2.

**V. 20.** *{I kept nothing back}* See v. 35. Neither for fear nor favor of men, concealed I anything (necessary for your salvation) from you.

**V. 21.** *{repentance toward God}* Earnestly preaching that they should turn unto God, by repentance and newness of life and believe in Christ, who freely justifies the sinner.

**V. 22.** *{bound in the spirit}* The Holy Ghost, and my conscience leading me, Chap. 21. 14.

**V. 23.** *{abide me}* Or, *wait for me*; *Parata sunt* —— Syriac. That is, by the Jews who waited to take and destroy him.

**V. 24.** *{none of these things move me}* None of them deter me from the duty which God enjoins me, nor do I value my life, so that I may finish that work which God has enjoined me, with joyful success. See 2 Tim. 4. 7.

*{the Gospel of the grace}* That which testifies of the grace and mercy of God in Christ.

**V. 25.** *{the kingdom of God}* The gospel which is the word of his kingdom.

*{shall see my face no more}* This he says, to fasten his speech in their memories: We know what deep impression the last words of dying or departing friends take in our minds.

**V. 26.** *{I take you to record.}* μαρτύρομαι ὑμῖν, I witness to you: So Syriac, *Testor vobis*, and they were witnesses also.

*{I am pure from the Blood, &c.}* The fault is not in me, if any of you perish. See Chapter, 18. 6. and Ezekiel, 3. 18.

**V. 27.** *{all the Counsel of God}* Which concerns your salvation. See Luke 7. 30. John 15. 15.

**V. 28.** *{overseers}* ἐπισκόπους bishops: from the supreme authority of him that committed the charge unto them, he presses them to care and diligence in the execution of the same: this name of bishop here, as elsewhere is put for a pastor of the *Church*, or *Minister of the Word*.

*{with his own Blood}* Which he that is *Christ hath purchased with his own blood*, so the Syriac expresses it: that which appertains to the humanity of Christ is here attributed to his divinity; because of the union of the two natures in one person, and communion of proprieties.

**V. 29.** *{grievous Wolves}* Matthew 7. 15. John 10. 12. false teachers and bloody persecutors, so Christ called them. Matthew, 10. 16.

**V. 30.** *{perverse things}* Broaching false and heretical doctrines, through their ambition, to be singular and popular.

**V. 31.** *{by the space of three years}* Three years current, not complete: for he taught in their synagogues six months, and in the school of Tyrannus two years, Chap. 19. 8, 10. To which reckon the time of his going from them, to his present return through Macedonia and Achaia (where he stayed three months, Chap. 20. 3.) and you have about three years’ time.

**V. 32.** *{to the word of his grace}* That is, the gospel which declares his grace and free mercy; as if he had said, *I know it is a difficult thing, and beyond all human ability, but God whose cause and work it is in which you labor, is all-sufficient, he that has laid the foundation, can and will perfect all, enabling you to perform your parts therein committed to you.*

*{to build you up}* To increase you in grace and knowledge, to edify and finish his work in you, being grounded on Christ, See Ephes. 2. 20, &c. 1 Tim. 4. 16.

*{to give you an inheritance}* The kingdom of heaven, to which you have right, as sons by adoption in Christ, 1 Peter 1. 4, 5. Rom. 8. 17. He proposes the reward as to encourage them to labor in the gospel, so to prepare them to a patient enduring afflictions for the same.

**V. 33.** *{I have coveted no man’s silver}* Because covetousness is the root of all evil, 1 Tim. 6. 10. specially in prelates, (as we may see John 12. 6. Matt. 26. 15.) He still urges his own example, not boastingly, as ambitious of their applause, but testifying his sincerity, for their imitation. See 1 Tim. 3. 3. Numbers 16. 15. 1 Samuel 12. 3. Luke 12. 15. 2 Peter 2. 3. 1 Peter, 5. 2.

**V. 34.** *{yourselves know that these hands}* 1 Corinth. 4. 12. 1 Thessalonians 2. 9. 2 Thes. 2. 8. He does not herein propose a law, or rule to ministers (for he had not power to take away that which God had given them, Leviticus, 27. 32. Matt. 10. 10. (see on Luke 11. 42.) and which himself had so often asserted, 1 Cor. 9. 14. Gal. 6. 6.) but in case of necessity to advance the gospel.

**V. 35.** *{it is more blessed to give}* It is likely this was spoken by Christ, and it may also be gathered out of the Scriptures, though it be not expressly written.

**V. 36.** *{he kneeled down}* Though the heart and inward affection in prayer, be the chief thing which God looks on, yet is *he the God of the Body which he redeemed, as well as the Soul*, (1 Corinthians 6. 20.) therefore such reverend gesture, as kneeling, lifting up the hands, &c. have these excellent uses. First hereby we exercise body and soul in God’s service. Secondly, They stir up our own devotion and others, where they are present. Thirdly, thus we testify the performance of our duty to God before men: as lifting up the hands is a sign of confidence to be heard, so is the bending of our knees of humility, which two are the wings of faithful prayer. See Ephes. 3. 4. Acts 21. 5.

**V. 37.** *{all wept sore}* as those who dearly love, use to do at parting.

**V. 38.** *{sorrowing most of all, &c.}* In consideration of the loss, which themselves, and all Asia should have of him.

*{they accompanied him unto the ship}* As long as they could they would enjoy him; and as far as they could look after him. Thus are we pulled one from another here: we part weeping, but shall at last come together again with joy, never more to part.

## CHAPTER 21

**Verse 1.** *{After we were gotten from them}* ἀποσπασθέντας ἀπ’ αὐτῶν, *pulled from them*, as they who part unwillingly, or are drawn away by force or necessity: so the Greek word ἀποσπάω, *abstraho*, so σπάω, *traho* signifies. See Luke 22. 41. ἀπεσπάσθη ἀπ’ αὐτῶν —— *avulsus fuit ab iis*, *he was drawn*, or *pulled from them*.

*{and had launched}* Or *put from the shore.*

*{Coos}* Or *Cea*, an island in the Ægean sea, one of the Cyclades mentioned, Plin. *l. 6. c. 34.*

*{Rhodes}* Another island in the Lycian sea, near the continent of Asia; the most noble of all the Cyclades, says Jerome *de loc. Hebr. Act.* See *Atlas min. Gerard. Mercator. Amsterodam. 1634. pag. 605.*

*{Patara}* A city of Lycia, a province in Asia. Jerome, *ibid.*

**V. 2.** *{Phenicia}* Chapter 11. 19. See Plin. *lib. 36. cap. 26. init.*

**V. 3.** *{at Tyre}* Which was the metropolis of Phenicia.

**V. 4.** *{that he should not go up}* That is, they prophesied of his danger, if he went up; that they did by the Spirit of prophecy; but through human affection they dissuaded him from going, so this crosses not that which is said, Chapter, 20. 22.

**V. 5.** *{they all brought us on our way}* This was their love to him that taught them, and a good testimony of their sincerity, who thus honored him whom the world generally contemned and persecuted. See Galatians, 4. 14. 2 Timothy, 1. 15.

*{we kneeled down on the shore}* See c. 20. 36. c. 7. 60. Probably, they prayed, as for the happy success of the gospel to God’s glory, the increase of the church, and the kingdom of Christ, and those things which concerned their bodies and souls in general; so for Paul and those with him, and their good voyage in particular.

**V. 6.** *{they returned home again}* Everyone to his own affairs and calling.

**V. 7.** *{Ptolemais}* A Sea-town of Phenicia. See Plin. *lib. 5. cap. 5. & lib. 36. cap. 26. init.* The Syriac calls it עכו (see *not. Tremel.*) So Judges 1. 31. עכו. 70. Ἀγχὼ, Plin. *lib. 5. cap. 19.* says it was in time past called *Ace*. Here the Greek uses the latter name, and the Syriac retains the more ancient.

**V. 8.** *{we that were of Paul’s company}* See Chapter, 13. 13.

*{Caesarea}* Chap. 18. 22.

*{of Philip, the Evangelist}* or who preached the gospel. This office seems to have been between an apostle and a pastor, Ephes. 4. 11. c. 8. 12.

*{which was one of the seven}* c. 6. 5.

**V. 9.** *{which did Prophesy}* That is, by the instinct of Spirit, tell things to come, not speak in the church, as teachers, that is prohibited, 1 Corinthians, 14. 34. 1 Timothy, 2. 12.

**V. 10.** *{named Agabus}* See c. 11. 28.

**V. 11.** *{bind the man that oweth this girdle}* The effect see verse 33. This said he, not so much for Paul’s sake, as for others. Paul was not ignorant that bonds and afflictions abode him, c. 20. 23. but hereby his constancy to testify the truth of Christ appeared, to the great confirmation of the believers. In taking Paul’s girdle he imitated the prophets, who used to represent that which they foretold, by certain signs. See Isaia, 20. 2. Jer. 27. 2. Ezekiel 12. 3. Jeremia, 32. 28. which manner the false prophets also took up. 1 Kings 21. 11. Jer. 28. 10, 11. In that he alleges the dictate of God’s Spirit, it shows that the sufferings of God’s children, are neither fortuitous, nor in the power of persecutors, but in God’s providence. See John 19. 11.

**V. 12.** *{besought him not to go}* Out of their affection to him, and grief to lose so excellent a light, they importune him with tears, to forbear going to Jerusalem.

**V. 13.** *{to break mine heart}* Meaning that it was bitterness of heart to him, to see them afflicted with grief for him, who was resolved, come life, come death, to be obedient to the guidance of God’s holy spirit.

**V. 14.** *{we ceased, saying, the will of the Lord, &c.}* When they saw he would not be persuaded, they committed him to God who was able to preserve him: this resolution is the only means to bridle extravagant affections, when we can patiently submit ourselves, our desires, and counsels to the will of God, in assurance of his mercy, who is able to make all things work together for good, to them that love him, Rom. 8. See Matt. 26. 39, 42.

**V. 15.** *{took up our carriages, and went, &c.}* ἀποσκευασάμενοι, *putting up our carriages and necessaries*. That which the Latins gave by *convaso*, or *colligo necessaria ad iter*, the Greeks expressed by σκευάζομαι, in the active signification; and ἀποσκευάζω —— *vasa aufero*. Suidas interprets ἀποσκευὴ —— ἡ τῶν ἐπιτηδείων κομιδὴ —— *carriage of necessaries for a voyage*. So the Syriac here gives it *prepared ourselves*, putting all necessaries to carry with them. It shows Paul’s admirable constancy, thus meeting with his foreseen afflictions, for the testimony of the gospel; and that they who had dissuaded him from going up, did not therein so much regard their own interest as the church’s loss; for they would also share with him, seeing he would needs go. True faith is not daunted by the cross, but resolutely follows God’s calling.

**V. 16.** *{with whom we should lodge}* Meaning at Jerusalem, where now at the great solemnity of the feast of Pentecost lodgings used to be very scarce.

**V. 17.** *{received us gladly}* Cheerfully and lovingly entertained us; for albeit they had heard many reports concerning him, which the enemies had injuriously cast forth to cause the brethren to suspect and avoid him, yet would they not for such rumors condemn him, or exclude him from their society.

**V. 18.** *{with us unto James}* Chapter, 15. 13. It seems he was assigned to stay at Jerusalem, whereunto many believers resorting, it was necessary, that one of the apostles should reside there, to advise those Christians, who upon any emergent controversies, should repair thither; in which case, it seems the elders were called together.

**V. 19.** *{what things God had wrought}* Which he did not in ostentation of his own excellency, who elsewhere says he was the least of the apostles, 1 Cor. 15. 9. but to set out the goodness of God in his reception of the Gentiles, and mercy showed divers ways to them.

**V. 20.** *{Thousands}* μυριάδες, *myriads*, or *ten thousands*, He means a great number, He defines it not.

*{all zealous of the Law}* He means the major part of them were zealous (though without knowledge Romans, 10. 2. Gal. 1. 14.) they were tenacious of legal ceremonies, in which they had been bred.

**V. 21.** *{to forsake Moses}* which was an odious and false aspersion: for the doctrine of the gospel which Paul preached, was according to *Moses’ testimony of Christ*: only he taught, how we must pass from shadows and representations to the substance which was in Christ, the end of the law, (see Gal. 5. 1.) and how they must use all those things indifferently in wisdom and charity, as knowing our freedom in Christ. See Chapter, 16. 3. and 18. 18. Gal. 2. 3. Romans 14. 13, 15, 19, 21. 1 Cor. 8. 13.

**V. 22.** *{the multitude must needs come together}* That is, it will be known that you are here, and the unbelievers will persecute you, as an enemy to their law.

**V. 23.** *{we have four men}* Faithful brethren, who had a vow, as Chapter, 18. 18. Thus would they make present use of these men’s legal obligations, in the knowledge of Christian liberty, for the advancement of the gospel; and indeed, the end of this ceremony was thanksgiving, which is nothing contrary to Christian religion, but well agreeing with it.

**V. 24.** *{them take}* The meaning is, forasmuch as you did shear your head at Cenchrea, Chap. 18. 18. according to your vow of a Nazarite, show (now you are at Jerusalem) the accomplishment thereof, by joining in the charge with these four men, offering according to the law on that behalf, by which it may appear that you are not a despiser of the law of Moses.

*{shave their heads}* Numb. 6. 18. Chap. 18. 18. which (says a learned author) the Hebrews call, *the shaving of purity*, (or *of cleanness*) —— this shaving was in thankfulness, to signify that he had the perfection of his *Nazariteship* from God; and therefore burned his hair under his sacrifice —— to signify the end of his vow performed acceptable to God in Christ, and presented unto him by the Spirit, which is like unto fire, Matt. 3. 11.

**V. 25.** *{we have written}* Chapter, 15. 20. as if they had said, as concerning the Gentiles, they are to observe only the moral law, not the ceremonial, concerning the Jews, we are so far indulgent, as we may by any means bring them to Christ, in whom they shall better understand their liberty, and learn to leave off these beggarly and antiquated rudiments.

**V. 26.** *{then Paul took the men}* Obtemperating to the advice of James, and the rest of the brethren, and to show publicly that he was no enemy of the law.

*{to signify the accomplishment}* The day on which the vow of a Nazarite did end, he was by the law to offer certain sacrifices, Numb. 6. 13. and therefore it was necessary to acquaint the priest herewith.

**V. 27.** *{seven days were ended}* That is, a week after that Paul had performed this ceremony: or the last of the seven weeks.

*{the Jews which were of Asia}* Who dwelling in Asia, had former contests with him there: these bloodhounds never give over their chase, so long as they could pursue him.

**V. 28.** *{against the People and the Law}* Thus falsely do they calumniate him, who indeed taught them the true end and use of the law.

*{Greeks also into the Temple}* Meaning, that he had brought the uncircumcised beyond the court of the Gentiles, which was death to do.

**V. 29.** *{Trophimus an Ephesian}* See Chap. 20. 4.

*{and they supposed}* The wicked are full of jealousy, and therefore conclude, that must be so, which they once dream of.

**V. 30.** *{the City was moved}* So easily are multitudes engaged in sacred quarrels.

**V. 31.** *{chief captain}* See on John 18. 12. The Roman tribune, who was in the nature of a colonel, and had centurions, or captains of hundreds in his regiment, he had the command of the soldiers, and therefore the address was made to him in this tumult: they purposed to kill Paul; and dragging him out of the temple, forthwith the doors were shut, lest they should defile the temple with blood; they thought it great impiety to stain the pavements of the temple, but none to defile their souls with the guilt of blood: but God provided a timely rescue.

**V. 32.** *{left beating of Paul}* For fear of the Roman power which came to keep peace: for the Jews had not power of life and death permitted them by the Romans: the fear of men awed these persecutors, whom neither fear of God, respect of religion, reverence of the temple, nor regard of man could restrain; which sufficiently demonstrates that malice, not zeal set them on to these outrages.

**V. 33.** *{to be bound with two chains}* Possibly he took him for the author of this tumult. See Chap. 23. 27. here appears God’s providence rescuing Paul by his hands who had no such intention.

*{and what he had done}* However he bound him, yet had he so much justice, as not to condemn him unheard: possibly, he neither being acquainted with Paul, nor the cause of the tumult; and seeing the people so generally violent against him, might suspect him to be some great malefactor or dangerous person; therefore he commanded him to be bound, that he might appease the present rage of the multitude, who seeing him so bound, might suppose that he was reserved to some capital punishment.

**V. 34.** *{could not know the certainty}* In seditious tumults nothing is certainly understood, where all is acted by affection, not by reason; violence, not equity.

*{into the Castle}* Which joined to the temple, that he might shut out the multitude, and understand the truth of the matter.

**V. 35.** *{he was born of the soldiers}* Whether by the throng, the people pressing on, or taken up by the soldiers, to secure him from their fury, is not expressed; the Syriac seems to understand this latter: there was little favor intended him by them, who reserved him (as probably they conceived) to punishment.

**V. 36.** *{away with him}* See Luke 23. 18. John 19. 15. αἶρε αὐτὸν, that is, *kill him*: so Budaeus among other significations interprets αἴρω, *de medio tollo*. So they cried against Christ.

**V. 37.** *{may I speak, &c.}* εἰ ἔξεστί μοι, *if it be lawful*; It is a Greek manner of speaking, to crave leave to speak, or ask: he desires liberty to answer for himself, that being cleared, he might suffer no scandal to rest upon God’s word and truth, which he preached; and also to show his invincible zeal for the salvation of his kinsmen according to the flesh: amidst these hideous storms, their raging malice against his life; in chains, clamors and voices presently to murder him among armed troops apprehending him, he takes care for their edification and salvation, being desirous to speak that he might clear his innocency, and satisfy them.

**V. 38.** *{art not thou that Egyptian}* Specified, chap. 5. 36. of whom Josephus *antiq. lib. 20. cap. 11. & de bel. Jud. lib. 2. cap. 12.* he inquires of an Egyptian who in Tiberius Caesar’s reign, made an insurrection, and after many of his followers were slain, escaped away.

*{that were murderers}* σικαρίων, this was a word borrowed from the Latins, signifying men ready to murder; it seems they were so named of a short weapon which they did wear, called *sica*, a poniard, skein, pocket dagger, or stiletto, of the Chaldee word סכן *sachin*, a knife, or the Hebr. שכן *sachan*, Prov. 23. 2. Gr. μάχαιραν, *thy knife*; Gen. 22. 10. Abraham took המאכלת, τὴν μάχαιραν, Chald. סכינא his knife. See of these Joseph. *Ant. Jud. lib. 20.*

**V. 39.** *{of Tarsus}* Chap. 9. 32. See Joseph. *Antiq. l. 1. c. 11.* it was the metropolis of Cilicia: it is now called Hama, G. Mercat. *Atl. min. Asia min.*

**V. 40.** *{beckoned with the hand}* See Chap. 12. 17. and 13. 16. and 19. 33.

*{in the Hebrew tongue}* Whether in the pure Hebrew tongue (in use before their captivity, but in and before Christ’s time becoming a dead tongue) or in the Syriac, it is not certain: for that was called the Hebrew, both in respect of the derivation thereof from the Hebrew, and forasmuch as it was then the mother tongue of them who were Hebrews.

## CHAPTER 22

**Verse 1.** *{Brethren and Fathers}* Thus he insinuates to gain attention, wherein he respects the honor which God had given them, not what they now deserved.

**V.†** *{in the Hebrew, &c.}* Chap. 21. 40.

*{they kept the more silence}* Our mother tongue most affects us; therefore, he that could speak all tongues, used his own.

**V. 3.** *{at the feet of Gamaliel}* Meaning that he was a continual and diligent hearer of that grave doctor of the law, Gamaliel, he alludes to the posture of teachers standing in high places, and auditors sitting below them. See Deut. 33. 3. Luke 10. 39.

*{according to the perfect manner}* Syr. *perfectly taught in the Law of the fathers*: which relates not to the ceremonial law, as if he had attributed perfection to that which made nothing perfect, but *the bringing in of a better hope did.* See Heb. 7. 11, 19. it relates to Paul’s instruction, as if he had said, I was perfectly taught.

*{zealous towards God}* I did as seriously and heartily endeavor to serve God, as any of you now do in that way. See Philip. 3. 6.

**V. 4.** *{I persecuted}* See chap. 9. 1. &c. 1 Tim. 1. 13. &c. I was as violent against all Christians, as you now are.

**V. 5.** *{as also the high priest, &c.}* The things which I speak are well known to the high priest himself, and all the chief of the Sanhedrim.

*{unto the brethren}* Of the synagogue of the Jews at Damascus.

*{to be punished}* To be beaten or imprisoned. See on chapter 9. 2.

**V. 6.** *{come nigh unto Damascus}* See this whole passage, chap. 6. 1, 2. &c.

**V. 9.** *{they heard not the voice}* Chap. 9. 7. it is said they heard, τῆς φωνῆς, *a voice*: that is the voice of Saul speaking to one whom they saw not; they heard not the voice of Christ: some think they heard only a confused sound (that they might perceive it was from heaven) as the people did, John 12. 29. when some said it thundered, some said an Angel spoke, but understood not what was said.

**V. 10.** *{what shall I do}* Chap. 9. 6.

**V. 11.** *{for the glory of that light}* Which struck him blind for a time. He that had brought servants and others to assist him in carrying away the servants of Christ from Damascus bound to Jerusalem, is fain to use their help in leading him blind into Damascus: thus God made him an example both of his justice, punishing him, and of his mercy, converting him.

**V. 12.** *{a devout man &c.}* εὐσεβὴς, a pious or just man according to the law: as all the Jews which knew him could testify.

*{according to the Law}* Which he inserts to show them that the gospel was not contrary to, nor destructive of the law, but only the accomplishment thereof: for, a man zealous of, and learned in the law, by God’s appointment, persuaded him to embrace the gospel. Thus he speaks of Ananias, to distinguish him who was a believing Jew, from a proselyte or pious Gentile.

**V. 13.** *{came unto me}* See Chap. 9. 17.

**V. 14.** *{the God of our fathers, &c.}* No words sounded so well in the ears of a Jew, as these of which that nation often boasted. See John 8. 41. but hereby Paul proves his purpose; that he was no apostate from the God of Abraham, or his holy worship; but he still adored, and served the same God, as he had learned in the law, under whose shadows was veiled all that which more clearly shined in the gospel.

*{hath chosen thee}* προεχειρίσατο σε, has designed or appointed you, as chap. 26. 16. אקימך Syr. *akimach, constituit te*, *ordained* and made you ready to his hand; πρόχειρος, that is says Suidas, ὁ ἕτοιμος, one fitted, furnished, and prepared to some action.

*{and see that just one}* That is Christ, whom all the patriarchs, prophets, and believers, before his coming desired to see: Mat. 13. 17. Luke 2. 29. See 1 Cor. 9. 1. and 15. 8. he saw Christ, either in some supernatural gifts, a rapt into heaven, or some apprehensible representation, as chap. 7. 55.

**V. 15.** *{thou shalt be a witness}* God does not fit men with extraordinary knowledge for their own sakes only, but as finally to his own glory, so in that which is subordinate thereto, for others’ good: as trees bear not fruit for themselves, fountains run not for themselves, the sun shines not for himself, but for others’ good: that good is best which is most communicable. You who have attained this honor of Christ’s disciples (Luke 24. 48. Acts 1. 8. John 1. 7.) live not to yourselves, but to the testimony of God’s truth, and others’ good, by communicating to them that which you have heard and seen of Christ.

*{what thou hast seen}* That is, not only this precedent apparition, but principally in that vision described, 2 Cor. 12. 2. These things he specifies, not that his commission should be, only to preach thus much and no more than now he heard and saw, but because he might hereby gain belief to those things which should be revealed to him, and which he should preach concerning the mysteries of salvation by Christ, in that he might truly avow his calling to that change by Christ, and that he was an ear-witness and an eyewitness, of these things: therefore not only now, but also again before King Agrippa, chap. 26. 12. &c. and so in his epistle, 1 Cor. 9. 1. 1 Cor. 15. 8. he mentions the same,

**V. 16.** *{why tarriest thou?}* These were Ananias’ words to Saul, exhorting him to be baptized: we may suppose that he did further instruct him in the doctrine of the gospel, by larger discourses, which Luke for brevity’s sake here omits.

*{wash away thy sins}* Chap. 2. 38. by the Spirit of God giving the inward effect of baptism by the application of the blood and merits of Christ, by purifying of your soul by faith, regeneration, and newness of life. This shows the effect of baptism; not that the water has this power, but the blood of Christ thereby represented, 1 John 1. 7. yet we must know, that God proposes not bare and fallacious signs in the sacraments, but accompanies his ordinance with the power of the holy Ghost, making them effectual for that which he promises and proposes therein, which is remission of sins.

**V. 17.** *{I was in a trance}* The circumstances of the time, place, and ecstasy, show that God appeared to him, and assigned him to his office: whether this was that rapt, 2 Cor. 12. 4. it is uncertain.

**V. 18.** *{and saw him}* Saw him in that trance, verse 17. the appearance of the Lord so speaking to me.

**V. 19.** *{Lord they know that I am imprisoned}* This intimates Paul’s unwillingness to have preached to the Gentiles, and the condition of carnal reason, ever opposing the motions of God’s Spirit, by objecting vain excuses against his commands. See Exod. 3. 11. and 4. 10. Acts 10. 14. Jonah 4. 1. Some interpret this, (*they know*) as relating to the Jews; and that Paul here intimated his hope rather of doing good at Jerusalem, where they knew how zealous he had been of the law, than of going to the Gentiles: which being admitted there appears no reason, but that we may say, that others out of Jerusalem, yea and the Gentiles also might hear these things of him; and that Paul respected that which God had before said to Ananias concerning him, chap. 9. 15. *he is a chosen vessel unto me, to bear my name before the Gentiles, &c.* which doubtless Ananias would not conceal from him: so that he may here intimate an improbability of his reception among the Gentiles, who would also be afraid of him, as he was chap. 9. 13. all concludes his declining his mission to the Gentiles, and God’s express command for the same.

**V. 20.** *{and when the blood of thy Martyr Stephen}* Martyr is a Greek word signifying a witness: and is in a peculiar manner used to import him, who in his constant profession, in grievous punishment, or violent death, gives testimony to the truth of the gospel.

**V. 21.** *{depart, for I will send thee hence, &c.}* God pressed his own command, which, against all vain pretenses of man to the contrary, must be obeyed: He does not here promise success among the Gentiles, but expressly sends him to them; because he will have us learn to lay by all our own counsels, and simply and readily obey him in his commands.

**V. 22.** *{and then lift up their voices, &c.}* They could not endure to hear of his going to the Gentiles, therefore here they interrupt him with tumultuous clamors: so proud a confidence had they of themselves, as that they were impatient that God should bestow his own favors upon any but themselves, and yet themselves refused them: we shall not be grieved to have sinners partners with us in repentance, and mercy, if we belong to Christ, who for the redemption of his, refused not to be crucified between thieves and malefactors.

**V. 23.** *{cast off their clothes}* To prepare themselves to do violence.

*{threw dust in the air}* This is a description of a seditious, and enraged multitude.

**V. 24.** *{by scourging}* To compel him to confess something.

**V. 25.** *{A Roman}* That is, which is a freeman of Rome; not there born, but by privilege of that city where he was born, that is, Tarsus (Chapter 16. 37.) where was a Roman colony.

**V. 26.** *{When the Centurion heard it}* He being doubtful what to do herein, acquaints his colonel: that he might receive further order from him. (See on John 18. 12.) the reason why they would either so soon believe Paul, or did so much startle at the naming of the Roman privilege, was because it was death for any man to plead it falsely, and very criminal for any in public authority to violate it.

**V. 27.** *{Art thou a Roman?}* S. Luke sets down but the abridgment of the things: so that it is likely that Paul made it sufficiently appear to him, that he was thus privileged, before he loosed him.

**V. 28.** *{I was free born}* The colonel says, he purchased πολλῷ κεφαλαίῳ, it is an Hebraism: they put ראש *rosh*, an head, instead of *sum*, as Exodus 30. 12. Numb. 4. 2. take ראש 70. τὸ κεφάλαιον, *the sum*: So Budaeus shows that among other things, κεφάλαιον signifies a sum of money: the Syriac בכספא סגיאא *bechespo saggio*, *with much silver*, *much money*; he says, he bought τὴν πολιτείαν, that is, *jus civitatis*, *the freedom of the city* (of Rome) which Paul had freely, being a native of Tarsus; for it was the Roman custom to privilege some cities who had well deserved of them in the wars or other weighty affairs assisting them.

**V. 29.** *{examined him}* Or *tortured him*; they who were appointed to examine him by tormenting him to make him confess something against himself.

*{chief captain also was afraid}* In respect of the severe punishments appointed to any that did abuse a freeman of Rome, he feared being called in question for this business.

**V. 30.** *{would have known the certainty}* Being ignorant of the Jewish religion, he refers him to their council, but probably he would gladly have found some fault in Paul worthy of bonds, that he might have excused his own fault in binding and scourging him.

*{brought Paul down}* From the castle, verse 24.

## CHAPTER 23

**Verse 1.** *{In all good conscience}* 2 Tim. 1. 3. this said he to meet with their calumnies who accused him of profanation: not that he was free from all sin, (See Rom. 7. 23.) but to show that they injuriously accused him of contempt of God’s law, or profanation of the temple.

**V. 2.** *{the high Priest}* So the surrogates were also called. See on Luke 3. 2. and possibly this was no more: He commanded him to be smitten, because he conceived that Paul’s profession of good conscience obliquely taxed his judges, and accusers as if they had evil consciences, inasmuch as they persecuted him.

**V. 3.** *{thou whited wall}* False hypocrite. See Ezek. 13. 10. Matt. 23. 27.

*{contrary to the Law}* Levit. 19. 35. Deut. 25. 1, 2. bear you the person of a judge pretending to do justice, and openly transgress the law, by injuring the innocent, and uncondemned? God will once revenge this.

**V. 4.** *{Revilest thou God’s High Priest}* These parasites, to ingratiate with Ananias, seem zealous of his honor, taxing Paul’s just reproof; but cannot or will not understand the High priest’s injustice and hypocrisy; thus they dissemble, and censure Paul’s just liberty of speaking, as he was more than a private man, but see not the unjust liberty which the priest took to injure the apostle.

**V. 5.** *{I wist not brethren}* I knew him not to be a lawful High priest, who thus violates the law; and indeed he was but an usurper. See Josephus *antiq. l. 20. cap. 3. 5.* Chr. Helvic. *Theatr. Hist.
