Anno Christi 46.* some think that Paul having now of long time been a stranger at Jerusalem, did not know the High priest or surrogate: others think he spoke ironically; as if he had said, I could not perceive by this deportment that he was an High-priest: sure it is that he did but justly reprove the tyranny of an unjust judge.

*{thou shalt not speak evil}* Exod. 22. 28.

**V. 6.** *{I am a Pharisee}* Phil. 3. 5. Which he spoke not to get favor of the Pharisees, or to approve all their traditions, but out of a prudent policy to set division among them, for his better escape.

*{of the hope, &c.}* Chap. 24. 21. Not but that there were more points in question, but because for this chiefly the Sadducees hated and persecuted him.

**V. 7.** *{there arose a dissension, &c.}* God by dividing these enemies, provided for Paul’s safety: they differing in their tenets, would not agree to condemn him, as otherwise they intended: the unity and conspiracy of the wicked, cannot be lasting: for God infatuates their counsels: and divides them, that they may not be so mischievous as they desire, and otherwise would be.

**V. 8.** *{for the Sadducees}* Matt. 22. 23. Mark 12. 18. Seeing the writings of Moses which they received, so expressly speak of Angels it is probable that they did not absolutely deny them, but possibly might think them to be but a transitory apparition, created of God, only to signify his pleasure to men; or to execute some commands, and so to dissolve and cease again: whereas the Pharisees acknowledge both, that is, the resurrection from the dead, and the real subsistence of Angels and spirits, in the state of separation.

**V. 9.** *{but if a spirit, or, &c.}* If he have had any divine or extraordinary revelation.

**V. 10.** *{when there arose great dissension}* When they grew hot in their contest, and possibly were falling from words to blows; then the captain rescued Paul, to prevent violence and more tumult.

**V. 11.** *{the Lord stood by him}* In a vision to comfort and encourage him.

**V. 12.** *{banded together}* ποιήσαντες συστροφὴν *making a combination*. So Chapter 19. 40. *whereby we may give account* τῆς συστροφῆς ταύτης *of this concourse*, *Hujus conspiratae coitionis*, Budaeus. He shows that it comes from the word which signifies, *agmen cogere, & factionem ciere*, to gather a company together, and stir up a faction against some others. So Suidas interprets συστροφὴ, ἡ ὁμόνοια, καὶ ἡ ἐπὶ κακῷ συμφωνία, a consent and agreement, to do some mischief.

*{under a curse}* With an oath of execration; as wishing, that such or such a plague or mischief might befall them, if they did not that which they had combined in, ἀναθεματίσαι ἑαυτοὺς *extremis diris & execrationibus devovere*. So Budaeus says, the apostle calls ἀνάθεμα, *Quasi quorum caput devotum inferis est*. See Matthew, 26. 74. Romans, 9. 3.

**V. 13.** *{more than forty}* This shows how great a party Satan has ever ready to oppose the gospel, and how prone men are to combine for any mischief.

**V. 14.** *{to the chief Priests}* Being confident of their assistance, otherwise they might have expected sharp reproof, and discovery of their intended murder, had their priests been any better than impious murderers themselves: for such are they, who having power, hinder not murder what they can.

*{we have bound ourselves under a great curse}* ἀναθέματι ἀνεθεματίσαμεν —— it is an usual Hebraism —— with a curse we have cursed, that is, grievously cursed, or bound ourselves with direful curses; that they would eat nothing until they had killed Paul, was their agreement and condition; the curse was the bond, whereby they obliged themselves: this shows their rage and bloody minds, more solicitous for others’ destruction, than their own safety: had they well considered, they might easily have known that many accidents, which they could not foresee, might hinder *their endeavor* and frustrate *their design*.

**V. 15.** *{ye with the council}* That is, with the consent, or in the name of the council, whose minds it seems they knew were bent to join with them to destroy Paul: the most pernicious impiety loves the veil of public authority; and Satan never did more mischief than by gaining councils to his party.

*{signify to the chief captain that he bring down, &c.}* Signify the cause or end of your request, (for they had no power to command the captain) that you would *inquire something more perfectly, &c.* thus they would carry on their design, under pretense of justice and religion.

*{are all ready to kill him}* Here appears unto how desperate a condition religion was now come among them, when bloody conspirators dared come to the chief priests, not only to acquaint them with a design so mischievous, but also to implore their assistance, therein.

**V. 16.** *{when Paul’s sister’s son heard, &c.}* God suffers the wicked to plot, and to be near the execution; and then defeats their counsels. There is no wisdom against God. See Prov. 21. 30. Isa. 8. 10. and 33. 9, 10, 11. Ester 9. 1. &c. Acts 12. 6. 1 Sam. 20. 35.

**V. 17.** *{called one of the Centurions}* That is, as the Syriac interprets שדר *shaddar*, *sending he called*; he sent to entreat that he might speak with him; and that he would do so much as bring the young man to Lysias, their chief commander. So Verse 18. and *prayed me to bring, &c.*

**V. 18.** *{Paul the prisoner}* ὁ δέσμιος, &c. Paul who is bound, for they used to put bonds on prisoners.

**V. 19.** *{went with him aside}* Supposing he had some secret to impart unto him, as indeed he had; in both these commanders we have an example of courtesy: in the one readily bringing the young man to be heard; in the other, as readily giving him audience and dispatch: in both of God’s good providence inclining their hearts to the preservation of the apostle.

**V. 20.** *{the Jews have agreed}* That is, some of them, or the chief of them, have conspired; Syriac, *have consulted*.

**V. 21.** *{but do not thou yield unto them}* I beseech you let them not by any pretenses, or persuasions prevail with you; μὴ πεισθῇς, be not you persuaded by them: so the word is used, c. 21. 14.

*{for there lie in wait}* Herein he shows the conspiracy of the Jews, the ground and cause of his request.

*{now are they ready, looking for a promise from thee}* Expecting what answer you will make them.

**V. 22.** *{see thou tell no man}* Which charge he might give him in respect of his own purpose, lest the matter being revealed the enraged Jews should attempt some other mischief: or in respect of the safety of the young man, upon whom probably they would turn their malice, because he defeated their plot on Paul.

**V. 23.** *{called unto him}* Knowing the malice of that nation, and their implacable fury showed in former actions, he easily believed the young man’s relation, and provided accordingly.

*{and spearmen two hundred}* δεξιολάβους such as held and carried javelins or darts in their right hands, and were of lifeguards to princes or commanders; δεξιολάβος is *stipator, qui alicui latus munit*, Jo. Drus. Suidas interprets it by παραφύλαξ, a keeper or guarder of a side. The Syriac here paraphrases, *qui mittentes jaculantur dextra*, or *spiculatores à dextra*, as reading δεξιοβόλους, *dartmen*.

**V. 24.** *{and bring him safe to Felix}* Lest the Jews should waylay and murder him: thus God accomplishes his will in the preservation of his servants; many times by their hands, who intend only their own ends.

**V. 25.** *{a letter after this manner}* Either in this form, or the contents thereof were as follows.

**V. 26.** *{sendeth greeting}* Wishes much health and happiness; or as the Syriac, *Peace*, So the Greeks used χαίρειν, to greet or salute, as Suidas notes in the beginning of their epistles. Some wrote εὖ πράττειν, or εὖ διάγειν to the same purpose, *well to do*, or *well to live*. This worldly-wise man, adoring the rising sun, gives this unworthy Felix, the title of *Most excellent Governor*; whereas he was indeed, but an obscure fellow thus advanced (by the abused favor of Claudius Caesar) to great wealth and honor. Paul gives the same to Festus, c. 26. 25. in order to God’s command for honoring superiors, Prov. 24. 21.

**V. 27.** *{this man was taken of the Jews}* This he writes in favor of Paul, who being by privilege a Roman, was illegally and evilly entreated of the Jews: God guides the tongues and pens of unbelievers to the advantage of his.

*{with an army}* That is, a party, or some companies of soldiers, c. 21. 32.

**V. 28.** *{and when I would have known the cause}* He conceals what he first did to Paul, and possibly touched in conscience, labored to make him amends, or at least to free himself from blame and danger, for so using an uncondemned Roman. See c. 22. 29.

*{I brought him forth into their council}* Who were to judge concerning matters of religion, See c. 22. 30.

**V.†** *{to be accused of questions of their Law}* Matters concerning the Jewish religion.

*{worthy of death or bonds}* Nothing but that which was worthy of favor; but he writes according to the sense of the Romans; as if he had said, nothing by our laws and imperial constitutions criminal.

**V. 30.** *{that the Jews laid wait, &c.}* This probably he writes as to condemn the Jews’ tumultuous practices, illegal and mischievous conventions against Paul’s life, and the empire’s peace; so also to give account of his intention in sending him with such a guard to Felix.

**V. 31.** *{brought him by night}* To avoid all further insurrections, and attempts, which the Jews would probably have made in Jerusalem.

*{to Antipatris}* A city in the tribe of Manasseh, between Jerusalem and Cesarea, *formerly called Capharsarama*, or *Capharsalama* (Adricom. *ter. sanct. Manas. 8.*) *Herod building or repairing it*, called it by his father Antipater’s *name*, Jerome *de loc. Hebr. Act.* See 1 Mac. 7.

**V. 32.** *{they left the horsemen to go with him}* Probably they were so commanded to do by their Colonel Lysias. And Paul was at that distance from Jerusalem, secure from the present pursuit of his enemies.

**V. 33.** *{to Caesarea}* See on Matt. 16. 13. and Jer. *de loc. heb. act.*

**V. 34.** *{of what Province he was}* By this name, the Romans called some countries by them subdued, and this was a common custom of examination of strangers, to ask, what countrymen, or whence they were. See 2 Sam. 1. 13. John 19. 9. Probably Felix did it, that he might be satisfied whether Paul belonged to his jurisdiction.

*{Of Cilicia}* chap. 6. 9.

**V. 35.** *{when thy Accusers are also come}* Lest the Jews should complain of an undue course of justice, that they had not audience: These very heathens knew it was not just to judge, before both parties complainant and defendant were lawfully heard: if it be enough to accuse, who shall be innocent? and if it be enough to deny, who would be guilty?

*{in Herod’s judgment hall}* Or, *Herod’s palace*. *Pretory*, or place which he built, or appointed to keep courts, and hear causes in: a fair prison, if any could be so.

## CHAPTER 24

**Verse 1.** *{Ananias the high Priest}* Chapter 23. 2.

*{descended}* From Jerusalem to Cesarea: Mark how industrious and unwearied mischief is; the High-priest and elders with Tertullus in pursuance of their malicious design: from Jerusalem to Cesarea, and that speedily travel they would spare no pains, nor lose any time: it were happy if men would be thus minded for good.

*{with the elders}* These came to countenance the Jews’ cause against Paul with show of great reverence, but indeed; as Ambrose said to Valentinian the emperor *ep. 5. 31. non annorum canicies est laudanda sed morum*. it is probable that all these came down, as to accuse Paul, so principally to prevent their own danger: for understanding that Lysias had sent away Paul, and written letters concerning the business, they might well fear some informations against themselves who had been so tumultuous.

*{a certain orator}* An advocate, or lawyer.

**V. 2.** *{by thee we enjoy}* To gain favor against Paul this flatterer thus begins with applauding Felix, whereas histories show, how cruelly and impiously he behaved himself against their nation.

**V. 3.** *{most noble Felix}* Felix was of obscure parentage, but Claudius Caesar promoted him to honors; he had by his diligence suppressed the sedition under Eleazarus: otherwise, he was covetous and a cruel man. See Josephus *Ant. Jud. l. 20. c. 11. & de bel. Jud. l. 2. c. 12.* See also on Chapter, 23. 26.

**V. 4.** *{that I be not further tedious}* He intimates that he would not long detain him from other his weighty affairs; Greek ἐνκόπτω, which among other things, signifies to hinder one’s course of affairs, (1 Thessalonians 2. 18. Galatians 5. 7. Romans 5. 22.) as here it might have been by a tedious discourse. Syr. *ne autem fatigemus te multis*: *but lest we should weary you with many words*.

**V. 5.** *{a pestilent fellow}* λοιμὸν, a plague. As the Latins put *scelus* in the abstract, *pro scelesto*, wickedness for a wicked fellow.

*{sect of the Nazarenes}* αἱρέσεως, *of the heresy*: this name they gave the Christians in those days, from Jesus of Nazareth, according to which they called him in contempt Jesus of Nazareth, from the city where he was brought up; afterward it was peculiarly attributed to the sect of heretics, of which *Epiph. haer.* 29. See on chap. 26. 5. See on verse 14 of this chapter.

*{a mover of sedition}* This aspersion he cast on Paul, probably because he knew how odious sedition was to the Romans, who being conquerors of Judaea, and many other nations, were very solicitous to hold that which they had gotten with much charge, hazard, and effusion of blood.

**V. 6.** *{profane the temple}* Chap. 21. 28.

*{according to our Law}* That is, to examine the quality of the offense, which the Romans permitted them to do; but not to execute sentence of death.

**V. 7.** *{chief captain Lysias}* The colonel Lysias. This they object as a secret accusation of Lysias, in hope to balance his information thereby, intimating that he restrained them of that liberty contrary to the Roman grant.

**V. 8.** *{accuse him}* That is, Paul.

**V. 9.** *{saying, that these things were so}* These false witnesses were ready to avow and confirm Tertullus’ lie.

**V. 10.** *{a judge}* A governor. Therefore you are experienced in their customs, and can more equally and readily judge.

**V. 11.** *{yet but twelve}* Meaning, he wanted not time, had he been so minded to stir up sedition: seven days were spent in the performance of that ceremony, which he undertook, that he might gain the weaker Jews to Christ, and five were spent since.

**V. 12.** *{disputing with any man}* Though he might lawfully have spoken in the temple, as being an apostle of Christ the Lord of the temple; yet he denies that he then and there used any such liberty.

**V. 13.** *{neither can they prove the things}* Being false calumnies: Greek, οὔτε παραστῆσαι δύνανται—neither can they set before you, as a thing evident, or to be confirmed by any certain arguments, as Acts 1. 3. Syr. neither have they ability *ut probent coram te*, to prove or make appear to, or before you, etc.

**V. 14.** *{which they call heresy}* Or *a sect*, as verse 5. The scribes and Pharisees called the doctrine of Christ so in the worst acceptation, as the enemies still do; this word, αἵρεσις, was sometimes taken in the good part, as chapter 26. 5. Some men’s misunderstanding prejudices not truth.

*{believing all things which are written in the law, etc.}* Herein he shows his consent with the church of God: and that Christians have the same faith which the patriarchs and prophets had. See 1 Cor. 10. 1, 2, etc.

**V. 15.** *{which they themselves also allow}* That is, the more prudent party, the Pharisees: the Sadducees denied it.

*{just and unjust}* Dan. 12. 2. John 5. 28, 29, for *we must all appear before the judgement seat of Christ, etc.* 2 Cor. 5. 10. This one article of his faith he mentions as the principal ground of faith and obedience, which he elsewhere more largely expresses, 1 Cor. 15. 13, etc.

**V. 16.** *{a conscience void of offence}* Because I know I must stand before God’s tribunal, I always endeavor to keep a good conscience, without which no man truly believes, 1 Tim. 1. 19.

**V. 17.** *{after many years}* It was a long time since he had been at Jerusalem: he came but seldom to Jerusalem, which will appear, if we consider that which himself writes, Gal. 2. 1, and therewith, this sacred history; wherein we read of Paul’s ascending to Jerusalem, only four times: first, Acts 9. 26, etc. Gal. 1. 18. Secondly, when he brought relief to the brethren, Acts 11. 30 and 12. 25; between these his comings they reckon eleven years. Thirdly, Acts 15. 1, etc. The fourth going up, we read of Acts 21. 17. Now the fourteen years mentioned Gal. 2. 1 must be reckoned from his conversion to the second going up to Jerusalem.

**V. 18.** *{whereupon}* Upon bringing the bringing up of relief from Antioch to the brethren which dwelt in Judaea, chap. 11. 29, 30. *{certain Jews}* Chap. 21. 27.

**V. 19.** *{who ought to have, etc.}* For his present accusers spoke but by hearsay, or that which themselves invented to hurt and disadvantage him: as if he had said, if there had been any such thing whereof they accuse me; or if any man could affirm such things against me; why are they not here present? Why appear they not against me?

**V. 20.** *{while I stood before the council}* That is, at Jerusalem. Let them say, if any can, what evil they found in me then, chap. 23. 9.

**V. 21.** *{touching the resurrection}* Chap. 23. 6. I then said in my defense, *of the hope and resurrection of the dead, I am called in question*; whereupon arose contention between the Sadducees, and Pharisees, that was their fault not mine.

**V. 22.** *{having more perfect knowledge of that way}* Syr. *because he plainly knew that way*: because he perceived that the Jews had out of malice accused him: well knowing their qualities, and how falsely and frivolously they had calumniated him, loading him with foul words, not matters.

**V. 23.** *{let him have liberty}* Let him not be kept close prisoner, but under a keeper with some chain on him. See chap. 26. 29 and 28. 20. Though he acquitted him from all crimes, yet he detained him to gratify the Jews, or expecting a bribe, *Chrys.*

**V. 24.** *{Drusilla}* This was Agrippa’s sister, a vicious woman. See *Joseph. Ant. Jud.* 20. chap. 5.

**V. 25.** *{Felix trembled}* Out of horror of conscience, being a wicked man.

**V. 26.** *{that money should have been given him}* Either he supposed Paul to be no vulgar man whom the Jews so violently prosecuted, but of some great concealed estate, (knowing that these storms of envy rarely trouble the low shrubs) or that his friends and disciples would have redeemed him with some considerable bribe. Here is an example of a mercenary and corrupt judge who will be hired to do justice.

**V. 27.** *{to shew the Jews a pleasure}* To do the Jews some pleasure, and after the many injuries he had done them, to pacify them, that they might not follow him with complaints. He was careful to please the Jews, though with displeasing God, because he loved himself, not God, nor good men.

## CHAPTER 25

**Verse 1.** *{When Festus was come into the Province}* After Felix was removed from that government, and Portius Festus made president of Judaea, reduced into the form of a province by the Romans.

**V. 2.** *{informed him against Paul}* Being thus precipitated by the spirit of Satan to persecute the gospel, they could not rest; and the leading actors were they who should have protected and taught the truth: so dangerous is the corruption of those who are in authority.

**V. 3.** *{and desired favour against him}* To wit, against Paul, that he would send for him to Jerusalem, that they might kill him by the way.

**V. 4.** *{that Paul should be kept}* Probably, Felix had informed him of the Jews’ malice and design against Paul: certainly, it was God’s providence so to preserve him.

**V. 5.** *{are able}* That can conveniently come.

*{if there be any wickedness in him}* ἄτοπον here (as Luke 23. 41) imports not some foolish or absurd thing, but something wickedly done. So 2 Thess. 3. 2. So the Syriac expresses it by סכלו *sachlu*, *sin*, *wickedness*, Matt. 18. 35. Indeed all wickedness is folly: the Hebrews well expressed it, as Josh. 7. 15, *because he hath wrought* נבלה *nebala*, *folly* (that is, wickedness) *in Israel*. 70. ἀνόμημα ἐν Ἰσραὴλ. See Judges 20. 10. 2 Sam. 13. 12. Job 42. 8.

**V. 6.** *{more than ten days}* Syr. *eight or ten days*. Some Greek copies have οὐ πλείους ἢ δέκα, *not more than ten, etc.*

**V. 7.** *{many and grievous complaints, etc.}* αἰτιάματα, criminations, or indictments: this is the condition of God’s servants, to be loaded with calumnies and slanders. See 2 Cor. 6. 4, 8. Yet happy are they in their innocency, by which their enemies make themselves so much the more unhappy, by how much more they belie them, and are injurious to them: see Matthew 5. 11, 12.

**V. 8.** *{neither against the Law of the Jews}* Neither against the law of God professed by the Jews, nor against the temple (though they charged him with the profanation thereof;) neither against Caesar, had he practiced anything.

**V. 9.** *{to do the Jews a pleasure}* To gain the people’s good word, and opinion of him.

**V. 10.** *{where I ought to be judged}* Doubting the sincerity of the judge, whose warping toward popularity he possibly perceived, and also the wait-laying of the Jews.

**V. 11.** *{I refuse not to die}* As if he had said, I beg no favor.

*{no man may deliver me}* No man justly can.

*{I appeal unto Caesar}* Syr. *protectiones Caesaris appello*, I appeal to Caesar’s protections. I require to be judged by Caesar himself. Thus claims he his Roman privilege.

**V. 12.** *{with the council}* Without whose consent he could do nothing.

**V. 13.** *{king Agrippa}* son of Herod Agrippa, whose death is mentioned, chapter 12. See *Joseph. Antiq.* lib. 18. 11 and lib. 19, chapter 5.

*{and Bernice}* Sister to the same Agrippa, with whom he lived in public infamy, and vehement suspicion of incest.

**V. 14.** *{Festus declared Paul’s cause}* Possibly, discoursing of many things, at last he fell on Paul’s business: however, God’s providence so disposed it for Paul’s deliverance from the Jews: he will never be wanting to his; where it shall make for his honor and their good, he will raise up some Festus to declare the oppressed man’s cause. See Genesis 41. 9, etc. 1 Sam. 18. 3 and 1 Sam. 19. 2, etc.

**V. 15.** *{to have judgment against him}* δίκην. Syr. *that I would judge him*. The word signifies, an action entered, or to be entered into a court of justice: so they said, δίκην ἐπάγειν *Budaeus*. Which the Latins expressed by, *dicam impingere, scribere*, or *diem dicere*, that is, *deferre, criminis postulare*, to implead or to indict one of some crime; or to enter an action against one, that they may have judgment against the impleaded. Sometimes it signified the penalty or punishment, the proceed and result of the judgment. Likely enough, here they desired to have him condemned, *indictâ causâ*, without hearing the cause, as they had done against Christ, and that might be the cause of Festus’ reply, verse 16, concerning the Roman custom.

**V. 16.** *{To deliver any man to die}* χαρίζεσθαι signifies to give freely, without cause or merit: he means for any man’s pleasure, or favor to execute men without lawful hearing of both parties, he is an unjust judge, who gives (though the most just) sentence, before legal hearing; the heathens gave leave even to the sacrilegious, traitorous, witches, or any malefactors to defend themselves in judgment, *nec praedamnari quemquam incognitâ causâ licet*. Etc. *Lactant.* lib. 5. chap. 1. Neither may any man be condemned before his cause is known: see Deut. 13. 14, 15 and 17. 4. Deuteronomy 19. 18. Yet such has ever been the Devil’s malice against the servants of Christ, to thrust on impious judges to condemn them without hearing them; because he knows the power of truth; if that come to be scanned, his kingdom falls: see John 7. 51.

**V. 17.** *{without any delay}* He commends his own justice herein: long-delayed justice often proves more heavy, than speedy injustice.

*{on the judgment seat}* Where they used to hear the weighty matters.

**V. 18.** *{no accusation of such things}* They accused him of sedition, but he took it for a mere calumny; because they alleged, but convinced not: objected much, but proved nothing.

**V. 19.** *{of their own superstition}* See on chapter 17. 22. Syr. *concerning their religion*; so profanely did heathens think and speak of true religion.

*{of one Jesus}* By this it appears how little Paul’s preaching had wrought upon Festus; and that many things were controverted between Paul and the Jews, which Luke, for brevity’s sake omitted. See 1 Cor. 1. 23.

**V. 20.** *{Because I doubted, etc.}* Or, *I was doubtful to enquire hereof*: this was his pretense; but the truth was, he would fain get favor with the Jews, verse 9. Here he betrays his own injustice: for why did not he acquit him, against whom nothing was proved?

**V. 21.** *{to the hearing of Augustus}* Or, *to the judgement*.

*{Augustus}* Nero was at that time emperor of Rome, but for the honor of their former emperor Augustus, they styled his successors after his name.

**V. 22.** *{I would also hear the man,}* So Herod desired to see Christ, Luke 23. 8 (and to hear John Baptist, Mark 6. 20) not to be converted, but to satisfy his own curiosity: Agrippa being born and bred among the Jews, had, doubtless heard of Christian religion, and perhaps of Paul also: therefore he might happily desire to see and hear him. See here the providence of God: by all these injuries, accusations, layings of wait, as Chrysostome notes, Paul becomes λαμπρότερος, *more eminent*: but for such his sufferings, none of these princes would have vouchsafed him audience with so much silence and patience.

**V. 23.** *{into the place of hearing}* εἰς τὸ ἀκροατήριον, *into the place of audience*, or *hearing causes*. Syriac, *into the house of Judgement*; this being public, hither came Agrippa, and Bernice with great pomp, that is, in a princely manner, and great state.

**V. 24.** *{See this man}* Thus God pleases to suffer his dear children to be made a spectacle unto the world, to men and angels. See 1 Cor. 4. 9.

**V. 25.** *{That he had committed nothing}* Thus the more malicious the Jews were in accusing Paul, the more (by God’s providence) was he justified by his judges. See chapter 23. 29 and 24. 24. Truth and innocency shine more clearly by their opposition.

**V. 26.** *{unto my Lord}* Unto Caesar, whose deputy I am in this place: this high title, Augustus, Tiberius, and some others, would not have given unto themselves or any man; it being, what they thought too great, and something newer than the Roman empire: but, as, אדני *adni*, was a civil title among the Hebrews, Gen. 32. 18, etc., and κύριος among the Greeks, so became *Dominus* to be among the Latins.

**V. 27.** *{It seemeth to me unreasonable, etc.}* All this made for Paul’s justification, and the Jews’ just blame, that the judge openly professed, that (notwithstanding their turbulent clamors, and frequent calumnies there appeared no crime of Paul’s whereof the deputy could inform Caesar), he thought it unreasonable to send any prisoner under the general notion of a delinquent, whereas, no particular thereof could be proved against him.

## CHAPTER 26

**Verse 1.** *{Paul stretched forth the hand}* This was with them a usual gesture of those who began to speak in public. See Prov. 1. 24. Isa. 65. 2.

**V. 2.** *{I think myself happy}* I take it for my advantage, that I may now speak before you, who know the law and the Scriptures; and are therefore a more competent judge, than he that is a stranger to all.

**V. 3.** *{I beseech thee to hear me patiently}* As he commended his knowledge; so he entreats his patience; they that know most, ought to be most attentive; where matters of religion are controverted, impatience in hearing matters of so high concernment, concludes ignorance, or impiety.

**V. 4.** *{my manner of life}* Because the calumnies cast on him by the Jews, might otherwise have prejudiced the cause of Christ, he first washes them off, by appealing to his accusers’ own consciences; who knew his conversation from his youth up.

**V. 5.** *{Sect}* Here αἵρεσις is taken in good part, or at least according to the vulgar opinion, and supposition of his adversaries, as if he had said, the Jews themselves account the sect of Pharisees to be the most holy and religious; and they well know that I was trained up in the same, and zealously professed the same; therefore ought they not to reprove my former course of life; if they will be constant to their principles: he calls it ἀκριβεστάτην, αἵρεσιν *the straitest*: or *most exquisite or exact sect*: there were then three sects among the Jews. Pharisees, Sadducees and Essenes: see *Jos. Antiq.* lib. 18. chap. 2. *Epiph. advers. haeres.* lib. 1. haer. 16. 1. 2. ib. haer. 14. ib. haer. 10. *D. Godwin. Moses & Aaron.* lib. 1. chap. 10. 11. 12. These were the principal, and among them the sect of Pharisees was the most strict. The Syr. here interprets αἵρεσιν, *the principal doctrine of the Pharisees*.

**V. 6.** *{of the promise}* Concerning the Messias, and his kingdom, and life eternal by him, in whom all the true worshipers of God among the Jews believed and reposed all their confidence: hereby he obliquely taxes the vain and preposterous persuasion of the Jews pretending to the covenant of God with them and their fathers, and the promises, and yet obstinately rejecting Christ whom God promised to send them, and in whom alone all the promises of God for their eternal happiness were grounded and ratified to all true believers.

**V. 7.** *{twelve tribes}* Meaning the remnant of the twelve tribes, as they returned from the captivity, 2 King. 17. 6.

*{serving God}* According to the discipline of the law.

**V. 8.** *{Why should it be thought incredible}* He that gave life and being, to that which before, was not, cannot he restore life to the dead? *idoneus est reficere, qui fecit*; *Tertul.* *He is best able to repair us who made us*, and indeed we may behold *a continual resurrection* (*Clem. Roman. ad Cor.* ep. 1) *In the several creatures; from the death*—like *winters unto the life of spring*, the several seeds buried under the winter clods, arise up in their several kinds much more glorious. See 1 Chronicles 15. 37, 38.

**V. 9.** *{verily I thought with myself}* He prepossesses an objection: some might say, were you brought up a Pharisee? You never learned of them to believe, that Jesus was the Messias: true indeed (says he) I was an adversary to Christians, as much as any of them; and thought myself bound in conscience to endeavor their suppression; neither did any man persuade me to the contrary, but God revealed to me from heaven, the truth of Christ, and diverted my course: therefore he here inserts the history of his admirable conversion.

**V. 10.** *{which thing, etc.}* Chap. 8. 3.

**V. 11.** *{Compelled them}* Either he effected this by the cruelty he used, or gave them occasion so to do by his own example. 1 Tim. 1. 13.

**V. 12.** *{as I went to Damascus}* Chap. 9. 2.

**V. 13.** *{at midday}* This he says to declare, that it was no dream, fantasy, or vision of the night; but the certain knowledge, of waking and attentive minds.

**V. 14.** *{I heard a voice}* Chap. 9. 4.

**V. 15.** *{who art thou Lord?}* Chap. 9. 5.

**V. 16.** *{but rise, etc.}* Chap. 9. 6.

**V. 17.** *{from the people}* Of the Jews, they shall ungratefully handle you (as madmen use their physicians) but I will deliver you.

**V. 18.** *{to open their eyes}* This is attributed unto ministers, as instruments and ordinary means, the effect whereof is only in the Spirit of God, working thereon. See 1 Cor. 3. 5, 6.

**V. 19.** *{The heavenly vision}* Gal. 1. 16, 17.

**V. 20.** *{unto them of Damascus}* Chap. 9. 20.

**V. 21.** *{about to kill me}* Chap. 21. 27, 28, etc.

**V. 22.** *{obtained help of God}* God assisting me, it were high ingratitude not to do this work cheerfully, and to live to him, by whom I live and am preserved.

*{witnessing both to small and great}* To all sorts of hearers, noble and obscure, without exception of persons. So Rev. 13. 16. Revel. 19. 18. So the Hebrews used to speak, as we read. 1 Sam. 30. 19, מן־הקטן ועד־הגדול *min hakkaton ve had haggadol*, *from the little one, and unto the great one*, by which Paul intimates here to Agrippa, that the things which he spoke as much concerned the king in his own person, as the poorest hearer: the gospel is a general doctrine, which Christ would have communicated to all sorts of people. See Psal. 49. 1, 2. Mat. 28. 19. Mark 16. 15. See Rom. 1. 14, for all are one in Christ. See 1 Cor. 12. 13. Gal. 3. 28. Col. 3. 11.

*{the Prophets and Moses}* Whose general authority the Jews dare not deny. And this he constantly affirms, that all might understand that he taught no other doctrine than that which Moses and the prophets taught from the beginning; so far was he from innovation.

**V. 23.** *{Christ should suffer}* That Christ should not be a temporal king, as the Jews dreamed, but a spiritual and eternal; redeeming and saving us, by suffering that which was due to us.

*{the first}* As head of his church, to give them a spiritual life. 1 Cor. 15. 20. Col. 1. 18. Rev. 1. 5. First as the author of life, not the first in time; for some were raised from the dead before he died.

*{shew light}* The knowledge of the gospel. Luke 2. 32. Matthew 4. 16.

*{unto the people}* Meaning the Jews.

**V. 24.** *{with a loud voice}* Chrysostome understands this as an effect of Festus’ anger.

*{Paul thou art beside thyself}* μαίνῃ, *Thou art distracted*, or *mad*. Here note the profane censure of carnal men, concerning the mysteries of eternal life and salvation by Christ. See 2 Kings 9. 11. See on Mark 3. 21.

*{much learning doth make thee mad}* You over-study yourself; or meddle with matters too deep for your apprehension, such estimates do the ignorant set on knowledge: so do they blasphemously conceive the gospel to be the foolishness of preaching, 1 Cor. 1. 18. It seems so to them that perish: to them that shall be saved, it is the power and wisdom of God. See Rom. 1. 16. 2 Cor. 4. 3, 4. It is a dangerous symptom to distaste the gospel of Christ.

**V. 25.** *{I am not mad, most noble Festus}* Paul is not transported with anger for Festus’ censure of him; but being more solicitous to free them, by whom he was bound, from the bonds of sin and Satan, than himself from their chains, he replies meekly, and as it became him to speak to a governor: though Festus were unworthy, yet for God’s ordinance sake, and lest the cause of religion might otherwise have suffered, he regards him: neither was Paul so much careful for the defense of his own reputation (see 1 Cor. 4. 3. 2 Cor. 5. 13) as for the truth of God, which indeed is of the highest and most serious concernment: it is truly the greatest madness of the world, wittingly and willingly to neglect that, in which is our eternal weal or woe: a soft answer turns away wrath, Prov. 15. 1. Meekness and patience of teachers much advances the truth of Christ. See 2 Tim. 2. 24, 25, 26. So it is in any weighty undertaking, to be master of oneself, subject only to right reason, not to disturbed affections.

**V. 26.** *{For the King knoweth}* Being a Jew, and always conversant in Judaea, he might know the prophecies, and those things which were there done and suffered by Christ in his life, death, and resurrection.

**V. 27.** *{that thou believest}* Meaning, that the king was persuaded of the truth of those things which were written by the prophets.

**V. 28.** *{almost}* ἐν ὀλίγῳ *in a little*, or *within a little*: this he speaks being forced to acknowledge the power of the gospel, which secular respects would not suffer to be fruitful in him.

**V. 29.** *{both almost}* As verse 28.

*{altogether}* ἐν πολλῷ, *in much*, in everything which concerns the accomplishment of a true Christian. Almost persuaded to be a Christian; is no better than to be a lukewarm Laodicean, Rev. 3. 16, or an Israelite halting between God and Baal, 1 Kings 18. 21. God will admit no partners in his service: either you must be entirely and altogether his, or you are not his at all.

*{except these bonds}* He attemperates this speech to them, who yet knew not that bonds, and suffering for the truth of Christ, was a high honor, whereof elsewhere he glories and rejoices, Ephes. 6. 20. 2 Tim. 2. 9. See also chap. 5. 41. The Romans used thus to produce their prisoners with a soldier guardian, and some chain on the prisoner, chap. 28. 20. 2 Tim. 1. 16.

**V. 30.** *{the King rose up}* Having dismissed the company who attended or came to hear; the king, president, and Bernice, with many others, depart nothing better than they came hither. So goes the black-Moor into the bath, coming out from washing, of the same complexion: they whose hearts are filled with love of the world, and secular businesses, impatiently hear the gospel; it is presently tedious to them.

**V. 31.** *{nothing worthy of death}* Thus the very heathen judges still acquitted him in their opinions, to the condemnation of the malicious Jews. As also of themselves, who finding in all their examinations that he was innocent, yet to please impious men, would not acquit and deliver him. Judges ought to take heed what they do, for they *judge not for man, but for the Lord.*—2 Chron. 19. 6.

**V. 32.** *{then Agrippa said}* Agrippa (of their governors most versed in these things) also justifies Paul: what got the Jews by their travel and importunity, but their own just condemnation, in the manifestation of Paul’s innocency? The enemies of the gospel and of the ministers thereof, do but justify, and (to the servants of Christ undoubted advantage) honor them the more.

## CHAPTER 27

**Verse 1.** *{Paul and certain other prisoners}* Thus do the saints share with the wicked in things secular and external. God the righteous judge will at last set them in conditions very different, Mat. 25. 34, 41, 46. Luke 16. 25.

*{a centurion}* A captain of a hundred footmen.

*{Augustus’ band}* The band or cohort was the tenth part of a Roman legion: and these bands or cohorts were of several numbers, names, and uses: the first, wherein the standard was, consisted of 1105 footmen, and 132 horsemen, etc. They used to have a pretorian band, or cohort, to guard the magistrate or governor in his province; this here mentioned, seems to be such as Augustus appointed to some the like offices. See *Vegetius de re militar.* lib. 2, chap. 7.

**V. 2.** *{Adramyttium}* A famous city and port of Mysia, *Plin.* lib. 5, chap. 30. It was formerly called Pedasus. Mysia was by the Hellespont near Troas, *Ger. Mercat. Atl. Asia min.*

*{Aristarchus}* It seems he only and Luke accompanied Paul in this journey, of all his disciples.

**V. 3.** *{at Sidon}* A city of Phoenicia: in times past it was a frontier-town of the Canaanites to the northward, near the mount Libanus, *Jerome de locis Heb. Act.*

*{courteously entreated Paul}* χρησάμενος, the word now used, is a particip. aor. 1. med. of χράω, *commodo, commodato, do*; to lend, as Luke 11. 5, to accommodate or to profit, to do good or pleasure to; Syr. *usus est Centurio benevolentia erga Paulum*; upon the matter the same, which *he courteously entreated Paul*. Thus God raised up friends to the afflicted, for he is with them according to his promise. See chap. 7. 9, 10. Hebr. 13. 5, 6. Josh. 1. 5.

**V. 4.** *{under Cyprus}* See chap. 11. 19. A great island in the Mediterranean Sea. See *G. Mercat. Atl. Plin.* lib. 5. chap. 31.

**V. 5.** *{Cilicia}* See on chap. 6. 9 and *Plin.* lib. 5. chap. 27.

*{Pamphylia}* Chap. 13. 13. *Plin.* lib. 5. chap. 27.

*{Myra a city}* A chief city of Lycia, as Strabo reckons it. See *Plin.* lib. 5. chap. 27.

*{of Lycia}* A province of Asia. *Jerome. de loc. Hebr. Act.* Pliny says it had once sixty towns, lib. 5. chap. 27.

**V. 6.** *{of Alexandria}* A city of Egypt, which was anciently called No, Jer. 46. 25, etc., between Egypt and Africa, where the evangelist Mark was buried, *Jerom. de loc. Heb. Act.* and *de scriptor. eccl.* G. Mercator. Atl. says, it was in times past a noble and beautiful city, built by Alexander the Great, in the bottom of the straights, or end of the Mediterranean Sea. The Turks now call it Scanderia.

**V. 7.** *{slowly many days}* The wind being against them, as verse 4, or very bare.

*{Cnidus}* A little island over against Caria.

*{Crete}* Since named Candie: it is a Grecian island, once abounding in wealth, and glorious for the number of a hundred towns. *Jerom. de loc. Hebr. Act.* See *G. Mercat. Atl. Creta*, and *Plin.* lib. 4. chap. 12.

*{Salmone}* This was an eastern promontory, or high cliff of Crete. See *Strab.* lib. 2.

**V. 8.** *{the fair havens}* It seems so called of the safety and commodiousness of the haven, or road; as we call a road of ours, the Downs.

*{Lasea}* A sea-town and city of Crete, *Jerom. de loc. Hebr. Act.*

**V. 9.** *{the fast was}* He reckons the time of the year, after the custom of the Jews, which the Syriac expresses, *the fast which the Jews were wont to observe*: hereof see Levit. 16. 29 and 23. 27. Numb. 29. 7. This was about October: so that Paul advised them to winter there, rather than to put to sea in the deep of winter, then at hand; wherein (as *Vegetius* notes *de re militar.* lib. 4. chap. 39) there is little light, and tedious nights, thick clouds, dark weather, rages of wind, and storms, hindering travel by sea and land: the heathen said well, καιρὸς δ’ ἐπὶ πᾶσιν ἄριστος. *Hesiod. op. & di.* *opportunity is best in all things*.

**V. 10.** *{hurt and etc.}* μεθ’ ὕβρεως, *with injury*: the interpreter of the Syriac gives it, *with trouble*.

*{but also of our lives}* With much danger of our lives: it had been with loss of their lives, had not God (as Oecumenius says) for Paul’s sake preserved all the rest. τῶν ψυχῶν ἡμῶν, of our souls, that is, a metonymy of the efficient for the effect, usual among the Hebrews.

**V. 11.** *{the centurion believed the master}* Not knowing Paul to be inspired by the Holy Ghost, but conceiving him to be no seaman, he believed the master of the ship in his own faculty; also the haven was not commodious: this man had at least some appearance of reason for this his belief; what reason have they, who in danger of their souls’ shipwreck, will most believe them who have least skill and experience in preaching the word for saving of souls?

**V. 12.** *{Phenice}* A port in Crete, not Phenicea a region in Syria.

**V. 13.** *{loosing thence}* Weighing anchor, and setting sail thence, ἄραντες, ἆσσον παρελέγοντο, the word ἆσσον is not here a name of a place; but is put for ἐγγὺς or πλησίον; *near*, or *nearer*: as if he said close aboard the shore of Crete, or Candie. See chap. 20. 13, 14.

*{they sailed}* παρελέγοντο, which the Latins give, *legebant*, or *praeterlegebant*, *Virg. Aen.* 3. *Litoraque Epiri legimus*, it has many significations, as Lyranus notes in this distich.

*Fur aurum, virgo flores, mare navita, libros
Clericus; aequivocè singula quisque legit.*

In the dialect of English mariners, *they went close aboard the shore*.

**V. 14.** *{there arose}* Or *beat*.

*{against it}* Against Crete, so that it carried them off to sea from Crete (which was now a windward shore) to Melita, which is between Africa and Sicilie.

*{Euroclydon}* A northeast wind, furious and stormy, usual about those places, in the beginning of winter: some call it, the seaman’s plague.

**V. 15.** *{when the ship was caught}* With this sudden storm, so they (not having time to take in their sails) were in danger of oversetting.

*{could not bear up into the wind}* A ship is said ἀντοφθαλμεῖν τῷ ἀνέμῳ, to wrestle with the wind (say the interpreters) which must needs be when she has the wind against her, so that her prow must encounter with adverse billows, and head-seas, or meet the sea right ahead; some think the original of the phrase was from that part of the prow which was called ὀφθαλμός, an eye: we have in use with us, *to make up into the eye of the wind*, (which here is ἀντοφθαλμεῖν) by divers tacks, and making sundry boards, to turn to the windward: that is, by standing sometimes one way, and sometimes another, for the gaining a place to windward; which here this ship could not do the violence of the storm and great head-seas driving them still to the leeward, and hindering her way.

*{we let her drive}* A ship is said to drive, when the anchor cannot hold her, but that she falls away with the wind or tide. So also when a ship is at hull, or a trie, that is, has taken in all her sails, and tied down the helm to the lee-side of the ship, going as wind and tide carry her: we say she drives, etc., or is adrift to leeward.

**V. 16.** *{Clauda}* A little island. *Plinie* lib. 4. chap. 12 calls it Gaudos: Ptolome and others Claudos.

*{to come by the boat}* To recover, take up, or save the boat.

**V. 17.** *{undergirding the ship}* This under-girding is by trusses, which are ropes made fast to the yards; used either to bind fast the yard to the mast, when the ship rolls a hull, or at an anchor, or to hale down the yards in a gust or storm.

*{into the quicksands}* Certain vast banks or mountains of sands in the sea, which swallow up ships that stick fast and sit upon them a little time; and so will the Goodwin sands, etc.

**V. 18.** *{they lightened the ship}* By casting the goods overboard.

**V. 19.** *{the tackling of the ship}* The Greek word σκευή, signifies store, munition and necessary furniture; and provision for ships. So Jon. 1. 5, *they cast overboard* את הכלים *eth hachalim*: *their instruments or furniture*, 70. ἐκβολὴν ἐποιήσαντο τῶν σκευῶν; the very word here used: so Syr. interpret. *vasa ipsius navis, etc.* See 2 Kings 11. 8, 11.

**V. 20.** *{all hope that we should be saved}* Meaning in respect of second causes, and man’s reason; κατὰ τὴν φύσιν, as Oecumenius speaks, it must have been so in the course of nature, that they had perished.

**V. 21.** *{have gained this harm}* That is, *prevented it*; as the Syr. interprets it, *avoided it*; in the conclusion, it will ever appear a gain to hearken to Christ’s ministers.

**V. 22.** *{of any man’s life}* Ψυχῆς: soul, for life, as verse 10. With such admirable confidence in the truth of God, who had revealed this unto him, verse 24, he comforts them, lest in despair of life they should neglect the means of their preservation.

*{but of the ship}* This he declares to them, that when they saw all this truly come to pass, they might have the more reverend esteem of the gospel which he preached, and for which he now suffered.

**V. 23.** *{the Angel of God}* Whom he sent to comfort and assure me; lest they should think he vainly promised them security, he confirms that which he said by alleging a divine authority.

*{whom I serve}* He professes to profane men, that he served God, who was able to deliver and would deliver him and them: they knew that he was now in bonds for his religion’s sake: now hearing that God had a care of him, sent his angel to him, and after seeing that he and themselves were according to his word delivered from the (otherwise inevitable) danger, they might easily gather, that God justified his religion, and that Paul served the true God.

**V. 24.** *{God hath given thee all them that sail with thee}* Or *given thee as a favour*. So the Greek κεχάρισταί σοι signifies, freely to give, as Rom. 8. 32. Gal. 3. 18. Phil. 1. 29. Acts 3. 14. 1 Cor. 2. 12. You have prayed for them, I have heard you, and will for your sake, save their lives. So God heard Lot for Zoar. Gen. 19. 19, etc. God preserves the wicked from temporal destruction, for the righteous’ sake, who are among them, and intercede for them.

**V. 25.** *{for I believed God}* I am confident out of good experience, that God will certainly perform his promise.

**V. 26.** *{we must be cast}* That they might not doubt of the truth of God’s promise, he foretells them, the manner and means by which they shall be preserved: thus it will fall out that we shall be cast upon an island: this he tells them that they might ascribe their delivery to God, not chance; so the ancient prophets were wont to foretell certain signs of things by them spoken, which should come to pass, that they whom it concerned might know that God had spoken by them, and would certainly bring it to pass. See 1 Sam. 10. 2, etc.

**V. 27.** *{in the Adria, etc.}* In the Adriatic Sea which divided Italy from Dalmatia, and reaches up to Venice.

**V. 28.** *{and sounded}* To find the depth of the waters by casting out a plummet of lead fastened to a line many fathoms long.

**V. 29.** *{out of the stern}* Or *hinder part of the ship*, to stay the course or way of the ship, lest falling upon some coast in the dark, in their shipwreck they should all have been cast away.

**V. 30.** *{out of the foreship}* The prow, or foremost part of the ship.

**V. 31.** *{except these abide in the ship}* Now because the will of God, or his power to save, or the effect of his promise depended on second causes; but because he that had ordained the end, ordained the means also; and that he would not have them tempt God, by dividing that which he had united.

**V. 32.** *{Cut off the ropes}* The centurion and soldiers following Paul’s counsel, cut off all hope of the seamen’s going away from the ship, and deserting them; they cut the boat-rope, and let her drive away.

**V. 33.** *{continued fasting}* He means, they ate no set meals; or so little in their perpetual danger and fear of being cast away, that it was in a manner, a fasting.

**V. 34.** *{hair fall from the head}* By this Hebrew phrase is meant, that they should all come safe and sound ashore (see 1 Sam. 14. 45. 1 King. 1. 52. Mat. 10. 30) without the loss of a man.

**V. 35.** *{he took bread and gave thanks}* Not only praying God to give a blessing, and to sanctify those creatures to their nourishment; to present their thanks to him for the same, and to acknowledge his good providence in bestowing them upon them; but also for their preservation from the great danger which they had run: and exhorting them to refresh themselves, in confidence of God’s future providence according to his word, he shows them his own example; we must not in dangers and afflictions, measure our condition by present sense, but consider God’s power, and promises to deliver us; it is a happy teaching, where ministers’ own actions, exemplify their doctrine.

**V. 36.** *{all of good cheer, etc.}* So great a comfort and blessing is one, or a few good men in a company: while they were all safe, and out of fear, Paul lay aboard like a poor despised prisoner, but now in the danger God makes him the only counselor, and comforter of all the company; common dangers, and afflictions, make the saints more evidently shine.

**V. 37.** *{two hundred threescore and sixteen, etc.}* This sets off the good providence of God, that of so many men, none perished in their tumultuous swimming ashore: it must teach men in midst of dangers to cast themselves upon the providence of God.

**V. 38.** *{they lightened the ship}* By casting her lading overboard : they resolved with the philosopher in the like case, *better these perish for us, than with us*.

**V. 39.** *{they knew not the land}* They could keep no good reckoning, by reason of the violent storm, dark weather, variable sets of tides, and possibly many shifts of winds: so discovering the land, knew not where they were.

*{to thrust in the ship}* They would there run her ashore, that they might save their lives, by getting ashore upon some planks or broken pieces of timber, or otherwise.

**V. 40.** *{taken up the anchors}* Or *cut the cables*, (to which the anchors were fastened) *in the hawse*: for in a storm, and such wrought seas, they could not weigh anchor: Syr. ופסקו *vapesaku*, *and they cut off the anchors*; the same word is given verse 20, all hope that we should be saved אתפסק *ethpesek* *was cut off*; Greek περιῃρεῖτο, *was taken away*.

*{loosed the rudder bands}* The rudder, is that piece of timber, which hanging at the stern-post of the ship, is as a bridle to direct the course; this they had bound when they would drive (verse 15) or let the ship go whither wind and tide would carry her; but now, because they would have it to direct the ship, that they might with the best advantage for their lives, run her ashore, they loose it again.

*{and hoised up the mainsail}* Which before they had struck, by reason of the violence of the storm; now they hoist it, that the ship might feel the wind, and work for their running ashore.

**V. 41.** *{broken with the violence, etc.}* So that nothing now remained, but that everyone should shift for his life, and swim ashore: thus God showed his delivering power in extreme hazards.

**V. 42.** *{the soldiers’ counsel was to kill, etc.}* They show their cruel ingratitude to Paul, who had comforted them, and for whose sake their lives were saved: this has been the common lot of the prophets, apostles, ministers, and generally the saints of God: let it trouble no good man to receive evil for the good he has done, it is a vulgar disease; let them know that when men requite evil for good, that yet their labor is with the Lord. See Luke 14. 13, 14.

**V. 43.** *{But the Centurion willing to save Paul}* God put into his heart, to defeat this more than barbarous counsel of soldiers: thus God saves Paul by the centurion, who was preserved for Paul’s sake: piety never dies unrewarded: it is a happy thing to do all the good we can; here again mark the present benefit which the other prisoners had for Paul’s sake: it is a sufficient motive to make men ambitious of the acquaintance and society of God’s children.

*{cast themselves first into the sea}* That they might be the more helpful to get the rest ashore; also to avoid the danger of one drowning another, if the multitude should all have leaped overboard together.

**V. 44.** *{some on boards, etc.}* Who could not swim without.

*{they escaped all safe}* Thus God ever fulfills his promises: thus his goodness overcomes man’s malice and impious counsels; reserving the wicked to other times, whose unbelief and ungrateful cruelty, willing to shed blood, deserved that they should never have come to shore again: the long-suffering and goodness of God should lead men to repentance, and teach them David’s use of God’s favors. Psal. 107. 15. *Oh that men would praise the Lord for his goodness, etc.*

## CHAPTER 28

**Verse 1.** *{Called Melita}* Now called Malta; an island between Africa and Sicilie; there is another of the same name in the Adriatic bay, between Italy and Epidaurus. See *D. Heylin Μικρόκοσμος: mediterran. Isles.* 448, etc., and *Plin.* lib. 3. chap. 9 and 4. 26.

**V. 2.** *{barbarous people}* They were so called who spoke neither Greek nor Latin, and were more rudely bred, or of fierce and evil manners, uncivil, ungodly, inhumane and cruel. *Cic. ad Q. fr.* lib. 1. *quod si te sors Afris, aut Hispanis, aut Gallis praefecisset, immanibus ac barbaris nationibus*: so *Virg.* *barbarus has segetes?* See 1 Cor. 14. 11.

*{shewed us no little kindness}* φιλανθρωπίαν humanity: so Suidas’ interpreter gives φιλανθρωπηθείς *humaniter exceptus*: and *[?]udaeus*§3 φιλανθρωπούμενος, *in comitatem benignitatemque compositus*: some despise the needy and calamitous, adding affliction to affliction, but these men (esteemed barbarous) were more humane to those who had lost ship and goods in the sea: Christians should not be ashamed to imitate their mercy, but to come short of it. See Hebr. 13. 3. It is no more honor to those who are barbarous to be called humane, than dishonor to the humane to be called barbarous: it is less evil to be styled evil, than to be so: be better than your name, show yourself good indeed, and you may securely despise fame. See James 2. 16.

**V. 3.** *{a viper out of the heat}* Thus God pleased by a new miracle to confirm Paul’s authority, thereby to prepare the barbarous to receive the gospel.

**V. 4.** *{a murderer}* This was rash judgment; according to the common custom of the world; who usually account the afflicted wicked; this principle sticks fast in the consciences of the most barbarous, that there is a divine providence which justly punishes the wicked and injurious, because therefore murder is a chief crime, they conclude that he was a murderer, whom so eminent a revenge followed.

*{yet vengeance suffereth not to live}* Vengeance they took to be a goddess, named δίκη; the same with Nemesis, which they called τῶν ἀλαζόνων τιμωρὸς, the revenger of the proud. *Suid. Νέμεσις.*

**V. 5.** *{shook off the beast, etc.}* That is the viper; which hung on his hand: the physicians called all such creatures which leave their venom in a wound which they bite or sting θηρία, especially the viper.

*{felt no harm}* The power of God, the Lord of nature and all creatures, restraining the venom; so that it could not hurt Paul: here Christ’s promise to believers in the primitive church, Mark 16. 18, was fulfilled in Paul.

**V. 6.** *{swollen and fallen down dead}* So deadly is their poison, especially in those hot countries, that it presently invades the heart and vital parts, and those that are bitten presently die: the more evident was the miracle in Paul’s preservation.

**V. 7.** *{Publius}* By this name he seemed to be a Roman, and that he entertaining the centurion, Paul and his company were invited with him: thus God has everywhere a care of his.

**V. 8.** *{prayed and laid his hands on him}* Praying, he showed that not his power, but God’s, restored this man: laying on his hands was a form of blessing; hospitality is commended by many examples. Gen. 18. 2. Gen. 19. 1. 1 Kings 17. 9. 2 Kings 4. 8. Exod. 2. 20. Luke 10. 38, and here by a special reward thereof.

**V. 9.** *{others also came and were healed}* For very probably the fame hereof quickly spread about the island. But why did not he who shook off the viper, and cured diseases, shake off his own bonds? We must know that Paul was not the author, but minister in the miracle; acting only where, when, how, and on whom the Spirit of God would: and miracles were not wrought for the ease of their persons, who did them, so much as for the confirmation of their ministry; for which God sometimes miraculously delivered them, chap. 5. 19. Chap. 12. 7. Sometimes he left them in bonds, where those bonds might advance the gospel. Phil. 1. 12.

**V. 10.** *{laded us with such things, etc.}* Furnished them with victuals, and other necessaries; this was their gratitude toward Paul, for the great benefits which they had received by his curing their sick: they much honored him, and accommodated him and those that were with him, with necessaries for their voyage: and this was a fruit of their faith.

**V. 11.** *{whose sign was}* Such as ships usually have whereby they may be the better known, and according to which sometimes they are named, and their bills of lading, or other commissions to them appertaining are formed.

*{Castor and Pollux}* The Paynims feigned these to be gods of the sea.

**V. 12.** *{at Syracuse}* A famous city of Sicilia.

**V. 13.** *{to Rhegium}* A city in Italy over against Messana of Sicilia.

*{to Puteoli}* A sea-town in the kingdom of Naples.

**V. 14.** *{we found brethren}* Whether these brethren were Christians before Paul came to them or not, seems not manifest, for the Jews though not yet converted, are elsewhere styled *brethren*, that is, *kinsmen according to the flesh*—*Israelites*, Rom. 9. 3, 4 (see also Acts 7. 1 and 22. 1 and 23. 1, etc.), so here verse 17. Paul calls them brethren, who yet verse 22 naming Christianity a sect, *every where spoken against*, seem not to have then been acquainted with the most wholesome and comfortable mysteries thereof, concerning which they desired to be informed by Paul; neither seems it probable that the faith (if it had been received in Italy, so near Rome as was Puteoli) should not by some means have been propagated to the Jews at Rome.

**V. 15.** *{Appii forum}* A market town upon a long highway; to the seaward, which Appius caused his soldiers to make good; by the which there stood three taverns, about a day’s journey from Rome.

**V. 16.** *{the Centurion}* Or *governor of the pretory or judgment-hall*.

*{suffered to dwell}* Paul was not put into the common prison; which favor God gave him for his comfort, and the advancement of the gospel.

**V. 17.** *{Paul called the chief of the Jews}* Who dwelt then at Rome; here again appeared the constant love of Paul to Israel; who, notwithstanding their many grievous injuries done him; would yet again adventure to preach Christ unto them, that they might be saved.

**V. 18.** *{when they had examined, etc.}* That is, when the Roman deputies and governors of Judaea had examined Paul: thus he endeavors to present the truth unto them, and for their better reception of himself, and it, to prevent the prejudicate opinion concerning his own person, lest that should disadvantage the gospel.

**V. 19.** *{not that I had ought to accuse him, etc.}* That is, I appealed to Caesar only to defend my innocency: nor purpose I to make any use of this accusation, to exasperate Caesar’s former evil opinion of the Jews.

**V. 20.** *{for the hope}* That is, for Jesus Christ his cause, whom they had long looked for, and who had now showed himself their Redeemer, in whom only the Israel of God, that is, all true believers (Rom. 2. 29. Gal. 3. 7, 9) repose all their hope and confidence of salvation. See chap. 4. 12.

**V. 21.** *{we neither received letters, etc.}* The high priests had little hope that they could reach him there at Rome, therefore probably they were silent.

**V. 32.**† *{concerning this sect}* See on chap. 26. 5, meaning, this profession of Christianity.

*{every where it is spoken against}* By the governors and people, Jews and Gentiles: here appears the truth and power of God, in the propagation of that gospel, which had the multitude, power, authority and wisdom; yea, and the seeming sanctity of the world, everywhere against it: what natural man would not at least have suspected that which all condemned? That must therefore be the truth of God, which so much, so universally, so constantly flourishes in spite of men.

**V. 23.** *{kingdom of God}* That the kingdom spoken of by the prophets, now was offered unto them by Christ. Which Paul, by competent and sufficient arguments cited out of Moses and the prophets, declared and proved unto them.

**V. 24.** *{some believed}* The gospel, like the seed, Mat. 13, finds divers soils, and brings forth according to the receiver; the same sun hardens the clay, and softens the wax. See 2 Thess. 3. 2. Heb. 4. 2.

**V. 25.** *{when they agreed not, etc.}* Here is the fruit of dissension, but accidental to the gospel, which causes not, but shows the secret wickedness of men’s hearts, as the sun shows, but causes not the diversities of colors which lie concealed and indistinct in the dark. See Luke 2. 34. Mat. 10. 34. John 10. 19.

*{well spake the Holy Ghost}* When he could by no fair means win them, he denounces the judgment which God had threatened them, Isaiah 6. 9, which the authority of the Holy Ghost declared to be most certain, 2 Pet. 1. 21.

**V. 26.** *{go unto this people}* Isaiah 6. 9. Mat. 13. 14. Mark 4. 12. Luke 8. 10. John 12. 14. Rom. 11. 8.

**V. 27.** *{they have closed}* They are in willful ignorance, and willingly know not; as, 2 Pet. 3. 5. Therefore God gives them over to strong delusions, to believe lies, to their own destruction. 2 Thess. 2. 11, 12.

**V. 28.** *{the salvation of God}* Τὸ σωτήριον τοῦ θεοῦ. So Luke 2. 30, which is revealed in the gospel by God, showing his counsel therein, and the powerful effect thereof by his Holy Spirit, to every believer.

**V. 20.**† *{the Jews departed}* So hypocrites and self-flatterers beguile their own souls, being impatient of all wholesome reproof, and offended at the truth. Gal. 4. 16.

*{had great reasonings}* This shows that some believed, in that they gainsaid the unbelievers.

**V. 30.** *{received all, etc.}* He was not ignorant, how much silence would have secured him from the malice of the Jews; but he valued not his life; so that he might finish his charge which was committed to him. See chap. 21. 13. 1 Cor. 9. 16.

**V. 31.** *{preaching the kingdom of God}* That is, as the Syr. has it, *concerning the kingdom of God*: and how we are interested therein by the free grace and mercy of God in Christ Jesus; in whom we receive remission of sins, regeneration, reconciliation, adoption of sons, faith, sanctification, patience; perseverance, obsignation and sealing up to redemption; and shall have the end of our hopes, the salvation of our bodies and souls in his kingdom of glory.

*{with all confidence, no man forbidding him}* Not because the Jews had lost their malice, but because God who shut the greedy lions’ mouths: Dan. 6, had now shut theirs: and he who brought out Israel from the incensed Egypt, in such a calm, that not a dog moved his tongue against them, now at present gave Paul freedom from the strife of tongues: well might he say, *the word of God is not bound.* 2 Tim. 2. 9. If you should endeavor to bind up the sunbeams, or silence the whispering winds, you could not prevail; nor can any creature, against that Spirit of God which breathes when, and where it lists. John 3. 8.

*Now unto the King Eternal, Immortal, Invisible, the only wise God, be honor and glory for ever and ever; to his Church peace: truth, and salvation, to all eternity, through Jesus Christ our Lord and only Savior. Amen.*
